Chapter 0-1: Introduction & Background

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p3-p17

Learning Objectives for This Section

  • Understand the historical significance of the FDA endorsement letter and the collaboration between the ISPE Baseline Guide series and the FDA
  • Understand why the Baseline Guide series came about: why industry needed a unified set of engineering design standards
  • Grasp the Scope of this Guide: new and renovated pharmaceutical water and steam systems
  • Become familiar with the four key concepts: the water quality definition methodology, Critical Process Parameters, Good Engineering Practice, and Design Options
  • Get to know the structure of the whole Guide (Chapters 1-10 + Appendix) and build an overall learning map

FDA Letter

Original Text

October 27, 2000

Dear Colleagues:

The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) has been pleased to cooperate with the International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering in the development of the Baseline® Pharmaceutical Engineering Guide for Water and Steam Systems. We appreciate the cooperative efforts and the dedicated intensive work of the engineers who voluntarily initiated the development of this Guide. This is an excellent example of how through public and private cooperative efforts, both industry and consumers can benefit.

This document covers engineering aspects of design, construction and operation of new water and steam systems. It expands on existing FDA guidance on water systems.

This Guide is solely created and owned by ISPE. It is not an FDA regulation, standard or guidance document and water and steam systems built in conformance with this Guide may or may not meet FDA requirements. FDA has provided comments for ISPE's consideration in preparing this Guide. It should be helpful to the engineering profession and the industry for the design, construction and operation of new water and steam systems.

FDA is pleased with the development of this document and we look forward to a continued working relationship as future Baseline® Pharmaceutical Engineering Guides are developed.

Sincerely,

Janet Woodcock, M.D.
Director, Center for Drug Evaluation and Research

Dennis Baker
Associate Commissioner for Regulatory Affairs

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: What FDA Endorsement Means

This letter was co-signed by Janet Woodcock, Director of FDA CDER, and Dennis Baker, Associate Commissioner for Regulatory Affairs. In the pharmaceutical world, this is a top-level signal of "recognition."

But note the key sentence: "This Guide is solely created and owned by ISPE. It is not an FDA regulation." An ISPE guide is not a regulation. Following it does not mean automatic compliance, and not following it does not mean a violation.

Analogy: The Michelin Guide vs. Food Safety Law

The ISPE Baseline Guide is to FDA regulations what the "Michelin Guide" is to the "Act Governing Food Safety and Sanitation." The Michelin Guide tells you how to run a great restaurant (best practice), but the real legal obligations come from the food safety law.

FDA's attitude is: "We have reviewed this Guide and think it is good, but in the end you are responsible for compliance."

Practical Points for CDMOs

During a customer audit or FDA inspection, if the auditor asks, "What standard is your water system design based on?", you can answer: "We referenced ISPE Baseline Guide Volume 4 and ensure compliance under cGMP 21 CFR 211."

The key point: an ISPE guide is a "best practice" reference, not a "compliance shield."

Historical Context: The Industry Background in 2000

The letter was written in October 2000. The problems facing the pharmaceutical industry at the time were:

  • Companies interpreted regulatory requirements inconsistently, leading to "arms race" style over-design
  • The cost of new facilities kept soaring, and time to market grew longer
  • The FDA also hoped to see unified engineering standards to reduce inspection disputes

This is exactly why the Baseline Guide series was born.

Foreword

Original Text

For many years, the pharmaceutical industry has experienced increases in the cost of new facilities. These increases in cost have been driven in part by uncertainty about the requirements for regulatory compliance. Some significant areas of concern are validation, particularly related to automation systems, and the trend to validate back to source utilities. The absence of a consistent and widely accepted interpretation of some regulatory requirements has led to one-upmanship. This practice of building increasingly technically advanced facilities has led to increased cost, longer lead times and, in some cases, delays in bringing new products to market.

In May 1994, engineering representatives from the pharmaceutical industry engaged in a discussion with the International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering (ISPE) and the Food and Drug Administration (FDA). As a result of that discussion in November 1994, ISPE began work on nine facility engineering Guides, now known as the Baseline® Pharmaceutical Engineering Guides. The first, "Bulk Pharmaceutical Chemicals," was published in June 1996. The second, "Oral Solid Dosage Forms," was published in February 1998. The third, "Sterile Manufacturing Facilities," was published in February 1999. This is the fourth such Guide, covering Pharmaceutical Water and Steam Systems. Each Engineering Guide was created by, and is owned solely by ISPE. FDA provided comments on this and previous Guides, and many of their suggestions have been incorporated.

As with the BPC Guide, OSD, and Sterile Guide, the Water and Steam Systems Guide has been sponsored by ISPE's Pharmaceutical Advisory Council, made up of senior pharmaceutical engineering executives from owner companies, and ISPE senior management. Overall planning, direction, and technical guidance in the preparation of the Water and Steam Systems Guide was provided by a Steering Committee most of whom were involved in the BPC Guide. The Water and Steam Systems Guide itself was produced by a Task Team of individuals who expended a great deal of their own time in its preparation and development.

The Water and Steam Systems Appendix contains material considered "informational" which, although necessary, would have been detrimental to the clarity of the dedicated chapter. The Appendix has not been reviewed by and therefore is not endorsed by the FDA.

Editors' Disclaimer: This Guide is meant to assist pharmaceutical manufacturers in the design and construction of new and renovated facilities that are required to comply with the requirements of the Food and Drug Administration (FDA). The International Society for Pharmaceutical Engineering (ISPE) cannot ensure, and does not warrant, that a facility built in accordance with this Guide will be acceptable to FDA.

Tutorial Commentary

The Story Behind the Baseline Guide Series

In May 1994, representatives of pharmaceutical industry engineers sat down with ISPE and the FDA to discuss one question: "Can we develop a set of engineering design standards that everyone accepts?"

The result was a publication plan for 9 Baseline Guides:

  • Vol.1 - Bulk Pharmaceutical Chemicals (1996)
  • Vol.2 - Oral Solid Dosage Forms (1998)
  • Vol.3 - Sterile Manufacturing Facilities (1999)
  • Vol.4 - Water and Steam Systems (2001) <- this Guide

Analogy: Building Codes for a House

Imagine you are building a house. The building code (= cGMP) says "the house must not collapse," but it does not tell you how to run the pipework or which materials are best. So the architects' association publishes a "best construction guide" (= Baseline Guide) so that everyone has a consistent reference.

Before the Baseline Guides existed, each pharmaceutical company interpreted the regulations on its own, resulting in a "one-upmanship" mentality: "If others did it, I must do it too, and do even more."

Key Reminder: The Appendix Is Not FDA-Endorsed

The Foreword states clearly that the Appendix was not reviewed by the FDA and therefore does not carry FDA endorsement.

This matters in practice. If you cite Appendix content as a design basis during an audit, the other side may say, "That is only reference information and does not mean FDA recognition." We recommend treating the Appendix as an engineering reference, while the core design basis remains the main chapters.

CDMO Scenario

You are the engineering head of a CDMO designing the water system for a new facility. The customer requires "compliance with the ISPE Baseline Guide." How should you respond?

  • First confirm: the ISPE guide is a best-practice reference, not a compliance standard
  • Discuss with the customer: the actual compliance standards are 21 CFR 211 and the USP monographs
  • Use the ISPE guide as the design framework, but every critical decision must have a cGMP regulatory basis
  • Remember: over-engineering is itself the problem the Baseline Guide set out to solve

Tutorial Commentary

Why Read the Acknowledgements?

Reading the acknowledgements page is more than politeness. It tells you the "DNA" of this document:

  • Author composition: The Core Team came from Bristol-Myers Squibb, CRB (engineering consultant), Kvaerner (engineering contractor) and Kinetics (equipment supplier), covering four key roles: owner, design, construction and equipment
  • FDA review team: Six FDA officials took part in the review, including Richard Friedman of the CDER Office of Compliance, who later became a key figure in FDA water system inspections
  • Funding sponsors: 13 large pharmaceutical companies provided funding, including Merck, Pfizer, Eli Lilly and BMS

The Industry Collaboration Model

This model of "competitors sitting down together to write a standard" is common in the pharmaceutical industry. Imagine engineers from Toyota, Honda and BMW writing a "best practice guide for car engines" together: they compete in the market, but cooperate on safety standards.

Pharmaceutical water systems are the same: every company has its own water system, but everyone needs a consistent design baseline.

CRB（Clark, Richardson & Biskup）

Note that CRB took part in several chapters. CRB is one of the leading pharmaceutical engineering consulting firms in North America and is still active today. If your CDMO needs an external engineering consultant to design a water system, CRB is one of the industry's top choices.

1.1 Background

Original Text

The design, construction, and validation (commissioning and qualification) of water and steam systems for the pharmaceutical industry represent key opportunities for manufacturers, engineering professionals, and equipment suppliers. These systems are required to meet current Good Manufacturing Practice (cGMP) regulations while remaining in compliance with all other governing codes, laws, and regulations.

The cost of bringing these systems on line is highly variable, owing to interpretation of regulatory requirements and overly conservative design approaches. This Guide is intended to offer a practical, consistent interpretation, while still allowing flexibility and innovation.

This Guide was prepared by ISPE, with feedback from industry representatives from all areas and disciplines, and comments provided by FDA. It reflects ISPE's current thinking related to engineering of new water and steam systems.

It is recognized that industry standards evolve, and this document reflects the understanding of them as of the publication date.

Tutorial Commentary

The Water System = The "Circulatory System" of a Pharmaceutical Plant

In an aseptic manufacturing environment, the importance of the water system cannot be overstated:

  • WFI (Water for Injection) is a direct component of the final product
  • Purified Water is used for equipment cleaning and compounding
  • Clean Steam is used for equipment sterilization (SIP)

If the water system has a problem, the entire production line must shut down. This is why ISPE made water systems their own Baseline Guide.

The Root of the Cost Problem

Imagine buying a car. If the specification only says "must be safe," some people will buy a Toyota Corolla and others will buy an armored vehicle. The difference can be 10 times.

Pharmaceutical water systems are the same: when the regulation only says "comply with cGMP" and gives no clear engineering standard, conservative engineers choose the "armored vehicle" option. The goal of the Baseline Guide is to tell you: "A Toyota Corolla is enough, and here are the specifics."

Note the Wording: "Validation (Commissioning and Qualification)"

The original text places commissioning and qualification in parentheses after validation. This was a new idea in 2001: validation is not just IQ/OQ/PQ but also includes commissioning.

ISPE later published a full guide dedicated to this topic, Vol.5 (Commissioning and Qualification).

1.2 Scope of This Guide

Original Text

This Guide is intended for the design, construction, and operation of new water and steam systems. It is neither a standard nor a detailed design guide. The validation of water and steam systems, which comprises commissioning and qualification activities, will not be discussed in-depth in this Guide, but is covered in the Commissioning and Qualification Baseline® Guide.

The purpose of this Guide is to focus on engineering issues, and provide cost effective water and steam systems. Where non-engineering issues (e.g., microbiological topics) are covered, the information is included to stress the importance of such topics and the impact they have on water and steam system design. Such non-engineering topics, therefore, are not covered comprehensively, and specific advice from QA departments and technical experts must be sought where technical input is required.

This Guide is intended primarily for regulatory compliance for the domestic United States (US) market, and follows US standards and references. European and other non-US standards and references may be incorporated in future revisions.

Tutorial Commentary

Division of Labor with Other Baseline Guides

The Guide states explicitly that the validation portion is "outside the scope of this Guide" and is covered by ISPE Vol.5 (Commissioning and Qualification Baseline Guide). This means that when planning a water system project you need to refer to at least two guides at the same time:

  • Vol.4: how to design, build, operate and maintain the water system
  • Vol.5: how to validate the water system (C&Q)

The Historical US-centric Limitation

When it was published in 2001, the European Pharmacopoeia (EP) and the United States Pharmacopeia (USP) differed fundamentally on how WFI could be produced: Europe allowed distillation only, while the US also allowed RO (reverse osmosis). This Guide takes a mainly US perspective, so be aware of your target market when using it.

If your CDMO supplies both the European and US markets, you need to refer additionally to European Pharmacopoeia requirements. (Note: since 2017 the European Pharmacopoeia has been revised to allow non-distillation methods for producing WFI.)

Three Elements of the Scope

The scope of this Guide can be understood as three "yes" items and three "no" items:

1.3 Applicable FDA Regulations and Guides

Original Text

Some Applicable FDA Current Regulations and Guides for Pharmaceutical Water Systems:

  • Food and Drug and Cosmetic Act
  • The United States Pharmacopoeia XXIV
  • Title 21 CFR, Part 211
  • FDA Guide To Inspections of High Purity Water Systems

Tutorial Commentary

Analysis of the Regulatory Hierarchy

These four regulatory documents form a hierarchy, and you need to understand how they relate:

  • FD&C Act (Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act): the highest-level law, which authorizes the FDA to regulate drug manufacturing
  • 21 CFR Part 211: the cGMP regulations; Section 211.48 specifically governs "water treatment systems"
  • USP (United States Pharmacopeia): defines the specification standards for each water quality (the chemical and microbiological attributes of WFI, Purified Water, etc.)
  • FDA Inspection Guide: tells inspectors "what to look at during an inspection," which is the most directly relevant to your CDMO

The Time Context of USP XXIV

The original text cites USP XXIV (the 24th edition, 2000). USP has since been updated to later editions, and the water monographs (such as <1231> Water for Pharmaceutical Purposes) have been significantly revised.

When reading this Guide, always cross-check the water quality specifications against the latest edition of USP.

Review Exercise

If an FDA inspector asks you, "What is the TOC limit of your WFI system, and what is it based on?", which document should you cite?

Hint: The USP monograph defines the chemical specifications of WFI (including TOC), not the Baseline Guide. The ISPE guide teaches you how to design a system to meet these specifications, but the specifications themselves come from USP.

1.4 Key Concepts

Original Text

The following key concepts covered in this Guide are:

  1. Methodology for defining the required water quality and configuring a water delivery system.
  2. Critical process parameters.
  3. Good Engineering Practices.
  4. Design Options.

a) Methodology for defining the required quality and configuring a water delivery system

Perhaps the most critical step in a new pharmaceutical water or steam system, from a regulatory as well as technical and financial standpoint, is the specification of water or steam quality required. The specification established is likely to have a larger impact on lifecycle costs of the system than any of the subsequent design decisions. In addition, regulated industries must consider the costs of noncompliance and water system failures. Therefore, it is essential for the designer to seek advice from the Quality unit and technical experts early in the process.

Once process water and/or steam requirements are determined, system design options need to be addressed. This Guide presents alternative baseline water and steam system building blocks and associated advantages and disadvantages of each. These baseline building blocks are qualified relative to such things as capital costs; feed water chemistry; product water quality; chemical handling; water consumption; energy consumption; outside service costs; maintenance requirements; and chemical/microbial/endotoxin removal performance.

Guide emphasis is on how the system design should be determined based on the quality of feed water; the design of the pretreatment and final treatment system; the storage and distribution system design; and operation/maintenance procedures.

The Guide aims to improve consistency of pharmaceutical water and steam quality throughout the industry, as a result of system performance and reliability improvements. It also provides the user with alternative basic system building blocks to permit reliable and consistent generation of the required water or steam quality.

b) Critical process parameters

Critical parameters are defined as those parameters that directly affect the product quality. For example, since microbial quality cannot be directly monitored in real time, the parameters relied upon to control microbial growth are normally considered critical. These may include temperature; UV intensity; ozone concentration; circulating systems under positive pressure; etc. In regard to chemical purity, the quality attributes themselves (properties of water produced), may be monitored at or after each process step, and the proper performance of that operation confirmed directly. For a system producing compendial water, properties mandated in the official monograph obviously constitute critical parameters.

Critical instruments are those instruments that measure critical quality attributes. This concept is discussed in Chapter 2 and used as a basis for subsequent chapter discussions where appropriate.

c) Good Engineering Practice (GEP)

GEP recognizes that all systems in a facility, whether they are water systems, steam systems, elevators, process reactors, safety valves, or rest rooms, require some form of commissioning and/or qualification. Nearly all systems require documentation, inspection, and field testing. Good Engineering Practice capitalizes upon this practice suggesting that manufacturers engage all stakeholders (engineers, operators, Quality Assurance, and others) very early in the planning, design, construction, commissioning/qualification phases to ensure that systems are documented only once.

d) Design Options

The Guide emphasizes that a water system can be designed in many different ways, yet meet the overall requirements of the system. It encourages a well-thought-out, planned approach to the design with input from many areas of the organization including Quality Assurance.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept (a): Water Quality Specification Determines Everything

This is the most important sentence in the whole Guide:

"The way water quality specifications are set has a far greater impact on the total life cycle cost of the system than any subsequent design decision."

In plain language: before designing a water system, you must first answer one fundamental question: "What quality of water do I need?" If you get that wrong, all the design work that follows is wasted.

Analogy: Confirm Your Needs Before Buying a House

It is like buying a house. If you define at the start, "I need a detached house that sleeps 10 people," when in fact a two-bedroom apartment is all you need, then no amount of careful budgeting on the renovation can rescue the original wrong decision.

Pharmaceutical water systems are the same: if the process only needs Purified Water but you specify WFI, the construction cost can differ by a factor of 2-5.

Critical Instruments

Instruments that measure CPPs are Critical Instruments. They require:

  • Regular calibration
  • Calibration traceability records
  • Alert Limits and Action Limits to be set

This concept is discussed in depth in Chapter 9 (Instrumentation and Control).

Key Concept (c): Good Engineering Practice (GEP)

The core idea of GEP is "documented only once."

In the traditional approach, the engineering team produces commissioning documents and then the QA team produces validation documents, so many tests are performed twice. The GEP mindset is to involve all stakeholders from the start and use one set of documents to complete both commissioning and validation.

The Essence of GEP

Imagine renovating a house. If the plumber and electrician, the interior designer and the user (you) each work separately and problems are only found at final acceptance, the cost of rework is high. GEP asks these three parties to sit at the same table from the design stage.

Key Concept (d): Design Options

The core philosophy of this Guide: there is more than one right answer. The same water quality specification can be met by several design solutions (RO + EDI, RO + distillation, double-pass RO, etc.), each with its own advantages and disadvantages.

The Guide provides "building blocks" that you combine according to actual conditions, rather than a one-size-fits-all "standard solution."

CDMO Application Scenario

Your CDMO takes on a new customer whose product is an Oral Liquid Dosage Form. The customer requires WFI as the compounding water. Do you think this is necessary?

  • Oral products usually need only Purified Water, not WFI
  • But some customers ask for a higher grade "to be safe"
  • Your role is to provide professional advice: explain that Purified Water already meets the regulatory requirements, and analyze the additional cost of using WFI
  • The final decision needs to be confirmed jointly by QA and the customer, but "over-specification" is a risk you should proactively identify

Key Concept (b): Critical Process Parameters

Definition of CPP: parameters that directly affect product quality. Common CPPs in water systems include:

Note the important point in the original text: microbial quality cannot be monitored in real time (culture takes 48-72 hours), so the "indirect parameters" that control microbial growth (temperature, UV, ozone, etc.) become CPPs.

1.5 Guide Structure

Original Text

The structure of the Guide is shown in Figure 1-1 below. The chapters have been organized to assist in a logical decision process to determine the type of water required and the system design needed to provide it.

Figure 1-1: Pharmaceutical Water and Steam Baseline® Guide Structure

The Guide follows this chapter organization:

  • Chapter 1: Introduction
  • Chapter 2: Key Design Philosophies
  • Chapter 3: Water Options and System Planning
  • Chapter 4: Pretreatment Options
  • Chapter 5: Final Treatment Options: Non-Compendial and Compendial Purified Water
  • Chapter 6: Final Treatment Options: Water for Injection (WFI)
  • Chapter 7: Pharmaceutical Steam
  • Chapter 8: Storage and Distribution Systems
  • Chapter 9: Instrumentation and Control
  • Chapter 10: Commissioning and Qualification
  • Chapter 11-12: Appendices

Tutorial Commentary

Learning Map: Understanding the Structure of the Whole Guide

The order of these 10 chapters + Appendix is not arbitrary. It reflects the decision flow of a water system project:

  1. Ch.1-2: First understand the basic concepts and design philosophy
  2. Ch.3: Decide "what quality of water do I need?" and plan the system architecture
  3. Ch.4-6: Select water treatment technologies (from pretreatment to final treatment)
  4. Ch.7: Steam systems (independent but interrelated)
  5. Ch.8: How water is stored and distributed to the points of use
  6. Ch.9: What instruments are used to monitor the system
  7. Ch.10: How to validate the system once it is complete
  8. Ch.11-12: Supplementary technical details

The "Life Journey" of Water Systems

Think of this structure as the journey of water through your plant:

City water enters the plant (Ch.4 Pretreatment) -> Advanced treatment (Ch.5/6 Final treatment) -> Storage and distribution (Ch.8) -> Points of use

Ch.7 Steam is another parallel path: Feed water -> Clean steam generator -> Sterilization/heating

Ch.9 Instrumentation and Control is the "nervous system" throughout, monitoring the quality at every step.

Key Reminder: Read Ch.2 and Ch.3 First

If your time is limited, the suggested reading priority is:

  1. Chapter 2 (Key Design Philosophies): understand core concepts such as Design Range vs. Operating Range
  2. Chapter 3 (Water Options and System Planning): learn how to choose the right water quality grade
  3. Then read Ch.4-8 selectively according to your actual needs

Skipping Ch.2 and going straight to the technical chapters is not recommended, because later chapters refer heavily to the conceptual framework of Ch.2.

Review Exercise

Suppose you are the engineering head of a CDMO planning the water system for a new aseptic filling facility. You need:

  • WFI for final rinse and compounding
  • Purified Water for preliminary cleaning
  • Clean Steam for SIP

Map this against the chapter structure: which chapters should you focus on, and why?

Hint: You need at least Ch.3 (planning), Ch.5 (PW treatment), Ch.6 (WFI treatment), Ch.7 (steam), Ch.8 (storage and distribution) and Ch.9 (instrumentation).

Section Summary

  • What FDA endorsement means: The ISPE Baseline Guide is industry best practice reviewed by the FDA, but it is not a regulation. The ultimate basis for compliance is cGMP (21 CFR 211) and the USP.
  • Why the Baseline Guide came about: To eliminate inconsistent interpretation of regulations, avoid "arms race" style over-design, reduce cost and speed time to market.
  • Defined scope: This Guide focuses on the engineering design of new water/steam systems, mainly under US regulations; for validation, refer to ISPE Vol.5.
  • Four key concepts: (a) the water quality specification determines life cycle cost; (b) Critical Process Parameters are the core of quality control; (c) GEP emphasizes "documented only once" and early stakeholder involvement; (d) there is more than one right design solution.
  • Structure of the Guide: 10 chapters + Appendix, arranged in the logical decision flow of a water system project; we suggest starting from Ch.2 and proceeding step by step.
  • Takeaway for CDMOs: When taking on a customer project, the first task is to confirm with the customer and QA the "water quality specification that is truly needed," to avoid unnecessary cost from over-design.

Chapter 2: Key Design Philosophies

p19-p29

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p19-p29

Learning Objectives for This Chapter

  • Understand the central role of pharmaceutical water in drug manufacturing, and how the water quality specification affects the life-cycle cost of the system
  • Distinguish the regulatory requirements for USP Purified Water from those for USP Water for Injection (WFI)
  • Master the logic and practice of the USP three-stage conductivity test
  • Understand the principle of Total Organic Carbon (TOC) testing, the types of instruments, and how to handle out-of-limit results
  • Master the definition of Critical Process Parameters (CPPs) and how they apply to water systems
  • Understand the core philosophy of cGMP compliance: the trinity of specification, validation records, and microbial control
  • Distinguish the hierarchy of the Design Range, the Allowable Operating Range, and the Normal Operating Range

Chapter Overview

2.1 Introduction 2.2 USP Requirements 2.2.1 Purified Water 2.2.2 WFI 2.2.3 Non-Monographed 2.2.4 Conductivity Testing 2.2.5 TOC 2.2.6 Microbial & Endotoxin 2.2.7 pH Testing 2.2.8 Backup Instrumentation 2.3 Water Quality Specification 2.4 Critical Process Parameters 2.5 cGMP Compliance 2.6 Design vs Operating Range
2.1 Introduction

Original Text

Pharmaceutical water is the most widely used ingredient in drug manufacturing and the main component in equipment/system cleaning. Therefore, systems for the production of pharmaceutical water constitute a key component in every manufacturing facility. The nature of producing pharmaceutical waters is to minimize or eliminate potential sources of contamination. This Guide considers this and the means by which engineers can design out, or ensure control of the risk.

The quality of Pharmaceutical Water and Steam is not only critical from a regulatory point of view, but also from a financial point of view. The Pharmaceutical Water and Steam specification has the largest impact on lifecycle costs of the system.

It must be demonstrated that all pharmaceutical waters (non-compendial and United States Pharmacopoeia (USP) monograph compendial waters) can be produced consistently to specification. Establishing the level of microbial control needed in a pharmaceutical water and steam system used in the manufacture of a non-sterile product requires an understanding of both the use of the product and the manufacturing process.

Manufacturers need to define the appropriate water purity based upon sound process understanding and system equipment capability. They must determine the specific purification capability for each processing step, the limitations of the unit operation, and the critical parameters, which affect the specified water/steam quality — chemically, physically, or biologically. Expert QA advice should be sought to provide further details about this important area.

USP covers two compendial water qualities (USP Purified Water and USP Water for Injection). This Guide supports both these water qualities plus additional non-compendial waters including "Drinking Water". It is common practice to name non-compendial waters (exclusive of "Drinking Water") used in pharmaceutical manufacturing by the final treatment step (i.e., Reverse Osmosis/RO water, deionized water/DI water, etc.).

Guidance on establishing specifications for monographed USP water is provided in the United States Pharmacopoeia (USP). Additionally, the FDA Guide to Inspections of High Purity Water Systems (which was developed for FDA personnel) also provides useful information to the user.

Trainer's Analysis

Core Concept: The Strategic Role of Pharmaceutical Water

Pharmaceutical water is much more than a "utility". It is:

  • The most heavily used raw material - nearly every dosage form depends on water
  • The core medium for equipment cleaning - CIP (Clean-in-Place) relies entirely on it
  • A key cost-driving decision - the water quality specification directly determines life-cycle cost

So the chapter opens by telling you plainly: choosing the wrong water quality specification costs more than choosing the wrong equipment.

Analogy: The Water Quality Specification Is Like a Building Design Grade

Imagine building a house. If you specify "magnitude 9 earthquake resistance" from the start, every material, structural design, and construction method has to be upgraded with it, and the cost naturally soars. But if the real need is only "magnitude 7", you have wasted a large sum of money.

Pharmaceutical water works the same way: the cost of building and maintaining a WFI system is far higher than for Purified Water. If your product needs only Purified Water but you chose WFI to be "conservative", the energy, maintenance, and validation costs that follow will overwhelm you.

Key Insight: The "Design Out" Philosophy

Note the wording in the original text: "design out, or ensure control of the risk". This reflects ISPE's consistent risk management philosophy:

  • First choice: eliminate the risk by design (Design Out) - for example, using hot water circulation to remove the opportunity for microbial growth
  • Second choice: control the risk (Ensure Control) - for example, periodic sanitization and microbial monitoring

This is fully consistent with the risk control hierarchy in ICH Q9.

The Two Compendial Waters

Non-compendial waters, such as RO water and DI water, are usually named after their final treatment step.

2.2 United States Pharmacopoeia (USP)

Original Text

USP is a Guide to producing medicinal products for consumption within the US. USP specify standards of quality, purity, packaging, and labeling for a number of waters including two bulk waters, "Water for Injection" and "Purified Water" used in the preparation of compendial (USP) dosage forms. This Guide is concerned with the production of these two compendial (USP) waters and does not address the other "packaged waters" monographed by the USP. USP 24 (and supplements) is the current version, at the time this Guide was prepared.

Trainer's Analysis

The Role of the USP

The USP (United States Pharmacopoeia) is the legally recognized quality standard in the United States. It defines the quality specifications for two "Bulk Waters":

  • Purified Water
  • Water for Injection

Note: the USP also has monographs for other "Packaged Waters", such as Sterile Water for Injection, but this guide focuses only on the bulk waters in production systems.

Version Reminder

This guide was written with reference to USP 24. The current edition has since been updated to the USP-NF (which is continuously revised). The basic framework of the three-stage conductivity test is unchanged, but the specific limit tables should be checked in the latest USP <645> and <643>.

2.2.1 USP Purified Water

Original Text

Official monograph requirements for "Purified Water" require that "Purified Water":

  • Is obtained from water complying with the "U.S. Environmental Protection Agency National Primary Drinking Water Regulations, or comparable regulations of the European Union or Japan, and will be referred to subsequently as "Drinking Water".
  • Contains no added substance
  • Is obtained by a suitable process
  • Meets the requirements for Water Conductivity
  • Meets the requirements for Total Organic Carbon (TOC)

Trainer's Analysis

The Five Requirements for Purified Water

To meet the USP Purified Water standard, your water system must satisfy all five of the following conditions:

  1. Acceptable source water - the starting point must be "Drinking Water" that complies with drinking water regulations
  2. No added substances - no preservatives or other additives may be added
  3. Suitable process - the USP does not restrict the process for PW (unlike WFI); it only has to be "suitable"
  4. Acceptable conductivity - per the USP <645> three-stage test
  5. Acceptable TOC - per USP <643>, with a limit of 500 ppb

Analogy: Purified Water Is Like an "Ordinary Driver's License"

For an ordinary driver's license, you only need to show that you can drive and pass the written and road tests. Which car you practiced in and which driving school you attended are not restricted.

Purified Water is the same: the USP does not care whether you use RO, EDI, distillation, or a hybrid process, as long as the final water passes the conductivity and TOC tests.

2.2.2 USP Water for Injection (WFI)

Original Text

Official monograph requirements for "Water for Injection" require that "Water for Injection":

  • Meets all of the requirements for "Purified Water"
  • Is obtained by a suitable process and purified by distillation or Reverse Osmosis
  • Meets the requirements of the Bacterial Endotoxin test and contains not more than 0.25 USP Endotoxin Unit per ml
  • Is prepared using suitable means to minimize microbial growth

Trainer's Analysis

WFI = Purified Water + Stricter Requirements

The WFI requirements are a "superset" of those for Purified Water, with these additions:

  • Process restriction - the final purification step must be Distillation or Reverse Osmosis
  • Endotoxin limit - ≤ 0.25 EU/mL (Endotoxin Unit)
  • Microbial control - appropriate measures must be taken to minimize microbial growth

WFI Key Specifications at a Glance

Conductivity:  ≤ 1.3 μS/cm @ 25°C (Stage 1)
TOC:           ≤ 500 ppb (0.5 mg/L)
Endotoxin:     ≤ 0.25 EU/mL
Microbial:     ≤ 10 CFU/100 mL (action limit)
Final step:    Distillation OR Reverse Osmosis only

Regulatory Evolution Reminder

When this guide was written (2001), the USP allowed only distillation and RO as the final treatment step for WFI. The European Pharmacopoeia (EP) was revised in 2017 and now also accepts non-distillation methods (such as membrane-based methods). However, the USP position remains unchanged to this day - it is still limited to distillation or RO.

For a CDMO, if a client's target market includes the United States, the WFI system must be designed so that the final step is distillation or RO.

2.2.3 Non-Monographed but Accepted Requirements

Original Text

The USP "General Information" provides background information, which clarifies regulatory intent. The following information is included in Chapter 11:

  • Purified water systems require frequent sanitization and microbiological monitoring to ensure water of appropriate microbiological quality at the points of use.
  • Water for Injection is "finally subjected to distillation or Reverse Osmosis", implying that the Still or RO unit is the last unit operation. "The system used to produce, store and distribute water for injection must be designed to prevent microbial contamination and the formation of microbial endotoxins, and it must be validated."
  • An action limit of 100 colony forming units per ml (10,000 CFU/100 ml) for "Purified Water" is suggested.
  • An action limit of 10 colony forming units per 100 ml (10 CFU/100 ml) for "Water for Injection" is suggested.
  • Minimum sample sizes are 1 ml for USP Purified Water and 100 ml for WFI. (FDA recommends 100 ml for Purified Water and 250 ml for WFI).

"It should be emphasized that the above action guidelines are not intended to be totally inclusive for every situation where ingredient waters are to be employed. It is therefore, incumbent upon the manufacturer to supplement the general action guidelines to fit each particular manufacturing situation" — USP24, page 2163.

Trainer's Analysis

Hidden Inspection Focus Points

This passage looks like "reference information", but it actually contains several inspection points:

  • "Finally subjected to" - this means distillation or RO must be the last step. If you add a UV sterilizer after the RO, does the RO no longer count as the "last step"? This is a common regulatory gray area and needs careful justification.
  • Difference in sample volume - the USP says a minimum of 1 mL for PW, but the FDA recommends 100 mL. In practice, it is best to use the FDA's recommended volume, because the FDA is the one inspecting your site.
  • "Supplement the general action guidelines" - you are responsible for setting stricter standards based on your own product.

Analogy: The Action Limit Is Like the "Speeding Ticket Threshold"

The highway speed limit is 110 km/h, but the police usually will not ticket you at 111 km/h. The Action Limit is like the "threshold at which tickets start": once the microbial count exceeds this value, you must start an investigation.

But a good manufacturer also sets an Alert Limit, like the speed warning in a car that sounds at 100 km/h, giving you time to react.

2.2.4 USP Testing and Instrument Requirements

Original Text

Three Stage Conductivity Testing

Conductivity Instrument Requirements for Acceptable Regulatory Measurement

Trainer's Analysis

The Logic of the Three-Stage Conductivity Test

Why not just test once? Because the USP designed a "progressive" testing logic whose purpose is to reduce unnecessary off-line testing while keeping the pass/fail decision rigorous:

  1. Stage 1 (in-line screening): measure in real time at the operating temperature. If the reading is ≤ the limit for that temperature (a value of 2.1 μS/cm is commonly used as a safe simplified criterion), it is an immediate PASS. Most cases end here.
  2. Stage 2 (off-line confirmation): if Stage 1 is not passed, take a sample and retest at 25°C ±1°C. This removes the variation caused by temperature.
  3. Stage 3 (final determination): if Stage 2 is still not passed, add KCl solution and measure the pH (range 5-7). If the pH is out of range or the conductivity still exceeds the limit → FAIL.

Analogy: The Three-Stage Test Is Like Hospital Triage

At the emergency room:

  • Stage 1 (nurses' station): temperature and blood pressure are taken. Normal? Go home.
  • Stage 2 (consulting room): temperature is slightly high, so blood is drawn to confirm. Nothing wrong? Go home.
  • Stage 3 (specialist examination): blood markers are abnormal, so deeper examination follows. A problem is confirmed → admission.

Likewise, the philosophy of the three-stage test is: if a simple step can establish acceptability, do not waste resources on complex testing.

Conductivity Instrument Accuracy Requirements at a Glance

Temperature accuracy:      ± 0.25°C
Conductivity resolution:   < ± 0.1 μS/cm
Cell constant accuracy:    ± 2%
Reading accuracy:          < ± 0.1 μS/cm

Key rule: Non-temperature-compensated measurement
         → Do NOT use the instrument's automatic temperature compensation function!

Practical Pitfall: Where to Install the In-Line Conductivity Meter

The original text clearly states the best location: after the last point-of-use valve and before the return to the storage tank.

Why? Because this location represents "the worst water quality in the system" - the state of the water after it has traveled the entire loop and passed every point of use. If the water is acceptable here, every other point must be acceptable too.

Common mistake: installing the conductivity meter at the tank outlet. This only represents the water quality "at the start of the journey", not the quality after "the full trip".

2.2.5 Total Organic Carbon (TOC) and Requirements for TOC Control

Original Text

TOC is an indirect measure, as carbon, of organic molecules present in high purity water. USP replaced the USP 22 "Oxidizable Substance" wet chemistry test with an In-Line capable, TOC test. A limit was determined by USP to be 0.5 ppm or 500 ppb, based on the results of studies and an industry wide survey of pharmaceutical water systems.

Instruments are available for measuring TOC In-Line from slipstreams and from grab samples manually removed from the water system. Automatic Off-Line sample introduction systems are available for processing large numbers of grab samples. USP have not prevented acceptable technologies from being used, but limit the methods to the following instruments that are capable of:

  • Completely oxidizing the organic molecules to Carbon dioxide (CO2)
  • Measuring the CO2 levels as carbon
  • Discriminating between Inorganic carbon (IC) and the CO2 levels generated from the oxidization of the organic molecules
  • Maintaining an equipment limit of detection of 0.05 mg per liter, or lower
  • Periodically demonstrating an equipment "suitability"

Types of TOC Analyzers

  • Laboratory Instruments
  • In-Line Instruments
  • Laboratory Instruments capable of operating in-line

Out of Range Excursions

Out of range readings may be experienced as the direct result of the above types of organic contaminations. Spikes may also occur as the result of extraneous electrical interference etc. All spikes must be identified and formally explained.

Procedures to handle out of range spikes should be available. These procedures should address short duration spikes, which occur following continuous acceptable readings and are followed by similar acceptable readings, specifically in In-Line applications.

Unexplainable spikes may be minimized when using In-Line batch sampling systems by extending the sample analysis period to 30 or 60 minutes rather than using shorter analysis periods.

Trainer's Analysis

The Nature of TOC: An Indirect Measure of Organic Contamination

TOC (Total Organic Carbon) does not measure the organic compounds directly. Instead:

  1. Organic molecules are completely oxidized to CO2
  2. The amount of CO2 produced is measured
  3. Inorganic carbon (IC, from dissolved CO2 and bicarbonate) is subtracted
  4. What remains is the amount of organic carbon

Formula: TOC = TC - IC (Total Carbon - Inorganic Carbon)

TOC Specifications at a Glance

USP TOC limit:          ≤ 500 ppb (0.5 mg/L)
Detection limit:        ≤ 0.05 mg/L (50 ppb)
Signal-to-noise ratio:  limit / LOD = 10:1

TOC = TC - IC
TC  = Total Carbon
IC  = Inorganic Carbon (from dissolved CO2 / HCO3-)

Analogy: A TOC Test Is Like a Drink-Driving Check

When police run a roadside drink-driving check, they do not analyze every alcohol molecule in your blood. They use a breath test to measure the alcohol in your exhaled breath (an indirect indicator).

TOC follows similar logic: instead of identifying exactly which organic compounds are in the water (far too complicated), it "burns" all organics into CO2 and uses a single number to represent the total contamination.

Philosophy for Handling Out-of-Limit Spikes

In-line TOC monitoring occasionally shows a sudden spike that quickly returns to normal. ISPE's recommendation is:

  • Investigation required - every excursion must have a formal written explanation
  • Investigation may be waived - if the readings before and after the spike are continuously acceptable and an SOP permitting this situation is in place
  • Extend the sampling interval - change from a short interval (such as 5 minutes) to 30-60 minutes, using statistical averaging to smooth out the noise

This reflects a regulatory philosophy of "science-based risk management" rather than "zero tolerance".

2.2.6 USP 23 Microbial and Endotoxin Testing

Original Text

Microbial contaminants and Endotoxins are traditionally sampled at the points of use in a water system. USP 24 has made no changes in this area.

Trainer's Analysis

Key Principle for Microbial and Endotoxin Sampling

This passage is short, but it implies an important principle: sampling must be done at the Point of Use (POU).

Why not just sample at the storage tank or the generator outlet? Because:

  • Microorganisms can multiply in the distribution piping (especially in dead legs and low-flow areas)
  • Endotoxin can accumulate in the biofilm on pipe walls
  • The point of use represents "the water quality the product actually contacts"

Common CDMO Issue

Many CDMO water systems have a large number of points of use (20-50), so testing all of them every day is not possible. The practical approach is to establish a Rotating Sampling Plan, ensuring that every POU is sampled at least once within a cycle (usually one week or one month), plus a few fixed "representative" points tested every day.

2.2.7 USP 23 pH Testing

Original Text

Testing water for compliance with the USP 24 pH limits is required as part of the stage 3 Off-Line conductivity testing. (pH must be confirmed as being in the range of 5 to 7.) Testing may use calibrated Off-Line meters. Calibration should be performed using solutions of a known pH, covering the range of 5 to 7. The frequency of calibration should ensure that the levels of accuracy are maintained.

Trainer's Analysis

pH Testing Is Required Only at Stage 3

pH is not an independent routine test. It is triggered only when the three-stage conductivity test reaches Stage 3. The logic chain:

  1. Stage 1 FAIL → proceed to Stage 2
  2. Stage 2 FAIL → proceed to Stage 3
  3. Stage 3: measure conductivity AND pH (5-7)
  4. Failure of either one → the water fails

Why Is the pH Range 5-7?

The theoretical pH of pure water is 7.0, but because CO2 from the air dissolves in the water to form carbonic acid, the actual pH tends to be lower (about 5.5-6.5). Setting the range at 5-7 allows for this "natural acidity" while excluding serious contamination (a pH < 5 may indicate acidic contaminants, and a pH > 7 may indicate alkaline residues).

2.2.8 Validated Backup Instrumentation

Original Text

Failure of a monitoring instrument should not be precluded when making decisions concerning type, location and the extent of validation. Since each excursion from the acceptable limits must be investigated, In-Line installations should be supplemented with a calibrated laboratory instrument as backup. Validation should include the operation in Off-Line mode as a supplement or alternate to In-Line instrumentation. Off-Line laboratory testing should also include a backup instrument to be maintained calibrated in case of failure of the primary unit.

Trainer's Analysis

A "Backup" Is Not Optional; It Is Required

ISPE's logic is very clear:

  • In-line instruments will fail at some point → a calibrated laboratory instrument must be available as a backup
  • The primary laboratory instrument may also fail → a second calibrated backup instrument is needed
  • The validation scope must include operation "using the backup instrument"

In other words: your validation program must answer the question "If the in-line conductivity meter fails, how do I demonstrate that the water is still acceptable?"

Analogy: The Twin-Engine Aircraft

Why do commercial airliners have two engines? Not because engines fail often, but because the consequences would be too severe if one did. The logic of backup instruments in a water system is exactly the same - you cannot skip a backup plan just because failures are "rare".

2.3 Specification of Pharmaceutical Water Quality

Original Text

2.3.1 Specifying Water Quality

The quality of water supplied in any pharmaceutical process must be consistent with the quality required for the final product. It may not be sufficient to specify a water quality that meets the specification of the two compendial grades of water outlined in the USP. These grades, USP Purified water and WFI, are minimum standards. A more stringent specification could be required depending on the intended use of the product and on the process used to manufacture that product. It is the responsibility of each drug manufacturer to establish the logic for their water quality specification based on the required quality of the end product.

Pharmaceutical water uses can be categorized as:

  • An ingredient in a dosage form manufacturing process
  • An ingredient in an Active Pharmaceutical Ingredient (API) process
  • Equipment cleaning or rinsing

Water intended for use as a dosage form ingredient must be USP monograph water and must be produced consistently to specification. Evidence of control is required for all critical process parameters that may affect the final drug characterization. USP WFI water would be expected to be used for parenteral manufacture, some ophthalmic and some inhalation products.

For some applications where there are no requirements for compendial waters, the manufacturer may establish quality specifications equivalent to USP-WFI or Purified Waters, depending on the specific application. Specifications for water used as an ingredient in the manufacture of API's or as the wash solvent must be determined by the manufacturer.

With the appropriate justification, non-compendial pharmaceutical waters (including "drinking waters") may be utilized throughout pharmaceutical operations including production equipment washing/cleaning as well as rinsing, laboratory usage and as an ingredient in the manufacture of bulk active pharmaceutical ingredients. Compendial water must, however, be used with preparation of (as an ingredient) compendial dosage forms.

In both compendial and non-compendial waters, the manufacturer must establish an appropriate microbial quality specification. The significance of microorganisms in non-sterile pharmaceutical products should be evaluated in terms of the use of the product and the nature of the product and the potential harm to the user.

The US Pharmacopoeia states action levels that are generally considered acceptable are 500 CFU/ml for drinking water, 100 CFU/ml for Purified Water, and 10 CFU/100 ml for WFI, and may be more stringent depending on its use.

The final quality of pharmaceutical water and steam is determined by the manufacturing process and end product, quality of feed water, pretreatment and final treatment sub-systems, storage and distribution system design and operator/maintenance procedures.

Trainer's Analysis

Core Point: The USP Is the "Minimum Standard", Not the "Best Standard"

This is the most important sentence in the chapter. Many engineers make the mistake of thinking "My water meets the USP, so there is no problem."

Wrong! The USP defines only the minimum threshold. Depending on your product, you may need:

  • Lower conductivity (for example, some biologics require < 0.5 μS/cm)
  • Lower TOC (for example, some API syntheses require < 100 ppb)
  • Lower microbial limits (for example, < 1 CFU/100 mL)

CDMO Practical Application

As a CDMO (Contract Development and Manufacturing Organization), you face challenges that are more complex than those of a typical pharmaceutical company:

  • Multiple clients, multiple products - different clients may have different water quality requirements
  • Strictest-specification principle - it is generally recommended to design the system to the "strictest client specification"
  • Documentation flexibility - define the water quality specification explicitly in the Quality Agreement to avoid later disputes
  • Cost allocation - if a client requires above-specification water quality (such as bioburden < 1 CFU/100 mL), the additional cost should be reflected in the contract

Analogy: The Water Quality Specification Is Like Ingredient Grades

The beef used by a Michelin-starred restaurant and the beef used by a street stall both have to pass food safety inspection (= the USP minimum standard), but the Michelin restaurant additionally demands higher specifications for origin, cut, and aging days.

Pharmaceuticals are the same: the USP guarantees only "safety", but "best quality" requires the manufacturer to set higher standards based on the needs of its own product.

2.4 Critical Process Parameters

Original Text

Critical parameters are defined as those parameters, which directly affect the water quality at, or after, a treatment step. For example, water temperature during a heat sanitization cycle has a direct effect on water quality.

Regarding chemical purity, the quality attributes may be monitored at or after each critical process step, and the proper performance of that operation confirmed directly. Since microbial quality cannot be directly monitored in real time, the parameters relied upon to control microbial growth are usually (depending on the system) considered critical, such as temperature, UV intensity, ozone concentration, circulation rate, sanitization procedures, positive pressure, etc.

For a system producing compendial water, properties mandated in the official monograph (including bioburden and endotoxins) constitute critical attributes. Critical instruments are those instruments, which measure critical attributes or parameters.

Trainer's Analysis

Key Insight: The "Invisible" Problem of Microorganisms

The original text points out a profound truth: microbial quality cannot be monitored in real time. The traditional plate count method takes 48-72 hours to give a result. This means:

  • You cannot "reject immediately" water that exceeds microbial limits
  • You can only control it indirectly through surrogate parameters - temperature, UV, ozone, and so on
  • These surrogate parameters become CPPs, because you rely on them to "predict" microbial quality

This is why most of the CPPs on a water system's CPP list are "microbial-control-related" parameters.

Thought Exercise

Suppose your WFI system is an 80°C hot water circulation system. Answer the following:

  1. Is the "circulating water temperature" a CPP? Why?
  2. If the temperature drops from 80°C to 70°C, does a deviation investigation need to be started?
  3. Is the "storage tank level" a CPP?

Hint: go back to the definition of a CPP, "directly affects water quality". Temperature directly affects microbial control, so it is a CPP. Level does not directly affect water quality (although it indirectly affects flow velocity and stagnation risk), so it is usually not classified as a CPP but as a KPP (Key Process Parameter).

Definition of a CPP in a Water System

A Critical Process Parameter (CPP) is a parameter that directly affects water quality. Note the word "directly" - parameters with only an indirect effect are not CPPs.

Typical CPPs for a water system include:

2.5 cGMP Compliance Issues

Original Text

Satisfying regulatory concerns is primarily a matter of establishing proper specifications, and using effective and appropriate methods to verify and record that those specifications are satisfied. Issues such as quality of installation, sampling and testing procedures, operating and maintenance procedures, record keeping, etc. often have greater significance than the particular technologies selected to purify and distribute the water.

Fundamental conditions expected to aggravate a microbial problem typically include system design conditions such as stagnant conditions, areas of low flow rate, poor quality feed water etc.

Measures to alleviate such problems include:

  • Continuous, turbulent flow
  • Elevated or reduced temperatures
  • Smooth, clean surfaces that minimize nutrient accumulation
  • Frequent draining, flushing or sanitizing
  • Flooded distribution loop (maintenance of positive distribution loop pressures)
  • Properly designed, installed and maintained system

While the control of chemical quality is important, the primary challenge in a pharmaceutical water system is maintaining the microbial quality. The industry and the regulatory community have recognized the effectiveness of maintaining a continuously recirculated system at high temperatures (65°C-80°C) in preventing microbial growth.

Distillation has a long and well-documented history of success, but need not be the only technology considered for producing water with endotoxin limits. Reverse osmosis is the only other technology accepted by the USP for WFI. Ultrafiltration has been successfully used to produce waters with strict endotoxin limits that meet WFI attributes, but it cannot, by regulation, be used to produce compendial grade WFI.

Each pharmaceutical steam and water treatment system must be viewed in its entirety, as design and operational factors affecting any unit operation within the system can affect the whole system. It is useful to identify both the quality parameters of water entering the system and the quality parameters of the water or steam to be produced. Water quality should be enhanced with each successive step. It does not necessarily follow that measures enhancing one quality attribute (such as conductivity, particulate level or color) will always enhance another (such as microbial population).

Trainer's Analysis

A Surprising View: Technology Is Not the Most Important Factor

This passage may surprise many engineers: ISPE says that installation quality, sampling procedures, operation and maintenance, and record keeping are often more important than the purification technology you choose.

In other words, a system using RO + EDI that is poorly operated and maintained may be more problem-prone than a distillation-only system. The core of cGMP compliance lies not in technology but in management.

Six Measures for Microbial Control

The six measures listed by ISPE are all, in essence, answers to the same question: how do you stop microorganisms from colonizing and multiplying?

  1. Continuous turbulent flow - keep the water always moving so microorganisms have no chance to "settle in"
  2. Temperature control (Elevated/Reduced Temperatures) - 65-80°C hot water or < 4°C cold water, outside the microbial comfort zone
  3. Smooth surfaces (Smooth, Clean Surfaces) - electropolished stainless steel with Ra ≤ 0.8 μm to reduce biofilm attachment
  4. Frequent sanitizing (Frequent Sanitizing) - periodic chemical or thermal sanitization
  5. Positive pressure (Positive Pressure) - prevents outside air (carrying microorganisms) from entering the system
  6. System integrity (Properly Designed System) - eliminate dead legs and avoid low points where water can pool

Analogy: Microbial Control Is Like Pest Control

Imagine keeping a food warehouse free of pests:

  • Keep the air moving (= continuous turbulent flow)
  • Hold the temperature in a range pests dislike (= hot water circulation)
  • Smooth walls with no crevices (= electropolished surfaces)
  • Spray insecticide regularly (= sanitization procedures)
  • Keep doors and windows closed and supply air at positive pressure (= positive pressure maintenance)
  • Leave no food residue (= eliminate accumulation of nutrient sources)

The logic is exactly the same; only the scale changes, from macroscopic to microscopic.

Important Warning: Quality Improvement Is Not Necessarily Across the Board

The last sentence of the original text is key: "improving one quality attribute (such as conductivity) does not necessarily improve another (such as microbial quality)".

For example, adding a mixed bed (MB) ion exchanger can reduce conductivity to a very low level, but if it is poorly managed, the resin bed can instead become a breeding ground for microorganisms. You must look at water quality from the perspective of the whole system, and not optimize a single indicator alone.

2.6 Design Range versus Operating Range

Original Text

This Guide recognizes the distinction between "Design Range" and "Operating Range" and the impact this distinction has upon validation and facility system operation. These criteria are defined as:

Design Range: the specified range or accuracy of a controlled variable used by the designer as a basis to determine the performance requirements for an engineered water system.

Allowable Operating Range: the range of validated critical parameters within which acceptable product water can be manufactured.

Normal Operating Range: a range which may be selected by the manufacturer as the desired acceptable values for a parameter (i.e., conductivity) during normal operations. This range must be within the Allowable Operating Range.

a) While a water or steam system should meet all stated Design Conditions, the acceptability of the system for operation from a cGMP standpoint depends on operating within the Allowable Operating Range.

b) Performance criteria for a Pharmaceutical Water Generation System may require a final product water quality conductivity of 0.5 μS/cm (2 Mohm-cm) or better as a Design Condition. The Allowable Operating Range for this pharmaceutical water may, however, allow for generation of water quality with a conductivity of 1.3 μS/cm (0.77 Mohm-cm) or better. The Normal Operating Range for generating water may, in the end, be set by the manufacturer at conductivity value approaching 1.0 μS/cm (1.0 Mohm-cm) or better to provide a comfortable environment for the operation.

c) Normal Operating Range cannot exceed the Allowable Operating Range for the product water. The Design Condition selection should reflect Good Engineering Practice.

d) It is also good practice for manufacturers to apply the concept of Alert and Action limits along with Normal Operating Range. Alert Limits are based on normal operating experience and are used to initiate corrective measures before reaching an Action Limit, which is defined as the process condition established by product acceptance criteria. The Action Limit deviations must be kept as a part of the batch record as they represent deviations from validated parameters.

Trainer's Analysis

The Hierarchy of the Three Ranges

This is one of the most important concepts in the chapter. The three ranges form a nested relationship:

Design Range - the widest
↓
Allowable Operating Range - the validated range
↓
Normal Operating Range - the narrowest

Conductivity Range Example (from the Original Text)

Design Condition:           ≤ 0.5 μS/cm  (2.0 MΩ·cm)
                            ↑ Value guaranteed by the equipment supplier

Allowable Operating Range:  ≤ 1.3 μS/cm  (0.77 MΩ·cm)
                            ↑ Acceptable range confirmed by validation

Normal Operating Range:     ≤ 1.0 μS/cm  (1.0 MΩ·cm)
                            ↑ Comfort zone for day-to-day operation

USP Stage 1 Limit @ 25°C:  ≤ 1.3 μS/cm
                            ↑ Regulatory minimum requirement

Analogy: Highway Driving Speed

Think of the three ranges in terms of highway driving:

  • Design Range = the car's design top speed of 200 km/h - the performance target when engineers designed the car
  • Allowable Operating Range = the legal speed limit of 110 km/h - exceeding it is a violation (deviation)
  • Normal Operating Range = your cruising speed of 90-100 km/h - leaving a safety margin

Why not drive right at the limit? Because changing road conditions (= fluctuations in feed water quality) could push you over the limit in an instant (= outside the Allowable Range), so you need a "buffer zone".

Thought Exercise

Your WFI system conductivity is normally stable at 0.8 μS/cm. One day it suddenly rises to 1.2 μS/cm, but is still within the USP limit (1.3 μS/cm). Answer the following:

  1. Is this a deviation?
  2. Where should your Alert Limit and Action Limit be set?
  3. Does QA need to be notified?

Hint: if your Normal Operating Range is set at ≤ 1.0 μS/cm, the Alert Limit at 1.0 μS/cm, and the Action Limit at 1.2 μS/cm, then 1.2 has already triggered the Action Limit, even though the USP limit is 1.3. Yes, QA must be notified and the event recorded in the batch record.

CDMO Practical Application: Water Quality Management for Multiple Clients

In a CDMO environment, the concept of Design Range vs Operating Range is especially important:

  • System design - use the strictest client's specification as the Design Condition
  • Allowable Operating Range - base it on the USP limit or the strictest client specification
  • Normal Operating Range - set at 70-80% of the Allowable Range, to ensure the needs of all clients can be met
  • Alert/Action Limits - set after trend analysis of at least 12 months of historical data

This way, even if feed water quality fluctuates, you still have enough buffer so that no client's product is exposed to a water quality risk.

Chapter Summary

  • The water quality specification is the biggest cost driver - before deciding on a technical solution, first determine the water grade you really need (2.1)
  • The USP defines two compendial waters: Purified Water (conductivity + TOC) and WFI (PW requirements + endotoxin + distillation/RO) (2.2.1-2.2.2)
  • The three-stage conductivity test is a progressive decision logic - in most cases a pass can be determined at Stage 1 (2.2.4)
  • TOC ≤ 500 ppb is an indirect indicator of organic contamination; excursion spikes require formal investigation but can be handled in a simplified way based on an SOP (2.2.5)
  • Microbial sampling must be done at the point of use; the Action Limit is the minimum requirement, and the manufacturer is responsible for setting stricter standards (2.2.6, 2.3)
  • CPP = a parameter that directly affects water quality; microbial-related parameters are especially critical because they "cannot be monitored in real time" (2.4)
  • The core of cGMP is management, not technology - operation and maintenance > equipment selection; microbial control is the greatest challenge (2.5)
  • Three-tier ranges: Design Range ⊃ Allowable Operating Range ⊃ Normal Operating Range, together with Alert/Action Limits forming a complete water quality monitoring system (2.6)

Chapter 3: Water Options & Planning

p31-p41

Chapter 3: Water Quality Options and System Planning
ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p31–p41

Learning Objectives

  1. Understand the quality hierarchy of pharmaceutical water: Drinking Water, Non-Compendial Water, Purified Water (PW) and Water for Injection (WFI), and where each is appropriate.
  2. Understand the difference between USP compendial water and non-compendial water, and explain when non-compendial water may be used and what documentation supports it.
  3. Recognize the factors that drive the cost of the different water quality options, including the NPV (net present value) analysis method.
  4. Master the four-step ISPE system planning methodology: define water quality, characterize the use points, establish system criteria, then revisit water quality.
  5. Be able to interpret the Use-Point Criteria matrix, including the concept and application of the Diversity Factor.
  6. Understand the advantages and disadvantages of a Central System versus Multiple Systems in system design.

Original Text — 3.1 Introduction

3.1 Introduction

This chapter outlines basic water system design criteria and, along with subsequent chapters, aims to provide a better understanding of pharmaceutical water, how it is used, and how it can be provided. The primary goal of this chapter is to provide the user with a methodology for:

  1. Evaluating water quality options for product manufacturing
  2. Evaluating basic system configurations available to provide the water

Detailed information on unit operation design, maintenance and cost factors is addressed in later chapters.

The chapter also outlines the system planning effort for pharmaceutical water systems. This planning starts with the selection of water quality based upon product requirements, processing operations, and end use. A decision tree concept is included to assist in selection of compendial and non-compendial waters for production, cleaning, and support. The program then provides steps to guide the user through a use-point and system analysis, to set-up the water system distribution strategy. Finally, evaluation points are provided for the selection of the primary system configurations.

Commentary

Core Concept: Where This Chapter Fits

This chapter is the "strategic starting point" of ISPE Vol.4. Before getting into pretreatment (Chapter 4), purification technologies (Chapters 5-6) and storage and distribution (Chapters 7-8), you must first answer two fundamental questions:

  • What quality of water is needed? - Water Quality Options
  • What kind of system will supply it? - System Configurations

The answers to these two questions set the tone for every technical decision that follows.

Everyday Analogy

Imagine planning the water supply for a building. You would not go straight out and buy pipes and pumps. You would first ask: "Do the residents need drinking water, hot water or softened water?" and "Do we want one central water station, or a separate unit on each floor?" That is what this chapter does: set the strategy first, then choose the technology.

Key Point: The Decision Tree

The Decision Tree provided in this chapter (Figure 3-1) is the core tool for selecting water. It guides you to an appropriate water grade based on your product type (oral, injectable, topical) and process characteristics (presence of a sterilization step or a concentration step). Every choice requires QA review and documentary support.

Original Text — 3.2 Water Quality Options

3.2 Water Quality Options

Quality requirements for water used in pharmaceutical manufacturing and product development are driven by the product characteristics, manufacturing processes, and the intended use of the product. To aid in the water selection process, the USP Monographs define minimum requirements for general types of pharmaceutical water used in almost every pharmaceutical application. However, there is also the opportunity for a manufacturer to determine water quality requirements, different from those in the USP, based on specific product characteristics and processing operations. If this option is taken, the product manufacturer is responsible for assuring that water used to manufacture the product is appropriate, to reliably produce safe product.

Though water quality requirements are product specific, it is impractical to reliably produce special water that is specific to each situation. Manufacturing operations typically generate and distribute only a few, or perhaps just one, quality of water. Therefore, products and operations requiring similar water qualities are commonly grouped. The most common segmentation is that defined in the USP.

Manufacturers agree that in many if not most cases, the requirements defined in the USP are adequate for production of safe product. More stringent water quality specifications may be appropriate for some products and processes. Others may be appropriately less stringent. Typically, more stringent requirements may apply to some processing operations involving significant concentration steps or products comprised of high water content, which may be applied in large volume doses. Likewise, processes involving reliable sterilization and purification steps which remove impurities may, in some cases, not require water qualities as strict as those defined in the USP.

In manufacturing operations with only one quality of water, the water system must be designed to meet the most stringent requirements of the most demanding product or process. With more than one quality of water, products and processes are often categorized and fed by the most appropriate system. The number of types of water generated is most often a function of volume of water consumed and variation of quality. Large consumers may find it economical to generate and distribute multiple grades of water, while small users often will generate only one quality of water.

Commentary

Core Concept: The Three Drivers of Water Quality Requirements

The starting point for selecting pharmaceutical water quality is three factors:

  1. Product Characteristics - injectables vs. orals vs. topicals carry different levels of risk
  2. Manufacturing Processes - whether there are subsequent sterilization or purification steps
  3. Intended Use - large-volume intravenous injection vs. low-dose oral products

Together, these three determine the minimum water quality standard you need.

Key Insight: USP Is a "Minimum Requirement", Not "Best Practice"

The source text states clearly that USP Monographs define minimum requirements. This means:

  • Complying with USP does not automatically mean the water is safe - a particular product may need a stricter standard
  • Not complying with USP does not automatically mean the water cannot be used - with sound scientific justification and documentation, a manufacturer may set its own water quality standard
  • The key point: you must be able to demonstrate that your choice is justified

Everyday Analogy: One Water for Everything vs. Graded Supply

Imagine a restaurant. With only one tap, it must supply the cleanest water (because it is used both for cooking soup and for washing dishes). With two taps (one for the kitchen, one for dishwashing), you can optimize water quality and cost for each use.

Drug manufacturers face the same choice: small plants usually run a single water grade (the highest standard everywhere), while large plants use graded supply to save cost.

CDMO Practice

For a CDMO (contract development and manufacturing organization), this "graded vs. unified" decision is especially important:

  • Multi-client portfolio - a CDMO makes many clients' products at the same time, and their water quality needs can differ widely
  • Flexibility - installing a single WFI system and using WFI for every product costs more but keeps validation simple
  • Cost - installing separate PW and WFI systems is more complex to operate and maintain but saves money over the long term
  • Practical recommendation - a CDMO whose core business is aseptic filling usually leans toward WFI throughout the facility, because it is the easiest position to defend in client audits
Original Text — Water Categories & Decision Tree

Three Categories of Pharmaceutical Water

The three main categories of water used in pharmaceutical manufacturing are:

  • Drinking water: meeting EPA national primary drinking water regulations. In Figure 3-1 drinking water is included in the category Suitable Non-Compendial.
  • Compendial water: meeting the compendial requirements for specific types of water in USP Monographs (i.e., Purified Water USP, Water for Injection USP).
  • “Suitable” non-compendial process waters: meeting the requirements of drinking water, but with additional treatment to meet process requirements. It may, or may not, contain added substances for microbial control and does not have to meet full compendial requirements for USP Water. In this Guide, we name the non-compendial process waters used in manufacturing by the final/major process step (i.e., Reverse Osmosis — RO water, Deionization — DI water, etc.).

Non-compendial water is not necessarily less critical, or less costly to produce or to qualify, than compendial water. It can enable the manufacturer to set product specific quality and/or test criteria that are appropriate for the specific product and processes.

Generally, more highly purified water is more expensive than less purified water. However, the specifics of each operation are different. For example, a plant with existing excess capacity of WFI might elect to use WFI over other grades even when unnecessary. In the example case, documentation defining water quality should identify the quality required for the product and why the WFI was used instead.

Decision Tree (Figure 3-1)

Figure 3-1 provides the framework of a diagram that can be developed by a manufacturer to show the requirements for water used in the pharmaceutical manufacturing processes. This diagram should be accompanied by documentation supporting the options chosen, with review and approval of Quality Assurance.
