The options chosen should be based on product and process specific requirements.

Decision Tree Notes:

  1. By test procedure definition, some analytical methods require USP Compendial waters. Quality should meet the needs of the analytical methods.
  2. Labs performing both cGMP and Non-cGMP operations should follow the cGMP path.
  3. Non-compendial water may be more highly purified than compendial water. Endotoxin and microbial quality is based on the process and quality standards of the product.
  4. Quality of final rinse water is determined by the type of product and subsequent processing steps. Where product contact surface is subsequently sanitized, final rinse with Suitable Non-Compendial water may be acceptable.
  5. Where product is purified downstream.
  6. More stringent endotoxin requirements (e.g., WFI quality) should be employed for water used in the final purification step for NON-Sterile parenteral grade APIs.

Note: Commitments made in drug applications override suggestions of this decision tree.

Commentary

Core Concept: The Three Categories of Pharmaceutical Water

ISPE divides pharmaceutical water into three tiers, from lowest to highest:

WFI (Water for Injection) - the most stringent
↓
PW / Compendial Water (Purified Water / compendial water)
↓
Non-Compendial Water (e.g. RO water, DI water)
↓
Drinking Water - the basic threshold

Common Misconception: Non-Compendial Water Is Not Low-Quality Water

The source text specifically stresses: Non-compendial water is not necessarily less critical, or less costly.

Non-compendial water simply means it does not need to meet every test in a USP monograph, but it may be:

  • Higher in purity than PW (for example, water from a three-stage RO/DI/UF treatment)
  • Not necessarily cheaper to validate (because you must define your own specification and provide the scientific justification)
  • Named after its final treatment step: RO Water, DI Water, UF Water, and so on

Everyday Analogy: The Decision Tree Is Like Ordering a Meal

The Decision Tree works like a restaurant's ordering process:

  • "Would you like a main course or a drink?" → Production use vs. Lab use
  • "Steak or salad for the main course?" → Parenteral product vs. Oral product
  • "How would you like the steak cooked?" → WFI vs. PW vs. Non-compendial

Each step narrows the options based on your needs until you arrive at the most suitable water quality. But note: if you have already committed to a specific water quality in your Drug Application, that commitment takes precedence over the Decision Tree's recommendation.

Practical Meaning of the Six Decision Tree Notes

These six notes reveal several key principles:

  • Notes 1-2 (laboratory): the analytical method itself may require compendial water; a cGMP laboratory always follows the cGMP path
  • Note 3 (non-compendial is not low quality): non-compendial water can be purer than compendial water; the focus is on controlling endotoxin and microorganisms
  • Note 4 (final rinse): if a subsequent sanitization step follows, non-compendial water may be used for the final rinse
  • Note 6 (API): the final purification step of a non-sterile injectable-grade API requires WFI-level endotoxin standards
Original Text — Table 3-1: Typical Pharmaceutical Water Types

Design Options for Pharmaceutical Water

Once water needs are determined based on usage, Table 3-1 identifies common design options for various types of pharmaceutical water in the industry. The order of components and actual installed equipment varies widely throughout the industry. Primary criteria in evaluating the options are:

  • To have suitable specification for water criteria (i.e., it must be adequate for the process and product)
  • To produce water consistent in composition and quality
  • To monitor key performance indicators for assurance that specifications are met

Table 3-1: Typical Pharmaceutical Process Water Types

Commentary

Core Concept: Three Criteria for Evaluating a Water Treatment System

  • Suitable Specification - the water quality must meet product and process needs
  • Consistent Quality - the system must reliably produce water of the same quality
  • Monitoring KPI - there must be a way to confirm that the water quality remains acceptable

Technical Comparison: Common Water Treatment Configurations

The table above yields several key technology combinations:

Pretreatment (common): Primary Filtration → Softening → Carbon Filtration
  ├─ RO route: RO (Single/Double Pass)
  │    ├─ + DI (Mixed Bed)  = RO/DI Water
  │    ├─ + EDI             = RO/EDI Water ← modern mainstream
  │    └─ + DI + UF         = RO/DI/UF Water
  ├─ DI route: Ion Exchange (Two Stage)
  │    └─ + UF              = DI/UF Water
  └─ Distillation route: Still        = Distilled Water

RO/EDI (reverse osmosis plus electrodeionization) is currently the most common PW production solution in industry, because it needs no chemical regeneration, is simple to maintain and has low running costs.

Key Point: How to Read Table 3-1 Correctly

This table is not a ranking that gets "better from left to right". It lists "technology combinations along different routes". Which combination to choose depends on:

  • Feed water quality (TDS - total dissolved solids, hardness, organics)
  • Product water specification (PW or WFI)
  • Site conditions (permits to handle chemicals, staff capability, space)
  • Economics (capital expenditure vs. operating cost)

Thought Exercise

Suppose your plant is in central Taiwan, with feed water TDS of about 200 ppm, and you need to supply both PW and WFI. From the options in Table 3-1, which pretreatment + purification combination would you consider first, and why?

Hint: consider the characteristics of Taiwan's water (moderate hardness, relatively high organics), the complexity of managing chemicals, and how WFI is produced (distillation vs. membrane methods).

Original Text — 3.2.1 Cost Implications

3.2.1 Cost Implications

Determining the economics of pharmaceutical/medical device water production is complex. Costs are quite predictable, but vary greatly depending on scale of operation, system design, actual usage, etc. The total operating cost to produce pharmaceutical waters is obtained by adding the cost of feed water to the costs of pretreatment (e.g., media filtration, carbon filtration, softening, and chemical addition) and final treatment (e.g., primary ion removal and polishing).

Other significant costs should be anticipated for validation, ongoing QA/QC, as well as waste treatment and sewerage. In addition, regulated industries must consider the risks (cost) of noncompliance and water system failures. Municipal feed water ranges from $1–3 per thousand gallons with even wider variations outside of the U.S.

Feed (surface or ground) water quality, generation technology and its associated capital cost, and product water specifications are then utilized to determine the total pharmaceutical water system net present value (NPV). The type of pharmaceutical water system design option selected is typically based on feed water total dissolved solids and hardness levels, organic and colloidal content, as well as anticipated water system utility costs (acid, caustic, salt, power, and source water). Consideration should also be given to maintenance requirements and available resources.

Although water treatment systems for generating either compendial (USP purified) or non-compendial pharmaceutical process waters significantly vary in system operational costs, NPV for each of these various types of process waters are quite similar. The only exception is DI process water generated through the use of a non-regenerable mixed bed bottle system, typically regenerated off site. However, membrane based systems do marginally produce the lowest net present values for pharmaceutical water generation. The NPV analysis is usually based on the water system capital cost and a five-year system operating cost.

Cost savings opportunities can be found in other places than just the quality of water and method of generation. Wastewater from the pretreatment or treatment systems can often be used for miscellaneous loads such as lawn irrigation, humidification, boiler feed, etc.

Commentary

Core Concept: Total Cost Structure of Pharmaceutical Water

The cost of pharmaceutical water is not just "buying equipment". The full cost includes:

  • Feed water cost (Feed Water): municipal water charges
  • Pretreatment cost (Pretreatment): filtration, softening, activated carbon
  • Final treatment cost (Final Treatment): RO, DI, EDI, distillation
  • Validation cost (Validation): IQ/OQ/PQ + ongoing monitoring
  • QA/QC cost: routine sampling, testing, trend analysis
  • Waste treatment cost (Waste Treatment): discharge treatment
  • Noncompliance risk cost (Noncompliance Risk): system failure, product loss

NPV Analysis Framework

Total NPV = Capital Cost + Σ (Annual Operating Cost / (1+r)^n)
                n=1 to 5

where Operating Cost includes:
  - Consumables (filter media, resin, membrane element replacement)
  - Chemicals (acids, bases, salt, sanitizing agents)
  - Energy (electricity, steam)
  - Labor (operation, maintenance, sampling and testing)
  - Water charges (feed water + discharge)

Key conclusion: the NPVs of the different water treatment systems are in fact fairly close, but Membrane-Based Systems (e.g. RO/EDI) have a slightly lower NPV, which is one reason they became the mainstream choice.

Key Point: Why a Five-Year Analysis Period

Why five years? The period must be long enough for operating costs to become a significant factor (compared with capital expenditure), but not so long that the forecasts become unreliable.

Exception: the NPV of non-regenerable mixed beds (Off-site Regenerated DI) is clearly higher, because the cost of replacing bottled resin and shipping it out for regeneration accumulates quickly over time.

Everyday Analogy: Buying vs. Leasing a Car

NPV analysis is like comparing "buying vs. leasing a car":

  • Distillation system = buying a premium car (high capital cost, stable maintenance cost)
  • RO/EDI system = buying a fuel-efficient car (moderate capital cost, lowest operating cost)
  • Off-site regenerated DI = a long-term lease (cheap at first but the most expensive after five years)

CDMO Practice: The Hidden Value of Wastewater Recovery

The source text mentions that wastewater can be used for irrigation, humidification, boiler make-up and so on. In a Taiwan CDMO setting, more practical recovery routes are:

  • RO reject water recovery can reach 75-85%, and the remaining 15-25% can be used as cooling tower make-up
  • Initial rinse water can be used for backwashing the pretreatment system
  • In regions where water resources are increasingly tight (such as central and southern Taiwan), wastewater recovery can be a plus point in the environmental impact assessment
Original Text — 3.3 System Planning

3.3 System Planning

High purity water and steam are the most widely used, and often the most expensive raw material or utility in a pharmaceutical facility. Improper sizing or selection of a steam or water system could limit or even shut down production if under sized; or compromise the reproducible quality and increase the capital cost if oversized. However, system sizing is not the starting point in design. Proper definition of water quality requirements and usage can save construction as well as operational costs.

Figure 3-2 shows a graphic representation of the system boundaries, limitations, and restrictions the designer faces when planning a pharmaceutical water system. Initial system planning reveals primary boundaries that establish the cornerstone for design criteria. These primary system boundaries are Water Quality, Use-Point Criteria, and System Criteria.

During initial planning, the limits of each boundary need to be established. The arrows encircling each boundary represent limitations that establish more specific operating strategies and ranges. When documenting these limitations, the designer should always indicate ranges of acceptability, rather than a specific value or position. This allows more flexibility in final planning and detailed design decisions.

The reality of certain restrictions will sometimes force a specific strategy. As long as the decision leads to an answer that is within the limits of the system boundaries, this is perfectly acceptable. An example is a facility where the use-point criteria require non-compendial water with microbial control. However, there happens to be an oversized WFI system in an adjacent area, so the designer decides to provide WFI to the use-point. In the example case, documentation defining water quality should identify the quality required for the product and why the WFI was used instead.

The primary emphasis of this section is to outline a systematic approach to planning a pharmaceutical water system. Figure 3-2 outlines a planning methodology that begins with the selection of water quality, given its own system constraints and limitations. Then the use point criteria are established, followed by an initial system planning exercise. Often, these sequential steps are repeated as information in the design process iterates, and further criteria about the overall system boundaries are identified.

Commentary

Key Warning: The Water System Is the Most Expensive Raw Material/Utility in a Drug Plant

The source text states directly that high-purity water and steam are the most widely used and most costly raw materials or utilities in a pharmaceutical plant. The consequences of selecting the wrong system:

  • Under-sized → limits capacity or even stops production
  • Over-sized → unstable quality (stagnant water) + wasted capital

But the main point is: system size is not the starting point of design - first define the water quality requirements and demand, then decide the system size.

Core Concept: The Three System Boundaries (Figure 3-2)

ISPE's water system planning methodology revolves around three Primary Boundaries:

  1. Water Quality - what grade of water is needed?
  2. Use-Point Criteria - what are the pressure, flow and temperature requirements at each point of use?
  3. System Criteria - the overall design of storage, generation and distribution

Each boundary has three layers of concept:

  • Boundaries: lines that cannot be crossed (e.g. regulatory requirements)
  • Limitations: operating ranges and strategies (e.g. acceptable ranges)
  • Restrictions: mandatory constraints imposed by real-world conditions (e.g. existing equipment, space)

Everyday Analogy: Three Checkpoints in Building a House

Planning a water system is like building a house:

  • Water Quality = building codes (the standard that must be met, non-negotiable)
  • Use-Point Criteria = residents' needs (how many taps each room needs and at what water temperature)
  • System Criteria = engineering plan (size of the water tower, pipe layout, pump selection)

These three influence one another, and several iterations are needed to reach the best solution.

Key Point: Ranges vs. Fixed Values

The source text specifically stresses that when documenting limitations, ranges of acceptability should be used rather than fixed values. This principle is very important in practice:

  • Writing "temperature 70-80°C" is better than "temperature 75°C" - it gives later design more flexibility
  • Writing "conductivity ≤ 1.3 μS/cm" is better than "conductivity 0.5 μS/cm" - it avoids over-design

This is fully consistent with the Design Range / Operating Range concept in Chapter 2.

Original Text — 3.3.1 Establish Water Quality

3.3.1 Establish Water Quality

The first step in the evaluation of water systems is the selection of water quality required for the specific product and process operation. Selection is based primarily on the dosage and form, and the microbiological and chemical purity criteria set for the product for which the water is used. The selection must consider underlying factors that have impacts on quality control; installed and operating cost; maintenance and practicality.

See Section 3.2 in establishing possible water quality via development of the decision tree. Making notes as the water quality is designated for each use-point, indicating the basis for each decision. Simple annotations from the supporting documentation will be useful in later stages of the planning process. System design constraints may provide the motivation to challenge water quality or other criteria, particularly when it can be demonstrated that the change does not affect product quality or manufacturing controls.

Commentary

Step 1: Define Water Quality Requirements

This is the first step of the four-step methodology. The core considerations in choosing water quality are:

  • Dosage Form: injectables → WFI; oral liquids → PW or Non-compendial
  • Microbial Criteria: sterile products require strict bioburden control
  • Chemical Purity: highly concentrated processes require lower TOC and conductivity

Practical Tip: Take Good Notes!

When specifying the water quality for each use point, the source text recommends recording the rationale for each decision at the same time. This is not a casual suggestion - these notes serve three major purposes in later stages:

  1. Traceability: during audits you can explain the reasoning behind each water quality choice
  2. Challengeability: if the system design is later constrained, you can go back and re-evaluate the water quality requirement
  3. Knowledge management: new projects can refer to the decision logic of past ones
Original Text — 3.3.2 Characterize Use Point

3.3.2 Characterize Use Point

Once the initial selection of water quality has been established, the operational criteria should be characterized for each use point. A matrix should be developed to outline the primary criteria required for system design.

Each use point should be annotated with the proper values for pressure, flow, and the temperature range of water entering unit operation, or process point from the water supply system. Establishing a range, rather than a fixed value, increases opportunities for system optimization by allowing a more flexible approach to final design.

This data can be organized in many ways, but a well-planned spreadsheet can simplify the planning process and provide clear decision pathways for future detailed design activities.

Table 3-2: Use Point Criteria (Example)

Flowrate is primarily used to size lines, whereas Daily Use leads to storage and generation decisions. The Diversity Factor is one way to level-out anticipated usage, assuming that not all loads happen every day or at the same time. This table indicates a Clean in Place (CIP) system and stopper-washer that are both likely to be used on the same day, but never at the same time. Therefore, only the higher flowrate is relevant to loop sizing as shown in the Design Flowrate column.

Once the location and qualities are finalized, the various properties can be charted on a requirements analysis histogram. This can be done with the aid of a computer and either simulation or spreadsheet software for larger systems, or done manually for small systems. At this point, basic Process Flow Diagrams also provide a good pictorial view of the water qualities, locations and the point-of-use properties.

Commentary

Step 2: Characterize the Use Points

The second step is to build a detailed requirements matrix for each point of use, covering three core parameters:

  • Pressure - the minimum supply pressure required at the use point
  • Flow - instantaneous flow (LPM) and daily consumption (LPD)
  • Temperature Range - the acceptable range of supply water temperature

Note: again the emphasis is on a range rather than a fixed value.

Key Concept: Diversity Factor

The Diversity Factor is a very practical concept in water system design:

  • Value = 1: the equipment will be in use in the design scenario
  • Value = 0: the equipment will not be in use at the same time during that period

Taking Table 3-2 as an example: CIP and the Stopper Washer will not draw water at the same time, so the instantaneous flow takes only the larger value (40 LPM), but the daily consumption of both must be counted (1200 + 460 = 1660 LPD).

Everyday Analogy: Diversity Factor = Elevator Capacity Design

In a 20-story office building, not all employees take the elevator at the same moment. The Diversity Factor is the tool for estimating this "simultaneous use rate":

  • Diversity of flow: like "the number of people waiting for the elevator at peak time" → determines pipe size
  • Diversity of daily consumption: like "the total number of rides in a day" → determines the size of the tank and the generation equipment

Key Point: Two Numbers for Two Different Purposes

  • Flowrate (instantaneous flow) → used to determine Line Sizing
  • Daily Use → used to determine Storage and Generation capacity

Confusing the two is a common beginner's mistake. Piping design looks at the "maximum simultaneous flow", while storage and generation look at the "total consumption in a day".

Thought Exercise

An aseptic filling line has the following water-using equipment:

  • Compounding tank cleaning (CIP): 50 LPM, 2000 L per batch, 2 batches per day
  • Stopper washer: 15 LPM, 600 L per batch, 1 batch per day
  • Filling machine rinse: 5 LPM, 200 L per batch, 3 batches per day
  • CIP and the stopper washer do not run at the same time

Calculate: (1) Design Flowrate for loop sizing? (2) Total daily demand?

Original Text — 3.3.3 Establish System Criteria

3.3.3 Establish System Criteria

Histogram analysis is beneficial for determining overall system peak demand(s), average demand, and the relationships between peak demand time periods and their flow rates. Figure 3-4 shows a hypothetical storage tank profile using the 24-hour demand profile from Figure 3-3.

There is no “Rule-of-Thumb” for minimum water level, or the optimum water level to turn on a still. However, these charts provide the tools for creating various scenarios to simulate recovery times from a failure, future expansion or reduction capabilities and analyze other factors that allow design of a properly sized water generation, storage and distribution system.

System planning and analysis also reveals other restrictions that influence design, and often lead the designer to re-evaluate the primary boundaries as discussed earlier. These restrictions might include items such as:

  • Must the system be available at all times?
  • What are the constraints on a shutdown?
  • Is the plant/personnel able to handle chemicals properly? Are permits in place?
  • Is production batched or continuous?
  • Are the products dedicated or multiple product groups?
  • How is campaigning between products handled?
  • How much time is available for sanitization? Should redundancy be provided to allow adequate time for sterilization?
Commentary

Step 3: Establish System Criteria

The third step uses the data gathered in the first two steps and applies analytical tools to establish the overall design criteria for the system:

  • Histogram Analysis - plot the 24-hour water usage curve to identify peaks and troughs
  • Storage Tank Profile - simulate the change in tank level under different scenarios
  • Failure Recovery Simulation - how long the system can last when a failure occurs

Everyday Analogy: Water Tower Design = Power Bank Capacity

Designing a water storage system is like choosing a power bank:

  • Peak demand = the period when your phone drains fastest (watching video, playing games)
  • Average demand = normal usage over a day
  • Recovery Time = how long it takes to recharge the power bank fully
  • Tank size = power bank capacity (too small is not enough, too large is bulky and wasteful)

There is no fixed "best water level"; it must be decided from your actual usage pattern.

Seven Must-Ask Questions (System Restrictions Checklist)

These seven questions are a "reality check" for system design, and each one may change your design direction:

  1. 24/7 availability? → is a backup system needed
  2. Downtime restrictions? → affects maintenance and sanitization scheduling
  3. Chemical handling capability? → determines whether regenerable DI (requiring acid and base) can be used
  4. Batch vs. continuous? → affects peak flow and storage requirements
  5. Dedicated vs. multi-product? → multi-product operation must consider different water quality needs
  6. Product changeover (Campaigning)? → is a full flush needed between changeovers
  7. Sanitization time? → is redundant equipment needed so that sanitization does not interrupt supply

CDMO Practice: Water System Challenges of Multi-Product Changeover

For a CDMO, questions 5-7 are especially critical:

  • Multi-client products → different clients may have different additional water quality requirements
  • Campaigning → cleaning validation of the water system at product changeover is an important topic
  • Sanitization redundancy → the water system of an aseptic filling CDMO usually needs Hot Water Sanitization, which requires a reserved time window and may require a backup loop
Original Text — 3.3.4 Revisit Water Quality

3.3.4 Revisit Water Quality

With all use points characterized for temperature range, and demand, the quality of water is revisited. A thorough review of use point criteria typically reveals a wide range of acceptable delivery conditions for the water. Since it is typically not practical to operate multiple water systems to provide the exact water conditions desired of the end product, compromises must be made.

These compromises might include providing water of a higher quality than required to simplify the water treatment or delivery systems, or provisions for controlling water consumption at a use point to limit peak demands. Whatever compromises are made, water must be delivered at conditions within the boundary limits.

Commentary

Step 4: Revisit Water Quality

This is the last step of the four-step methodology, and the one most easily overlooked. After the analysis in the first three steps, you may find that:

  • The water quality requirements of some use points can be merged (reducing system complexity)
  • Some use points can accept a higher quality of water (replacing PW with WFI to unify the system)
  • The consumption of some use points can be adjusted (limiting peak demand)

Key Principle: Compromise Is Inevitable

The source text uses the very pragmatic phrase "compromises must be made". In real planning, common compromise strategies include:

  • Upgrade strategy: use high-quality water everywhere → simplifies the system but increases cost
  • Demand Control strategy: limit the water use of certain use points → lowers peak demand
  • Scheduling strategy: stagger the operating times of high-consumption equipment → smaller tanks and generation equipment

Whatever compromise is made, the bottom line is that water quality must stay within the boundary limits.

Everyday Analogy: The Four Steps = a PDCA Cycle

These four steps are in effect a mini Plan-Do-Check-Act cycle:

  1. Plan: define water quality requirements (Step 1)
  2. Do: characterize the use points (Step 2)
  3. Check: establish system criteria and identify restrictions (Step 3)
  4. Act: revisit and make compromises (Step 4) → then return to Step 1 and iterate

Good water system planning usually goes through 2-3 iterations before it is finalized.

Original Text — 3.4 System Design

3.4 System Design

Once the support areas, back up requirements, future growth, or expansion capabilities are established, detailed design of the system can begin. The process requirements determine all the points-of-use (POU) on the distribution system. User locations determine how to distribute the system (e.g., central storage, multiple loops/branches, etc.). One or several of the use points may need either different qualities or other properties that single it out from the rest. In this case alternatives to the water system criteria are considered, such as using an ambient or cold distribution versus a hot system. Plant shift operating hours must also be considered since there may be an inability to perform regular heat sanitizations of cold systems, for example.

The boundaries, limitations, and restrictions that were identified in the initial planning stages should now be integrated into the design approach. Further considerations might include the physical area a system needs for support, one production area, one building, or multiple buildings on a site. This could determine the size of the system and whether it is made up of multiple tanks, or multiple loop storage and distributed systems.

For example, central systems are higher in initial capital, but lower in operation and maintenance and possibly lower overall cost on per unit basis. The capital is higher primarily due to the larger generation, storage, and distribution equipment or system. Alternatively, multiple generation systems may require less initially for each smaller system but more cost in terms of capital and operating and maintenance for the same total capacity.

All systems have a fixed capacity and will eventually have a failure. Therefore, if a piece of equipment fails a plan should be in place to deal with the down time. Having backup generation equipment for the critical components such as a still or deionization equipment, should be considered. The backup equipment can be used in a lead-lag type operation and/or to meet a specific duration of a peak demand.

More detailed descriptions of the alternatives for the various unit operations required for production of pharmaceutical grade water are discussed in the following chapters. Rationale is provided for decisions that will surface regarding quality, cost, performance, maintenance, and reliability as the system is developed in detail.

Commentary

Core Decisions in System Design

Once in the system design phase, the main decisions to be made are:

  1. Distribution architecture: Central Storage vs. Multiple Loops
  2. Temperature strategy: Hot Distribution (≥70°C) vs. Ambient/Cold
  3. Redundancy strategy: Lead-Lag Operation vs. N+1 Redundancy
  4. Scale strategy: One Large System vs. Multiple Small Systems

Key Point: Hot vs. Cold Distribution

The choice of temperature strategy directly affects the microbial control approach:

  • Hot Distribution (≥70°C): continuously bacteriostatic and needs no periodic sanitization, but energy consumption is high and the piping material requirements are demanding
  • Cold/Ambient Distribution: low energy consumption, but requires periodic Heat Sanitization
  • Key restriction: if the plant runs 24/7, a cold system may have no time window for heat sanitization → hot distribution is forced

Key Point: Lead-Lag Redundant Operation

Lead-Lag Operation is a common redundancy strategy:

  • Lead (duty equipment): runs normally
  • Lag (standby equipment): on standby and takes over automatically if the duty equipment fails
  • The two can also be rotated so that both units stay in good condition
  • Particularly suitable for critical equipment such as stills and RO membrane skids

CDMO Practice: Special Considerations in System Design

For an aseptic filling CDMO, system design needs some additional considerations:

  • Client audit expectations: large pharmaceutical clients usually prefer a Hot WFI Loop, as it is the industry "gold standard"
  • Future expansion: CDMO businesses grow quickly, and reserving 30-50% expansion capacity is common practice
  • Multiple buildings: if several filling lines are spread across different clean areas, consider whether to supply centrally or by zone
  • Redundancy requirements: the loss from an aseptic filling stoppage is huge, so Lead-Lag redundancy is recommended for all critical equipment

Comprehensive Exercise

You are planning a new CDMO aseptic filling plant with the following conditions:

  • Two filling lines, on different floors of the same building
  • WFI is needed for filling, compounding, CIP and stopper washing
  • The plant operates 24/7, with an 8-hour maintenance window each week
  • A third filling line is expected to be added within three years

Based on what you learned in this chapter, answer the following questions:

  1. Would you choose a Central System or Multiple Systems? Why?
  2. Would you choose Hot Distribution or Cold + Periodic Sanitization? Why?
  3. What is your redundancy strategy?

Section Summary

  • Water quality hierarchy: Drinking Water → Non-Compendial → PW (Compendial) → WFI; the selection is based on product characteristics, process requirements and intended use. Non-compendial water does not mean low-quality water.
  • Decision Tree: Figure 3-1 provides a systematic process for selecting water quality, but commitments in the drug application take precedence over the Decision Tree.
  • Cost analysis: use NPV (five-year period) to compare options. Membrane systems (RO/EDI) have the lowest NPV; the NPV differences between water treatment systems are in fact small, except for off-site regenerated DI.
  • Four-step planning method: (1) define water quality → (2) characterize the use points → (3) establish system criteria → (4) revisit water quality. This is an iterative process, usually taking 2-3 rounds.
  • Diversity Factor: used to estimate simultaneous use; instantaneous flow determines pipe size, and daily consumption determines storage and generation capacity.
  • System design: Central vs. Multiple Systems, Hot vs. Cold Distribution, and Lead-Lag Backup are the three core decisions. All compromises must stay within the boundary limits.
4

Chapter 4: Pretreatment Options

p43-p55

Chapter 4: Pretreatment Options
ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p43-p55

Learning Objectives

  1. Explain the role of pretreatment in pharmaceutical water systems and its relationship to final treatment.
  2. Understand the requirements for feedwater quality testing and documentation, and the strategy for monitoring seasonal variation.
  3. Distinguish the four categories of impurity control: fouling, scaling, organics, and microbial growth.
  4. Understand when microbial control agents are added and removed, especially the differences between chlorine and chloramine.
  5. Explain the importance of pH in pretreatment and the strategies for removing carbon dioxide and ammonia.
  6. Understand the principles for selecting materials of construction for pretreatment systems and the related cost-optimization considerations.

Section Navigator

4.1 Introduction 4.2 Process Design 4.3 Feedwater Testing 4.4 Output Water Quality 4.5 Control of Fouling 4.6 Control of Scaling 4.7 Removal of Organics 4.8 Microbial Growth Control 4.9 Removal of Microbial Control Agents 4.10 Anion Changes 4.11 Importance of pH 4.12 Materials of Construction 4.13 Pretreatment Summary
4.1

4.1 Introduction

Introduction to Pretreatment  |  p45

4.1 Introduction

Pretreatment is all process steps or unit operations prior to the last (final) water treatment step. Pretreatment is a series of unit operations to modify the feed water quality so that it will be of adequate quality to be fed to a final treatment step. This final step may be Reverse Osmosis, Ultrafiltration, Multi or Mixed Bed Deionization or Distillation. These final steps are discussed in Chapters 5 and 6.

Reverse Osmosis is unique since it can be a pretreatment step, in addition to being a final treatment step. Reverse Osmosis applications in pretreatment are discussed in this chapter and Chapter 11, but Reverse Osmosis as a technology is discussed in Chapters 5 and 6.

The initial sections of this chapter discuss the process design (programming issues) for pretreatment design including feed water quality and output water quality from pretreatment. The chapter then discusses the selection of treatment options (i.e. unit operations) for four groups of impurities:

  • Control of fouling — removal of turbidity and particulates
  • Control of scaling — removal of hardness and metals
  • Removal of organics and microbiological impurities
  • Removal of microbial control agents

Pre-treatment options are summarized in Figure 4-1 at the end of the chapter.

The final sections of the chapter discuss the importance of anion composition/concentration, pH, materials of construction, and pretreatment system control.

Commentary

Core Concept: The Role of Pretreatment

Pretreatment refers to all process steps that come "before" the final treatment step. Its purpose is to bring feedwater quality up to the level that the final treatment equipment can accept.

The final treatment step may be:

  • Reverse Osmosis (RO)
  • Ultrafiltration (UF)
  • Deionization (DI) - multiple-bed or mixed-bed
  • Distillation

When pretreatment is done well, the final treatment equipment runs stably; when it is done poorly, the final treatment fails frequently and maintenance costs soar.

Everyday analogy: pretreatment is like preparing ingredients before cooking

Before cooking you wash, peel, and cut the ingredients - that is "pretreatment". If the ingredients are not properly cleaned (impurities in the water not removed), the dish will go wrong no matter how good the cooking technique is (no matter how advanced the final treatment equipment is). Pretreatment is the key step that determines the long-term stability of the water system.

Key point: the dual role of RO

Reverse osmosis is special - it can be a pretreatment step (for example, using RO to remove most of the ions before distillation) or a final treatment step (directly producing Purified Water). This dual role requires careful attention to RO's position in the system design.

4.2

4.2 Process Design of Pretreatment

Pretreatment Process Design  |  pp. 45-47

4.2 Process Design of Pretreatment

Process design of the pretreatment system is the specification of the unit operations or process steps to treat the feed water. Typical information includes flow rates, temperatures, pressure, and composition of all streams. Detailed mechanical design of the equipment for a given unit operation or process step is beyond the scope of this Guide.

The process design (programming issues) for a pretreatment system may include:

  1. Required quantity and quality of the water from the final treatment process.
  2. Temperature constraints on the water used in a pharmaceutical process and the approach to microbial control.
  3. The final treatment option that has been chosen, as this defines the required water quality leaving pretreatment.
  4. Quality of the feed water that is the input to the pretreatment system (water quality to be validated over a one year period).
  5. Difference between input water quality and desired output water quality. The difference determines impurities that must be removed by the pretreatment system. The difference is determined by performing a material balance. Attention should be paid to impurities and minor components.
  6. Pretreatment options to provide the desired removal of impurities considering other factors such as capabilities of the labor force, economics, waste disposal, environmental considerations, validation, and the available space and utilities.

In addition to defining the options for removal of impurities, the approach taken to microbial control is an integral part of the process design of the pretreatment system. Considerations include:

  1. If the drinking quality water to the pretreatment system comes from a municipality in the United States, it will typically contain chlorine, or chloramines, as a microbial control agent. In Europe, ozone is the more common microbial control agent.
  2. If the quantity of microbial control agent is insufficient, additional agent may be added or provision made to periodically sanitize the initial equipment in the pretreatment system.
  3. At some point in the pretreatment process, the microbial control agent must be removed before going to the final treatment. At this point, a means of either continuous or periodic sanitization needs to be selected.

Commentary

Core Concept: Six Considerations in Pretreatment Process Design

Pretreatment design is not a matter of picking a few pieces of equipment and stringing them together; it is a systematic engineering decision process. The six considerations, in order, are:

  1. Quantity and quality of the final product water - the end point determines the starting point
  2. Temperature limitations and microbial control strategy - cold system vs hot system
  3. Final treatment method - RO, distillation, and DI each have different feedwater requirements
  4. Feedwater quality - requires a one-year validation cycle to capture seasonal changes
  5. Material balance - the difference between feedwater quality and target quality determines which impurities must be removed
  6. Overall considerations - staff capability, economics, wastewater treatment, space, utilities, and so on

Key point: regional differences in microbial control agents

  • United States: municipal water usually contains chlorine or chloramine
  • Europe: ozone is used more commonly

This difference directly affects pretreatment system design - chlorine and chloramine are removed by different methods and affect downstream equipment (especially RO membranes) differently.

Everyday analogy: the role of the microbial control agent

A microbial control agent is like the chlorine in a swimming pool - it suppresses bacterial growth and protects upstream equipment from microbial contamination. But pool water is not drinkable, and in the same way chlorinated water cannot go directly into the final treatment equipment. So at some point the "disinfectant" must be removed, and after that cleanliness is maintained through periodic sanitization.

Added Substances in Pretreatment

The USP requirement that compendial waters should contain "no added substances" eliminates addition of chemicals to "Purified Water" or Water For Injection. However, addition of chemical agents is not prohibited in pretreatment. Substances are frequently added in pretreatment and subsequently removed in the pretreatment or final treatment. Some examples are:

  • Chlorine (to control microbial growth, removed in later stages of pretreatment)
  • Sodium ions (in softener with exchange for multivalent ions, removed in ion removal process)
  • Acid (for degasification to remove carbon dioxide, counter ions removed in a subsequent ion removal process)
  • Sulfite (to reduce chlorine to chloride, or chloramines to ammonium and chloride while forming sulfate, removal by softening or ion removal process)
  • Sequestrants (to prevent scaling in final treatment, removed by RO in final treatment)
  • pH control agents (removed in ion removal process)

Added substances are an issue if they result in an increase in microbial growth or endotoxins.

Commentary

Core Concept: "Chemicals may be added in pretreatment, but not in the final product water"

The USP requires that pharmaceutical waters (Purified Water or WFI) contain no "added substances". This does not mean chemicals cannot be added during pretreatment - the key is that every added substance must be completely removed in pretreatment or final treatment.

Common pretreatment additives and their removal routes:

  • Chlorine → activated carbon adsorption or reduction with sodium bisulfite
  • Sodium → removed by ion exchange
  • Acid → residual ions removed by ion exchange after degasification
  • Sequestrants → rejected by the RO membrane

Key point: the risks of additives

Additives in pretreatment become a problem if they increase microbial growth or raise endotoxin levels. For example, sodium bisulfite is used to remove chlorine, but it also removes the water's microbial control capability, which can lead to rapid microbial proliferation in the piping.

Investment and Operating Cost Relationship

A final consideration is the relationship between investment and operating dollars in pretreatment, and the performance and cost of the final treatment process. The following are generally true:

  • A final treatment system will not operate reliably over the long term, without reliable operation of the pretreatment system.
  • Inadequate operation in pretreatment (breakthrough of particulates, hardness, or chlorine) may not immediately affect water quality from final treatment, but it will be reflected in long term maintenance and operating reliability, and possibly in water quality.
  • Investment in pretreatment capability and reliability can return many times the investment in final treatment maintenance costs.
  • Pharmaceutical water systems are expected to generate water meeting final pharmaceutical product water standards. The system should be designed to control impurity spikes in the incoming water quality, or seasonal impurity profile changes.

There is no single "right" answer to the process design of the pretreatment system. Pretreatment system process design is a series of choices and options, each with advantages and disadvantages.

Commentary

Key point: the "leverage effect" of pretreatment investment

ISPE conveys an important business concept here: every extra dollar invested in pretreatment can save several times as much in maintenance costs in final treatment.

  • A pretreatment failure (such as hardness breakthrough) does not immediately affect product water quality, but it shortens RO membrane life or accelerates scaling of the still
  • Pretreatment design must be able to cope with peak fluctuations (impurity spikes) and seasonal changes in feedwater quality
  • There is no single correct answer - every pretreatment design is a combination of choices, each with advantages and disadvantages

Practical application: economic thinking in pretreatment design

In a CDMO environment, multiple product lines share the same water system. If the pretreatment is not designed to be robust enough, any single breakthrough can lead to:

  • Early replacement of RO membranes (each membrane costs several thousand US dollars)
  • Still shutdown due to scaling (affecting all downstream production lines)
  • Microbial excursions that delay batch release

The return on investment (ROI) of the pretreatment system is therefore often far higher than that of upgrading the final treatment equipment.

4.3

4.3 Feedwater to Pretreatment Quality: Testing and Documentation

Feedwater Quality: Testing and Documentation  |  pp. 47-48

4.3 Feedwater Quality: Testing and Documentation

Compendial pharmaceutical water systems are required to use feed water complying with "Drinking Water" standards.

Most pharmaceutical manufacturers utilize municipal water supplies. This water generally meets "Drinking Water" quality standards and is treated with a microbial control agent. Historically in the US, the microbial control agent is chlorine, but chloramine is now used with increasing frequency. Either feed water composition or microbial control agent concentration may be subject to occasional and seasonal variations. These variations may negatively impact water quality, and can be detected only by extensive sampling.

Typical contaminants in feed water include:

  • Particulates — Silt, dust, pollen, pipe scale, iron and silica, undissolved minerals and organics
  • Inorganics — Calcium and magnesium salts, heavy metals (iron, aluminum, and silica) with their corresponding anions
  • Organics — Naturally occurring byproducts of vegetative decay (humic and fulvic acids), man-made organics (pesticides, automotive pollution)
  • Bacteria — Bacterial contamination and its byproducts, endotoxins, and pyrogens

Testing recommendations include:

  • Documentation that feed water meets drinking water quality, based on municipality testing possibly supplemented by local or in-process testing.
  • Monitoring for microbial control agent levels at the start of the pretreatment system. A chlorine level of 0.2–1.0 ppm is generally considered adequate to control microbial growth.
  • Specific testing for contaminants known or suspected of being present in the feed water (e.g., pesticides in agricultural areas).

Commentary

Key point: the shift toward chloramine

Municipal water treatment in the United States is gradually shifting from chlorine to chloramine. This has a major impact on pharmaceutical water systems, because chloramine:

  • Is adsorbed on activated carbon much more slowly than chlorine and needs a longer contact time
  • Decomposes to produce ammonia, which can pass through RO and distillation systems
  • Requires a different carbon bed design and tighter monitoring

If the feedwater contains chloramine, the complexity of the system design increases significantly.

Discussion Questions

Q1: Why does ISPE recommend that feedwater quality go through a "one-year validation cycle"? Is testing once or twice not enough?

Q2: Your plant is in an agricultural area, and the tap water often becomes turbid after summer rains. Which of the four contaminant categories does this belong to? What additional tests would you add?

4.4

4.4 Output Water from Pretreatment: Quality of Feedwater to Final Treatment

Pretreatment Product Water Quality  |  pp. 48-49

4.4 Output Water Quality to Final Treatment

The goals for pretreatment are to provide water quality that minimizes the operating and maintenance problems in the final treatment equipment and to permit the final treatment step to produce water meeting the desired specifications for final treatment.

If pretreatment is inadequate, resulting problems can become very large in magnitude, as seen in Table 4-1:

*Membrane dependent  |  Table 4-1 from ISPE Vol.4

For Membranes: The concerns are fouling by suspended solids (particulates) and scaling (precipitating solids) as water is removed. A typical goal for control in pretreatment might be a silt density index (SDI) of 3–5 and hardness of <1 grain/gallon. Membranes tolerate chlorides but only some membranes tolerate chlorine.

For Distillation: The concerns are scale formation due to hardness and corrosion due to chlorides. Distillation has no tolerance for chlorine due to corrosion and carryover to the product. Distillation has some tolerance for particulates.

Commentary

Core Concept: The Decision Implications of Table 4-1

This table is the risk matrix for the whole pretreatment design - it tells you how much the final treatment equipment will be affected if a given class of impurity is not properly handled in pretreatment.

  • RO systems are most sensitive to: particulates (fouling), hardness (scaling), and chlorine (membrane degradation), but are not sensitive to chloride
  • Distillation systems are most sensitive to: chlorine (corrosion + carryover into the product) and chloride (corrosion), while being more tolerant of particulates

When designing pretreatment, first determine your final treatment method, then check this table to identify the impurities that most need priority control.

Key indicator: SDI (Silt Density Index)

SDI (Silt Density Index) is the standard test method for measuring the ability of particulates in water to plug an RO membrane.

  • Test method: at a fixed pressure (30 psi), measure the flux decline through a 0.45 µm filter membrane over a specified time
  • Pretreatment target: SDI 3-5 (RO membrane suppliers usually require SDI < 5)
  • Hardness target: < 1 grain/gallon (about 17 ppm as CaCO3)

Everyday analogy: different kitchenware is afraid of different things

An RO membrane is like a fine filter cloth - it is most afraid of being punctured by hard particles (particulate plugging) and of chemical attack (chlorine). A still is like a stainless steel pot - it is most afraid of scale building up on the heating surface (hardness) and of salts corroding the metal (chloride). Once you know what your "kitchenware" is afraid of, you know what to watch for in the "ingredient preparation" (pretreatment).

4.5

4.5 Control of Fouling: Removal of Turbidity and Particulates

Fouling Control: Removing Turbidity and Particulates  |  p49

4.5 Control of Fouling

The principal methods for removing particulates and reducing turbidity are:

  • Clarification and the accompanying operations of flocculation, coagulation, and sedimentation
  • Depth or Media filtration including single and multimedia filtration (particles retained by the media)

Clarification is not applicable, as feed water sources are potable quality or better.

Depth or media filtration is used in pharmaceutical water systems and is often the first step in a pretreatment system. Multi-sized sand is the most common media, but other media may provide better performance with some feed waters. Removal of particulates down to 10 microns is possible and depends on selection of media.

Microbial growth is a key concern in a media filter, unless the feed water contains a microbial control agent. Otherwise, microbial control in the depth filter is required (e.g., periodic sanitization using either heat or a chemical sanitizing agent).

Commentary

Core Concept: Fouling Control

Fouling is the deposition of particulates on membrane or equipment surfaces, causing performance to decline. Control methods:

  • Clarification: including flocculation, coagulation, and sedimentation - but because the feedwater for pharmaceutical use is already drinking-water grade, this step is usually not needed
  • Depth/media filtration: the most commonly used first step; multimedia sand filters can remove particles larger than 10 microns

Key point: the microbial risk of media filters

A media filter is a breeding ground for microorganisms - the media has a large surface area, the water flows slowly, and nutrients are retained. If the feedwater contains no microbial control agent (such as chlorine), the filter must be sanitized regularly (thermal or chemical sanitization); otherwise the filter becomes a "petri dish" for microorganisms.

This is a very important design consideration: while preventing particulate fouling, you cannot neglect microbial control.

Discussion Questions

Q: In your pretreatment system, the media filter is located after the activated carbon bed (where the chlorine has already been removed). What risks could this configuration bring? How would you address them?

4.6

4.6 Control of Scaling: Removal of Hardness and Metals

Scaling Control: Removing Hardness and Metals  |  pp. 49-51

4.6 Control of Scaling

When water is separated from its impurities in the final treatment process, those compounds with low solubility are concentrated to the point where they precipitate. This precipitation, or scaling, is the result of exceeding the solubility of the divalent and trivalent cations, usually as a sparingly soluble salt such as carbonate or sulfate. The methods of control are:

  • Removal by ion exchange — principally calcium and magnesium, may include divalent and trivalent ions such as iron, aluminum and silica. Pretreatment is usually water softening (exchanging the ions causing hardness for sodium ions).
  • Removal of carbonate by acidification — converts carbonate to carbon dioxide, which is removed by subsequent degasification.
  • Removal by nanofiltration — water passes through the membrane and compounds are retained by the membrane and removed as a purge stream.

Water softening ion exchange is a very common process used in pretreatment. It is applicable for all flow rates and hardness levels, well understood and easy to operate. It involves the handling of salt only, and produces a non-hazardous waste stream. However, the high total dissolved solids (TDS) in the waste stream may limit disposal options.

For large flow rates (>50 gpm or 0.18 m3/min) and high hardness (>50 ppm), degasification (after acidification) may be the process of choice.

Nanofiltration is a membrane process that may be applicable with certain feed waters. Removal of divalent ions can be greater than 98%.

Chemical injection (sequestrants) is an alternate method to control scaling. These compounds "tie up and complex" the offending ions to form a more soluble compound that will not precipitate in the final treatment process.

Commentary

Everyday analogy: scale is like the white deposit in a kettle

At home, boiling water often leaves white scale on the bottom of the kettle - that is calcium carbonate. In an industrial water system, these deposits can plug RO membranes or coat the heating surfaces of stills, seriously affecting performance. A softener acts like a "water conditioner", exchanging the scale-forming calcium and magnesium ions for sodium ions that do not form scale.

Softener Location: A Key Design Choice

A key choice in the process design of the pretreatment system is location of the softener. The two options are either before or after removal of the microbial control agent (often chlorine).

Softener located prior to removal of microbial control agent: The principal advantage is protection of the softener from microbial growth by the microbial control agent present in the feed water. If the microbial control agent is chlorine, it will have only a minor effect on resin life and efficiency at levels typically encountered (<1 ppm).

Softener located after removal of microbial control agent: The advantage is better resin life and capacity (due to absence of chlorine). However, this must be balanced by the need to protect the softener from microbial growth and endotoxin load (i.e., by periodic sanitization with the associated cost of heat or chemicals, labor, down time, and waste stream disposal).

Commentary

Key point: equipment position determines microbial risk

In a pretreatment system, the relative position of each piece of equipment affects the microbial control strategy. All equipment downstream of the point where the microbial control agent (such as chlorine) is removed needs additional microbial control measures (periodic sanitization, UV treatment, and so on). This is the part of pretreatment design that most requires systematic thinking.

Core Concept: The softener-position dilemma

In most cases, placing the softener "before chlorine removal" is the better choice - you sacrifice a little resin life in exchange for continuous microbial protection, and the overall cost is lower.

4.7

4.7 Removal of Organics

Organics Removal  |  pp. 50-51

4.7 Removal of Organics

The methods for removal of organics are:

  • Ozone
  • Strong Base Ion Exchange
  • Barrier filtration (microfiltration, ultrafiltration or Reverse Osmosis)
  • Polymer Flocculant
  • Carbon

Ozone is a powerful oxidant that controls microbial growth and reduces the concentration of organics due to oxidation, but requires compatible materials of construction.

Strong base ion exchange removes organics but results in a purge stream containing high concentrations of brine and organics.

Barrier filtration, when appropriately sized, captures organics and microbial growth on the barrier and can be aided by addition of a polymer flocculant. A potential problem is microbial growth "growing through the barrier" which results in contamination on the downstream side.

Carbon is probably the most common method of reducing organics. It provides multiple functions, including removal of organics as well as removal or reduction of chlorine and chloramines. The advantages are that it is a frequently practiced technology, it performs multiple functions, and effectively "cleans up the feed water". The disadvantage is that it is a source of microbial growth, as well as a source of nutrients.

Commentary

Key point: activated carbon is a double-edged sword

Activated carbon is the most commonly used "multi-purpose performer" in pretreatment: it removes organics and removes chlorine/chloramine. But its biggest problem is that it is itself an ideal growth medium for microorganisms - the huge surface area and the adsorbed organics become a nutrient source.

Activated carbon beds must therefore be supported by:

  • Periodic thermal or chemical sanitization
  • Rigorous microbial monitoring
  • A reasonable replacement/regeneration cycle

Everyday analogy: activated carbon is like a refrigerator deodorizer

Activated carbon is like a bamboo charcoal deodorizer pack in the refrigerator - it adsorbs odors (organics), but after long use the pack itself goes moldy (microbial growth). Regular replacement or sanitization is necessary.

4.8

4.8 System Design for Control of Microbial Growth

Microbial Growth Control  |  pp. 51-52

4.8 Control of Microbial Growth

The methods used in pretreatment to control microbial growth are:

  • Microbial control agent such as chlorine or chloramine
  • Periodic sanitization (heat or chemical)
  • Ultraviolet light
  • Avoiding dead legs and avoiding water stagnation

A common strategy in the design of the pretreatment system is to leave the microbial control agent provided by the municipality in the water through as many pretreatment steps as possible, in order to protect these steps from microbial growth.

However, at some point the microbial control agent (chlorine or chloramine) must be removed since it is not compatible with the final treatment processes. At this point, the only option is periodic sanitization, either with heat or a chemical disinfectant. This must be included in the design of the pretreatment system, along with the provisions for validation and monitoring its effectiveness via sampling and testing.

Ultraviolet light (UV) is effective in inhibiting microbial growth but is only effective when the light is present. UV light is often used before a unit operation to minimize the microbial growth in the unit operation by controlling the microbial counts in the feed water. The most common places for use of UV light are before Reverse Osmosis units and some filters.

Commentary

Core Concept: Four layers of defense in microbial control

  1. Microbial control agent: chlorine or chloramine, which comes from municipal water treatment and should be retained as far into the pretreatment train as possible
  2. Periodic sanitization: the main means after the control agent is removed, using hot water (>80°C) or chemical sanitizers
  3. UV light: provides immediate suppression of microorganisms, often installed ahead of the RO feed
  4. Design practices: avoid dead legs and water stagnation

Key point: the chlorine "umbrella" strategy

ISPE's recommendation is very clear: keep the chlorine in the municipal water for as long as possible, until the last stage of pretreatment. This means:

  • The activated carbon bed (where chlorine is removed) should be placed as late as possible in the pretreatment train
  • Equipment ahead of the activated carbon bed (such as softeners and media filters) is protected by the chlorine
  • Equipment after the activated carbon bed must have independent microbial control measures

This "umbrella" concept is one of the core philosophies of pretreatment system design.

Practical application: the correct placement of UV

UV is effective only while light is present - unlike chlorine, it has no "residual protection". The best places to use UV are therefore:

  • Before the RO feed: reduces microbial counts and protects the RO membrane
  • Before storage tanks: prevents microorganisms from entering the storage stage
  • Before critical filters: protects filters from becoming a microbial growth medium

UV is a "point defense" rather than an "area defense" - it cannot replace a systematic sanitization strategy.

4.9

4.9 Removal of Microbial Control Agents

Removal of Microbial Control Agents  |  pp. 52-53

4.9 Removal of Microbial Control Agents

At some point in pretreatment, microbial control agents must be removed because of their detrimental effect on final treatment equipment and performance. Chlorine causes deterioration of most Reverse Osmosis membranes and is corrosive in distillation. Chloramines can pass through pretreatment and decompose in the distillation process with an adverse effect on water quality.

For chlorine removal, activated carbon is a straightforward process for the absorption of chlorine. The carbon will reduce some of the chlorine to chloride ion, which is then removed in the final treatment ion removal process. Sulfite reduction is also straightforward, with sulfite being oxidized to sulfate and chlorine being reduced to chloride ion.

For chloramine removal, this is more complex. Chloramine adsorption on carbon occurs at a much slower rate than chlorine, necessitating longer contact times and lower hydraulic flow rates. The potential for dissociation of the absorbed chloramines into ammonium ion and ammonia is a problem.

  • Ammonium is removed by Reverse Osmosis but decomposes to ammonia in a distillation process.
  • Ammonia passes through both Reverse Osmosis and distillation processes in final treatment.

Sulfite reduction for chloramines results in ammonium and chloride ions. These can be removed by Reverse Osmosis. The ammonium ion partially decomposes to ammonia in higher temperature distillation, resulting in carryover and affect on water quality.

Commentary

Key point: ammonia is the biggest challenge in chloramine systems

Ammonia is a decomposition product of chloramine and one of the most difficult substances to handle in pharmaceutical water systems:

  • Under alkaline conditions, ammonia is in its molecular form (NH3), which can pass through RO membranes and distillation systems
  • Under acidic conditions, ammonia is in its ionic form (NH4+), which can be removed by RO
  • But at the high temperatures of distillation, ammonium is partly converted back to ammonia, causing carryover

If your feedwater contains chloramine, the system design must give additional consideration to an ammonia control strategy.

Discussion Questions

Q1: The city where your plant is located has just switched from chlorine to chloramine disinfection. What adjustments does your pretreatment system need?

Q2: Why is chloramine-containing feedwater more challenging when distillation is the final treatment than when RO is?

4.10

4.10 Changes in Anion Composition / Concentration

Changes in Anion Composition  |  pp. 52-53

4.10 Changes in Anion Composition

Pretreatment systems typically remove non-ionic impurities and cations. Thus, any change in anionic composition or concentration is usually secondary. However, some distillation processes in final treatment are affected by chlorides, which can be removed by an RO prior to the final treatment step.

The pretreatment processes that affect anionic composition are:

  • Deionization
  • Degasification
  • Carbon bed filtration for removal of chlorine and chloramine
  • Reduction to remove chlorine and chloramine
  • Barrier filtration (nanofiltration, ultrafiltration and Reverse Osmosis)

Degasification and the accompanying process of acidification changes anionic composition. The water is acidified with a non-volatile acid (usually sulfuric) to convert carbonate and bicarbonate to dissolved CO2, which is removed by degasification. The net effect is replacement of bicarbonate and carbonate with sulfate.

Carbon bed filtration adsorbs chlorine and chloramines from feed water. However, some of the chlorine is reduced to chloride and is removed in a subsequent ion removal process.

The removal of chlorine and chloramines by reduction, often with bisulfite, changes ionic composition as the bisulfite is oxidized to sulfate and the chlorine or chloramines are reduced to chloride and ammonium.

Commentary

Core Concept: How pretreatment changes anion composition

Pretreatment mainly targets non-ionic impurities and cations (such as Ca2+ and Mg2+), but some pretreatment steps indirectly change the anion composition:

  • Acidification + degasification: carbonate/bicarbonate → sulfate (because sulfuric acid is what is added)
  • Dechlorination by carbon bed: chlorine → chloride ion (Cl-)
  • Sodium bisulfite reduction: sodium bisulfite → sulfate (SO42-), chlorine → chloride ion

These anion changes are usually removed in final treatment (RO or ion exchange), but designers need to understand them in order to perform correct material balance calculations.

Key point: the threat of chloride to distillation systems

If the final treatment is distillation, the chloride produced in pretreatment is an important consideration. At the high temperatures of distillation, chloride causes pitting corrosion and stress corrosion cracking of stainless steel. An RO step may then be needed ahead of the still to reduce the chloride concentration.

4.11

4.11 The Importance of pH in Pretreatment

The Importance of pH in Pretreatment  |  pp. 53-54

4.11 The Importance of pH

EPA drinking water standards require a pH range of 6.5–8.5. In reality, the pH range of most drinking feed water is narrower, due to the corrosive nature of acidic water and the scaling potential of alkaline waters.

The pH of the feed water and its seasonal variations need to be known because of its impact on pretreatment and final treatment process design. The pH determines the form of the carbon dioxide, its scaling potential and where carbon dioxide (carbonate) is removed.

A complicating factor in pretreatment design is the potential presence of ammonia as a result of chloramine presence in the feed water. Ammonia is a dissolved gas at the pH values where carbon dioxide is an ion (carbonate), and exists as an ion (ammonium) at pH values where carbon dioxide exists as a dissolved gas.

Thus it is not possible to remove both carbon dioxide and ammonia at one pH. If both are present, two pH adjustment steps are required:

  • pH adjustment followed by removal of either carbon dioxide or ammonia
  • A change in pH to remove the other compound

These operations may be part of pretreatment or final treatment.

Commentary

Chemical Equilibrium: pH Dependence of CO2 / NH3

Carbon Dioxide System:
CO2 (gas) ↔ H2CO3 ↔ HCO3⁻ ↔ CO3²⁻
  pH < 4.3    pH 4.3-8.3    pH > 8.3

Ammonia System:
NH4⁺ (ion) ↔ NH3 (gas)
  pH < 9.3      pH > 9.3

Key conflict: CO2 removal needs LOW pH
              NH3 removal needs LOW pH (as NH4⁺ for RO)
              BUT CO2 is ionic at HIGH pH
              → Cannot remove both at same pH!

Everyday analogy: pH is like temperature control

Imagine you need to store chocolate (which needs refrigeration) and bread (which needs room temperature) at the same time - one temperature cannot satisfy both. CO2 and NH3 are like this: their optimal removal conditions on the pH "thermometer" conflict with each other, so they must be handled in two steps.

Core Concept: How pH affects the forms of CO2 and NH3

pH determines the chemical form of carbon dioxide and ammonia in water:

The problem is: no single pH value turns both CO2 and NH3 into forms that are easy to remove. If both are present, pH adjustment and removal must be carried out in two steps.

4.12

4.12 Materials of Construction and Construction Practices

Materials of Construction and Installation Practices  |  pp. 53-54

4.12 Materials of Construction

Piping to the pretreatment system may be copper, galvanized steel, or a suitable thermoplastic. Piping in the pretreatment system, where high temperatures are not encountered, is usually plastic (PVC, CPVC, polypropylene, or other material) based upon cost and corrosion resistance. Leaching from some plastics such as PVC and CPVC may make these materials undesirable to the user. Vessels may be fiberglass, lined carbon steel, or stainless steel.

The piping and equipment in a portion of the pretreatment system may encounter high temperature (periodic heat sanitization) or high pressure (RO plus degasification). In these portions, piping is typically stainless steel or a plastic that can be heat sanitized, such as PVDF. Equipment designed for high pressure may be carbon steel, lined carbon steel, or stainless steel. Mill finish is satisfactory; electropolishing is unnecessary.

The cost of sanitary construction practices such as orbital welding and sanitary fittings may not be warranted in the pretreatment system. Use of plastic pipe that is solvent cemented or heat fused, stainless steel pipe that is welded or flanged with mill finish, or tubing with compression fittings is common.

Ball or diaphragm valves predominate for flow diversion, with globe and needle valves for flow control. Selecting the minimum cost piping components that will not degrade water quality is an area for major cost savings.

Sample points should be provided upstream and downstream of each piece of equipment for monitoring and troubleshooting. Points for field measurement of pressure and temperature are also useful.

Commentary

Key point: pretreatment does not need to be "over-designed"

ISPE states clearly here that pretreatment systems do not need distribution-system-grade construction specifications:

  • Electropolishing is not required - a mill finish is sufficient
  • Orbital welding is not required - conventional welding or flanged connections are sufficient
  • Sanitary fittings are not required - compression fittings or solvent-bonded joints are sufficient

This is an important cost-optimization opportunity. Many plants mistakenly use the same high-specification materials and construction methods in the pretreatment system as in the distribution system, creating unnecessary cost.

Practical application: a concrete expression of Good Engineering Practice

The correct approach is to select materials according to the actual needs of each section:

  • Feed piping to the softener: PVC piping + solvent-bonded joints (lowest cost)
  • Between the activated carbon bed and the RO: if thermal sanitization is required, use PVDF or stainless steel instead
  • Upstream and downstream of each piece of equipment: provide sampling points and pressure/temperature measurement points (for monitoring and troubleshooting)

Principle: select the lowest-cost material that does not degrade the water quality.

4.13

4.13 Pretreatment Summary

Pretreatment Summary  |  pp. 54-55

4.13 Pretreatment Summary

The philosophy of control selected for pretreatment can have a major impact on both investment and continuing operating cost. Reliable operation and control of pretreatment can significantly reduce operating and maintenance costs in final treatment. The important process steps in pretreatment are:

  • Removal of turbidity and particulates to minimize membrane and equipment fouling
  • Removal of hardness and metals to prevent scale formation in final treatment
  • Removal of organics and microbiological impurities
  • Control of microbial growth and removal of microbial control agents to prevent degradation of final treatment

These process steps are important because of their immediate effect on water quality from final treatment or their long-term effect on final treatment equipment performance and hence, their indirect effect on water quality from final treatment.

Pretreatment, like other parts of the water treatment system, should be subject to Good Engineering Practices. Validation of pretreatment, as a component of the water treatment system, is required as part of the entire water treatment system validation and should include microbiological monitoring.

Commentary

Core Concept: Summary of the four tasks of pretreatment

  1. Remove turbidity / particulates → prevent fouling
  2. Remove hardness / metals → prevent scaling
  3. Remove organics / microbiological impurities → maintain water quality
  4. Control microbial growth + remove microbial control agents → protect the final treatment equipment

The effects of pretreatment fall into two categories:

  • Direct effects: immediately affect final product water quality
  • Indirect effects: affect the performance and life of the final treatment equipment over the long term

Key point: GEP and validation requirements

Pretreatment systems follow Good Engineering Practice (GEP) rather than GMP-level requirements. However, validation of the pretreatment is part of the validation of the whole water system and must include microbial monitoring.

This means that pretreatment can be designed with lower-cost materials and construction methods, but its performance validation and ongoing monitoring must not be neglected.

Chapter 4 Summary

  • Role of pretreatment: Pretreatment comprises all process steps before final treatment, and its purpose is to bring feedwater quality up to a level the final treatment equipment can accept. The stability of pretreatment directly determines the operating reliability and maintenance cost of final treatment.
  • Feedwater quality: Pharmaceutical water systems must use feedwater that meets drinking water standards. Feedwater quality must be monitored for at least one year to capture seasonal variation. The four contaminant types are particulates, inorganics, organics, and bacteria.
  • Four pretreatment tasks: (1) Fouling control - media filtration removes particulates; (2) Scaling control - softeners remove hardness; (3) Organics removal - activated carbon adsorption; (4) Microbial control - the chlorine "umbrella" + periodic sanitization + UV.
  • Chlorine vs chloramine: Chloramine is more complex to remove than chlorine, and its by-product ammonia can pass through RO and distillation systems. When the feedwater contains chloramine, system design complexity increases significantly.
  • The key role of pH: pH determines the chemical forms of CO2 and NH3 and affects the removal strategy. The two cannot both be optimally removed at the same pH, so they must be handled in separate steps.
  • Materials and cost: Pretreatment does not need distribution-system-grade materials and construction specifications. Electropolishing, orbital welding, and sanitary fittings are generally unnecessary in pretreatment. Selecting "the lowest-cost material that does not degrade water quality" is an important cost-optimization strategy.
  • Validation requirements: Pretreatment follows GEP (not GMP), but as part of the water system, its validation (including microbial monitoring) is required.
5

Chapter 5: Final Treatment: Purified Water

p57-p73

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p57-p73

Learning Objectives

  1. Understand the principles and differences of the three main final treatment technologies: ion exchange (IX), continuous electrodeionization (CEDI), and reverse osmosis (RO)
  2. Understand the role of polishing technologies such as ultrafiltration (UF), microfiltration (MF), and ultraviolet (UV) light in purified water systems
  3. Understand the advantages and disadvantages of each technology, its sanitization method, pretreatment requirements, and cost considerations
  4. Be able to select a suitable final treatment configuration (IX vs. membrane system vs. distillation) based on water quality requirements
  5. Recognize the industry shift from ion exchange-dominated to membrane-based systems and what is driving it

Section Navigation

5.1 Introduction 5.2 Ion Exchange (IX) 5.3 CEDI 5.4 Reverse Osmosis 5.5.1 Ultrafiltration 5.5.2 Microfiltration 5.5.3 UV Light Table 5-4 Comparison
Original Text

5.1 Introduction

This chapter discusses the final treatment technologies and basic system configurations related to the manufacturing process of USP Purified Water and non-compendial water.

Various system configurations are presented, and reflect a significant shift from ion exchange based systems to membrane based systems.
