Equipment and system materials, surface finish and other design factors are discussed to promote the use of Good Engineering Practice for proper selection of components, piping, instrumentation, and controls.

USP Purified Water and non-compendial water can be produced by an almost unlimited combination of unit processes in various configurations. The most common pretreatment and final treatment technologies used in purified water production are shown in Figure 5-1, Figure 5-2, and Table 5-4 at the end of the chapter.

Ion exchange based systems were the dominant systems for decades in purified water production and are still successfully utilized in facilities today. The last decade has seen the growth of reverse osmosis membrane based systems increase to the point where over 90% of new systems employ primary reverse osmosis, with final polishing by continuous electrodeionization, ion exchange, or a second reverse osmosis stage.

Membrane based systems usage has increased due to chemical consumption reduction, contaminant rejection (ionized solids, organics, colloids, microbes, endotoxins, and suspended solids), reduced maintenance, consistent operation, and effective lifecycle cost.

Many material selections are made erroneously to conform to cGMP requirements that do not actually dictate the details of construction for most final treatment components. Good Engineering Practice should be employed to optimize the system for consistent operation to specifications and lifecycle cost optimization.

Commentary

Key Points of the Introduction

Shift in Industry Trends

One of the most important pieces of information in this chapter: more than 90% of new systems use reverse osmosis (RO) as the primary treatment technology, with CEDI, IX, or a second-pass RO for polishing. Ion exchange is no longer the default first choice.

Why Are Membrane-Based Systems Rising?

  • Less chemical use — no acid or caustic regeneration
  • Removal of multiple contaminants — ions, organics, colloids, microorganisms, endotoxin
  • Lower maintenance cost — more stable operation
  • Better life-cycle cost

Analogy: GEP vs. Over-Engineering

Just as you do not need racing-grade parts in every component when buying a car, not every component in a water system needs the highest grade of surface finish (such as electropolishing). ISPE stresses Good Engineering Practice: choose a "good enough" specification for each component based on its function, location, and microbial control needs, rather than blindly pursuing the highest standard.

Compendial vs. Non-Compendial

This chapter does not deliberately distinguish between compendial water (such as USP PW) and non-compendial water (process water, etc.). In practice, non-compendial water is usually produced and validated in the same way as compendial water.

Original Text

5.2 Ion Exchange (IX)

5.2.1 Description

Cation and anion exchange resins are regenerated with acid and caustic solutions, respectively. As water passes through the ion exchange bed, the exchange of ions in the water stream for the hydrogen and hydroxide ions, held by the resin, occurs readily and is driven by concentration. Thus, the regeneration process is driven by excess chemical concentrations.

A two-bed ion exchange system includes both cation and anion resin tanks. Two-bed ion exchange systems often function as the workhorse of a strictly deionization (DI) water system in terms of salt removal. Mixed-bed ion exchange systems are typically used as a secondary or "polishing" system.

Ion exchange resins are available in on-site and off-site regenerable systems. On-site regeneration requires chemical handling and disposal, but allows for internal process control and microbial control. Off-site regeneration can be accomplished through new resin to be used one time, or through repeated regeneration of the existing resin.

5.2.2 Application

The major purpose of ion exchange equipment in USP purified water systems is to satisfy the conductivity requirements of the USP. Deionization (DI) systems are often used alone or in conjunction with reverse osmosis to produce USP Purified Water. Typical ion exchange systems do not effectively remove other contaminants noted in the USP purified water specification.

5.2.3 Pretreatment Requirements

Ion exchange systems require pretreatment to remove undissolved solids from the water stream and to avoid resin fouling or degradation. Although dechlorination is also recommended to avoid resin degradation by oxidation, the low levels of chlorine commonly found in most potable water supplies normally demonstrate only long-term effects on most ion exchange resins.

5.2.5 Advantages and Disadvantages

Advantages:

  • Simple design and maintenance
  • Flexible in water flow production
  • Good upset recovery
  • Low capital cost for single train DI systems
  • Removes ionizable substances (ammonia, carbon dioxide, and some organics)

Disadvantages:

  • High cost of operations on high TDS in-feed-water
  • Requires chemical handling for on-site regenerable DI (safety and environmental issues)
  • Full on-site DI system can take significantly more floor space
  • Off-site DI systems will require outside service and significant costs for regeneration services
  • Off-site regeneration involves loss of control over the use, handling, and care of DI vessels
  • DI vessels are excellent places for microbial growth to occur between regenerations

5.2.6 Sanitization

All ion exchange resins can be sanitized chemically with various agents. Chemical cleaners include peracetic acid, sodium hypochlorite, and others. Some resins are capable of hot water sanitizations at temperatures between 65°C to 85°C. Ion exchange resins suitable for limited thermal sanitizations include: strong acid cation resin, and standard polystyrene cross-linked with divinylbenzene Type 1 strong base resin.

Commentary

Ion Exchange (IX) Explained

Core Principle of IX

The basic concept of ion exchange is simple:

  • Cation resin: regenerated with acid (H+) → replaces Na+, Ca2+, etc. in the water with H+
  • Anion resin: regenerated with caustic (OH-) → replaces Cl-, SO42-, etc. in the water with OH-
  • H+ + OH- = H2O → produces pure water

Two-Bed vs. Mixed-Bed

  • Two-Bed: cation and anion resins in separate vessels; the system "workhorse"
  • Mixed-Bed: cation and anion resins mixed in the same vessel, used as a "polishing" step to reach higher purity

The Biggest Weakness of IX: Microbial Growth

DI resin vessels are an ideal place for microbial growth between regenerations. This is one of the most critical disadvantages of ion exchange systems and a major reason membrane systems are gradually replacing IX.

Analogy: Ion Exchange Is Like a Self-Service Laundromat Machine

The resin is like the filter screen of a washing machine: it "adsorbs" ionic impurities from the water. After a period of use it needs "regeneration" (like cleaning the screen), which can be done yourself (on-site) or sent out (off-site). But if the screen is left uncleaned for too long, it grows mold, and this is the microbial risk of IX.

Choosing a Sanitization Method

Chemical sanitization (peracetic acid, sodium hypochlorite) is suitable for all resins; hot water sanitization (65-85°C) is suitable only for certain types. When purchasing resin, confirm its heat resistance specification.

Table 5-1: Comparison for Ion Exchange Unit Operations

*Note: Having the DI bottles regenerated by an outside service does not relieve the manufacturer of the responsibility to have quality control of their ion exchange system.

Table 5-2: Limits of Operation and Expected Performance

Reading the Table

Quality Responsibility for Off-Site Regeneration

The footnote to Table 5-1 is very important: even when an off-site regeneration service is used, the pharmaceutical company remains responsible for the quality of the DI system. Quality control cannot be abandoned because the work is outsourced. This is the basic spirit of GMP.

Key Operating Parameters

  • TDS < 200 ppm: above this value, operating cost rises sharply (frequent regeneration)
  • Conductivity < 1.0 μS/cm: can meet the USP Stage 1 requirement
  • Unstable TOC performance: IX may increase or decrease TOC depending on the resin type and feed water quality; this is an often overlooked risk

Decision Key: Feed Water TDS Determines the Technology Choice

If the raw water TDS is high (> 200 ppm), the operating cost of a pure IX system will be very high. In that case, adding an RO stage ahead of the IX can greatly reduce the IX load and regeneration frequency, or you can switch directly to an RO + CEDI configuration.

Original Text

5.3 Continuous Electrodeionization (CEDI)

5.3.1 Description

Electrodeionization removes ionized or ionizable species from water using electrically active media and an electrical potential to effect ion transport. It is distinguished from electrodialysis by the use of electrically active media, and from other ion exchange processes by the use of an electrical potential.

The continuous electrodeionization (CEDI) process is continuous rather than batch or intermittent. CEDI devices typically comprise semi-permeable ion exchange membranes, permanently charged media, and a DC electrical field.

A CEDI cell has alternating ion depleting (purifying) and ion concentrating cells. Water is purified through ion transfer: ionized species are drawn from the purifying cells into the concentrate stream.

The water purified in CEDI units passes only through the electrically charged ion exchange media, and not through the ion exchange membranes. The membranes are permeable to ionized species, but not to water.

As the ionic strength of the purified water decreases, high voltage gradient at the water-ion exchange media interfaces causes water decomposition to H+ and OH-. These ions continuously regenerate the ion exchange media — allowing production of 1 to 18 MΩ-cm quality water.

5.3.2 Application

CEDI units may be utilized downstream of RO units for USP Purified Water or non-compendial water production. For USP WFI water, CEDI units are utilized upstream of RO units.

5.3.3 Limitations

CEDI cannot remove all contaminants; the principal mechanism is for ionized or ionizable species. A concentrate stream is always required. Most CEDI units operate between 10-40°C (50-104°F).

5.3.5 Performance

Ionized solids reduction is generally greater than 99%, allowing 1-18 MΩ-cm quality water from RO feed. Organic rejection varies from 50% to 95%. UV light (185 nm) upstream can substantially increase organic rejection. CO2 is converted to bicarbonate and removed. Silica removal: 80-95%.

5.3.7 Advantages and Disadvantages

Advantages:

  • Attainment of Stage 1 conductivity
  • Elimination of chemical handling
  • Elimination of outside service (off-site regenerated resin)
  • Electric field provides some bacterial control
  • Removal of ionizable substances (CO2, ammonia, some organics)

Disadvantages:

  • Does not remove non-ionic contaminants
  • Unique designs for each manufacturer (modules not interchangeable)
  • May require UV, sub-micron filtration, or RO for further bacterial reduction
  • May require RO pretreatment
  • Rinse up after chemical sanitization may take hours to reach peak resistivity and TOC

5.3.8 Sanitization

CEDI units are typically chemically sanitized with agents including: peracetic acid, sodium percarbonate, sodium hydroxide, hydrogen peroxide, and others.

Commentary

Continuous Electrodeionization (CEDI) Explained

How CEDI Works (Simplified)

  1. Water passes through "dilute chambers" filled with ion exchange resin
  2. A direct current electric field pulls ions from the dilute chambers toward the "concentrate chambers"
  3. Ions pass through the ion exchange membranes into the concentrate chambers and are discharged with the reject water
  4. The higher the water purity, the greater the voltage gradient, and water splits to produce H+ and OH-
  5. H+ / OH- continuously regenerate the resin, which is why it is called "continuous" electrodeionization

Analogy: CEDI Is Like a Self-Recharging Water Purifier

A traditional IX resin must be "recharged" (acid and caustic regeneration) when exhausted, but CEDI uses an electric field to split water into H+/OH-, which is equivalent to "automatic recharging" and never requires shutdown for regeneration. This is the biggest advantage of CEDI.

Key Data

  • Ion removal > 99% — can produce 1-18 MΩ-cm water
  • Organics removal 50-95% — highly variable, depends on the type of organics
  • SiO2 removal 80-95%
  • Operating temperature 10-40°C — hot water sanitization is not possible

CEDI Position Depends on the Water Grade

  • PW system: RO → CEDI (CEDI as polishing)
  • WFI system: CEDI → RO (CEDI as pretreatment, RO as the final barrier)

This difference in position reflects different microbial control requirements. WFI requires a stricter microbial barrier, and an RO membrane is more effective than CEDI at blocking microorganisms and endotoxin.

Modules Are Not Interchangeable

The module designs of CEDI manufacturers (such as Ionpure and E-Cell) are proprietary, unlike RO membranes, which have standard size specifications. This means that once a supplier is selected, there is a degree of "lock-in", which must be considered in the purchasing strategy.

Discussion Question

If your feed water TOC is high (> 2 ppm) and CEDI removes only 50-95% of organics, how would you strengthen the system design?

Hint: the source text mentions that a 185 nm UV placed upstream of the CEDI can greatly improve organics removal.

Original Text

5.4 Reverse Osmosis (RO)

5.4.1 Description

Reverse osmosis (RO) is a pressure driven process utilizing a semi-permeable membrane capable of removing dissolved organic and inorganic contaminants from water. The membrane is permeable to water while being impermeable to many salts, acids, bases, colloids, bacteria, and endotoxins.

RO membranes are produced commercially in a spiral wound configuration for pharmaceutical water production. Membranes are available in two basic materials: cellulose acetate and thin film composite (polyamide).

Table 5-3: RO Membrane Operating Parameters

*Best operation at 0.0

5.4.2 Application

RO can be utilized upstream of regenerable or off-site regenerated deionizers to reduce chemical consumption. Two-pass RO units (product staged) with proper pH control are generally capable of producing water that meets USP requirements for TOC and conductivity.

5.4.3 Limitations

RO cannot remove 100% of contaminants and has very low to no removal capacity for extremely low molecular weight dissolved organics. Carbon dioxide passes directly through the RO membrane and may increase product conductivity beyond USP Stage 1 limit.

A concentrate flow is always necessary. Many users reuse the RO waste stream for cooling tower make-up, compressor cooling water, etc.

5.4.5 Performance

A single stage of RO typically reduces salts, colloids, organics, bacteria, and endotoxin by 90 to 99%. Single stage RO does not normally meet USP without further purification. Some two-pass units produce water meeting USP 24 Stage 1 conductivity requirements.

5.4.6 Advantages and Disadvantages

Advantages:

  • Eliminates or significantly reduces chemical handling relative to IX
  • More effective microbial control than IX
  • Integrity testing by salt challenge and differential conductivity
  • Removes wide variety of contaminants: ionized solids, non-ionic materials (colloids, bacteria, endotoxin, some organics)

Disadvantages:

  • Higher water consumption than IX (unless wastewater is reused)
  • Energy consumption higher than IX, less than distillation
  • No removal of dissolved gases (CO2 and ammonia)

5.4.8 Waste Water Reuse

RO wastewater is frequently used as cooling tower make-up, non-contact cooling, or re-purified in a wastewater RO unit. The wastewater from the second pass of a two-pass RO is normally returned to the feed of the first pass.

5.4.9 Sanitization

All RO membranes can be sanitized with chemical agents that vary by membrane type. Specially constructed membranes are available for hot water sanitization at 60° to 80°C.

Commentary

Reverse Osmosis (RO) Explained

Core Principle of RO

High pressure pushes water through a semipermeable membrane. The membrane allows water molecules to pass but blocks salts, organics, colloids, bacteria, endotoxin, and so on. RO is the most "comprehensive" single treatment technology and removes many types of contaminants at the same time.

CO2 Is the Blind Spot of RO

Carbon dioxide is a dissolved gas that an RO membrane cannot block at all. CO2 affects the conductivity of the product water and may exceed the USP Stage 1 limit. Solutions:

  • Lower the pH in pretreatment → CO2 degassing
  • Follow RO with CEDI or IX (CO2 is converted to bicarbonate, which can then be removed)
  • Membrane degasifier

Analogy: RO Is Like a Precision Sieve

Imagine an extremely fine sieve: most impurities cannot pass, and only water molecules get through. But a "small gaseous molecule" such as CO2 passes through the sieve like air. This is why RO can block everything except dissolved gases.

Reject Water Recovery Strategies

RO reject water (concentrate) still has value:

  • Cooling tower makeup — the most common reuse
  • Compressor cooling water
  • Filter media backwash water
  • Secondary RO treatment — recovers some additional purified water
  • Two-pass RO: second-pass reject returns to the first-pass feed — a standard design

Good use of reject water recovery can greatly mitigate the biggest disadvantage of RO systems, high water consumption.

Hot Water Sanitizable RO Membranes

Special high-temperature-tolerant RO membranes (60-80°C) are now commercially available, allowing RO systems to be hot water sanitized as well and greatly simplifying the sanitization process. This is an important design consideration, because rinse-up after chemical sanitization is time-consuming and troublesome.

Key Points in Comparing the Two Membrane Materials

In practice, TFC membranes have become the mainstream choice. The only advantage of CA membranes is chlorine tolerance, but TFC is better in almost every other performance aspect.

Original Text

5.5 Polishing Components — Non-Ionic Contaminants Reduction

5.5.1 Ultrafiltration (UF)

5.5.1.1 Description

Ultrafiltration (UF) is a cross-flow process similar to RO. A pressurized feed stream flows parallel to a porous membrane. The membrane rejects particulates, organics, microbes, pyrogens, and other contaminants too large to pass through. UF does not reject low molecular weight ionic contaminants, as does RO.

Membranes are available in both polymeric and ceramic materials. Polymeric elements come in spiral wound and hollow fiber configurations. Ceramic modules are in single/multiple channel configurations.

5.5.1.2 Application

UF is frequently used downstream of ion exchange for organic, colloidal, microbial, and endotoxin reduction. Purified Water with low endotoxin levels (<0.25 EU/ml) is utilized for ophthalmic solutions, topicals, and bulk pharmaceutical chemicals for parenteral manufacturing.

UF is also frequently used in still feed water systems, in combination with IX, to limit endotoxin and colloidal silica feed levels to the still.

5.5.1.5 Performance

Organic molecular weight cutoffs range from 1,000 to 100,000. Endotoxin reduction varies from 2 log10 to 4 log10. UF can consistently meet USP WFI endotoxin limit of 0.25 EU/ml. Microbial reduction: 3 log10 to 4 log10. Some UF membranes operate continuously at temperatures up to 90°C.

5.5.1.6 Advantages and Disadvantages

Advantages:

  • Better endotoxin and organic removal than microfiltration
  • More effective operating costs than MF in high particle loading
  • Some UF elements tolerate steam or ozone sanitization
  • Generally less waste stream than RO
  • Less energy intensive than RO

Disadvantages:

  • Cannot remove ionic contaminants (unlike RO)
  • Generally requires a waste stream (2-10%)
  • Sometimes more difficult to integrity test than MF cartridges

5.5.1.8 Sanitization

Most polymeric membranes tolerate various chemical agents (NaOCl, H2O2, peracetic acid, NaOH). Some polymeric membranes can be hot water or even steam sanitized. Ceramic UF elements can tolerate all common agents, hot water, steam, and ozone.

5.5.1.9 Waste Water Recovery

Most pharmaceutical UF units are fed deionized water. The wastewater is still low conductivity water that can be recycled upstream to RO units or fed to boilers, cooling towers, or other uses.

Commentary

Ultrafiltration (UF) Explained

Role of UF

UF is not used to remove ions (that is the job of RO and IX). UF specializes in:

  • Endotoxin — 2-4 log removal
  • Microorganisms — 3-4 log removal
  • Organics — depends on the MWCO
  • Colloids and particulates

UF is an ideal polishing step after an IX system, making up for IX weaknesses in microorganisms and endotoxin.

MWCO (Molecular Weight Cut-Off)

UF membranes are classified by molecular weight cut-off (MWCO), ranging from 1,000 to 100,000 Dalton. The lower the value, the smaller the organic molecules that can be blocked, but the lower the flux. The selection must balance removal efficiency against system output.

Role of UF in WFI Distillation Systems

Many facilities install UF ahead of the still in order to:

  • Reduce the endotoxin load entering the still
  • Remove colloidal silica (to avoid scaling)
  • Extend the still maintenance interval

Ceramic vs. Polymeric UF Membranes

The advantage of ceramic membranes is that they are nearly "indestructible": all common sanitization methods can be used. For critical systems requiring frequent sanitization, ceramic membranes may offer better long-term cost-effectiveness.

Original Text

5.5.2 Microfiltration (MF)

5.5.2.1 Description

Microfiltration is a membrane process for the removal of fine particles and microorganisms. No waste stream is generally employed. Virtually all microfiltration cartridges are disposable and available in a wide range of materials and pore sizes, generally from 0.45 microns down to 0.04 microns.

Microfilters are generally employed for microbial retention downstream of components where some microbial growth may exist. Microfilters are most appropriately employed in central purified water production systems and their use is discouraged in distribution systems. The filters should not be the only microbial control unit operation — they need to be part of a comprehensive microbial control plan.

5.5.2.2 Advantages and Disadvantages

Advantages:

  • Simple design and maintenance
  • Flexible in water flow production
  • No waste stream
  • Cartridges are integrity testable
  • Heat and chemical sanitization possible

Disadvantages:

  • Can only be used as a safety net for microbial production
  • No ion or endotoxin removal
  • Shorter life due to dead head design; replacement required
  • Not recommended for use in distribution piping

5.5.2.3 Performance

Microfiltration can be as effective as UF in microbial reduction and minimizes water consumption (no waste stream). However, MF cannot reduce dissolved organic levels as UF can, and cannot remove particles as small as UF due to pore size differences.

Commentary

Microfiltration (MF) Explained

Position of MF: The Last Safety Net

MF is not the "workhorse" treatment of the system but a "safety net". Its function is clear:

  • Blocks microorganisms and particulates
  • Cannot remove ions, endotoxin, or dissolved organics
  • Disposable cartridges — replaced when spent

Important Limitation: Not Recommended for Distribution Systems

ISPE clearly states that MF should not be used in distribution piping. The reasons are:

  • MF easily becomes a "breeding ground" for microorganisms (dead-end design)
  • Distribution piping should control microorganisms by means of circulation, temperature, ozone, and so on
  • MF is suitable only at the central production point of the water generation system

This point is frequently asked about in actual audits.

Analogy: MF Is Like a Screen Door

A screen door can keep out mosquitoes (microorganisms) but not dust and odors (ions, organics, endotoxin). It is not the air-conditioning system (the main treatment), just an extra layer of protection. And if the screen is placed deep inside the house (distribution piping), it can instead become a place where insect eggs hatch.

Integrity Testing

A major advantage of MF cartridges is that they can be integrity tested (for example, bubble point test or diffusive flow test). This makes MF easier to validate in a GMP environment. By comparison, integrity testing of UF is usually more complex.

Original Text

5.5.3 Ultraviolet Light Treatment

5.5.3.1 Description

Ultraviolet light rays strike microorganisms (bacteria, virus, yeast, mold, or algae) and break through their outer membrane to modify the DNA, bringing about the destruction of the organism. UV radiation is a point of use application with no residual radiation characteristics. Proper prefiltration should be implemented to keep particulate from shielding organisms from UV light.

5.5.3.2 Advantages and Disadvantages

Advantages:

  • Simple design and maintenance
  • 254 nm design for microbial reduction
  • 185 nm design for TOC reduction
  • No waste stream
  • Heat, ozone, and chemical sanitization are possible

Disadvantages:

  • Can only be used as a safety net for microbial production
  • No ion or endotoxin removal
  • No disinfection residual
  • Particulate can shield organisms from UV light

5.5.3.3 Performance

UV light is used as a final treatment step to address microbial control and TOC reduction (where necessary), after deionization processes.

Commentary

Ultraviolet (UV) Treatment Explained

The Fatal Weakness of UV: No Residual Effect

UV is effective only at the moment of irradiation; once the water leaves the UV lamp there is no further disinfection protection. This means:

  • Microorganisms can still grow in the piping downstream of the UV
  • UV cannot replace a systematic microbial control strategy
  • UV must be combined with other control measures (such as circulation and temperature maintenance)

Particle Shadowing Effect

If the water contains suspended particles, they block UV light and allow the shielded microorganisms to survive. Therefore appropriate prefiltration is required upstream of the UV. This is also why UV is usually placed at the very end of the system, after other treatments.

Discussion Question: The Best Installation Location for UV

In a typical RO + CEDI + UV system, where should the 185 nm UV and the 254 nm UV each be placed, and why?

Hint: review the CEDI section; a 185 nm UV upstream of the CEDI improves organics removal, and a 254 nm UV at the very end provides microbial control.

Two UV Wavelengths, Two Functions

During system design, the correct wavelength must be chosen according to the purpose. Some equipment provides both wavelengths.

Original Text

Table 5-4: Purified Water Systems Comparison Chart

Ratings: N = None, L = Low, M = Medium, H = High

Notes: (1) High water consumption unless wastewater is reused. (2) Total chemical requirement dependent upon pretreatment. (3) Total water consumption dependent upon pretreatment. (4) USP TOC met in most cases but may not if feed TOC >2 ppm.

Commentary

Interpreting the System Comparison Overview

How to Read This Table

This is the most important decision reference table for selecting a purified water system. Key points:

  • Microbial performance: only Two-Pass RO and distillation are rated H (high); all others are M or L
  • Chemical handling: On-Site IX is highest, membrane systems are lowest
  • Water consumption: all systems containing RO are H (unless reject water is recovered)
  • Capital cost: distillation is highest, Off-Site IX is lowest

Recommended Mainstream Configurations

  • RO + CEDI: the most mainstream PW system configuration today. Low chemical needs, no external service required, conductivity can reach Stage 1
  • Two-Pass RO: best microbial performance among membrane systems, suitable for situations with stricter microbial requirements
  • Distillation: best microbial performance, but highest capital and energy cost; usually used for WFI

Hidden TOC Risk

Note (4) points out that a pure IX system (without RO) cannot always reliably meet the TOC limit, especially when feed water TOC is > 2 ppm. This is because ion exchange resin can release organics and so increase product water TOC. Systems containing RO show stable TOC performance at <500 ppb.

Decision Exercise

Scenario: you need to design a PW system for a new pharmaceutical plant. Raw water TDS is 350 ppm and TOC is 3 ppm. The budget is limited, but you want reasonable long-term operating costs. Which configuration would you choose, and why?

Considerations: TDS is too high for pure IX; high TOC calls for RO pretreatment; both RO + CEDI and RO + IX are reasonable choices, but RO + CEDI is chemical-free and simpler to maintain.

Chapter Summary

Core Technology Comparison

Key Takeaways

  • Industry trend: more than 90% of new purified water systems are built around RO, with CEDI/IX/second-pass RO for polishing
  • GEP principle: material specifications should be chosen according to functional needs; not every component needs the highest grade of surface finish
  • CO2 issue: RO cannot remove dissolved gases, so downstream treatment (CEDI or IX) is needed
  • Microbial control: IX systems carry the highest microbial risk; membrane systems and distillation perform better
  • Reject water recovery: good use of RO reject water can significantly reduce the system biggest disadvantage (high water consumption)
  • Sanitization strategy: hot water sanitization is simpler than chemical sanitization, but equipment temperature ratings must be confirmed
  • Position of MF: only a safety net; should not be used in distribution piping and cannot be the sole means of microbial control
  • Dual UV wavelengths: 254 nm for disinfection, 185 nm for TOC reduction, each with a different optimal installation location

Chapter 6: Final Treatment: WFI

p75-p87

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p75-p87

Learning Objectives

  1. Understand the definition, uses and regulatory requirements of WFI (Water for Injection), the highest grade of pharmaceutical water
  2. Master the principles and differences of the three distillation technologies: Single Effect (SE), Multi-Effect (ME) and Vapor Compression (VC)
  3. Understand the capacity range, pretreatment needs, materials of construction and economics of each distillation method
  4. Understand the technical characteristics, advantages, disadvantages and regulatory acceptance of Reverse Osmosis (RO) as a means of producing WFI
  5. Use the WFI Systems Comparison table (Table 6-4) to compare options and make GEP-compliant selection decisions

Original Text

6.1 Introduction

This chapter addresses the USP approved final treatment methods for the production of compendial WFI. WFI is the purest grade of bulk water monographed by the USP and would be expected to be used for the manufacture of parenteral, some ophthalmic and inhalation products, and for finishing steps of parenteral grade active pharmaceutical ingredients (API's).

Recommended systems include either distillation or RO as the final processing step, but may also include ultrafiltration (UF), deionization (DI) and/or ion exchange (IX), to compliment the RO or distillation unit operation.

The technology, operation, maintenance, and relative cost issues for the approved process methods are discussed. This chapter includes USP monograph information, regulatory issues, and subsections that cover the unit operations:

  • Single effect (SE) distillation
  • Multi-effect (ME) distillation
  • Vapor compression (VC) distillation
  • Reverse osmosis (RO)

Feed water pretreatment is covered along with economic factors such as construction materials, surface finishes, and instrumentation and controls. A comparison table on USP-WFI final treatment options and relative attributes is provided.

Commentary

Key Points of the Introduction

The Status of WFI

WFI (Water for Injection) is the highest grade of pharmaceutical water in the USP. Its uses include:

  • Manufacture of parenteral products
  • Certain ophthalmic and inhalation products
  • The final steps of injectable-grade active pharmaceutical ingredients (APIs)

In short, any product that goes directly into the bloodstream or bypasses the body's natural defenses requires WFI.

Analogy: A Hierarchy of Water

Think of pharmaceutical waters as the zones of a hospital:

  • Potable water = the hospital lobby (general public area)
  • Purified Water = an ordinary ward (basic controls)
  • WFI = the operating room (the strictest aseptic control)

WFI must keep endotoxin to an extremely low level, because endotoxin entering the bloodstream can trigger a life-threatening pyrogenic reaction.

The Four Technologies in This Chapter

This chapter covers four final treatment technologies for WFI, which fall into two groups:

  • Distillation: SE (single effect), ME (multi-effect), VC (vapor compression)
  • Membrane: RO (reverse osmosis)

Distillation is the traditional "gold standard"; RO is an alternative that has gradually gained acceptance in recent years.

Original Text

6.2 US Pharmacopoeia Issues

The United States Pharmacopoeia (USP) allows WFI to be "purified by distillation or by RO". This statement does not imply that the regulated process step is the only process step, but the USP advisory section does imply that it is the final step in the process.

  • Only distillation may be used to produce WFI under current European regulations
  • Distillation, RO and UF are allowable methods to produce WFI under the Japanese regulations

There are few regulations, which govern the design and construction of pharmaceutical water purification systems. There are no existing regulations governing materials of construction, type, or level of instrumentation, surface finish, or operating temperatures.

Among U.S. government publications, including the Code of Federal Regulations (CFR) and the FDA Guide to Inspection of High Purity Water Systems, there are few stipulations related to design and construction of WFI processing equipment. Two notable stipulations are:

  • "Heat exchangers, other than the double concentric tube type or double tube sheet type, must employ a pressure differential and a means for monitoring the differential."
  • "All stills and tanks holding liquid requiring microbial control shall have air vents with non-fiber releasing sterilizable filters capable of preventing microbial contamination of the contents."
Commentary

Regulatory Requirements Explained

USP vs. EP vs. JP: Regulatory Differences (at Time of Publication)

Important Regulatory Update (2017 EP Revision)

This guide was published in 2001, when the EP allowed WFI to be produced by distillation only. In April 2017 the EP was formally revised to also accept "purification methods equivalent to distillation" (including RO) for producing WFI.

This was a milestone for pharmaceutical water systems: it means all three major pharmacopoeias now accept membrane technology as a means of producing WFI.

What "Final Step" Means

The USP stresses that distillation or RO must be the final step. This means:

  • You may use RO as pretreatment before distillation
  • You may use DI as pretreatment before RO
  • But after distillation or RO, no further treatment step may be added (otherwise that step would become the "final step")

Two Hard Requirements in the CFR

Although the regulations say little about design details, two explicit requirements must be met:

  1. Pressure differential monitoring of heat exchangers — to prevent the cooling water side from leaking into the pure water side (except for double tube sheet types)
  2. Vent filters on storage tanks — to prevent microorganisms from entering the storage system from the air

These two look simple, yet they are frequent inspection focus points.

Original Text

6.3 Distillation

The pharmaceutical still chemically and microbiologically purifies water by phase changes and entrainment separation. In this process water is evaporated, producing steam. The steam disengages from the water leaving behind dissolved solids, non-volatiles, and high molecular weight impurities. However, low molecular weight impurities are carried with the water mist/droplets, which are entrained in the steam. A separator removes fine mist and entrained impurities, including endotoxins. The purified steam is condensed into WFI.

Distillation systems are available to provide a minimum of 3 log10 (99.99%) reduction in endotoxin concentration. Specific endotoxin loading limits should be reviewed with the manufacturer.

A variety of different designs are available including single effect (SE), multi-effect (ME), and vapor compression (VC). The distilled water quality expected from an SE still is equivalent to an ME design, by virtue of the fact that water is distilled only once in both systems. The benefit to the user of ME versus SE distillation are the significantly lower operating costs associated with utilities.

In an ME system, purified steam produced by each effect is utilized to heat water and generate more steam in the subsequent effect. Due to this staged evaporation and condensation process, only the first effect requires heat from an external source, and only the purified steam produced by the final effect is condensed, using an external cooling medium.

VC stills can produce similar quality water using a different technique. Energy imparted to the generated steam, by a mechanical compressor, results in compressed steam with increased pressure and temperature. The higher energy steam is then discharged back into the evaporator/condenser vessel to generate more steam in a continuous cycle.

Areas of concern are carry over of impurities, evaporator flooding, stagnant water, and pump and compressor seal design. These concerns may be addressed using mist eliminators, high water level indicators, use of sanitary pumps and compressors, proper drainage, adequate blow down control, and conductivity sensing to divert unacceptable water to drain.

Commentary

Distillation Principles Explained

The Three Steps of Purification by Distillation

  1. Evaporation — water is heated into steam, leaving solid impurities and large molecules in the liquid phase
  2. Separation — the steam passes through a separator, which removes entrained water droplets and endotoxin
  3. Condensation — the pure steam condenses back to liquid, which is the WFI

Key point: distillation achieves endotoxin removal of more than 3 log (99.99%), which is the core reason distillation has long been regarded as the gold standard for WFI.

Analogy: Distillation = Boiling Water, Upgraded

Boiling water at home only kills germs, whereas distillation is more like "taking only the steam and condensing it". Imagine a pot lid fitted with a collection device:

  • The water in the pot contains impurities such as salts and endotoxin
  • As steam rises, the impurities stay at the bottom of the pot
  • But some tiny water droplets are carried away by the steam (entrainment) — this is why a separator is needed to intercept them

Telling SE, ME and VC Apart Quickly

  • SE (single effect): the simplest; one evaporation and one condensation; the highest energy consumption
  • ME (multi-effect): several stages in series, with the steam from each stage heating the next, which greatly saves steam
  • VC (vapor compression): a compressor raises the energy of the steam and recycles it; only electricity is needed, with no external steam

The water quality from all three is essentially the same (each distills only once); the difference lies in energy efficiency and cost structure.

Risk Points of Distillation Systems

  • Carry over (entrainment) — steam carries impurity-bearing droplets away → a mist eliminator is needed
  • Flooding — excessive liquid level → a high-level alarm is needed
  • Stagnant water — water that is not fully drained breeds microorganisms → good drainability in the design is needed
  • Seal failure — pump or compressor seals → hygienic design is needed
Original Text

6.4 Distillation Applications and Capacities

The majority of USP WFI currently produced in the United States is produced by distillation. WFI production is shared by both ME and VC stills. SE stills are found in areas where only small quantities of WFI are required. However, where large amounts of WFI are required, economics of operation dictate the use of either ME or VC.

Table 6-1 shows typical capacities and temperatures of WFI produced by each process.

Table 6-1: Capacities and WFI Temperature Options

Commentary

Capacity and Application Scenarios

Selection Logic

  • Small demand (<100 GPH) — SE is sufficient; simple equipment and small footprint
  • Medium demand (25-3,000 GPH) — ME is the most common, with an energy efficiency several times better than SE
  • Large demand (100-6,000 GPH) — VC has the widest capacity range and can also produce ambient-temperature water

What the Temperature Options Mean

The WFI outlet temperature directly affects the design of the downstream storage and distribution system:

  • 80-100°C (hot) — can go straight into a hot circulating storage system, which is naturally bacteriostatic
  • Ambient — offered only by VC; additional microbial control measures are required
  • 37°C — achievable with ME, but note that this temperature range carries a higher microbial risk

GPH Unit Conversion

GPH = Gallons Per Hour

1 GPH ≈ 3.785 L/h

Example: ME maximum 3,000 GPH ≈ 11,355 L/h ≈ 11.4 m³/h

VC maximum 6,000 GPH ≈ 22,710 L/h ≈ 22.7 m³/h

Original Text

6.5.1 Single Effect Distillation (SE)

SE systems incorporate a single evaporator heat exchanger, separator mechanism, and a condenser.

SE systems are available in electrically or steam powered versions, although electrical units are limited to very small production rates (<30 gallons per hour).

Steam powered units typically require 30-60 psig plant steam. Cooling fluid is required for both steam and electric powered versions. When water is the coolant, the rate is approximately 8-10 gallons per gallon of WFI produced, based on a supply temperature of 4 - 16°C, and temperature rise of 67°C.

SE systems typically operate at atmospheric pressure and 100°C, and incorporate non-ASME code vessels. WFI is delivered at atmospheric pressure and 80 - 100°C; thus a distillate transfer pump is required, unless the WFI tank is at a lower elevation than the still.

Commentary

Single Effect Distillation Explained

Characteristics of SE Systems

  • Simplest construction: evaporator + separator + condenser, a three-piece set
  • Atmospheric-pressure operation (100°C); the vessels do not need ASME pressure vessel certification
  • High cooling water consumption: every gallon of WFI produced needs 8-10 gallons of cooling water
  • No outlet pressure: a transfer pump is needed, or use of elevation difference (still located above the tank)

Analogy: SE Is Like a Miniature Still

An SE still is like a scaled-up laboratory distillation flask — one evaporation, one condensation, pure water out. Simple and effective, but high in energy use and low in output, so it suits R&D or small formulation plants.

Calculating Cooling Water Consumption

Take an SE producing 50 GPH of WFI as an example:

Cooling water demand = 50 × 8~10 = 400~500 GPH

This means cooling water cost is one of the main running costs of an SE.

Original Text

6.5.2 Multi-Effect Distillation (ME)

ME systems incorporate two or more evaporator heat exchangers, separator mechanisms, and a condenser into a staged evaporation and condensation process. Typical systems have 3-8 effects. Each effect includes an evaporator and a separator.

ME systems typically require plant steam at 80 - 120 psig, and cooling fluid at a supply temperature of 4°C - 16°C, based on a temperature rise of 65°C - 70°C. The quantity of steam and cooling fluid required varies significantly based upon the WFI production rate and the number of effects. Capital costs increase while steam and cooling fluid consumption decrease, as the number of effects to produce a given quantity of WFI increases. ME systems operate under pressure, and typically deliver WFI at 80°C - 100°C.

Normally, water used for cooling is not the same as the feed water, and does not require special pretreatment for the purpose of scale prevention. However, corrosion prevention measures, such as chlorine and chloramine removal, are necessary.

Some designs deliver the water at atmospheric pressure and require a transfer pump unless the WFI storage tank is at lower elevation than the still. Other designs which may operate at 5-10 psig condenser pressure, do feature a distillate transfer pump for higher pressure deliveries.

Commentary

Multi-Effect Distillation Explained

Core Principle of ME: Reusing Energy

The essence of an ME system is "cascaded heat recovery":

  1. Effect 1 uses external steam to heat the feed water
  2. The pure steam produced by effect 1 becomes the heating source for effect 2
  3. The steam from effect 2 in turn heats effect 3, and so on
  4. Only the steam from the last effect needs external cooling water to condense it

More effects → less steam consumption → but higher equipment cost. A typical choice is 3-8 effects.

Number of Effects and Steam Economy

Approximate relationship: steam economy ≈ number of effects × 0.8~0.9

Example: a 6-effect still has a steam economy of about 4.8~5.4, meaning each 1 kg of steam input can produce about 5 kg of WFI.

By comparison, the steam economy of an SE is only about 0.9~1.0.

Note: ME Needs Higher-Pressure Steam

ME needs plant steam at 80-120 psig, far higher than the 30-60 psig for SE. Before building, confirm that the boiler system can supply steam at sufficient pressure.

Trade-offs of ME

The optimal number of effects depends on a combined analysis of steam cost, capacity requirements and investment budget.

Original Text

6.5.3 Vapor Compression Distillation (VC)

VC is a distillation method where water is evaporated inside, or outside, a bank of tubes arranged in a horizontal or vertical configuration. The horizontal design is normally of the forced circulation type with recirculation pump and spray nozzles, while the vertical design is of the natural circulation type.

Major system components are the evaporator, compressor, heat exchangers, deaerator, pumps, motors, valves, instruments, and controls.

The VC process operates on the same principle as the mechanical refrigeration cycle.

In a VC still, feed water is evaporated on one side of the tubes. The generated steam passes through the disengagement space, through the separator, and into the compressor.

The energy imparted by the compressor results in compressed steam with increased pressure and temperature. The higher energy steam is then discharged back into the evaporator/condenser vessel. There, the steam condenses and gives up its latent heat, which is transferred through the tube wall to the water. More water is boiled off, generating more vapor, and the process is repeated. The outgoing distillate and blow down streams preheat the incoming feed water, thus saving energy. Since the latent heat is recycled, there is no need for a stand-alone condenser as in the SE or ME systems.

Commentary

Vapor Compression Distillation Explained

VC Principle: A Latent Heat Recovery Cycle

The core concept of VC is a compressor-driven energy cycle:

  1. Feed water evaporates into steam on the tube bundle
  2. The steam passes through the separator and then enters the compressor
  3. The compressor raises the pressure and temperature of the steam
  4. The high-energy steam returns to the evaporator/condenser and condenses on the other side of the tube wall
  5. The latent heat released on condensation heats the water on the other side of the tube wall, producing more steam
  6. The cycle repeats, with no separate condenser needed

Analogy: VC Is Like a Heat Pump (Air Conditioner)

VC distillation works on exactly the same principle as a household reverse-cycle air conditioner — both are "mechanical compression cycles":

  • The air conditioner compressor compresses refrigerant → the VC compressor compresses water vapor
  • The air conditioner's condenser releases heat → in VC, the high-pressure steam releases heat on condensing to heat the feed water
  • Energy is recycled continuously, with only the compressor's electricity as input

Therefore the main energy cost of VC is electricity, not steam.

Unique Advantages of VC

  • No separate condenser needed — latent heat is fully recovered
  • No external steam needed (ambient-temperature outlet mode) — electricity only
  • Can produce ambient-temperature WFI — SE and ME can only produce hot water
  • Widest capacity range — up to 6,000 GPH

Challenges of VC

The compressor is the most critical and most vulnerable component of a VC system. Compressor seal design, vibration management and compatibility with pharmaceutical hygiene requirements are the key concerns in VC system design.

Original Text

6.5.4 Distillation Pretreatment Requirements — General

All distillation units are susceptible to scaling and corrosion, if the appropriate feed water pretreatment is not provided. VC and some SE stills operate slightly above atmospheric pressure, and the removal of calcium and magnesium, by way of water softening, is normally required as a minimum. ME stills operate at a much higher pressure and temperature, and require higher quality feed water in order to prevent scaling and corrosion. Normally, ion exchange beds are employed as feed water pretreatment to a multiple effect still. RO is also used as feed water pretreatment for either the VC or multiple effect stills.

All distillation units will invariably experience some form of scale build-up and must therefore include routine visual inspections plus cleaning of the still during shutdown periods when appropriate. Both types of stills are susceptible to attack by chlorine. Chlorine removal is essential if damage is to be avoided. Activated carbon filters and sodium bisulfite injection are effective and common methods for chlorine removal.

From a microbiological perspective, the bacterial and endotoxin load should be consistently controlled to a level that does not overload the still.

6.5.5 Pretreatment Requirements — Specific

6.5.5.2 ME Still: The baseline pretreatment for ME must provide very low TDS feed water, preferably less than 10 mg/l, and less than 1 mg/l silica. Some manufacturers offer ME stills to operate on softened water. Others allow higher levels of silica, up to 5 mg/l. The pretreatment must also remove chlorine and objectionable volatiles, such as ammonia.

6.5.5.3 VC Still: The baseline pretreatment for VC stills is softening, the removal of chlorine, and other objectionable volatiles such as ammonia, if present.

6.5.5.4 Economics

a) SE Economics: Commercially available SE systems are inherently simple in design. Operating costs are associated mainly with plant steam and cooling fluid.

b) ME Economics: Opportunities for cost savings exist in the areas of construction materials, surface finishes, and instrumentation. Operating costs are associated mainly with plant steam and cooling fluid. Utilities consumption rates vary among ME manufacturers.

c) VC Economics: Significant opportunities exist to reduce capital cost associated with selection of construction materials, surface finishes, and instrumentation. Operating costs of VC systems are associated mainly with electrical power.

Commentary

Pretreatment and Economic Analysis

The Danger of Chlorine — Not to Be Ignored

The stainless steel of every still is corroded by chlorine. Chlorine in the feed water must be thoroughly removed; common methods are:

  • Activated carbon filter — the most common
  • Sodium bisulfite injection — a chemical reduction method

Inspection focus: confirm the maintenance records of the dechlorination equipment and the carbon bed replacement interval.

Exercise

Your plant has ample high-pressure steam (120 psig) but expensive electricity. WFI demand is about 1,500 GPH, and hot storage at 80°C or above is required. Which type of still would you choose, and why?

Hint: consider steam supply, electricity cost, outlet temperature and capacity range.

Pretreatment Requirements Comparison Table

ME has the most demanding feed water quality requirements, because the risk of scaling is greater at high temperature and pressure.

Original Text

6.5.6 Construction Materials, Surface Finish, and Instrumentation

Recommended Construction Materials (Table 6-2)

6.5.6.1 Surface Finish

Mechanical polishing (MP), electropolishing (EP), and passivation processes are implemented in stainless steel distillation systems in order to improve corrosion resistance. These processes are neither necessary, nor applicable, to other alloys such as tin-coated copper, titanium, and Inconel.

MP and EP/passivation processes affect the microscopic amplitude and chemical composition, respectively, of the stainless surface. These processes are not considered necessary to control microbial growth due to the relatively high operating temperatures. MP is advocated for final finishing of mechanical welds and EP/passivation for all stainless steel surfaces to optimize the formation of the corrosion resistant chromium oxide barrier.

The impact of progressive mechanical polishing on the capital cost of stills and other equipment is considerable, and often can account for 25% to 30% of a ME or VC still cost. MP processes, except when used to smooth out a mechanical weld or misalignment, may be removed from the applicable specification without fear of compromising the water quality.

6.5.6.2 Instrumentation and Controls

For WFI applications, the level of instrumentation should be sufficient to monitor parameters considered critical because they relate to ensuring proper hydraulic/thermodynamic functionality and the production of the appropriate quality of WFI. Instrumentation for critical operating parameters should be calibratable using National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) traceable equipment.

Commentary

Materials and Surface Finish

Principles of Material Selection

  • SE: tin-coated copper can be used, at the lowest cost
  • ME: 316/316L stainless steel throughout; titanium (corrosion resistant) is an option for the tubing
  • VC: 304 SS shell, 316 SS for wetted parts, and 316 SS or Inconel (a nickel-based alloy resistant to high temperature and pressure) for the compressor
  • Frame/skid: carbon steel is acceptable for all three (no contact with pure water)

Major Cost Insight: MP Accounts for 25-30% of Cost

ISPE states explicitly that in distillation systems, mechanical polishing (MP) is not a necessary condition for microbial control, because the system operates at high temperature.

Over-specifying MP can account for 25-30% of equipment cost without improving water quality. This is a classic case of over-specification, which Good Engineering Practice stresses should be avoided.

Analogy: Gold-Plated Door Handles

Applying precision polishing to a distillation system running at 100°C is like fitting gold-plated door handles on a fire station — it looks upmarket but does nothing for the function (putting out fires). The high temperature itself is the best means of microbial control.

The Proper Use of Surface Treatments

  • MP (mechanical polishing) — used to smooth weld seams, and nothing more
  • EP (electropolishing) + passivation — used on all stainless steel surfaces to build a protective chromium oxide layer for corrosion protection (not microbial protection)
  • Non-stainless alloys (tin-coated copper, titanium, Inconel) — neither need nor suit MP/EP

Instrument Calibration Requirements

Instruments for critical operating parameters must be calibrated with NIST-traceable equipment. This ensures the accuracy of measurement data and regulatory compliance. In Taiwan, the corresponding traceability system is the National Measurement Laboratory (NML).

Original Text

Table 6-3: Process and System Comparison (Distillation)

Commentary

Key Points of Distillation System Comparison

Three Differentiating Features

  • VC needs neither a condenser nor cooling water — because latent heat is fully recovered internally
  • Only ME needs ASME pressure vessel certification — because it operates at high pressure (100-120 psig)
  • ME can co-produce Clean Steam — an added value of ME (VC has no such function)

The Added Value of Clean Steam

An ME still can not only produce WFI but also produce pure steam (Clean Steam) at the same time for SIP (steam-in-place sterilization). This gives ME a particular advantage in aseptic filling plants that need to supply both WFI and Clean Steam.

Tips for Reading the Table

When faced with a multi-parameter comparison table, it helps to first ask yourself:

  1. What utilities does my plant have? (steam pressure, cooling water, electricity)
  2. What additional functions do I need? (Clean Steam, ambient-temperature outlet)
  3. Where am I willing to invest capital in exchange for operating savings?

Then screen the table row by row, and the selection range narrows quickly.

Original Text

6.6 Reverse Osmosis (RO) for WFI

RO employs a semi-permeable membrane and a relatively high pressure differential to force water through the membrane to achieve chemical, microbial, and endotoxin reduction, critical in USP WFI applications. The feed water is converted into two streams, permeate and reject. The permeate water flows through the membrane and is produced cold and as such does not have the temperature protection for microbial growth afforded by the alternate distillation processes.

6.6.1 Application

RO systems are used as USP WFI pretreatment for distillation processes, or as final treatment for USP Purified water systems. RO is also an accepted means of producing WFI, and may provide a low capital and operational cost alternative to distillation.

Membranes that are hot water sanitizable at 80°C are now available for pretreatment and final treatment, thus eliminating the need for chemical sanitization and simplifying the validation process.

Membranes, which may allow for continuous operation at 80°C, are under development. Failure of a membrane or seal will result in permeate contamination. These problems may be controlled by:

  • Pretreatment of the feed water
  • Appropriate membrane material selection
  • Latest technology membrane design
  • Integrity challenges
  • Periodic sanitization
  • Monitoring of microbial levels, conductivity, total organic carbon and differential pressures

6.6.2 Description

Semi-permeable RO membranes are produced commercially in spiral wound and hollow fiber configurations. Membranes are available in four basic materials: cellulose acetate, polyamide, thin film composite, and polysulfone.

Bacteria and endotoxin removal, required for WFI applications, can be performed at ambient temperatures. This significantly reduces utility costs compared to alternative elevated temperature processes (distillation). By operating at ambient temperatures, distribution piping may not require insulation and may not need to be constructed of stainless steel.

For WFI applications, opportunities exist for enhanced control of the single pass unit, by utilizing multi-pass-product-staged or other combination designs.

Commentary

Producing WFI by RO Explained

RO as a Means of Producing WFI

RO (Reverse Osmosis) uses a semipermeable membrane under high pressure to separate water from impurities:

  • Permeate — the pure water that passes through the membrane, i.e. the product water
  • Reject (concentrate) — the concentrated water carrying the impurities blocked by the membrane, which is discharged

The biggest characteristic of RO: it produces ambient-temperature water. This is both an advantage (low energy consumption) and a challenge (no protection from temperature-based microbial control).

The Core Challenge of RO-WFI: Microbial Control

Distillation systems operate at 80-100°C, which naturally suppresses microorganisms. RO operates at ambient temperature and must control microorganisms by other means:

  • Hot water sanitizable membranes (80°C)
  • Periodic chemical sanitization/cleaning
  • Integrity challenge
  • Continuous monitoring of conductivity, TOC, microbial counts and differential pressure

Analogy: RO vs. Distillation = Washing Hands in Cold Water vs. Hot Water

Distillation is like washing hands in hot water — the high temperature itself kills germs. RO is like washing hands in cold water with soap (the membrane) — it needs an additional cleaning agent (the sanitization procedure) to ensure hygiene, but it saves energy.

Original Text

6.6.3 Pretreatment Requirements

RO, as the final processing step, may require pretreatment using ion exchange, deionization, RO, and/or ultrafiltration to improve operability and quality attributes.

Pretreatment requirements normally include gross particle filtration, scale prevention, and chlorine removal. Carbon dioxide and ammonia gas are not removed by the RO process, and may be removed by degasification, caustic addition, ion exchange, or electrodeionization, prior to the final RO process step.

Due to the stringent microbial and endotoxin control required, the pretreatment prior to the RO should incorporate additional provisions for control and monitoring of microorganisms. Disinfectants, such as chlorine or chloramine, should be maintained at appropriate levels throughout the pretreatment chain. Stagnant water should be avoided by design, or by the inclusion of recirculation systems with in-line UV sterilizers.

Regular and appropriate sanitization and cleaning of all unit operations subsequent to the disinfectant removal should be scheduled.

6.6.4 Economics

Operating costs of RO systems are associated mainly with replacement membranes, water concentrate discharge, electrical power, cleaning and sanitizing chemicals, replacement filters, and pretreatment cost.

6.6.5 Construction Materials

Construction material selection for RO systems are driven by structural integrity (high pressure), chemical compatibility, and the need to control micro-organism growth.

The low operating temperature of the RO system allows the use of non-metallic construction materials. Sanitary piping and valves are generally optional features. For the final purification step, it may be very cost effective to utilize mill finish 304 stainless steel for the feed and concentrate waste piping, maintaining 316L stainless steel or PVDF and sanitary design for the product piping only.

6.6.6-7 Surface Finish & Instrumentation

MP and EP processes are not applicable to non-metallic systems. RO control systems usually use local control and indication and do not typically require PLCs as a standard feature. The typically monitored parameters are feed pH, feed conductivity, and product quality (TOC and conductivity), using calibratable, NIST traceable instruments.

Commentary

RO Pretreatment and Economics

RO-WFI Pretreatment Strategy

When RO is the final step for WFI, the pretreatment train must be stricter than for an ordinary PW system:

  • Coarse filtration — protects the membrane from particle damage
  • Antiscalant treatment — prevents calcium and magnesium scaling
  • Dechlorination — protects the polyamide/TFC membrane
  • Microbial control — maintain the disinfectant (chlorine/chloramine) up to the dechlorination point
  • Prevention of stagnant water — circulating system + UV sterilization

Cost Advantages and Hidden Costs of RO

Obvious advantages:

  • No steam needed — only electricity to drive the high-pressure pump
  • Ambient-temperature operation — piping needs no insulation, and material choice is more flexible

Easily overlooked costs:

  • Membrane replacement (a periodic consumable)
  • Cleaning and sanitization chemicals
  • Concentrate discharge (water wastage)
  • Maintenance of the pretreatment system

GEP Principles for Material Selection

A major cost advantage of RO systems: because they run at ambient temperature, non-metallic materials (such as PVDF and PVC) can be used. Only the product water piping needs 316L SS or hygienic-design PVDF. The feed water and waste piping can even be ordinary 304 SS.

This contrasts sharply with distillation systems, which use 316L SS almost throughout, and is one important reason why the capital cost of RO is lower.

Original Text

6.6.8 RO Advantages and Disadvantages

Multi-pass can, in most cases, produce water quality consistent with the minimum requirements of USP WFI. In cases where the feed water quality is such that this is not possible, the use of some type of deionization as a pretreatment may be required.

Advantages:

  1. RO systems designed for production of USP WFI may provide for significantly reduced capital costs when compared with distillation processes, while maintaining the appropriate USP WFI quality.
  2. The utility requirements are significantly lower for RO systems (electricity for pump horsepower) than for distillation, resulting in lower operating costs, which may be a very significant factor over the lifetime of the system.

Disadvantages:

  1. Membrane fouling and integrity:
    • Bacteria grow-through
    • Seal leakage or by-passing
    • Seal failure or damage caused by chemical attack
    • Membrane damage during installation
    • Membrane damage due to chemical or high temperature attack
  2. Membrane material sensitivity to bacteria and sanitizing agents
  3. Inherent sanitization limitations:
    • Periodic chemical or hot water sanitization may be required
    • Periodic chemical cleaning may be required
  4. Pretreatment cost may be high

RO systems provide a method for consistently producing ambient water in accordance with the USP WFI requirements. This not only reduces utility requirements, but also may reduce installation costs, since thermal insulation may not be required for storage and distribution.

Commentary

Advantages and Disadvantages of RO

The Biggest Risk: Bacteria Grow-Through

"Bacterial grow-through" is the risk of greatest concern in RO-WFI systems. Unlike the phase-change separation of distillation (evaporation at 100°C), the RO membrane operates at ambient temperature, and after long-term use the microscopic structure of the membrane may allow bacteria to gradually grow through. This requires:

  • Periodic integrity testing
  • Strict sanitization cycles
  • Continuous monitoring of product water microbiology

Exercise: RO or Distillation?

Consider the following scenarios. Would you recommend RO-WFI or distillation-WFI?

  1. A new small biologics plant with a limited budget, in a Southeast Asian region with cheap electricity
  2. A large European filling plant (before 2017) that needs to supply both WFI and Clean Steam
  3. An expansion of an older plant that already has well-established cooling water and high-pressure steam systems

Hint: consider regulations, utility conditions, Clean Steam needs and life-cycle cost.

6.7 USP — Water for Injection Systems Comparison
