To avoid the problem of condensation in the filter and the resultant potential for colonization and grow through, hydrophobic vent filters are used and/or the filters are maintained at a temperature above the tank temperature with steam jacketing or electric tracing.

To help avoid microbial growth, and avoid the change in conductivity resulting from absorption of atmospheric gasses into the water, nitrogen blanketing on the head space may be used. This eliminates outside air passing into the tank through the vent filter. Note that gasses added to storage tanks should be appropriately filtered to avoid objectionable contamination.

Tutorial Commentary

Storage Tank -- The "High Risk Zone"

ISPE identifies storage tanks as high-risk for microbial contamination. Four reasons:

  1. Large surface area: More surface = more potential biofilm attachment sites.
  2. Low velocities: Unlike the loop (3+ ft/sec), water in the tank moves slowly -- bacteria can settle.
  3. Venting requirement: The vent filter is the one point where the system communicates with the ambient environment.
  4. Head space cold spots: In hot systems, the tank head space above the water line may be cooler than the water, creating condensation that harbors bacteria.

Design principle: Smaller tanks are microbiologically superior. Size the tank for operational needs, not "just in case" capacity.

Spray Ball Design -- Critical Details

  • Purpose: Wet the entire head space to prevent alternating wet/dry surfaces (which promote both corrosion and microbial growth).
  • Temperature benefit: In hot systems, spray keeps head space at operating temperature -- no cold spots for condensation.
  • Top connections: Keep relief devices, instruments as close to the tank head as possible -- they benefit from spray coverage.
  • Vent filter exception: Keep the vent filter AWAY from spray action. Water on the vent filter can blind (block) it, and a wet filter is a microbial colonization site.
  • Shadow effect: If dip tubes or instruments protrude downward from the head, they can block spray coverage, creating unwetted "shadow" zones. Solution: multiple spray balls or 360-degree coverage designs.

Analogy -- Tank Head Space

The head space of a storage tank is like the ceiling area of a shower room. Even though the shower (spray ball) keeps the walls wet, the ceiling often has cold spots where condensation forms. Over time, mold (= biofilm) grows on those damp, cool surfaces. In a pharmaceutical tank, the spray ball is your "shower head" keeping the ceiling wet and warm. But you must be strategic about placement -- you want spray coverage everywhere EXCEPT the vent filter (which must stay dry to function properly).

Vent Filter & Nitrogen Blanketing

Two complementary strategies for tank isolation:

  • Hydrophobic vent filter: Allows air exchange during fill/drain cycles while blocking particles and microorganisms. "Hydrophobic" means it repels water -- critical because a wet filter promotes "grow through."
  • Heat the filter: Steam jacketing or electric tracing keeps filter above tank temperature -- prevents condensation inside the filter housing.
  • Nitrogen blanketing: Eliminates air exchange entirely. Benefits: no airborne contamination risk, no CO2 absorption (which lowers conductivity and pH). The nitrogen itself must be filtered (0.2 μm) before entering the tank.

For CDMO operations: Nitrogen blanketing is particularly valuable for WFI tanks and for products sensitive to oxidation. The cost of filtered nitrogen is modest compared to the microbial risk reduction.

Practice Questions

  1. Your facility is considering switching from 316L SS piping to PVDF for a new ambient PW loop. The loop has UV lights for ozone destruction. What design modification is needed near the UV units?
  2. During a routine inspection, you find the vent filter on your WFI tank is wet (water droplets visible inside the housing). What are the immediate risks? What corrective actions do you take?
  3. Your WFI tank is 10,000L but typical daily usage is only 2,000L. From a microbial perspective, what is the problem? What solutions would you consider?
  4. An engineer proposes using silicone gaskets in the distribution system because they are cheaper than PTFE. What concerns should you raise, referencing 21 CFR 211.65?

Materials of Construction -- Decision Guide

21 CFR 211.65 reminder: Materials must not be "reactive, additive, or absorptive." This means your sanitization method cannot cause the materials to leach substances into the water. Chemical sanitization is the riskiest in this regard -- you must control concentration, pH, temperature, and contact time.

Section Summary

  • Three enemies of microbial quality: stagnation, temperatures between 15-55°C, and poor feedwater. All design decisions in this chapter aim to counteract these fundamental risks.
  • GEP vs regulatory mandate: ISPE clarifies that finishes, tank orientation, turnover, drainability, and deadleg rules are Good Engineering Practices, NOT absolute regulatory requirements. Collective compliance reduces risk; individual deviations may be acceptable if justified.
  • Deadleg measurement: ISPE resolves the 6D/3D confusion by recommending measurement from the outer wall of the main pipe. No hard rule of thumb -- focus on water quality outcomes.
  • Three operating strategies: Hot (≥65°C, self-sanitizing), Cold (4-10°C, reduced sanitization), and Ambient (cheapest but highest risk, requiring frequent sanitization). Each must be validated system-specifically.
  • Continuous microbial control tools: Ozone (0.02-0.1 ppm in tank, UV removal at loop), UV light (reduces, does NOT sterilize), filtration (MF/UF upstream of tank, not downstream due to "grow through"), and circulation (≥3 ft/sec turbulent flow).
  • Periodic sanitization: Chemical (H2O2, peracetic acid), ozone (up to 1 ppm for biofilm), and heat (80°C ± 3°C, most reliable). Steam is NOT required for PW/WFI systems.
  • Materials compatibility: 316L SS is the most versatile; PVDF degrades under UV. All materials must comply with 21 CFR 211.65 (non-reactive, non-additive, non-absorptive).
  • Storage tank design: Smaller tanks are microbiologically preferred. Spray balls keep head space wet/warm. Vent filters must be hydrophobic and heated. Nitrogen blanketing eliminates airborne contamination risk.
9

Chapter 9: Instrumentation & Control

p147-p156

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p147-p156

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the three functions of instrumentation in pharmaceutical water systems: controlling equipment operation, monitoring the performance of critical equipment, and recording water quality data
  • Master the logic for defining critical vs non-critical instruments: a parameter that affects final water quality is a critical parameter, and its instrument requires qualification
  • Learn the selection and installation points for each type of online instrument: conductivity, TOC, pH, ozone, flow, temperature, pressure and level
  • Understand the difference between temperature-compensated and non-compensated conductivity measurement, and why USP requires non-compensated values for final quality testing
  • Master the three-tier structure of Design Condition, Allowable Operating Range and Normal Operating Range, and the use of Alert/Action Levels
  • Recognize the causes of instrumentation spikes and strategies for handling them
  • Understand the four levels of control system automation, and how control software validation relates to 21 CFR Part 11

Chapter Contents

9.1 Introduction 9.2 Principles 9.3 General Requirements 9.3.1 Selection & Installation 9.3.2 Calibration 9.3.3 Types of Instrumentation 9.4 Design vs Operating Range 9.5 Instrumentation Spikes 9.6 Control Systems
9.1 Introduction

Original Text

Instrumentation and controls are often used within pharmaceutical water systems to:

  • Control the operation of equipment and components
  • Monitor and document the performance of critical equipment
  • Monitor and document pharmaceutical water quality

The concepts and regulatory philosophy of defining critical versus non-critical parameters is discussed as it relates to instrumentation and controls. This definition could be summarized as:

"All instruments and control systems should be commissioned following Good Engineering Practices. Critical instruments and control systems should be commissioned and qualified."

There is no regulatory requirement that requires the use of On-Line instrumentation. A monitoring program may include a combination of On-Line instrumentation, manual documentation, and laboratory analysis.

If On-Line instrumentation is used to measure or record a critical parameter, action and alert limits may be established. The methods of addressing "spikes" are also discussed.

Automation can have a significant impact on the cost and performance of a pharmaceutical water system. There is no single optimum level of instrumentation and control for all systems. The optimum level for a given system balances the benefits of improved process control, improved documentation, and lower labor costs against the cost of procuring, installing, validating, and maintaining the instruments and control systems. In many cases, the level of automation for a pharmaceutical water system should be consistent with that utilized for the manufacturing process it supports.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The three roles of instrumentation: In a pharmaceutical water system, instruments are not just tools for "reading numbers"; they play three roles at once:

  • Controller: automatically controls the operation of valves, pumps, heaters and other equipment
  • Monitor: continuously monitors the performance of critical equipment to ensure it operates within specification
  • Documenter: generates electronic or paper records of water quality and operating data as evidence of compliance

The dividing line between critical and non-critical: ISPE's logic is clear: if a parameter affects final water quality, it is a critical parameter, and its instrument requires qualification. Otherwise, commissioning to GEP is sufficient. This classification determines your validation scope and maintenance cost.

Analogy

Think of the instruments in a water system as the monitoring equipment in a hospital. The ECG monitor in the ICU (a critical instrument) must be calibrated regularly, have alarms, and have its data fully recorded, because it bears directly on the patient's life. The thermometer in the waiting room (a non-critical instrument) only needs to be roughly accurate and does not need the same calibration procedure. The same applies to a pharmaceutical water system: not every instrument needs the same level of rigor. What matters is whether the parameter it measures affects final water quality.

Key Notes

No regulation mandates online monitoring! This is a common misunderstanding. ISPE states clearly that a monitoring program can be a combination of online instruments, manual records and laboratory analysis. In practice, however, almost all modern systems use online monitoring, for three reasons:

  • Continuous data captures transient excursions better than sampling
  • Less manual intervention reduces the risk of human error
  • It meets data integrity expectations: electronic records are more traceable than hand-copied data

In a CDMO setting, a customer auditor who sees complete online trend charts will have far more confidence than one who sees handwritten daily sampling records.

Practice Questions

  1. Your water system has 50 instrument points. How would you decide which are critical and which are non-critical? List your criteria.
  2. If management asks you to classify all instruments as non-critical to save validation costs, how would you respond?
  3. Why does ISPE recommend that the level of automation of a water system be consistent with the process it supports? What does this mean in a CDMO?
9.2 Principles

Original Text

a) To achieve GMP compliance, the manufacturer must demonstrate, through documented evidence, that the pharmaceutical water system is in control and consistently produces and delivers water of acceptable quality.

b) Although many quality attributes can be continuously monitored using On-Line instrumentation, there is no compendial or regulatory requirement for On-Line monitoring of pharmaceutical water quality. A monitoring program typically includes a combination of On-Line instrumentation, manual documentation of operational parameters, and laboratory analysis of water samples.

c) Instruments and control systems are critical and must be qualified when they are used to measure, monitor, control, or record a critical process parameter. A critical parameter is a processing parameter that affects the final water quality.

For example, the temperature of the final water product may be considered critical for microbial control. In this case, the temperature controls (e.g., sensors and alarms) would be considered critical. However, it is not necessary to consider the temperature of the heating media (e.g., steam) as a critical parameter.

Documentation should clearly indicate which instruments are critical and which are not. It is also advisable to identify non-critical instruments as such on the field device.

d) All instruments and control systems should be designed, installed, calibrated, and maintained appropriately, according to Good Engineering Practice. All critical instruments and controls require qualification.

e) Items that should be recorded in the system documentation include maintenance procedures and maintenance work performed, procedures for sampling and analysis, reporting the results, and trend analysis of the laboratory data. The monitoring program during start-up typically defines maintenance frequency and alert and action levels for the process variables.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Logic for determining a critical parameter:

  • Ask yourself one question: "If this parameter deviates, will final water quality be affected?"
  • If the answer is Yes → Critical Parameter → the instrument requires qualification
  • If the answer is No → Non-Critical → GEP commissioning is sufficient

ISPE's example is a classic: the temperature of the product water (which directly affects microbial control) = Critical. The temperature of the heating steam (an indirect effect, with an intermediate buffer) = Non-Critical. This logic can be extended to the classification of all instruments.

Key Notes

The recommendation to "label non-critical instruments in the field" is very practical. Why? Because:

  • Maintenance staff who see the instrument in the field immediately know its calibration level and frequency requirements
  • Auditors walking the plant can quickly confirm that the instrument classification matches the documentation
  • It avoids a "better safe than sorry" mindset that leads to unnecessary calibration work and wasted resources

Recommended practice: attach a label to every instrument stating Critical / Non-Critical, calibration due date and calibration frequency.

CDMO Application

The value of trend analysis in a CDMO:

ISPE mentions that the monitoring plan for the start-up period should define alert/action levels. In a CDMO, trend analysis is even more valuable:

  • During customer audits you will be asked to show water quality trend charts for the past 6-12 months
  • The slope of the trend matters more than any single reading: a slowly rising conductivity may indicate declining membrane performance
  • Alert levels should be set from statistical analysis of historical data, not arbitrarily

Practice Questions

  1. A temperature sensor on your formulation tank monitors temperature during mixing. Is this sensor critical or non-critical? Why?
  2. ISPE says "there is no regulatory requirement for online monitoring", but your customer insists that every POU have online conductivity monitoring. How would you assess whether this requirement is reasonable?
9.3 General Instrumentation Requirements
9.3.1 Instrument Selection and Installation

Original Text

a) Instruments should be selected for accuracy and reliability over the entire process range.

b) Instruments should be selected and installed in a way that reduces the potential for contamination.

  • Water contact surfaces should be constructed of materials that are compatible with the water they contact. Materials of construction and surface finishes are commonly specified for instruments installed in distribution systems.
  • Sensors in direct contact with waters with strict microbial limits should be of sanitary design. Non-sanitary instrumentation is commonly used in feed water and pretreatment systems.
  • Instruments may be installed directly in the water system or in a side stream that may, or may not, be returned to the main system.
  • Deadlegs should be avoided.

c) When possible, instruments should be installed such that exposure to harsh process conditions, such as pH and temperature extremes, is avoided. For example, In-Line sensors used to monitor effluent from a deionizer should be positioned such that exposure to regeneration chemicals is avoided.

Instruments that are not compatible with passivation agents, sanitization agents, or sanitization temperatures should be installed so that they may be easily removed or bypassed. Such devices may need to be sanitized off line.

d) Instruments should be installed in accordance with manufacturers' requirements to ensure proper operation. For example, flow meters should be installed in the proper orientation and with the correct upstream and downstream straight run of pipe. The impact of process and ambient conditions on an instrument's accuracy and reliability should be addressed.

e) Conductivity cells are especially sensitive to the presence of air or steam bubbles, which can be present where there is turbulence, cavitation, or flashing. Such locations should be avoided.

f) Accessibility for maintenance should be considered, but improving control response is usually more critical. Poor response time may be a consequence of the poor placement of a device and, in most cases, can be improved by installing the device closer to the point of measurement.

Tutorial Commentary

Analogy

Imagine installing a smoke detector at home. Would you put it right above the kitchen stove? No, because it would give false alarms every day (just as a conductivity probe installed in a turbulent zone suffers bubble interference). Nor would you put it in the farthest corner of the garage, "easy to maintain but unable to sense anything" (just as a sensor installed too far from the measurement point for ease of maintenance). The best location is the hallway ceiling: close to the bedrooms (fast response) and not disturbed by daily activity (suitable environment).

Key Notes

Advantages and disadvantages of side stream installation:

  • Advantages: instruments can be maintained/calibrated without opening the main piping; instruments that are incompatible with sanitization (such as pH probes) can be installed; no additional deadleg is added to the main piping
  • Disadvantages: the measured value may lag behind the main piping; the side stream flow rate must be controlled to ensure accurate measurement; the side stream itself needs periodic flushing or draining
  • Key decision: does the side stream water return to the main system? If there is a risk of buffer leakage from a pH probe, it is usually sent to drain rather than recirculated

Common Mistakes

The hidden trap of deadlegs: Many people look only at deadlegs in the piping and overlook that the instrument installation itself can create deadlegs. For example:

  • Improper insertion depth of a thermowell
  • Pressure gauge connection not kept as short as possible
  • Level instrument stubs on the tank that are too long

Every "protrusion" can become a breeding ground for microorganisms, especially where flow is not continuous.

Key Concepts

Summary of the six installation principles:

9.3.2 Instrument Calibration

Original Text

a) The calibration of critical instruments should follow a regular program, which provides evidence of consistently acceptable performance. Non-critical instruments may be calibrated on a frequency deemed appropriate for the service.

b) Calibration should follow approved procedures and the results should be documented. Each component in a control loop should be calibrated individually or the loop may be calibrated in its entirety. All calibrations should be traceable to certified standards (e.g., NIST).

c) Vendor-supplied calibration certificates should reference the applicable instrument serial numbers. The impact of shipment and installation on the vendor's calibration should be addressed.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Three key principles of calibration:

  • Tiered frequency: critical instruments = periodic calibration (typically every 6-12 months); non-critical = determined by service needs (can be longer)
  • Traceability: all calibrations must be traceable to a certified standard (such as NIST), forming an unbroken calibration chain
  • Loop calibration: each component in a loop can be calibrated individually, or the whole loop can be calibrated together. The latter better reflects the actual accuracy of the system

Key Notes

Factory calibration vs post-installation calibration: ISPE specifically cautions that a calibration certificate supplied by the vendor may be invalidated by shipping and installation. In practice:

  • Precision instruments (such as conductivity meters and TOC analyzers) must always be recalibrated after installation
  • The serial number on the calibration certificate must match the serial number of the equipment in the field one to one: this is a common audit check
  • If there was shock or temperature extremes during shipping, an additional functional test should be performed

Under the data integrity framework, calibration records are also part of the GMP documentation and must be fully retained and protected against tampering.

9.3.3 Types of Instrumentation

9.3.3.1 Conductivity

Original Text

a) Although non-ion specific, conductivity is a valuable tool for measuring the total ionic quality of water and is a critical parameter for many high purity water systems. Conductivity limits for Purified Water and WFI are specified in the USP.

b) On-Line conductivity instrumentation is frequently used to monitor and control the performance of many types of purification equipment and to continuously monitor the quality of pharmaceutical waters. On-line conductivity instrumentation may also be used for final quality assurance testing, thus eliminating the need for periodic laboratory analysis of water samples.

c) Temperature has a profound impact upon conductivity measurement. To eliminate this temperature dependence, most instruments include a temperature sensor in the conductivity probe and one or more algorithms to correct the actual measurement to a standard temperature. However, due to the inaccuracy inherent in temperature compensation algorithms, compensated conductivity measurements are not suitable for critical quality assurance testing of USP purified water and WFI. When In-Line conductivity measurements are used for final quality assurance testing of USP purified water and WFI, a non-compensated conductivity value and the water temperature must be measured as required by the USP.

Compensated conductivity values used strictly for process control and monitoring are not subject to USP requirements.

d) To operate properly, conductivity sensors must be installed such that there is continuous water flow through the sensor and air bubbles or solids cannot become trapped inside the electrodes. Air bubbles will result in lower-than-expected conductivity readings while solids can impact the conductivity in either direction. Clean steam must be condensed prior to conductivity measurement.

e) Conductivity meters may be used throughout a pharmaceutical water system to monitor and control purification processes or to monitor pharmaceutical water quality. Some examples are:

  • Feed water monitoring can detect seasonal or unanticipated quality changes that could impact pretreatment equipment operation.
  • RO influent and effluent monitoring allows calculation and trending of percentage rejection. Changes in percentage rejection may be a sign of membrane failure, scaling or fouling, seal failure, improper pH, inadequate feed pressure, or too high a recovery rate.
  • Deionizer effluent or in-bed monitoring detects, or predicts, resin exhaustion and allows automatic initiation of regeneration cycles.
  • Final treatment step monitoring verifies acceptable quality prior to delivery to a storage tank. In addition, conductivity meters are often installed in the return piping of distribution loops downstream of the final point of use. Many systems include provisions for automatic diversion to drain or recirculation back through purification equipment when water quality entering the tank is outside the acceptable range.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Conductivity: the "thermometer" of a water system:

Conductivity is the single most important parameter in a pharmaceutical water system. Although it cannot identify which specific ions are present (it is non-ion specific), it quickly reflects the overall ionic quality of the water. It is like a thermometer: it cannot tell you which organ is inflamed, but a rising temperature tells you something is wrong with the body.

The key distinction on temperature compensation:

  • Compensated value: the instrument automatically corrects the reading to its equivalent at 25°C. Suitable for process monitoring and convenient for trend comparison
  • Non-compensated value: the raw conductivity reading at the actual temperature. USP requires this value, plus the water temperature, to be used for final quality testing, checked against the USP temperature-conductivity table

Why does USP not accept compensated values? Because every temperature compensation algorithm has an inherent error, and in the very small conductivity range of high-purity water, this error can lead to a wrong pass/fail decision.

Common Mistakes

False passes caused by bubbles: Air bubbles cause conductivity readings to read low (because air does not conduct). This means the system may show "pass" while the actual water quality is already out of specification. This is a hidden risk, and requires particular attention in the following situations:

  • Near pump outlets (bubbles from cavitation)
  • Locations with sharp temperature changes (flashing)
  • Where the piping changes diameter or direction abruptly (turbulence)

Key Notes

Layout strategy for conductivity monitoring points:

9.3.3.2 Total Organic Carbon (TOC)

Original Text

a) Total Organic Carbon (TOC) is a measure of the carbon dissolved in water in the form of organic compounds. It is a valuable tool for measuring the aggregate level of organic impurities in pharmaceutical water systems. A TOC test with a nominal limit of 500 ppb for USP Purified Water and WFI is a required test in the USP.

b) TOC meters are relatively sophisticated analytical instruments. The USP provides guidance on how to qualify an instrument and how to interpret the instrument results.

c) In addition to "continuous" monitoring of equipment performance and pharmaceutical water quality, On-Line TOC meters may be used for final quality assurance testing, thus eliminating the need for periodic laboratory analysis. When used for critical assurance testing of USP purified water and WFI, instrument precision, system suitability, test methodology and calibration procedures must meet USP requirements. Instruments used strictly for process control and monitoring are not subject to USP requirements.

d) TOC is often monitored at several locations in a pharmaceutical water system. Some examples are:

  • Feed water monitoring can detect seasonal or unanticipated quality changes that could impact pretreatment equipment operation or the potential for resin or membrane fouling.
  • Downstream of carbon filters, organic scavengers, RO units, and UV lights can verify proper equipment operation and provide advance warning of bed exhaustion, compromised membranes, or the need for lamp replacement.
  • Final treatment step monitoring of pharmaceutical water verifies acceptable quality prior to delivery to a storage tank. TOC meters are often installed in the return piping of distribution loops downstream of the final point of use. Many systems include provisions for automatic diversion to drain or recirculation back through purification equipment when water quality is outside the acceptable range.

e) There has been much interest in the possible use of TOC analyzers to indicate endotoxin contamination. While this type of contamination will lead to higher TOC levels, there is no quantitative correlation to TOC levels. TOC results cannot substitute for microbial or endotoxin testing.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

TOC: the "general ledger" of organics:

Conductivity measures ions (inorganics), while TOC measures organic carbon. The two are complementary and form the two pillars of water quality monitoring. USP sets the TOC limit for PW and WFI at 500 ppb (0.5 mg/L). This is the modern method that replaced the older wet-chemistry Oxidizable Substances test.

Different requirements for the two uses:

  • For process monitoring: not bound by the USP instrument requirements; simpler equipment may be used
  • For final quality testing: must meet the USP requirements for instrument precision, system suitability, test method and calibration procedure

Key Notes

TOC and endotoxin: a common misunderstanding:

There has always been a wish in industry to use TOC to monitor endotoxin "indirectly". ISPE's position is clear: TOC cannot replace endotoxin testing.

  • Endotoxin is a lipopolysaccharide; it does contain carbon and does contribute to TOC
  • But the TOC reading includes all sources of organic carbon (detergent residues, resin leachables, piping materials, etc.), and cannot distinguish how much comes from endotoxin
  • There is no reliable quantitative correlation from which endotoxin concentration can be calculated from a TOC value

Conclusion: passing TOC ≠ passing endotoxin. The two tests must be performed independently.

CDMO Application

TOC auto-dump strategy:

ISPE mentions that many systems automatically divert to drain or back to the purification equipment when water quality is out of limits. In a CDMO, the design of this function needs special consideration:

  • The auto-dump trigger (setpoint) should be before the action limit, usually set at the alert limit
  • Diversion events must be recorded automatically, including time stamp, duration and trigger value
  • Frequent auto-dumps may point to a deeper problem in the system and require root cause investigation

9.3.3.3 pH

Original Text

a) pH measurement is relatively straightforward for high conductivity water. Reliable results can generally be obtained using pH indicators or laboratory, field, or On-Line pH meters.

b) Accurate pH measurement is difficult in many pharmaceutical waters due to the low conductivity. Low conductivity water is susceptible to pH swings due to contaminants introduced from the air, sample containers, and test equipment, as well as instrument difficulties associated with measuring low ionic strength solutions.

c) Common locations for On-Line pH measurement and control include:

  • Upstream of cellulose acetate RO membranes, where acid is injected to minimize membrane hydrolysis
  • Upstream of a degasifier, where acid is injected to increase CO2 removal

d) While pH limits for purified water and WFI are no longer specified in the USP, On-Line pH meters are rarely used for process control or for final quality assurance testing of pharmaceutical waters for several reasons:

  • Conductivity is a more sensitive measurement of overall ionic quality since changes in pH reflect logarithmic changes in water quality
  • A pH sensor's reference electrode contains a buffer solution that may leak through the reference electrode into the water being measured. To prevent contamination of the pharmaceutical water system, a pH sensor is installed in a side stream that flows to drain.
  • pH meters require frequent (daily in some cases) calibration with standard buffer solutions

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Why is pH so hard to measure in high-purity water?

The conductivity of high-purity water is extremely low (< 1.3 μS/cm), which means there are almost no ions in the water. Under these conditions:

  • CO2 from the air dissolves in the water to form carbonic acid, changing the pH instantly
  • Trace leachables from the surface of the sample container can affect the reading
  • The reference solution (buffer) of the pH electrode can leak into the water, both affecting the reading and contaminating the system

Sensitivity of conductivity vs pH: pH is a logarithmic scale: a change in pH from 7.0 to 6.0 means the H+ concentration has increased 10-fold. Conductivity, however, is linear and reflects small changes in ions more sensitively. This is why USP removed the pH specification for PW/WFI and uses conductivity as the main quality indicator.

Key Notes

Contamination risk of pH probes: ISPE specifically notes that the reference electrode of a pH sensor contains buffer solution that may leak into the water. This is not a theoretical risk: in actual cases, KCl buffer leaking from a pH probe has caused the conductivity of an entire formulation batch to exceed limits. Therefore:

  • Always install pH probes in a side stream that discharges to drain
  • Never return side stream water to the main system
  • Online pH meters are almost never installed in PW/WFI distribution loops

9.3.3.4 Ozone

Original Text

a) Dissolved ozone levels should be monitored in storage and distribution systems that utilize ozone for microbial control. Ozone levels can be determined periodically in the laboratory using several wet chemistry methods, or continuously using an On-Line analyzer.

b) For effective and safe system operation, ozone levels should be monitored at the following locations:

  • At the storage tank discharge to control operation of the ozone generator
  • Downstream of the UV light to ensure ozone destruction prior to water use
  • In loop return piping to ensure proper ozone levels are maintained during sanitization

c) Since Oxidation Reduction Potential (ORP) analyzers are nonspecific and unable to differentiate ozone from other oxidants, ORP analyzers should not be used for controlling ozone levels in pharmaceutical water systems.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Three key locations for ozone monitoring:

  • Tank outlet: controls the start/stop of the ozone generator, ensuring the tank maintains a sufficient residual ozone concentration
  • Downstream of the UV lamp: confirms that ozone has been completely destroyed. Residual ozone entering the process can oxidize product components and cause quality problems
  • Loop return line: confirms that the ozone sanitization effect is maintained at an effective concentration throughout the loop

Key Notes

ORP cannot replace a dedicated ozone analyzer!

ORP (oxidation-reduction potential) looks like a convenient substitute, but it cannot distinguish ozone from other oxidants (such as chlorine and hydrogen peroxide). In a pharmaceutical water system, if the feed water contains trace residual chlorine, the ORP reading will be "inflated", leading you to believe the ozone concentration is sufficient. ISPE explicitly prohibits using ORP to control ozone.

9.3.3.5 - 9.3.3.8 Flow, Temperature, Pressure, Level

Original Text

9.3.3.5 Flow

A wide variety of flow meters may be used in the feed water and pretreatment portion of a pharmaceutical water system including magnetic flow meters, mass flow meters, vortex shedding meters, and ultrasonic meters. All meters should be installed according to the manufacturer's instructions, to ensure proper operation.

Water flow rate (or velocity) may help to reduce microbial growth and maintain temperature within hot or cold systems. It is commonly verified upon startup, but not continuously monitored. Flow rate may vary. It may be monitored for information only.

9.3.3.6 Temperature

Temperature is often monitored and/or controlled at various locations to ensure optimum equipment operation and/or for microbial control. Temperature interlocks may be used to prevent damage to membranes, resins, or equipment if water temperatures drift outside allowable ranges.

In distribution systems where temperature is controlled or where heat sanitization is used, temperature is considered critical to ensure proper system operation or effective sanitization.

9.3.3.7 Pressure

Pressure may be monitored and controlled throughout the purification process to ensure optimum equipment operation. Monitoring differential pressure across filters indicates when backwashing or element replacement is needed. Differential pressure measurement across resin beds is useful in detecting resin fouling and poor flow distribution. Monitoring RO feed, interstage, permeate, and concentrate pressures provides early warning of membrane fouling and scaling. Back pressure control in distribution systems may be critical, if minimum pressures are required at points of use.

Pressure is not normally considered a critical parameter, however, the system should maintain positive pressure at all times. It may typically be monitored for information only.

9.3.3.8 Level

Various types of level measurement are used in the feed water and pretreatment portion of a pharmaceutical water system, including simple float switches, ultrasonic sensors, capacitance sensors, and differential pressure transmitters. The stub from the tank must be kept as short as possible to minimize deadlegs. Calibration of this type of transmitter is time consuming, since it requires filling the tank to verify proper operation. Tank nozzles with integral valves minimize deadlegs and allow calibration while the tank is in service.

Tank level may be monitored to control the supply of water into a tank and for control and protection of downstream pumps. In some instances, level may not normally be considered a critical parameter and may be monitored for information only. In these cases, it is usually not validated.

Tutorial Commentary

Analogy

Imagine you are managing the fire protection system of a building:

  • Flow is like the output of a fire hydrant: you do not need to measure it all the time, but you must confirm it during a fire drill
  • Temperature is like the freeze protection design of a sprinkler system: critical in cold regions, but not in the tropics
  • Pressure is like the water pressure in the pipes: as long as there is water it is fine, but if the pressure drops to zero the hydrant cannot be used
  • Level is like the water level in a water tower: as long as it is neither empty nor overflowing, the exact value matters less

Key Notes

The importance of positive pressure: Although ISPE says pressure is usually not a critical parameter, it specifically stresses that the system must "maintain positive pressure at all times". Why?

  • Negative pressure can draw outside air (and the microorganisms in it) into the system
  • Imperfectly sealed joints and gaskets can become pathways for microbial ingress under negative pressure
  • In particular, after water is drawn at a POU, without proper back-pressure control the end of the piping can momentarily go into negative pressure

Practical Tip: the challenge of level calibration

ISPE raises a very practical problem: calibrating a differential pressure level instrument requires filling the tank with water for verification, which is very time-consuming. Solutions:

  • Select a tank nozzle with an integral valve, so that calibration can be performed while the tank is in operation
  • Keep the stub as short as possible to reduce deadlegs
  • Ultrasonic or capacitance level instruments are usually easier to calibrate than differential pressure types

Key Concepts

Critical classification of the four physical parameters:

Note ISPE's wording: "may not be considered critical" and "monitored for information only". This means the Critical/Non-Critical classification of these parameters is not absolute and depends on your system design and microbial control strategy.

9.4 Design Conditions versus Operating Range

Original Text

The control system may recognize the distinction between design conditions and operating ranges, and the impact this distinction has upon validation and facility operation. These criteria are defined as:

  • Design Condition: the specified range, or accuracy, of a controlled variable used by the designer as a basis to determine the performance requirements for an engineered system.
  • Allowable Operating Range: the range of validated critical parameters within which acceptable water product can be produced.
  • Normal Operating Range: a range that may be selected as the desired acceptable values for a parameter during normal operations. This range must be within the Allowable Operating Range.

a) While it is desirable that a facility should meet all stated design conditions, the acceptability of the water system for operation from a cGMP standpoint depends on operating within the Allowable Operating Ranges.

b) Normal Operating Ranges cannot exceed the Allowable Operating Range for the product water. Design condition selection should reflect Good Engineering Practice.

c) It may be desirable to apply the concept of Alert and Action points along with Normal Operating Range. Alert levels are based on normal operating experience and are used to initiate investigations or corrective measures, before reaching an Action level. Action levels are defined as the level at which some corrective action must be taken to avoid jeopardizing water quality.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The nested relationship of the three tiers:

Design Condition: the widest
↓
Allowable Operating Range: validated and confirmed
↓
Normal Operating Range: the narrowest

Key differences:

  • Design Condition: the basis on which the engineers designed the system, including a safety margin. The range in which the equipment "can" operate
  • Allowable Operating Range: the range within which validation has demonstrated that acceptable water can be produced. This is the criterion for cGMP compliance
  • Normal Operating Range: the target range for daily operation, which must lie within the Allowable Range

Key Notes

Use of Alert vs Action Levels:

  • Alert Level: set from normal operating experience. When triggered, it starts an investigation but does not necessarily require production to stop. Equivalent to a "yellow light"
  • Action Level: corrective action must be taken. It may require stopping water supply, diverting to drain, or recirculating for reprocessing. Equivalent to a "red light"

In practice, Alert/Action Levels should be set from the historical data collected during Phase 1-3 validation, not by feel. A common approach is to use statistical methods (such as mean + 2σ as Alert and mean + 3σ as Action), but you must ensure the Action Level still lies within the Allowable Operating Range.

Practice Questions

  1. Your tank temperature has a Design Condition of 80±5°C, an Allowable Range of 75-85°C and a Normal Range of 78-82°C. If the temperature drops to 74°C one day, what does that mean? What should you do?
  2. If the Alert Level and Action Level are set too close together, what operational problems will result?
  3. Why does ISPE say that "from a cGMP perspective, the acceptance criterion is the Allowable Operating Range" rather than the Design Condition?
9.5 Instrumentation Spikes

Original Text

"Spikes" may be experienced in the measurement of some parameters. These excursions may be the result of the measurement technique or sensor and may not be representative of the actual parameter value. If a spike occurs in a system with a significant physical lag or mass, the rapid changes in a parameter as evidenced by spikes may be physically impossible and consequently can be treated as instrumentation spikes.

In other cases, it may be decided to treat these spikes as Alert Level deviations based upon their frequency and duration even though their magnitude may exceed the Action level.

A procedure for defining and handling spikes should be developed in conjunction with Quality Assurance based on the specific water system.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Logic for identifying a spike:

ISPE provides a very practical, physics-based method of identification:

  • If the system has a large physical inertia (such as a 10,000 L tank), the temperature cannot jump from 80°C to 60°C in 1 second
  • If such a "physically impossible" step change appears in the data, it can be judged to be an instrument spike rather than a true process excursion
  • But on a low-volume, fast-responding line (such as conductivity at a POU outlet), the same step change may be real

Key Notes

Three key requirements for spike handling:

  1. There must be a written procedure (SOP): defining what a spike is, the criteria for identifying it and the handling process. It cannot be "explained" after the fact
  2. It must be developed jointly with QA: this is not a purely engineering decision and requires approval from Quality Assurance
  3. It must be based on the specific system design: each water system has different physical characteristics, so the criteria for identifying a spike also differ

The data integrity pitfall: Spike handling is one of the areas auditors focus on most. If your system frequently shows excursions "classified as spikes" without root cause investigation, you will be questioned on whether spike classification is being used to cover up real quality problems.

Analogy

Imagine taking your temperature. You put an ear thermometer in your ear and read 39.5°C. Measure again: 36.8°C. A third time: 36.7°C. The first 39.5°C reading was probably a "spike", perhaps because the probe was not fully aimed at the eardrum. But if you get three readings in a row above 38.5°C, you really do have a fever. The same applies to pharmaceutical water systems: occasional, physically implausible readings can be treated as spikes, but sustained excursions must be taken seriously.

21 CFR Part 11 Regulatory Connection

If your control system automatically filters or flags spike data, electronic record integrity is involved:

  • Raw data (including spikes) must be retained and must not be deleted
  • Any reclassification of data (for example from Action to Spike) must have an audit trail
  • The logic of the filtering algorithm must be validated
  • This is an item frequently checked in data integrity audits
9.6 Control Systems

9.6.1 Level of Automation

Original Text

Selection of a control strategy for a pharmaceutical water system should consider feed water quality and reliability, the complexity of the purification and/or distribution system, labor costs, personnel skill levels and capabilities, and documentation and reporting requirements. Options for control include:

a) Local instrumentation with manual control: In this option, a combination of instrumentation, periodic samples, and visual examination is used to monitor critical process parameters. Data is collected and recorded manually, and analysis and trending capabilities are limited. Excursions of critical parameters outside acceptable ranges typically trigger local alarms to reduce the risk of unacceptable water quality. Satisfactory manual operation requires significant human intervention. This requires detailed operating procedures and conscientious documentation of critical quality parameters. This option has the lowest installed cost, but is very labor intensive and may be subject to human error.

b) Semi-automatic control: These systems use local operator control panels, relay logic control, local chart recorders and printers, and some manual data collection to monitor and control the water system. These systems are less labor intensive over the manual systems, but are still labor intensive due to the manual data collection and monitoring required to control the process.

c) Automatic control: Automated systems use a computer (PLC or DCS), or computers, to control the pharmaceutical water system. The computer system utilizes appropriate process monitoring instrumentation (conductivity probes, flow meters, etc.) to gather data and make appropriate adjustments to the system automatically. As water generation systems become more sophisticated, relying on human intervention to control and monitor the water system becomes more difficult and labor intensive. An automated system requires less operator involvement, but requires a more highly trained maintenance and engineering support staff.

d) Fully integrated systems: These systems include a fully automated system and a wide area network connected to other computer systems in the building or site. These systems allow for central site monitoring, automatic electronic data collection, centralized alarm monitoring with automatic recording, response, and report generation.

Additional information on control system design is available in the Good Automated Manufacturing Practice (GAMP) Guide and in various guidelines by the Instrument Society of America (ISA).

Whichever level of automation is selected, the validation effort should verify operation of the complete system, including vendor-supplied sub-systems.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Notes

The choice for a modern CDMO: In the 2020s, almost all newly built pharmaceutical water systems use Level 3 or Level 4. The reasons:

  • Data integrity requirements: the data integrity risk of manual records is too high; automated electronic records meet the expectations of 21 CFR Part 11
  • Higher audit expectations: customers and regulators expect to see complete electronic trend charts, automatic alarm records and audit trails
  • Increased complexity: modern water systems integrate multiple technologies (RO, EDI, UV, ozone, etc.), and manual operation is no longer practical
  • Labor cost: the cost of 24/7 manual monitoring is far higher than the cost of maintaining an automated system

CDMO Application

The practical value of a Level 4 integrated system:

In a CDMO, plant-wide Level 4 integration has additional advantages:

  • Water system data can be automatically linked to batch records, demonstrating water quality compliance during production
  • Remote monitoring lets the engineering team respond to alarms outside working hours
  • Centralized report generation greatly reduces the preparation time for Annual Product Review and customer audits

But note: the higher the level of integration, the larger the validation scope for 21 CFR Part 11 and GAMP 5.

Key Concepts

Comparison of the four automation levels:

9.6.2 Control System Software

Original Text

The software/control system may be used to measure, monitor, control, or record critical process parameters. Programming and design standards, especially concerning operator interface, control techniques, alarm handling, and interlock processing should be applied during the development, validation and maintenance phases of the project. The control system software consists of:

a) Firmware, Operating System and Application Software: This is software permanently loaded into memory that may or may not be accessible to the user. While the functions performed by the control system may be divided between critical and non-critical functions, it is impossible to divide or isolate the firmware, operating system, application software, and associated hardware functions. Therefore, if some of the functions of a control system are considered critical, all of the above software is considered critical, and should be validated.

b) User Configurable Software: The functions of the user configurable software may be defined as critical or non-critical. The critical functions or modules require enhanced documentation, including validation. In some cases, it may be impossible to divide or isolate software adequately. In such cases, if some of the functions are critical, it may be necessary to validate all the software.

The type of process control required is often the determining factor in the type of software needed, and software requirements often define the type of system selected. Major considerations are:

  • Number of I/O points
  • Mathematical or statistical functions required
  • Reporting features required (particularly if the control system is to be further integrated into higher systems)
  • Whether or not advanced control techniques are required (e.g., neural nets; fuzzy logic controllers; adaptive gain; dead-time compensation)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The "joint responsibility" principle of software validation:

ISPE sets out an important validation principle: if any function of the control system is considered critical, the underlying firmware, OS and application software must all be considered critical and validated. Why?

  • A firmware bug can affect all higher-level functions
  • An OS crash will cause all critical and non-critical functions to fail at the same time
  • The execution paths of critical and non-critical functions cannot be completely isolated at the software level

This principle directly affects the scope and cost of validation: you cannot validate only "the functions used"; you must validate the whole platform.

21 CFR Part 11 Regulatory Connection

Control system software and electronic record regulations:

When a control system is used to record critical process parameters, the requirements of 21 CFR Part 11 apply:

  • Audit Trail: any change to a setpoint, configuration or alarm setpoint must be recorded with a time stamp and the identity of the operator
  • Access Control: personnel at different levels (operators, engineers, managers) have different privileges
  • Electronic Signatures: critical operations (such as changing an alarm limit) may require an electronic signature
  • Data Backup: all electronic records must be backed up regularly and be fully recoverable when needed

Key Notes

GAMP 5 categories and water system control software:

The higher the category, the more stringent the validation requirements. The modern trend is to use Category 3-4 (off-the-shelf platform + configuration) wherever possible and reduce Category 5 (custom development) in order to lower the validation burden.

9.6.3 Control Hardware and Operation Interface

Original Text

a) Critical software requires enhanced documentation and should be designed and tested in accordance with the Lifecycle Methodology.

b) The water system, field instruments and control requirements all affect control hardware selection. Plant standards, or a large installed base of a particular system may drive the selection.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Lifecycle Methodology:

The Lifecycle Methodology cited by ISPE is the V-Model later defined in GAMP 5:

  • Left side (development): User Requirements → Functional Spec → Design Spec → Code/Config
  • Right side (verification): Unit Test → Integration Test → OQ → PQ
  • Each level of development documentation corresponds to a level of verification activity

Practical considerations in hardware selection: ISPE mentions that the "plant standard" and the "existing installed base" influence selection. This is especially important in a CDMO: if the whole plant uses Siemens PLCs, the water system should be standardized on them too, so the maintenance team can share skills and spare parts.

Practice Questions

  1. Your plant plans to build a new water system, and the IT department suggests using the latest Internet of Things (IoT) platform to replace the traditional SCADA. From a GMP validation perspective, how would you evaluate this suggestion?
  2. If the PLC program delivered by the vendor contains 50 function blocks, of which only 10 relate to critical parameters, can you validate only those 10? Why?
  3. When selecting the HMI (human-machine interface) for a water system, which 21 CFR Part 11 requirements will affect your design?

Chapter Summary

  • The core logic of Critical vs Non-Critical: a parameter that affects final water quality = Critical Parameter, and its instrument requires qualification; everything else needs only GEP commissioning. The documentation and field labeling must clearly distinguish the two.
  • Conductivity is the most important online monitoring parameter: USP final quality testing must use the non-temperature-compensated value plus the water temperature, judged against the USP table. Temperature-compensated values are suitable only for process monitoring. Bubbles are the biggest hazard in conductivity measurement.
  • TOC and conductivity are complementary: TOC measures organic carbon and conductivity measures ions. Together they give a complete picture of water quality. TOC cannot replace endotoxin testing.
  • The role of pH in high-purity water systems is limited: because it is less sensitive than conductivity, the probe carries a contamination risk and frequent calibration is needed, USP has removed the pH specification. It is mainly used for acid/base adjustment in the pretreatment section.
  • Design Condition ⊃ Allowable Operating Range ⊃ Normal Operating Range: the criterion for cGMP compliance is the Allowable Operating Range. Alert/Action Levels should be set from statistical analysis of historical data.
  • Spike handling requires a written procedure: the criteria for identification are based on physical plausibility and must be developed jointly with QA. Raw data (including spikes) must not be deleted, and reclassification requires an audit trail.
  • Level 3-4 automation is the mainstream for modern water systems: PLC/DCS automatic control with SCADA centralized monitoring. Control software validation follows the "joint responsibility" principle: the presence of any critical function means the entire software platform requires validation. 21 CFR Part 11 requirements apply to all electronic record functions.
