Chapter 10: Commissioning & Qualification

p157-p163

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p157-p163

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the special requirements for water system qualification documentation, and why water systems need "more attention than ordinary equipment"
  • Master the purpose, duration and sampling strategy differences of the three-phase sampling program (Phase 1 / 2 / 3)
  • Understand the logic behind setting chemical and microbiological acceptance criteria, including the concept of Alert / Action Limits
  • Know when qualification reports are written and how they are approved (interim report vs. final summary report)
  • Master the triggers for change control and requalification
  • Build a cross-reference to the ISPE Vol.5 C&Q framework

Chapter Contents

10.2 Documentation Requirements 10.3 Sampling Program 10.3.1 Phase 1 10.3.2 Phase 2 10.3.3 Phase 3 10.4 Acceptance Criteria 10.5 Qualification Reports 10.6 Change Control
10.2 System Qualification Documentation

Original Text

The system qualification documentation for water systems requires additional emphasis on certain areas. The documentation set should include:

  • A schematic drawing of the system (P&ID)
  • Written system specifications
  • Detailed design drawings
  • Vendor manuals and drawings
  • Field inspection and test reports
  • System qualification test results

Because of their critical impact on pharmaceutical production, water systems require additional emphasis on certain sections of this documentation. Specific design requirements for water and steam systems are contained within the body of this Guide. When compiling documentation related to water systems, particular attention should be paid to the following:

a) Schematic documentation may be enhanced by the inclusion of a system isometric diagram (or diagrams) indicating location and numbering of welds, relative elevations, slope of lines, and points of drainage.

b) The system specification should indicate performance criteria, as well as design parameters.

c) Field inspection and test reports should include cleaning and passivation procedure and record, weld parameter documentation and inspection reports, slope verification, and verification of the absence of "dead-legs".

d) System qualification tests may or may not be subject to a pre-approved protocol addressing qualification test requirements. In either case, test results should be reported in direct comparison to acceptance criteria derived from system design and operating specifications.

e) System qualification tests should include verification of all automated functions, specified temperature control, distribution system velocity, and initial water quality determination.

"Additional details regarding water system qualification may be found in the associated ISPE Baseline® Guide on Commissioning and Qualification."

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Why Water System Documentation Needs "Extra Attention"

Qualification documentation for ordinary equipment usually follows the standard IQ/OQ/PQ pattern, but water systems are special because:

  • Direct product contact: Water is the most widely used pharmaceutical raw material and cleaning solvent, so its effect on product quality is direct and broad
  • Continuously operating system: Unlike a single piece of equipment that can be verified batch by batch, a water system runs 24/7 and quality must be maintained at all times
  • Dynamic microbial risk: The microbial load in a water system changes with time, season and usage rate

The documentation therefore has to cover far more engineering detail (isometric drawings, weld records, slope verification, etc.), not just a simple pass/fail record.

Key Point: The Practical Value of the Isometric Diagram

The original text specifically says the isometric diagram must show:

  • Weld locations and numbers — every weld is traceable
  • Relative elevations — to confirm the piping drains properly
  • Pipe slope — to prevent standing water that becomes a breeding ground for microorganisms
  • Drain points — to confirm the system can be drained completely

In CDMO audits, inspectors often ask, "Take me to where this weld is." If the isometric diagram does not match the installation on site, it is a major observation.

Cross-Reference: ISPE Vol.5 C&Q Framework

The original text ends by saying "refer to the ISPE Commissioning and Qualification Baseline Guide for details", i.e. ISPE Vol.5. The framework provided in Vol.5 includes:

  • System classification (Direct / Indirect / No Impact) — determines the depth of qualification
  • Risk-assessment-driven testing — not every item needs GMP qualification
  • Leveraging Commissioning — using commissioning data as qualification evidence

In the Vol.5 framework, a water system is a Direct Impact System, so all qualification tests need full GMP documentation support. But the core message of Vol.5 is that good commissioning can greatly reduce duplicated work in the qualification phase.

Analogy

Water system qualification documentation is like the "as-built file" of a building. An ordinary house may need only basic electrical and fire safety inspection reports, but a hospital (the equivalent of a critical-grade water system) needs far more: X-ray weld inspection reports for every pipe run, balancing reports for the air-conditioning system, switchover test records for the emergency power... because when something goes wrong, patient safety is affected.

Practice Questions

  1. The original text lists six categories of documents. If you could finish only three first, which three would you choose, and why?
  2. The original text says qualification tests "may or may not be subject to a pre-approved protocol". In your company, do qualification tests use pre-approved protocols? What is the risk of not using them?
10.3 System Qualification Sampling Program

Original Text

The qualification of water systems is unique in that performance must be proven over an extended period of time, and is subject to variations in use rate and initial feed water quality. Therefore, the sampling program associated with pharmaceutical water systems validation is unique and specialized.

Extensive sampling is required to establish and confirm that the entire system will operate within specified operating ranges, to develop and evaluate the system operation and maintenance procedures, and to verify that the water produced and delivered to the points of use consistently meets the required quality specifications and acceptance criteria. This portion of the program is sometimes termed performance qualification.

Because of the critical impact that water has upon pharmaceutical quality, the sampling program and evaluation of results is usually subject to a pre-approved plan or protocol, with clearly defined acceptance criteria. Also included should be procedures to deal with deviations from specified parameters and analytical results.

The sampling program consists of three successive phases, each with a specific purpose and sampling scheme, as outlined below. The initial phase of the sampling program typically begins after the water system is shown to be fully operational, as demonstrated through integrated system testing in Operational Qualification.

The water generated during the various phases may be used for manufacturing as long as analytical results are acceptable. The intended applications and impact of water quality should be considered in determining how much data is required before use.

Table 10-1: Sampling Program

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Why Water System Qualification Takes "Time"

Most equipment qualification can be completed in days to weeks. But the whole water system qualification process takes more than a year, for three reasons:

  • Time dependence of microbial behavior: Biofilm takes weeks to months to form and mature
  • Seasonal variation in feed water quality: Microbial load is higher in summer, and low water temperature in winter affects system efficiency
  • Variation in usage patterns: Differences in water demand between peak and off-peak production seasons affect the residence time of water in the piping

So water system qualification is not "taking a snapshot" but "recording a full year of video" to prove that the system operates stably under all reasonable operating conditions.

Phase 1
2-4 weeks
Establish operating ranges
→
Phase 2
2-4 weeks
Demonstrate consistency
→
Phase 3
1 year
Long-term performance verification

Key Point: Can the Water Be Used for Production During the Phases?

The original text states clearly: water produced in each phase may be used for production, as long as the analytical results are acceptable. This is very important for a CDMO, because a new facility or new system that cannot produce during qualification = a whole year with no revenue.

In practice:

  • After Phase 1, if all results are acceptable, an interim report is usually issued to support "conditional use"
  • After Phase 2, formal approval is issued, allowing routine production use
  • Phase 3 is continued monitoring under normal production conditions

The key is that you must define in the protocol in advance "under what conditions use is allowed", rather than ratifying it after the fact.

Analogy

Imagine opening a new restaurant. Phase 1 is the soft opening: testing the menu, adjusting workflows, training the team. Phase 2 is limited opening (by invitation): proving you can serve consistently with consistent quality. Phase 3 is the first full year of operation: only after getting through the summer peak, the winter slow season and the Lunar New Year rush can you truly say "our quality is stable."

10.3.1 Phase 1 — Intensive Monitoring

Original Text

The purpose of this phase is to establish appropriate operating ranges and provide data for the development of cleaning and sanitization procedures and frequencies. Sampling should be performed after each step in the treatment process and from each point of use. In addition, the incoming feed water to the water system should be tested and verified to comply with the relevant "Drinking Water" regulations. The FDA Guide to Inspections of High Purity Water Systems suggests daily sampling for two to four weeks, but recognizes that other sampling programs may be acceptable.

In devising the sampling scheme, consideration should be given to the system configuration, maintenance cycles, how the water is drawn for use, and the expected or potential variation in chemistry and microbiological attributes, at each potential sample point. In treatment, chemistry testing is specific for each processing step and microbiological testing between each component is important to determine the microbial load and the component's ability to manage the load.

At the end of this phase, the SOPs for system operation and maintenance should be developed and approved for continued interim use during the next phase. System logs, documentation for critical parameters (e.g., conductivity and TOC data, sanitization data, etc.), and responses to critical alarms or action levels should be reviewed, to verify the appropriate procedures are in place. In addition, the process that will be followed to investigate a confirmed test failure should be developed at this time. The intent of this process is to assess whether a failure is localized (i.e., isolated to a specific port) or systematic, and to define how different types of failures will be handled.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Three Outputs of Phase 1

Phase 1 is not just "sampling"; it must produce three key results:

  • Operating ranges: System operating parameters based on actual data rather than design assumptions
  • Initial SOPs: Standard operating procedures for operation, cleaning and sanitization, verified in actual use
  • Deviation investigation process: The decision logic for distinguishing a "localized problem" from a "systematic problem"

Key Point: Every Point of Use Must Be Sampled

The original text stresses sampling from each point of use. In a large water system this means:

  • A typical CDMO WFI loop may have 20-40 points of use
  • Sampling every point daily = 20-40 samples per day
  • Over 2-4 weeks = 280-1,120 samples

This is a very large workload and cost. The sampling program must therefore be designed to balance "statistical validity" against "practical feasibility".

CDMO Practice: Localized vs. Systematic Failure

The original text requires a deviation investigation process to be established before the end of Phase 1, distinguishing between:

  • Localized problems: for example, a contaminated valve at one specific point of use — only that point needs to be cleaned and its sampling frequency increased
  • Systematic problems: for example, microbial counts rising across the entire loop — the whole system may need to be sanitized, or even taken out of service

The CDMO pain point: customer products are on the production line, and if a systematic problem is judged to require shutdown and sanitization, the revenue loss and the impact on customer relationships are significant. The logic of this investigation process must therefore be established in Phase 1, not decided ad hoc after a problem occurs.

Practice Questions

  1. The original text says FDA recommends daily sampling for 2-4 weeks, but also acknowledges that "other sampling programs may be acceptable". If you were to design an "alternative program", what would your scientific rationale be?
  2. At the end of Phase 1, if microbial results at some sampling points are high but still within specification, how would you handle it? Proceed directly to Phase 2, or extend Phase 1?
10.3.2 Phase 2 — Consistent Operation

Original Text

The second phase is intended to demonstrate that the system consistently operates within pre-determined operating ranges and delivers water of the required quality when operated in accordance with the SOPs. The FDA Guide suggests that the sampling scheme and duration should be the same as for phase one. During phases 1 and 2, multiple samples should be taken from each point of use. Sampling methodology should be representative of the way water will be used. For example, sampling should not involve a lengthy preliminary purge if water usage will be direct and immediate. If water is used through an attached hose, then the sample should be taken from the hose.

It is recommended that each point be sampled at least once per week as a minimum. In this manner, localized contamination may be discovered. (Note that too frequent sampling of little used points may mask incipient localized microbial growth by artificial purging.) Phase 2 allows the gathering of sufficient data to establish microbial alert and action limits (see Section 10.4).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Core Requirement of Phase 2 — "Consistency"

Phase 1 showed that "the system can produce acceptable water"; Phase 2 must show that "the system produces acceptable water every time". The difference is like this:

  • Phase 1: "This pitcher can throw a 150 km/h pitch"
  • Phase 2: "This pitcher can throw 150 km/h pitches in every game"

Key Point: The Sampling Method Must "Represent Actual Use"

This is a principle that is often overlooked but extremely important. The original text gives two examples:

  • Do not pre-flush for a long time: If in actual use the valve is simply opened and the water used directly, sampling should also be done directly, not after flushing for 5 minutes. Otherwise you are collecting "water after flushing", not "the water the user actually gets".
  • Sample from the end of the hose: If a hose is attached to the point of use, sample from the end of the hose, because the hose itself may be a source of contamination.

Hidden Trap: Over-Sampling Can Mask Problems

The original text contains a very important warning that is easily missed:

"Too frequent sampling of little used points may mask incipient localized microbial growth by artificial purging."

This means that if a point of use is rarely used (for example, a standby line), frequent sampling amounts to frequent flushing, which washes away biofilm that is starting to form. The sampling results look good, but the problem is actually being masked.

CDMO recommendation: for low-usage points of use, the sampling frequency should simulate the actual usage frequency, rather than sampling every day to make the data "look good".

Alert Limit vs. Action Limit

Another key output of Phase 2 is the establishment of Alert / Action Limits:

  • Alert Limit: An early warning of a trend deviation, usually triggering increased sampling frequency and investigation
  • Action Limit: Close to the specification boundary, triggering corrective action (sanitization, repair, etc.)

These limits should be established from a statistical analysis of actual data from Phase 1 and Phase 2, rather than simply quoting USP or industry convention.

10.3.3 Phase 3 — Extended Performance

Original Text

The third phase is intended to demonstrate that, when operated for an extended time period (typically one year), the system produces and delivers water of the required quality, despite possible seasonal variations of the feed water. Sample locations, frequencies, and test requirements are based on the established procedures.

For WFI systems, the FDA Guide recommends sampling daily from a minimum of one point of use, with all points of use tested weekly. At the end of this phase (i.e., after a full year of testing), the validation is considered completed. In most cases, ongoing monitoring will establish a continuing record of water quality.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: One Year = All Four Seasons Covered

Phase 3 lasts one year. The purpose is not "another year of samples" but to make sure the system goes through all possible environmental changes:

  • Spring: Snowmelt degrades feed water quality (more organics and microorganisms)
  • Summer: High temperatures promote microbial growth, and warmer feed water affects RO efficiency
  • Autumn: Falling leaves affect surface water quality
  • Winter: Low temperatures affect system efficiency, but microbial risk is lower

The situation in Taiwan is similar: the plum rain season (high feed water turbidity), the typhoon season (possible disruption of municipal water supply) and high summer temperatures are all variable conditions that must be covered.

Key Point: Sampling Frequency Requirements for WFI

For WFI systems, FDA's recommended sampling frequency is:

  • Daily: Sample from at least one point of use
  • Weekly: All points of use must be sampled

Note that this is the frequency for Phase 3 (long-term monitoring) and is already lower than in Phase 1/2. For Purified Water (PW) systems, the guide does not have such strict requirements, which reflects the critical effect of WFI on injectable product quality.

Analogy

Phase 3 is like the "first-year warranty" of a new car. The manufacturer has you drive it for a year in all kinds of road, weather and load conditions, and only if the car performs normally throughout the year is its quality truly confirmed. Likewise, a water system has to be tested through spring, summer, autumn and winter before you can say "validation is complete".

CDMO Practice: Commercial Operations During Phase 3

The good news is that Phase 3 is usually carried out under normal production conditions and needs no additional downtime. But note:

  • Deviations (OOS/OOT) during Phase 3 still require formal investigation and handling
  • If a major system change occurs during Phase 3, you may need to reassess whether to "restart" Phase 3
  • The final Phase 3 report is the key document for the complete validation of the water system, and inspectors will always look at it
10.4 Acceptance Criteria

Original Text

Acceptance criteria for water are dependent upon its use. For both Purified Water and WFI, the chemical acceptance criteria are clearly described in the US Pharmacopoeia (USP). It is expected that a well designed water system, operating within specified design parameters will consistently be able to meet these criteria. Therefore, failures in chemical analysis during phases 1 and 2 must be investigated, the reason for failure corrected, and (except where errors in sampling or laboratory error are clearly indicated) the sampling phase extended to re-establish consistency of performance.

Microbial quality is not specified by the USP, but is established by the user based upon water use. The USP does recommend action limits for the different waters in its General Information chapter. These are 10 CFU/100ml for WFI and 100 CFU/ml for Purified Water. These may be employed as initial acceptance criteria for system qualification, although some flexibility is allowable, depending upon system design and use.

It is permissible that a single excursion, followed by acceptable re-sampling would not constitute a failure. In addition, because of the inherently bacteriostatic nature of WFI production and distribution systems, it should be expected that the large majority of samples should be well below the initial acceptance criterion. Therefore, for WFI it is prudent to establish a sample average acceptance criterion, which will be below the limit for a single sample. Failure investigation would be handled similarly to chemical analysis failure.

During phases 1 and 2, normal system microbial limits may be established. Acceptance criteria may then be converted to alert and action limits for use during phase 3 and beyond. These would take into account repeated excursions from the norm as well as step increases in micro count.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Point: The "Sample Average" Acceptance Criterion

The original text presents a very practical concept: for WFI systems, in addition to limits for individual samples, a sample average acceptance criterion should be established.

The logic is that a WFI system (usually circulating at 80°C or above, or using distillation) is inherently bacteriostatic. If the system is designed and operated properly, most sample results should be well below 10 CFU/100 ml. If the average is close to the limit, the system still has a problem even if every individual sample "passes".

Suggested approach:

  • Action Limit: 10 CFU/100 ml (single sample)
  • Alert Limit: 1-3 CFU/100 ml (based on statistical analysis of actual data)
  • Average target: <1 CFU/100 ml (expected performance of a system operating normally)

Important Point: The Seriousness of Chemical Failures

The original text states clearly that a chemical analysis failure is more serious than a microbiological failure. The reasons are:

  • A "well-designed" water system should not have chemical failures
  • A chemical failure indicates a fundamental problem in system design or operation
  • The root cause must be found and corrected, then the sampling period extended to demonstrate consistency again

By comparison, an occasional high microbial result may be caused by sampling technique, environmental factors and so on, and a single excursion followed by acceptable resampling is acceptable.

Analogy

Chemical parameters are like a person's "blood pressure": in a well-built body (system), blood pressure should always be normal. An occasional high reading may be nerves, but persistently high readings point to a systemic problem. Microbial parameters are more like "the number of colds": even a very healthy person may catch an occasional cold (a sporadic microbial excursion), but if it happens often you need to check the immune system (a systematic problem).

Practice Questions

  1. During Phase 1 of your WFI system, one point of use gives a microbial result of 5 CFU/100 ml on two consecutive days (below the 10 CFU/100 ml limit but clearly higher than the <1 CFU/100 ml at other points). How would you handle it?
  2. The original text says an "extended sampling phase" is needed when a chemical failure occurs. If a conductivity failure occurs in week 3 of Phase 2 and is later confirmed to be caused by a contaminated sample bottle, do you still need to extend Phase 2? Why?
  3. Your customer requires the microbial limit for the PW system to be 10 CFU/ml (10 times stricter than the 100 CFU/ml recommended by USP). How would you evaluate the reasonableness of this requirement?
10.5 Qualification Reports

Original Text

Qualification data should be compiled and conclusions written into a summary report. This is to be reviewed and approved by those responsible for operation and quality assurance of the water system.

An interim report should be written and approved at such time during the qualification sampling program, as it is desired to use water in production activities.

A summary report should be prepared at the conclusion of phase 2, periodic updates provided throughout phase 3 and a major update issued at the conclusion of phase 3.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Point: The Importance of the Interim Report

The interim report is a key document for CDMO operations. Without it, you cannot use the water for production during qualification. It should include:

  • Compilation and statistical analysis of all Phase 1 data
  • Pass/fail determination for all test items
  • Any deviations and their investigation conclusions
  • Restrictions on conditional use (if applicable)
  • Confirmation of the sampling plan for the subsequent Phase 2/3

This report must be reviewed and approved by QA. Only when inspectors see this document will they accept that production batches made during qualification were legitimate.

CDMO Practical Recommendations

It is recommended to include trend analysis charts in the periodic updates during Phase 3:

  • Time trend charts of microbial counts (plotted separately for each point of use)
  • Trend charts of conductivity and TOC
  • Comparison with seasonal changes in feed water quality
  • Time markers for sanitization/maintenance events

These charts are not only part of the qualification documentation but also the basic data for the water system section of the later Annual Product Quality Review (APR/PQR).

Key Concept: The Timeline of Qualification Reports

Water system qualification reports are not "write one and done" but a series of documents:

10.6 Change Control and Requalification

Original Text

Changes to the system must be assessed with regard to potential impact of the change on the entire system. Required action would be determined based on that assessment. It may involve extensive re-qualification, localized increase in sampling frequency, or inclusion in the routine monitoring program.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Point: Impact Assessment of the "Whole System"

The original text specifically stresses assessing the impact of a change on the whole system, not just the point of change. The reason is that a water system is a continuously circulating closed system:

  • Replacing a component upstream may change the water quality of the whole loop
  • Adding a point of use may change the flow distribution of the whole system
  • Adjusting the sanitization frequency affects microbial control of the entire piping

This differs from change control for ordinary equipment: changing the tooling on a tablet press affects only that machine, but replacing a section of water system piping may affect every product that uses that water.

Cross-Reference: ISPE Vol.5 Change Control

Chapter 8 (Change Management) of the ISPE Vol.5 C&Q Guide provides a more complete change control framework:

  • Change classification: Like-for-Like / Minor / Major / Critical
  • Risk assessment tool: Impact Assessment Matrix
  • Depth of requalification: A spectrum from "document review" to "full revalidation"

It is recommended that the water system change control SOP refer directly to the Vol.5 framework and add specific assessment criteria for the particularities of water systems (continuous circulation, microbial risk).

CDMO Special Consideration: Customer Communication

Change control for a CDMO water system has an additional dimension: customer notification. A major change to the water system may affect the products of all customers using it, so:

  • The Quality Technical Agreement (QTA) should clearly define the scope of notification for water system changes
  • High-impact changes may need customer approval before implementation
  • Requalification data after a change may need to be provided to customers as supporting documentation for their own product changes

Practice Questions

  1. Your WFI system needs its RO membranes replaced (from brand A to brand B, same specification but different material). Which impact level would you assign, and what degree of requalification is needed?
  2. The production team asks to add a new point of use on the WFI loop (for a new customer's filling line). Besides the physical piping installation, what qualification and monitoring matters do you need to consider?
  3. Your feed water (municipal water) supplier notifies you that next month they will change the disinfectant (from chlorine to chloramine). What are the potential effects on your water system? How should your change control process respond?

Key Concept: Three Levels of Change Impact

Although the original text is only a short paragraph, it contains the core framework of change control. Based on the impact assessment, every change should map to one of three actions:

Chapter Summary

Key Takeaways

  • Documentation: Water system qualification requires enhanced documentation including isometric diagrams, weld records, passivation records, and slope verification — beyond standard IQ/OQ/PQ packages.
  • Three-Phase Sampling: Phase 1 (2-4 weeks) establishes operating ranges; Phase 2 (2-4 weeks) demonstrates consistency; Phase 3 (1 year) confirms long-term performance across seasonal variations.
  • Acceptance Criteria: Chemical criteria from USP are absolute; microbial criteria (10 CFU/100ml WFI, 100 CFU/ml PW) are user-established based on USP recommendations, with alert/action limits derived from actual system data.
  • Qualification Reports: A series of reports — interim, summary, periodic updates, and final — are required throughout the multi-phase qualification program.
  • Change Control: Every change must be assessed for system-wide impact, with response ranging from full requalification to inclusion in routine monitoring.

Instructor Summary

Core Message of This Chapter

The essence of water system qualification is "using time and data to prove the system's reliability". It is not a one-time pass/fail test but a process of accumulating evidence that lasts more than a year.

For a CDMO, this means:

  • New facility planning: The water system qualification timeline (>1 year) must be built into the overall schedule for starting up the new facility
  • Daily operations: After Phase 3 is complete, continued monitoring is your "insurance" — using ongoing data to prove the system has stayed in control
  • Customer trust: A complete three-phase qualification package plus continuing trend monitoring data is the strongest evidence of water system control for customers and inspectors

Links to Other ISPE Guides

  • ISPE Vol.5 (C&Q Guide): Provides the complete framework for system classification, risk assessment and leveraging commissioning. As a Direct Impact System, a water system is subject to the strictest qualification requirements.
  • Earlier chapters of ISPE Vol.4: System design decisions (Chapters 3-7) directly affect the qualification strategy. For example, a hot loop vs. an ambient loop determines the sanitization frequency and the microbial control strategy.
  • FDA Guide to Inspections of High Purity Water Systems: The FDA guide cited several times in this chapter, which offers a more specific inspection perspective.

Comprehensive Exercise

Suppose you are the engineering/quality head of a CDMO. The company is building a new WFI system that will start supplying a new aseptic filling line in 6 months. Complete the following tasks:

  1. Draft a schedule for the three-phase sampling program, marking the start conditions, sampling frequency and exit criteria for each phase
  2. Define preliminary chemical and microbiological acceptance criteria and explain your rationale
  3. List the delivery schedule for the qualification reports, marking who must review/approve each report
  4. Design a decision matrix for change control: list 5 common water system change scenarios and define the impact level and corresponding requalification requirements for each

Learning Objectives

  1. Understand the USP three-stage conductivity testing methodology and temperature-dependent limits
  2. Describe TOC measurement principles, oxidation methods (conversion to CO2), and CO2 detection techniques

11.1 USP Regulated Water Quality

Original Text

11.1.1 USP Three-Stage Conductivity Levels

In order to set the maximum allowable conductivity, USP determined the limit concentration of the least conductive water attribute species used in USP 22 to be chloride at 0.47 ppm and 1.01 µS/cm at the standard temperature. When actual measurements of conductivity are evaluated at temperatures above the standard 25°C, the least conductive water attributes between pH 5 and 7, changes to favor ammonia at the maximum acceptable concentration of 0.3 ppm, using chloride as the electro-neutrality-balancing counter ion.

Stage 1 — In-Line Test

Primarily intended as an in-line test. Measure water conductivity and temperature using a non-compensated conductivity sensor using a suitable container or an in-line measurement.

Stage 2 — Off-Line Grab Sample

If the sample fails the Stage 1 test, adjust the temperature to 25°C and stir. If the sample stabilizes to the minimum conductivity value listed in the Stage 3 table, the water meets the requirements.

Stage 3 — Laboratory Test

Measure pH within 5 minutes of the Stage 2 conductivity reading after increasing its ionic strength to allow a pH reading, using saturated potassium chloride solution at 3%.

Commentary

Three-Stage Conductivity Testing Framework

Core concept: why three stages?

USP designed the three-stage test to balance efficiency and accuracy:

  • Stage 1 (in-line): a quick screen; more than 90% of samples pass at this stage
  • Stage 2 (off-line): the sample is normalized to 25°C to remove the effect of temperature
  • Stage 3 (laboratory): adds a pH measurement to account for the effect of alkalinity on conductivity

Real-life analogy

Think of three airport security checkpoints: the first is the metal detector (quick pass-through), the second is the handheld scanner (further confirmation), and the third is the bag check (full inspection). Most travelers clear the first checkpoint.

Practical points

  • Stage 1 uses a non-temperature-compensated sensor. This is deliberate, because the limits are already set by temperature band
  • Stage 3 is needed only if Stage 2 fails; not every sample has to go through all three stages
  • The pH measurement in Stage 3 must be completed within 5 minutes of the conductivity reading to avoid CO2 absorption affecting the result

11.1.3 In-Line (Stage 1) Conductivity Limits

Based on the chloride-ammonia model at the least conductive water attributes between pH 5 and 7.

Interpreting the Conductivity Limits Table

Relationship between temperature and conductivity

The higher the water temperature, the greater the ion mobility and the higher the conductivity. The USP limits relax as temperature rises:

  • At 0°C the limit is only 0.6 µS/cm
  • At 25°C the limit is 1.3 µS/cm (the standard reference point)
  • At 100°C the limit relaxes to 3.1 µS/cm

Common pitfalls

  • An in-line conductivity of 2.5 µS/cm in a hot water loop (80°C) is acceptable. Do not judge it against the 25°C limit
  • The 75-94.9°C range is combined into one band (2.7 µS/cm), because the limit changes little in this high-temperature range
  • In practice, a well-run system usually reads far below the limit

Stage 2 & 3 supplement

Stage 2: the sample is adjusted to 25°C and measured; the limit is 2.1 µS/cm.

Stage 3: pH is measured at 25°C and the result is checked against the pH-conductivity table. This stage accounts for the effect of dissolved atmospheric CO2.

11.1.5-11.1.6 Total Organic Carbon (TOC)

Original Text

11.1.5 TOC Requirements

TOC is an indirect measure, as carbon, of organic molecules present in high purity water. A TOC limit was determined by USP to be 0.5 ppm or 500 ppb, based on the results of studies and an industry wide survey of pharmaceutical water systems.

Instruments are available for measuring TOC in-line from slip streams and from grab samples. USP have not prevented acceptable technologies from being used, but limit the methods to instruments that are capable of:

  • Completely oxidizing the organic molecules to carbon dioxide (CO2)
  • Measuring the CO2 levels as carbon
  • Discriminating between Inorganic Carbon (IC) and CO2 from organic oxidation
  • Maintaining an equipment limit of detection of 0.05 mg/l or lower
  • Periodically demonstrating equipment "suitability"
Commentary

TOC Measurement Basics

What is TOC?

Total Organic Carbon is an indirect indicator of the organic content of water. The USP limit is 500 ppb.

Basic principle: the organic matter in the water is fully oxidized to CO2, and the CO2 concentration is measured and converted back to carbon content.

TC = TOC + IC (Total Carbon = organic carbon + inorganic carbon)

Real-life analogy

TOC measurement is like burning rubbish to weigh it: you burn all the organic matter to CO2 and measure how much CO2 there is, which tells you how much organic matter you started with. But you must first subtract the CO2 that was already in the water (inorganic carbon) to get the true organic carbon content.

Why the detection limit matters

USP requires an instrument detection limit of ≤0.05 mg/L (50 ppb), which is 1/10 of the 500 ppb limit. In pharmaceutical water, IC is usually very low and does not need to be specifically removed.

11.1.6 Types of TOC Analyzers

Oxidation Methods

CO2 Detection Methods

Four CO2 detection methods are currently used:

  • NDIR (Non-Dispersive Infrared) — measures IR absorption of CO2 in gas phase; dynamic range ~1.5-2 orders of magnitude; LOD 2-10 ppb
  • CO2 Selective Conductivity — uses special membrane to diffuse CO2 into DI water collector; calibration stability 6-12 months; LOD 0.05 ppb
  • Direct Conductivity — measures conductivity of sample directly; requires water to be CO2/DI water equilibrium only; LOD ~0.05 ppb
  • Differential Conductivity — pre/post oxidation difference; partially oxidizes organics to organic acids stage

Comparison of TOC Analyzer Technologies

Four oxidation methods

  • High-temperature combustion (>500°C): the most aggressive and thorough method, suited to unknown samples, but it requires compressed oxygen
  • Heated persulfate: thermal energy generates strongly oxidizing radicals and can handle large molecules
  • UV-promoted persulfate: activated by ultraviolet light; can measure high TOC concentrations of 50-100 ppm
  • UV light alone (<195 nm): no added chemicals needed! Best suited to on-line monitoring, but with a lower upper limit (~250 ppb without oxygen, ~2000 ppb with oxygen)

Selection guide

  • Continuous on-line monitoring: UV light alone is the simplest (no chemicals needed) and suits pharmaceutical water
  • Laboratory analysis: high-temperature combustion or persulfate methods, which have a wider measuring range
  • Persulfate methods have difficulty oxidizing large particles (>30 µm)

Comparison of CO2 Detection Methods

11.1.7 Limit Response & System Suitability Testing

Apparatus Requirements:

  • Reagent Water (rW): complying with USP definition, TOC ≤ 0.25 mg/L
  • Standard Solution (rS): containing 1.19 mg of sucrose
  • System Suitability Solution (rSS): containing 1,4-Benzoquinone RS (0.5 mg/L carbon)

Instrument Standardization (Limit Response Test):
The test solution meets the requirement if:

rt < (rS − rW)

Instrument Suitability (Response Efficiency):
An instrument is "suitable" if its response efficiency is within ±15% of theoretical:

100 × [(rSS − rW) / (rS − rW)] = 100% ± 15%

For in-line testing, standardization once per operating day may be appropriate. Suitability tests may range from each sample test (off-line) to once per month (in-line).

Limit Response and System Suitability Testing

Two key formulas

Limit Response test:

r_t < (r_S - r_W)
Test sample response < (sucrose standard response - reagent water response)

Suitability test:

100 x [(r_SS - r_W) / (r_S - r_W)] = 100% +/- 15%
The benzoquinone response efficiency must be between 85% and 115%

Why sucrose and benzoquinone?

Sucrose: very easy to oxidize; serves as the "best case" standard.

1,4-Benzoquinone: harder to oxidize; used to test whether the instrument can handle "difficult" organic compounds. If the instrument's response efficiency for benzoquinone is within ±15%, it can oxidize organics of all kinds effectively.

On-line vs. off-line testing frequency

  • Standardizing an on-line instrument requires disconnecting it from the water system, which is costly
  • Standardization once a day is acceptable; the suitability test can be performed monthly
  • Some TOC instruments have built-in automated testing functions, which can save a great deal of time and cost

11.1.8 USP Microbial and Endotoxin Testing

Microbial and Endotoxins are traditionally sampled at the points of use in a water system.

11.1.9 USP pH Testing

pH testing to support in-line conductivity testing was eliminated in May 1998.

Calibration of pH instruments: See Chapter 2, Section 2.3 USP Regulated Water Quality.

Dual Purpose TOC/Conductivity Meters

A number of TOC meters designed for in-line applications include conductivity measurements. To use this dual purpose instrument, the conductivity cell must be separately calibrated with a NIST-traceable precision resistor accurate to ±0.1%.

Microbial, Endotoxin, and pH Testing

Microbial and endotoxin sampling

Samples must be taken at the point of use (POU), because microorganisms can proliferate at the end of the piping. A sample taken only at the point of generation cannot represent the whole system.

Evolution of pH testing

Since May 1998, USP has dropped the requirement for pH testing to support in-line conductivity testing. The reasons:

  • The pH of high-purity water is extremely difficult to measure accurately
  • The three-stage conductivity test already covers the effect of pH
  • EP still retains a separate pH test requirement

Dual-purpose TOC/conductivity instruments

More and more on-line TOC instruments also measure conductivity. To meet USP requirements, the conductivity cell must be calibrated independently, using NIST-traceable precision resistors (accuracy ±0.1%).

11.2 European Perspective

Original Text

11.2.1 Regulatory Structure

The pharmaceutical industry within the EU is regulated by the European Agency for the Evaluation of Medicinal Products (EMEA), based in London. EMEA has three major functions:

  • Centralized drug approval — granting marketing authorization across Europe; mandatory for biotech products, optional for others
  • Supervision — continued use of medicinal products, including site inspections and pharmacovigilance
  • Coordination — European and international harmonization (ICH framework)

Within Member States, each nation has a national agency (e.g., MCA in UK, BfArM in Germany). The Pharmaceutical Inspection Convention (PIC) has been superseded by the Pharmaceutical Inspection Cooperation Scheme (PIC/S).

11.2.2 Regulations Governing Water and Steam

The EC recognizes the European Pharmacopoeia (EP) as the source of compendial water standards. The EP does not regulate Clean Steam. In Europe, chemical quality of steam for sterilization is governed by European Standard EN 285. UK guidance on steam quality is found in HTM-2010 and HTM-2031.

Commentary

European Regulatory Framework

European medicines regulatory system

Europe has a two-tier structure of central (EMEA) + national authorities:

  • EMEA handles centralized approval across Europe (mandatory for biotech products)
  • National authorities handle domestic registration and site inspections
  • PIC/S provides non-binding inspection guidance and promotes harmonization

Regulatory sources for water and steam

  • Water quality standards: EP (European Pharmacopoeia)
  • Clean steam quality: EN 285 + HTM-2010/2031
  • EP does not specify clean steam, which is an important difference between Europe and the US
  • The EU cGMP annex only requires that steam quality be "appropriate" and free of contaminants

11.2.3 Water Issues

  • While USP requires starting point must meet national drinking water standard, EP requires only that the history of the starting water be documented.
  • EP allows production of WFI by distillation only. Some pharmacopoeias allow WFI by reverse osmosis.
  • EP requires sample sizes of 100 ml for purified water and 500 ml for WFI. USP is not definitive on sample sizes.
  • EP has not accepted substitution of conductivity/resistivity for traditional wet chemistry analyses.
  • EP has not added the test for TOC.
  • EP continues to require the test for pH as an independent test.
  • European inspectors are sensitive to the drainability of systems through points of use.

11.2.4 Steam Issues

  • HTM-2010 emphasizes dryness requirements. EN-285 requires dryness value ≥ 0.9 (0.95 for metal loads).
  • Superheat concern: superheated steam is less effective for sterilization; may result from adiabatic expansion.
  • Non-Condensable Gases (NCGs): air and CO2 from failure to degas feed water; can create pockets preventing steam penetration.

Key Differences: EP vs. USP

Three key concerns for steam quality

  1. Dryness: ≥0.9 (≥0.95 for metal loads). Steam that is too wet leaves porous loads damp; steam that is "too dry" may be superheated
  2. Superheat: superheated steam has poor sterilizing effect. It can be prevented by controlling jacket temperature and pressure drop across valves
  3. Non-condensable gases (NCGs): air and CO2 form "air pockets" that prevent steam penetration. Degas the feed water by pre-heating it above 80°C

Practical implications

If your product is sold in both the US and European markets, the water system design must meet both USP and EP requirements. The safest approach is to adopt the most stringent combination of standards: USP conductivity/TOC testing + EP distillation-based WFI + EP sample volumes.

11.3 Passivation

Original Text

11.3.1 Introduction

Pharmaceutical equipment and high purity water systems are designed so that product contact surfaces are not reactive, additive, or absorptive. High purity water systems are primarily composed of austenitic stainless steel (SS) due to their corrosion resistant and contaminant free properties.

Passivation is performed to maximize the metal's corrosion resistance. The stainless steel is sulfuric/nitric/hydrofluoric acid pickled at the mill to remove manganese sulfide inclusions, scale, and other impurities. As the steel is removed from the pickling bath, a thin oxide layer forms immediately over the surface. This oxide layer is what renders the stainless steel passive and non-reactive to corrosion.

Any 300 series chromium steels containing 17% or more chromium that has been handled, welded, or worked should be passivated prior to service.

11.3.3 The Chemical Process

Establishing a passive surface or film on austenitic SS is essential to maximize corrosion resistance. Passive surfaces occur naturally when exposed to an oxidizing environment. Nitric acid solution (HNO3) is an oxidizing acid which erodes the metal, and this initial reaction resists further chemical reaction on the surface. The chromium oxide film thickness typically ranges from 0.5-5.0 nm, averaging 2.0-3.0 nm. The chrome to iron ratio should be at least 1:1, with ratios of 2:1 or more being optimal.

Commentary

Fundamentals of Passivation

What is passivation?

Passivation is the process of forming a protective chromium oxide film on the stainless steel surface. This film:

  • Is only 0.5-5.0 nm thick (nanometer scale, invisible to the naked eye)
  • Averages 2-3 nm
  • Makes the metal surface "passive", so it no longer reacts chemically with the environment

Real-life analogy

Passivation is like "waxing" stainless steel. A new car leaves the factory with protective paint, but if it gets scratched (as welding damages the surface), it has to be repainted (passivated). This "wax" is chromium oxide; it is only a few nanometers thick, yet it blocks corrosion effectively.

Key data

  • Chromium-to-iron ratio (Cr/Fe ratio): at least 1:1, optimally ≥2:1
  • 300-series stainless steels with chromium content ≥17% all require passivation
  • Any surface that has been welded, machined, or handled should be passivated
  • Welding destroys the existing passive film, especially in the heat-affected zone

11.3.4 Passivation Procedures

All passivation procedures consist of four main steps:

  1. Wash (Solvent Degreasing) — Remove dirt, dust, oil, and grease using a water-soluble detergent or solvent
  2. Water Rinse — Remove dissolved soils and detergent
  3. Acid Wash (Passivation Step) — Remove free iron, metallic residues, oxides, and corrosion products. Inorganic acids are typically used.
  4. Final Water Rinse — Flush until effluent quality equals influent quality

Reference: ASTM A 380-96, "Standard Recommended Practice for Cleaning and Descaling Stainless Steel Parts, Equipment and Systems."

Guidelines for establishing effective procedures:

  • Start with an accepted or specified procedure
  • Obtain weld coupons from the system for testing
  • Perform specified and alternate procedures for comparison
  • Confirm effectiveness with field and/or laboratory testing
  • Include procedure confirmation in validation documentation

The Four-Step Passivation Procedure

Four-step process

Step 1: Degrease
↓
Step 2: Water rinse
↓
Step 3: Acid wash (passivation)
↓
Step 4: Final water rinse

Verification points

  • Use the system's own weld coupons to test the effectiveness of the procedure; do not rely on theory alone
  • Acceptance criterion for the final rinse: outlet water quality = inlet water quality
  • All procedure development data should be included in the validation documentation package
  • Referencing ASTM A 380-96 is standard industry practice

11.3.5 Passivation Chemical Alternatives

Nitric acid (HNO3) is the most common acid specified for passivation. Reference: Federal Specification QQ-P-35C (1988).

The trend is to reduce chemical aggressiveness and consider safety, cost, and environmental impact.

Citric acid and ammonium citrate are gaining popularity as alternatives. ASTM A 380 refers to these as cleaning acids, not passivating acids (they are not oxidizers). To achieve true oxidation, chelating agents in conjunction with citric acid have been introduced.

Phosphoric acid is a weak oxidizing acid sometimes specified, but with no formal documentation as a passivating acid.

Chelants (sequestering agents) such as STPP, NTA, and EDTA may be compounded into acid passivation solutions to enhance metal ion extraction.

Orbital welding with electropolished tubing decreases the aggressiveness required of passivating acids during initial passivation.

Choice of Passivation Chemicals

Industry trend

The move from nitric acid to citric acid + chelant combinations:

  • Safety: citric acid is safer for personnel and the environment
  • Cost: waste treatment is simpler and cheaper
  • Effectiveness: combined with chelants such as EDTA, similar results can be achieved
  • Electropolished tubing + orbital welding = milder passivation conditions can be used

The ASTM distinction

ASTM A 380 classifies citric acid as a cleaning acid rather than a passivating acid, because citric acid is not itself an oxidizer. To achieve true oxidative passivation, a chelant or oxidizer must be added.

11.3.6 Chemical Application Methods

11.3.7 Tests for Cleanliness and Passivity

Cleanliness tests: Water break free surface test, wipe test, UV light testing (per ASTM A 380).

Indirect passivity tests (free iron detection):

  • Ferroxyl Test — test solution applied; blue stain within 15 seconds indicates free iron
  • Water Immersion/Wetting and Drying Test
  • High Humidity Test
  • Copper Sulfate Test
  • Ferroxyl/Potassium Ferricyanide-Nitric Acid Test

Direct passivity tests:

  • XPS (X-ray Photoelectron Spectroscopy) — measures oxidation states of surface elements
  • AES (Auger Electron Spectroscopy) — measures elemental Cr/Fe ratio with depth profiling (destructive)

Application Methods and Testing

Guide to choosing an application method

  • Circulation (preferred): suited to piping systems, flow velocity ≥1.5 m/s
  • Note: high flow velocity does not equal particle removal! A 1000-foot pipe run needs about 25 hours of filtered circulation to remove particles completely
  • Spraying: suited to the inside of tanks
  • Immersion: suited to small parts and prefabricated fittings
  • Wiping: a last resort, for areas that cannot be sprayed or circulated

Practical recommendations

  • The Ferroxyl test is the most commonly used: inexpensive and fast (result in 15 seconds)
  • XPS/AES are more expensive and best used on weld coupons to verify the effectiveness of the procedure
  • Underlying logic: no free iron → surface clean enough → oxide film can form uniformly

11.3.13 Rouging

Rouging is seen in many water systems, usually high temperature (80°C) distilled water and clean steam systems. Rouge is not limited to storage and distribution; it also can be found in distillation and clean steam generating equipment.

The main constituent of the rouge film is ferric oxide, but it can contain iron, chromium, and nickel of different forms. From AES analysis, the outer layer is carbon rich, and the underlying region is iron and oxygen rich.

Rouge can be observed in a variety of colors: orange, light-red, red, reddish-brown, purple, blue, gray, and black. It can range from a loose dust-like film to a tight pertinacious film requiring scraping.

"Traditionally the red rouges are most common in high purity high temperature water systems, while the blue/black rouges are typically found in clean steam systems."

Key places to look: still and clean steam generator discharge lines, tank water/vapor interface, pump heads, Teflon® diaphragms (rouge has affinity for Teflon®), and heat affected area of welds.

The presence of rouge has not been proven to affect water quality. The FDA has no written position addressing rouging. Their criterion remains meeting established USP standards.

Removal: phosphoric, citric, oxalic acids, and ammonium citrates depending on severity. Oxalic acid for worst cases. Passivation with nitric acid required after oxalic acid rinse.

The Rouging Phenomenon

What is rouge?

Rouge is a thin iron oxide film that forms naturally in stainless steel water systems. Its main component is Fe2O3 (ferric oxide), and it also contains chromium and nickel.

Commonly found in:

  • 80°C hot purified water systems
  • Clean steam systems
  • Distillation equipment

What do the colors mean?

  • Red tones (orange, red, reddish brown): most common in hot purified water systems
  • Blue-black tones (purple, blue, gray, black): common in clean steam systems
  • It can range from a loose, dusty deposit (easily wiped off) to a stubborn film (requiring scraping)
  • Rouge is sometimes multi-layered, with different colors and textures in different layers

FDA's position

FDA has not issued any written position on rouging. The only standard is whether the water meets USP requirements. Rouge itself has not been shown to affect water quality.

The industry's concern, however, is that over time the film may flake off and disperse into the system, as can be observed in the discoloration of point-of-use filters.

Derouging Strategy

Case: a cold WFI system was derouged and passivated three times, and each time it was fully rouged again within a week. The cause was never determined; rouging is highly site-specific.

11.3.14 Preparing Systems for Passivation

  • Hydrostatic pressure testing first — all new or modified systems
  • Confirm compatibility of all components with passivating solutions (instrumentation, flow meters, valves, UV lights, pumps, seals, filter membranes, gaskets)
  • Remove incompatible items and replace with blanks, valves, or temporary jumper hoses
  • Isolate the system from existing systems, process equipment, utility tie-ins
  • Eliminate all dead legs — critical for chemical contact and complete rinsing
  • Ensure high point vents and low point drains for complete filling and draining
  • Review automated controls: valve alignments, temperature sensors, diverter valves

11.3.11 System Chemistry (Summary)

11.3.15-11.3.16 Disposal & Documentation

Waste solutions can be neutralized; the primary concern is heavy metals (chromium, nickel) in the acid wash effluent. Three disposal options: chemical drains, contractor neutralization, or off-site disposal.

Documentation must include: passivation procedure, development data, testing procedures, calibration records, passivation log sheets, chemical batch records, and marked-up system drawings.

System Preparation, Chemical Formulations, and Documentation

Pre-passivation preparation checklist

  1. Complete the hydrostatic test to confirm the system has no leaks
  2. Confirm that all components are compatible with the passivation solution; remove those that are not
  3. Isolate the system from other systems
  4. Eliminate all dead legs
  5. Ensure venting at high points and draining at low points
  6. Check the automatic controls, so that abnormal temperatures do not trigger diverter valves

Quick reference: six chemical formulations

  • C1 Nitric acid: standard passivation, 30-60 minutes at room temperature
  • C2 Alkaline degreaser: removes organic films and particles
  • C3 Citric acid/chelant: removes metals such as iron, manganese, aluminum, and copper
  • C4 Mineral acids: phosphoric/sulfuric/sulfamic acid, 3-10%
  • C5 Enhanced acid/chelant: for high-temperature oxide films and silica scale
  • C6 Sodium hydrosulfite: a strong reducing agent, used for derouging

Three options for spent solution disposal

  1. Discharge to a chemical drain system (requires a compatible treatment facility)
  2. Neutralize in the contractor's equipment, then discharge
  3. Off-site disposal (the most expensive; the qualifications of the transporter and treatment facility must be verified)

Critical: chromium and nickel in the spent solution are priority pollutant metals and must not be discharged directly to the storm drain system.

Documentation Requirements

Passivation documentation should be included in the final validation documentation package, and should contain:

  • Passivation procedure
  • Procedure development data (coupon test results)
  • Test procedures and equipment
  • Calibration documentation
  • Passivation log sheets
  • Chemical batch records
  • Marked-up system drawings (point check list)

Practice Questions

  1. Your hot WFI system at 80°C shows an in-line conductivity of 2.6 µS/cm. Does it pass Stage 1? What is the limit at this temperature?
  2. An auditor asks why you are not performing pH testing on your PW system. How do you respond?
  3. Your system serves both US and EU markets. Which WFI production method must you use?
  4. After passivation, the Ferroxyl test shows a blue stain at one weld location. What is your next step?
  5. Your facility discovers rouging in a 6-month-old hot WFI system. The water still meets USP specs. What do you recommend?

Section Summary

  • USP Three-Stage Conductivity: A tiered testing approach (in-line → off-line → lab) with temperature-dependent limits from 0.6 to 3.1 µS/cm
  • TOC Testing: Limit 500 ppb; four oxidation methods (conversion to CO2) and four CO2 detection methods; suitability testing with sucrose and benzoquinone
  • EP vs USP Differences: EP requires distillation-only WFI, retains pH testing, has not adopted conductivity or TOC testing
  • Passivation: Four-step process (degrease, rinse, acid wash, rinse) to form protective chromium oxide film (0.5-5 nm); Cr/Fe ratio ≥1:1
  • Chemical Alternatives: Industry trending from nitric acid to citric acid + chelants for safety and environmental reasons
  • Rouging: Common but unregulated phenomenon; ferric oxide film in hot water/steam systems; site-specific; does not necessarily affect water quality
  • Documentation: Comprehensive records (procedures, logs, batch records, system drawings) required for validation
A4-6

Chapter A4-6: Appendix: Treatment Details

p186-p210

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p186-p205

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the four categories of water system pretreatment: turbidity/particulate removal, hardness/metal ion removal, organics/microbial control, and pH and CO2 management
  • Know the principle, advantages, disadvantages and selection logic of each pretreatment technology: media filtration, barrier filtration, softening, degasification, nanofiltration, activated carbon, UV disinfection and chlorine treatment
  • Understand the specific removal challenges of the three "problem ions": iron, silica and aluminum
  • Understand how pH affects the carbonate/bicarbonate/CO2 equilibrium, and why this is critical for the design of downstream RO and ion exchange systems
  • Know the design parameters, component configuration, operating limits and performance indicators of ion exchange and reverse osmosis in final treatment
  • Be able to compare the applications, advantages and disadvantages of ion exchange (IX) and reverse osmosis (RO) in pharmaceutical water systems

Section Navigation

11.4.1 Barrier Filtration 11.4.2 Hardness & Ion Exchange 11.4.3 Acidification & Nanofiltration 11.4.4 Organics & Microbiology 11.4.4.4 Microbial Growth Control 11.4.5-11.4.6 pH & CO2 11.5.1 Ion Exchange for PW 11.5.2 RO for High Purity
11.4.1 Barrier Filtration Details

Original Text

Barrier filtration includes cartridge filtration, pre-coat filtration, ultrafiltration, and nanofiltration. This type of filtration has a "barrier" through which the water must flow. The barrier retains particulates that are removed by changing the barrier (cartridge and pre-coat) or by a purge stream (ultra and nanofiltration).

Barrier filtration is typically not used as the primary method for removal of particulates because of cost of the barrier, labor, and the frequent need for replacement due to the relatively high particulate loading in the water entering pretreatment. It is frequently used as a "final clean-up" after other pretreatment processes such as ion exchange or carbon bed filtration, and before going to a final treatment step such as reverse osmosis.

As a "final clean-up," the barrier is often cartridges with a nominal particle size retention of 1-10 μm for removal of particulate carry-over. If the objective is to remove insoluble forms of silica and iron and achieve a SDI (silt density index) of less than 5 for feed to an RO system, the absolute particle size retention might be <1 μm -- at the lower end of microfiltration or upper end of ultrafiltration.

Ultrafiltration (0.1-0.001 μm) and nanofiltration (0.005-0.0005 μm) are methods of barrier filtration. They are not used for primary removal of particulates due to cost and rapid blinding of the membrane.

Advantages: Low initial cost; can vary particle size retention by cartridge selection.

Disadvantages: Cartridge filtration is expensive for high solids concentration; potential for microbial growth in a non-chlorinated environment.

Tutorial Commentary

Key concept: the role of barrier filtration

In pretreatment, barrier filtration plays a "final cleanup" role, not a "main removal" role. The reason is simple: the feed water carries a large particulate load, so replacing cartridges frequently would be too costly.

A typical configuration places it after the softener or activated carbon and before the RO, protecting the downstream membrane system from particulate fouling.

Key point: what SDI < 5 means

SDI (Silt Density Index) is a key indicator of RO feed water quality. SDI < 5 is the minimum requirement of most RO membrane manufacturers, and SDI < 3 is the ideal value.

If the water contains insoluble silica or iron, cartridges with an absolute rating of <1 μm or ultrafiltration (UF) may be needed to reach this target.

Analogy: filtration as building access control

Think of the water system as a building:
Media filtration = the security guard at the front door, stopping most visitors (coarse particles)
Barrier filtration = the access card reader at the elevator, letting in only those who pass the check (fine-particle control)
RO membrane = the fingerprint lock on the CEO's office, the highest level of filtration

Every layer has a cost, so use the cheap guard first to stop most of the load, then use the expensive access control for fine-grained protection.

11.4.2 Hardness and Metals — Ion Exchange

Original Text

11.4.2.1 Water Softening

An ion exchange system consists of a tank containing small beads of synthetic resin treated to selectively absorb either cations or anions and exchange these ions based upon their relative activity. The process will continue until all available exchange sites are filled, at which point the resin is exhausted and must be regenerated.

The presence of calcium (Ca) and magnesium (Mg) in a water supply is commonly known as "hardness," usually expressed in grains per gallon (gpg). Ion exchange is the principal method of removing hardness. This is required to prevent scale formation in final treatment operations such as RO and distillation.

A standard water softener has four major components: a resin tank, resin, a brine tank, and a valve or controller. The resin beads initially absorb sodium ions during brine regeneration. The resin has a greater affinity for multi-valence ions such as calcium and magnesium than for sodium. As hard water passes through the resin, Ca, Mg, and other multivalent ions adhere to the resin, releasing sodium ions until equilibrium is reached.

Regeneration is achieved by passing a sodium chloride (NaCl) solution through the resin, exchanging the hardness ions for sodium ions. The resin's affinity for hardness ions is overcome by using a highly concentrated brine solution.

Advantages: Industry standard, low cost and effective. Works well in a chlorinated environment. Inherent flushing of some microbial growth during regeneration.

Disadvantages: Salt handling for brine regeneration and disposal of spent brine solution.

11.4.2.2 Demineralization/De-ionization

Three multivalent ions present unusual removal problems:

a) Iron — May change valence states from water-soluble ferrous to insoluble ferric state. Organisms such as Crenothrix, Sphaerotilus, and Gallionella use iron as an energy source, forming a rusty, gelatinous sludge that plugs piping and membranes.

b) Silica — May be soluble ionized species or insoluble material, sometimes as colloidal mixture. Insoluble silica removed by barrier filtration <0.5 μm (UF) or by nanofiltration/RO.

c) Aluminum — Can exist in multiple valences; its solubility is a function of pH. Between pH 6-8, hydrated aluminum oxides are only partially (~80%) rejected by RO, often leading to fouling.

Tutorial Commentary

Key concept: the principle and necessity of softening

Softening is one of the most basic steps in pretreatment. Its purpose is clear: remove calcium and magnesium to prevent scaling of the downstream RO membrane or still.

The principle is ion exchange: sodium ions (Na+) on the resin are exchanged for calcium (Ca2+) and magnesium (Mg2+) in the water. Because the resin has a higher affinity for divalent ions than for monovalent ions, the exchange occurs naturally.

When all the exchange sites are occupied, the resin must be regenerated with concentrated salt solution (brine), which strips off the calcium and magnesium and reloads the resin with sodium ions.

Analogy: the softener as a parking lot

Imagine the resin as a parking lot with a small car (Na+) in every space. When large vehicles (Ca2+, Mg2+) arrive, their bulk (multivalence) means they automatically push out the small cars and take the spaces.

When every space is taken by large vehicles (resin exhausted), the attendant sends in a flood of small cars (concentrated NaCl) and uses sheer numbers to force the large vehicles out. This is "regeneration".

Key point: the three "problem ions"

  • Iron (Fe): it changes valence. Dissolved iron (Fe2+) meets oxygen and becomes insoluble Fe3+, which precipitates and fouls the membrane system. Some bacteria also use iron as an energy source and produce rust-like gels.
  • Silica (Si): it occurs as both ionic and colloidal forms. A softener can reduce the ionic form; the colloidal form needs UF (<0.5 μm) or NF/RO for removal.
  • Aluminum (Al): it is most difficult between pH 6 and 8, where RO removes only about 80%. If aluminum is the main problem, a three-step combination of "softening + pH adjustment + RO" may be needed.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the softener placed before the RO rather than after it? What happens if the water goes to the RO without softening?
  2. Your source water analysis report shows high iron and a pH of 7.5. Which pretreatment combination would you recommend, and why?
11.4.3 Hardness and Metals — Other Processes

Original Text

11.4.3.1 Acidification and Degasification

Use of a pH adjustment step is common to favor or inhibit the formation of CO2. Injecting acid to a pH of approximately 5.5 will maximize the concentration of CO2, whereas addition of a base to pH 8.3 will minimize CO2, converting it to carbonate ion.

If high levels of CO2 are present, it can be removed down to about 5-10 ppm with an atmospheric degasifier. An atmospheric degasifier has the potential of increasing bacterial burden and should be located where bacterial control measures are available.

The incoming water is adjusted to a pH in the range of 3.8-4.2 with sulfuric acid. The acidified water is sent to a packed column degasifier for removal of free CO2 by air. Removal efficiency is better than 98%. The pH after removal of CO2 will be in the range of 6.5-7.0. Just prior to feeding the RO unit, the pH should be adjusted to approximately 8.0-8.5.

Advantages: Replaces softener and handling of large amounts of salt; CO2 released to atmosphere; the added sulfate ion from acidification is easier to remove in RO than added sodium ion from softening.

Disadvantages: Handling acid for acidification; instrumentation and chemical handling for two pH adjustments.

11.4.3.2 Nanofiltration

Nanofiltration is a pressure driven membrane process with performance characteristics between RO and ultrafiltration. The theoretical pore size is one nanometer (10-9 meter). These membranes are sometimes referred to as "softening membranes".

The nanofiltration membrane offers high rejection of salts of divalent anions as well as organics with molecular weights above 200. Operating at 70-150 psig (4.76-10.2 bar) vs. 200-350 psig for RO.

Tutorial Commentary

Key concept: the logic of acidification and degasification

The core idea of this process is to use acid to convert all carbonate/bicarbonate to CO2 gas, then blow it off in a degasifier.

Steps: add acid (pH down to 3.8-4.2) → remove CO2 in the degasifier (>98%) → add caustic (pH up to 8.0-8.5) → feed to RO

This method can replace the softener and is particularly suitable for high flow rates (>50 gpm) or high hardness (>50 ppm).

Key point: microbial risk of the degasifier

A degasifier introduces air, and bacteria in the air can enter the water. Solutions:

  • Fit a HEPA filter on the air inlet
  • Locate the degasifier where microbial control measures exist (for example, between the two passes of a two-pass RO)
  • The degassed water is usually collected in a storage tank, where TOC removal and microbial control can be applied

Typical nanofiltration rejection rates

Pattern: the higher the molecular weight and the higher the valence, the higher the rejection. Monovalent ions such as NaCl are rejected at only 60%.

11.4.4 Organics and Microbiological Impurities

Original Text

11.4.4.1 Introduction

Organic and microbiological contaminants need to be addressed in water treatment systems. The concerns are twofold: contaminants entering the system and contaminants created/growing in the system.

The first issue is water source: well water typically has low organic loading; surface water (lake, river, reservoir) will probably contain relatively high organics with seasonal variation. Municipal water is usually chlorinated, keeping microbiological content low until chlorine/chloramine is removed.

11.4.4.2 Organic Contaminants

a) Bacterial Contamination — Usually expressed as "total viable microbial counts per ml" or Colony Forming Units (CFU) per unit volume.

b) Pyrogenic Contamination — Pyrogens are substances that can produce fever in mammals. Often endotoxins (lipopolysaccharides) shed by bacterial cells. Molecular weight generally 12,000 to 320,000. Quantified in Endotoxin Units (EU) per milliliter. Chemically and physically stable — not necessarily destroyed by conditions that kill bacteria.

c) Total Organic Carbon (TOC) — A measure of organic materials in mg/l or μg/l. A direct measure of oxidizable organic material. Not a good measure of microbial contamination.

d) Dissolved Organic Compounds — Naturally occurring organics include Tannin, Humic acid, and Fulvic acids. Under conditions of free halogen compounds, they form trihalomethanes (THMs), suspected carcinogens.

11.4.4.3 Removal of Organics

Technologies available include:

  • Ozone — Twice as powerful an oxidant as chlorine. Not frequently used in pretreatment due to material compatibility issues.
  • Strong Base Ion Exchange — Organic scavengers that absorb organics with slightly negative charge. Regenerated with NaCl brine.
  • Carbon — Activated carbon adsorbs organics. Periodically replaced when capacity diminishes.
  • Microfiltration — Depth cartridge, pleated, and cross-flow membrane filters; 100 μm down to 0.1 μm. Captures bacteria, giardia cyst, large MW organics.
  • Ultrafiltration — 0.1 to 0.001 μm; removes organics, bacteria, viruses, pyrogens. Cross-flow design is self-cleaning.
  • RO — Also removes organics and microbiological impurities through membrane rejection and purge stream.

Tutorial Commentary

Key concept: two sources of organics and microorganisms

ISPE stresses that organic/microbial contamination in a water system has two sources:

  1. Entering from outside: organics, bacteria and pyrogens carried in with the raw water
  2. Growing inside: microorganisms that proliferate within the system (especially after chlorine removal)

This "two sources" concept is the basic framework for water system design. For every piece of equipment, consider both how much external contamination it can remove and how much internal contamination it will generate itself.

Key point: four categories of organic contaminants

Note in particular: pyrogens are not destroyed by conditions that kill bacteria. Even when the water is "sterile", it may still contain high levels of endotoxin. This is why a WFI system needs multiple barriers.

11.4.4.4 Control of Microbiological Growth & Chlorine Management

Original Text

Periodic Sanitization

Methods include heat, chemical sanitization, regeneration or replacement of media, flushing, or drainage. With heat, USP indicator organisms are killed above 60°C; temperatures above 80°C result in complete kill. Sanitization times might be 1-2 hours at temperature, with total cycle time of 4-8 hours.

Chemical sanitization agents include hydrogen peroxide, iodine, ammonium compounds, and per-oxygen compounds. Temperatures below 15°C slow microbial growth.

Pressurized Carbon Dioxide

During shutdown periods, cartridge filters and RO are deadheaded and pressurized with CO2 to 2-4 bar, lowering pH to <5.5 to inhibit microbial growth while also dissolving scaling chemicals. This has the potential to eliminate the need for a softener before the RO.

Ultraviolet Light (UV)

UV light de-activates DNA in microbes preventing duplication. Feed water must be free of suspended solids which can "shadow" bacteria. UV does not leave a residual in treated water. Typically used for controlling feed water to an RO unit that cannot accept chlorine or heat.

Chlorine

Municipalities use chlorine at dosage levels of 0.2 to 2.0 ppm. Chlorine can adversely affect: polyamide membranes (oxidative deterioration), DI resins (degradation), and stainless steel (corrosion at elevated temperatures). Two principal methods of chlorine removal:

  • Activated Carbon — Removes chlorine by adsorption; bed depths of 2-3 feet at 2-4 gpm/ft3. Creates perfect conditions for microbial growth: slow flow in warm media with nutrients. Heat sanitization (190°F hot water) required daily to weekly.
  • Reduction — Sodium bisulfite reduces chlorine to chloride: SO3- + Cl2 + H2O → 2Cl- + 2H+ + SO4=

Chloramines

Chloramines (formed by chlorine + ammonia) are longer-acting disinfectants. Removal by carbon requires hydraulic rates 3-6X less than for chlorine. Removal results in dissociation to ammonium ion and ammonia. It may be desirable to locate the carbon bed before the water softener if chloramines are present.

Tutorial Commentary

Key concept: a multi-layer strategy for microbial control

Microbial control in a pretreatment system does not rely on a single method but on multiple layers of defense:

  • Continuous control: chlorine/chloramine (feed end), UV (chlorine-free environment)
  • Periodic control: regular sanitization (heat, chemical, regeneration)
  • Design control: avoid dead legs, maintain circulation, temperature management (<15°C or >80°C)

Key point: activated carbon, the biggest contradiction

Activated carbon is the most contradictory piece of equipment in pretreatment: it removes chlorine (protecting the downstream membranes) but also removes the very agent that suppresses microorganisms. The result:

  • The carbon bed itself becomes a breeding ground for microorganisms (low flow velocity + warm temperature + nutrients = a perfect culture medium)
  • It must be thermally sanitized regularly: daily to once or twice weekly, with 190°F (88°C) hot water or steam
  • After sanitization it must be rinsed to remove carbon fines

This is why the location of the activated carbon and its sanitization program are among the key decisions in water system design.

Difference in removing chloramine vs. chlorine

Chloramine is 3-6 times harder to remove than chlorine. If your municipal source uses chloramine disinfection:

  • The carbon bed needs 3-6 times the contact time (a larger bed or a lower flow velocity)
  • Removal produces ammonium ions (NH4+) and ammonia (NH3), which must be removed by downstream ion exchange
  • The carbon bed may need to go before the softener (rather than the usual position after it), so that the softener also removes the ammonium

At the design stage, always first confirm whether the source water is disinfected with chlorine or chloramine.

CO2 pressurization during shutdown: an innovative approach

This is a clever energy-saving solution: during shutdown, pressurize with CO2 (2-4 bar), which achieves three goals at once:

  1. Lowers the pH to <5.5, inhibiting microbial growth
