  2. Dissolves scale such as calcium carbonate, cleaning the RO membranes
  3. Saves water and power during the shutdown

Before start-up, you only need to flush out the acidic water and dissolved salts. If the shutdown frequency matches the RO scaling frequency, the softener may even be eliminated.

11.4.5-11.4.6 pH, Carbon Dioxide & Feed Water pH

Original Text

11.4.5 pH and Carbon Dioxide

pH, the negative log of the hydrogen ion concentration, affects the chemistry of the water: it can tell us if the water will corrode piping or if contaminants (carbonates) are likely to precipitate and cause scaling.

Free carbon dioxide in water is produced by decay of organic matter, dissolution from underground sources, and solution from the atmosphere. In high purity water, low levels of CO2 from the atmosphere can cause pH to drop from 7.0 to 5.5 and conductivity to increase from 0.1 μS to 1 μS.

The equilibrium between carbonate, bicarbonate, and carbon dioxide is affected by pH. As pH is lowered, equilibrium shifts toward CO2. As pH is increased, equilibrium shifts toward bicarbonate and then carbonate.

11.4.6 Importance of Feed Water pH

EPA drinking water standards require pH within 6.5-8.5. Key considerations:

  • If feed pH < 8.3, analyze for CO2 — the lower the pH from 8.3, the higher the dissolved CO2 potential.
  • CO2 passes directly through pretreatment and RO membrane, depressing conductivity and pH, making it difficult to meet USP requirements.
  • Feed pH 6-10: RO has potential for hardness scaling. Adding acid controls scale but creates CO2; adding base converts bicarbonate to carbonate, removed by RO but may cause scaling.
  • Most pharmaceutical companies use ion exchange softening to prevent scaling in RO.
  • Chloramine in feed water can result in ammonia, which affects conductivity and pH in final product.

Common pH adjustment chemicals: sulfuric acid (readily available, less corrosive) and sodium hydroxide (readily available, sodium removed by final treatment).

Tutorial Commentary

Key concept: the pH-carbonate equilibrium system

This is one of the most important equilibria in water chemistry and directly affects water system design:

  • pH < 5.5: almost entirely CO2 (a neutral gas), which can be removed by a degasifier
  • pH 5.5-8.3: CO2 and HCO3- coexist
  • pH 8.3-10: almost entirely HCO3- (ionic), which can be removed by RO or ion exchange
  • pH > 10: mainly CO32-

When designing a pretreatment system, choose the method for handling the carbonate system according to the pH.

Key point: the critical effect of CO2 on pharmaceutical water

CO2 is a "silent killer":

  • It passes straight through RO membranes (it is a neutral molecule with no charge)
  • A trace of CO2 can lower the pH of pure water from 7.0 to 5.5 and raise the conductivity from 0.1 μS to 1 μS
  • This may cause the water to fail the USP conductivity test
  • In a two-pass RO system, even a trace of CO2 may prevent production of water below 1 μS

So knowing the feed water pH and CO2 content is the first step in system design.

Analogy: pH adjustment as traffic management

Imagine the three forms in the carbonate system (CO2, HCO3-, CO32-) as traffic on three roads.

Adding acid = steering all the cars onto the "CO2 highway", then sending them away via the "exit ramp" of the degasifier. Drawback: when the exit is congested (high CO2), some cars "slip into" the RO system.

Adding caustic = steering the cars onto "carbonate avenue", where the RO membrane can stop the ionic vehicles. Drawback: too many cars gathering can cause a "traffic jam" (scaling).

So most pharmaceutical plants choose a softener to remove the scale-prone calcium and magnesium first, then let the RO handle the remaining ions.

Practice Questions

  1. Your feed water has a pH of 7.2, total hardness of 200 ppm and a CO2 test result of 35 ppm. What challenges will this create for the downstream RO system? Which pretreatment combination would you recommend?
  2. Why does ISPE recommend adjusting the pH to 8.0-8.5 before the RO feed? What potential risks does this carry?
11.5 Final Treatment: Ion Exchange for Purified Water

Original Text

11.5.1.1 Ion Exchange Use in USP Systems

The primary purpose of ion exchange equipment in USP high purity water systems is to satisfy the ionic quality portion of the specification. Ion exchange can effectively reduce organics with proper resin selection, but may not meet USP TOC requirements without additional membrane processes where high feed water TOC levels exist.

11.5.1.2 Functionality

Cation Exchange: Exchange of cations (Ca, Mg, Na, etc.) for hydrogen ion (H+). Regenerated with dilute acid (H2SO4 or HCl). All salts converted to corresponding acids.

Anion Exchange: Exchange of anions (SO4=, HCO3-, Cl-, etc.) for hydroxide ions (OH-). Regenerated with NaOH. Following cation exchange, this completely demineralizes water.

Strongly ionized resins: Greater affinity for all ionized constituents, capable of removing weakly ionized species (acetates, silica). Capacities: 18 and 14 kgs as CaCO3 per ft3 for cation and anion respectively.

Weakly ionized resins: Ineffective at removing weakly ionized constituents but have 2-3X the exchange capacity and can be regenerated more efficiently.

Two-bed deionizers: Separate columns for cation and anion resin. Simple regeneration. Types: co-current (regeneration same direction as process water) and counter-current (opposite direction; produces higher quality water with ~50% less chemicals).

Mixed-bed deionizers: One column with both resins intimately mixed. Functions as infinite series of two-bed exchangers. Superior removal of monovalent ions. More complex regeneration requiring resin separation and mixing.

11.5.1.3 Operating Parameters

Tutorial Commentary

Key concept: complete deionization by ion exchange

The "softening" in pretreatment uses only cation exchange (Na form), whereas ion exchange in final treatment is complete deionization:

  1. Cation exchange (H+ form): Ca2+, Mg2+, Na+ → H+ (the water becomes an acidic solution)
  2. Anion exchange (OH- form): SO42-, Cl-, HCO3- → OH-
  3. H+ + OH- → H2O (pure water)

This is the basic principle of deionization (DI).

Key point: choosing between two-bed and mixed-bed

  • Two-bed: simple regeneration, suited to high throughput. A counter-current regenerated unit can produce 0.2 μS/cm water with half the chemical consumption.
  • Mixed-bed: gives the best product water quality (down to 0.055 μS/cm, close to the theoretical value for pure water of 0.055), but regeneration is complex (the resins must be separated and remixed).
  • A common configuration is RO + mixed-bed polisher: the RO removes 90-99% of the ions and the mixed bed provides the final safeguard.

Counter-current vs. co-current regeneration

Counter-current regeneration is the preferred choice in modern ion exchange:

  • Chemical consumption reduced by about 50%
  • Higher product water quality
  • Principle: the regenerant enters from the outlet end and contacts the cleanest resin layer last, ensuring the outlet end always has the freshest resin

Analogy: mixed-bed as a coffee blend

A two-bed is like first drinking a cup of sour coffee (the acidic water after cation exchange), then a cup of bitter alkaline coffee (the alkaline water after anion exchange), and neutralizing them in your mouth.

A mixed-bed is like grinding the two kinds of beans together and brewing them as one: every sip neutralizes acid and base at the same time, so every sip is of very high quality. This is why mixed-bed product water is far better than two-bed.

11.5.1.4-11.5.1.10 Ion Exchange Components, Regeneration & Sanitization

Original Text

11.5.1.4 Component Description

An ion exchange system comprises: tank(s), ion exchange resin, piping and valve system, water/chemical distributors internal to tank(s), regeneration system, and conductivity or resistivity meter. Available in on-site regenerable and off-site regenerable (rechargeable) versions.

Tanks may be constructed from fiberglass, stainless steel, or carbon steel with inert interior lining (vulcanized rubber or PVC). Off-site units: fiberglass or light gauge SS, sizes <1 ft3 to 50 ft3 per tank.

11.5.1.5 Tanks

For pharmaceutical applications, tanks rarely need to be more than 3-4 feet (0.91-1.22 m) in diameter with straight shells of 6-8 feet (1.82-2.44 m). Steel tanks welded per ASME Code for operating pressures between 100-150 psig (7-10.5 kgs/cm2).

11.5.1.6 Distributors

Each tank includes distributors at all pipe-to-tank interfaces. Required to prevent resin escape and provide adequate flow distribution. Materials: stainless steel, PVC, CPVC, polypropylene, or PVDF. Structural integrity is a key design element — a ruptured distributor can cause significant resin loss.

11.5.1.7 Piping and Valves

Most systems use Schedule 80 PVC or CPVC. Specialty plastics (polypropylene, PVDF) offer lower organic leachables. Stainless steel offers structural integrity and hot water/steam sanitization capability, but is more vulnerable to corrosion from acid/caustic.

11.5.1.8 Regeneration System

Requires chemical pumps/eductors, chemical tanks, piping, and instrumentation. Positive displacement pumps driven by electric motors or compressed air are ideally suited.

11.5.1.9 Microbiological Concerns

Regeneration of resin beds effectively sanitizes the system; however, as resin exhausts, pH approaches neutral, and organic matter fosters bacteria growth. Regeneration frequency is critical for systems without auxiliary microbiological control (UV lights). Polishing systems positioned with sub-micron filters and UV may operate several weeks without regeneration.

11.5.1.10 Advantages and Disadvantages

Advantages: Flexibility in flow rate; consistent Stage 1 conductivity; regeneration doubles as microbial control; extensive installed base.

Disadvantages: Acid/caustic storage and handling; waste neutralization; reduced ability to reduce dissolved organics vs. membrane systems.

Tutorial Commentary

Key point: the distributor is the hidden critical part

The distributor is the part of an ion exchange vessel that is most easily overlooked yet most critical:

  • Function: prevent resin leakage + distribute the water flow evenly
  • Material options: SS, PVC, CPVC, PP, PVDF
  • A broken distributor = disaster: large resin leakage, time-consuming repair, and possible contamination of downstream systems

Pay particular attention to structural strength at the design stage; regular inspection is a maintenance priority.

Trade-offs in piping material selection

  • PVC/CPVC: low cost and corrosion resistant, but higher leaching of organics
  • PP/PVDF: low organic leaching, and piping can be designed close to sanitary stainless steel welded practice, but at higher cost
  • Stainless steel: structurally strong and thermally sanitizable, but at risk of corrosion on contact with acid and caustic

The choice depends on budget, product water quality requirements and the sanitization method. If thermal sanitization is needed → stainless steel; if low leaching is the priority → PVDF.

Microbial control and regeneration frequency

The microbial control strategy of an ion exchange system is "regeneration in place of sanitization": the acid and caustic regenerants (extreme pH) are themselves bactericidal.

But as the resin becomes exhausted, the pH approaches neutral, and with deposited organics, microorganisms begin to grow. Therefore:

  • Systems without UV support: regeneration frequency must take microbial factors into account, not just the degree of ion exhaustion
  • Systems with UV + microfiltration (polishing): the regeneration cycle can be extended to several weeks

Key concept: on-site vs. off-site regeneration

This is the first decision point in ion exchange system design:

11.5.2 Reverse Osmosis (RO) for High Purity Water Applications

Original Text

11.5.2.1 Description

RO is a pressure driven process utilizing a semi-permeable membrane capable of removing dissolved organic and inorganic contaminants. Commercial membranes available in spiral wound and hollow fiber configurations. Two basic materials:

  • Cellulose acetate (CA) — Oldest commercially; lowest initial cost; chlorine tolerant; disadvantages include fastest loss of rejection, requires pH 5-6, and free chlorine in feed stream. Higher pressure (300-500 psig) and temperature (60-80°F).
  • Thin film composite (TFC/polyamide) — Highest rejection; works at low temperatures (40°F+) and low pressure (150 psig+); intolerant of chlorine. Majority of pharmaceutical systems use TFC membranes.

Most spiral wound membranes incorporate a brine seal at the leading end. The seal can cause bacterial problems due to stagnant water conditions. Some membranes are produced without a brine seal ("loose wrap" or "full fit") — superior for pharmaceutical RO units as bacterial contamination is minimized.

11.5.2.2 Application

RO can be utilized upstream of deionizers to reduce chemical consumption, or as product staged (two-pass) RO to produce water meeting USP TOC and conductivity requirements. Some installations produce Stage 1 conductivity (on-line measurement); others pass Stage 2 or 3 (laboratory testing).

11.5.2.3 Instrumentation and Control

Even small changes in feed pH can have dramatic effects on final conductivity. Monitoring parameters: feed/permeate conductivity, permeate flow, waste flow, feed temperature, pressures (feed, concentrate, permeate). Instruments must be calibrated, NIST traceable. Protective devices for pumps (low suction) and membranes (high pressure, high temperature).

Tutorial Commentary

Key point: microbial risk of the brine seal

The brine seal at the front of a conventional RO membrane element creates a stagnant water zone between the membrane and the pressure vessel, which becomes a breeding ground for microorganisms.

Pharmaceutical-grade RO systems should preferably use membranes without a brine seal (loose wrap / full fit), which allow a small controlled flow of water between the membrane and the vessel and eliminate the stagnant zone.

Architecture of a two-pass RO (product staged)

A single-pass RO usually cannot meet the USP purified water standard. The water path of a two-pass RO:

Raw water → pretreatment → first-pass RO (removes 90-99%) → second-pass RO (removes a further 90-99%) → product water

The product water resistivity of a two-pass RO can usually reach above 0.5 MΩ/cm, enough to meet USP Purified Water. Some systems can even pass the Stage 1 conductivity test (measurable online).

Key monitoring parameters for an RO system

  • Feed/product conductivity → calculate real-time rejection
  • Product flow + reject flow → recovery
  • Feed temperature → affects membrane flux
  • Pressure (feed/concentrate/product) → indicator of membrane condition
  • Differential pressure across membranes + conductivity → indicators of fouling and degradation
  • ORP / residual chlorine → protect the TFC membrane

All instruments must be calibrated and traceable to NIST.

Key concept: choosing the RO membrane material

This is the first and most important decision in RO system design:

Conclusion: TFC membranes are the mainstream choice in pharmaceutical use, provided that chlorine is removed first.

11.5.2.4-11.5.2.8 RO Limitations, Scaling, Cleaning & Performance

Original Text

11.5.2.4 Limitations

RO cannot remove 100% of contaminants and has very low removal capability for some extremely low molecular weight dissolved organics. A concentrate flow is always necessary. Typical recovery: 75%; small units may be lower; large systems up to 85%.

Carbon dioxide passes directly through the RO membrane — for design purposes, CO2 will be in the product at the same level as in the feed. Excess CO2 in product may cause quality problems or increase anion resin loading.

Present RO technology requires ambient operation with occasional sanitization. Operating at ambient temperatures can result in biological growth. The inability to continuously operate at high temperature (80°C) is the most significant factor limiting RO use in WFI applications.

Performance is dependent on numerous O-rings between membrane elements and housings. O-ring slippage may result in poor water quality, detected by high conductivity.

11.5.2.5 Scaling

Scale formation is predicted by calculation using concentrate stream levels of calcium, alkalinity, TDS, and temperature. Proper pH to operate without antiscalant is minimum 0.5 pH units below the scaling pH.

Antiscalant chemicals increase the time necessary for crystal formation of precipitate. Various membranes have different maximum operating temperatures. Many systems utilize feed water preheating to optimize membrane area and minimize pumping energy.

11.5.2.6 Cleaning and Sanitization

Virtually all RO units need periodic cleaning. Acid cleaners for metals/salts; alkaline detergent for silt/organics. Cleaning frequency should not exceed four times per year if pretreatment is working properly. Chlorine tolerant membranes: chlorine solutions. Non-chlorine tolerant: peracetic acid/hydrogen peroxide. Some membranes allow thermal sanitization at 80°C.

11.5.2.7 Purification Capability

Single stage RO reduces raw water salts by 90-99%; colloids, bacteria, endotoxin reduced by 1-3 logs. Single pass may not meet USP Purified Water. Product staged (two-pass) RO can produce water exceeding USP PW or WFI in most applications, typically ≥0.5 MΩ/cm.

11.5.2.8 Performance

Sufficient membrane area is required. SDI reading of 3 or less is recommended. Higher membrane area for fixed flow = lower fouling rate (though recovery is a factor). Design must optimize membrane area vs. maintenance and cleaning requirements.

Advantages: Reduces/eliminates chemical handling; better TOC reduction than IX alone; membrane barrier to bacteria, endotoxin, organics, salts.

Disadvantages: Water consumption (high waste flow); maintenance can be expensive if pretreatment does not properly handle raw water changes.

Tutorial Commentary

Key concept: the four main limitations of RO

  1. CO2 passage: a neutral molecule passes straight through the membrane, affecting product conductivity and pH
  2. Cannot remove 100%: a concentrate bleed (typically 25%) is always needed, and very low molecular weight organics are hard to remove
  3. Cannot run continuously at high temperature: operation at ambient temperature carries a risk of microbial growth, which is the biggest limitation of RO in WFI applications
  4. O-ring leakage risk: loosened seals between membrane elements can cause a sharp drop in water quality

Key point: cleaning frequency is an indicator of pretreatment quality

ISPE states clearly: if the pretreatment system is properly designed and operated, RO cleaning should not be needed more than 4 times a year.

If your RO cleaning frequency clearly exceeds this standard, the problem is not the RO itself but the pretreatment system. Go back and check:

  • Is the softener regenerating properly? (hardness scaling)
  • Is the carbon bed effectively removing organics? (organic fouling)
  • Is particulate filtration adequate? (SDI > 3?)
  • Is the feed water pH in the correct range?

Key RO performance calculations

Recovery = product flow / feed flow × 100%
  Typical value: 75% (may be lower for small units, up to 85% for large ones)

Salt Rejection = (1 - product conductivity / feed conductivity) × 100%
  Single pass: 90-99%
  Two passes in series: 99-99.9%

SDI (Silt Density Index) targets:
  Ideal < 3
  Maximum acceptable < 5

Analogy: RO cleaning as car maintenance

Acid cleaning = using a rust remover to clear calcium and magnesium scale (like removing rust from the underbody)

Alkaline cleaning = using a detergent to remove organics and silt (like cleaning engine oil grime)

Usually the two are performed in sequence to ensure thorough cleaning. If your car needs a major overhaul every month, the problem is not the car but the road (poor pretreatment).

Practice Questions

  1. The product conductivity of your two-pass RO system suddenly rises from 1.2 μS/cm to 8 μS/cm. What are the possible causes, and what would you check first?
  2. Why does ISPE point out that RO has limited use in WFI applications? If your company wants to replace distillation with a two-pass RO to produce WFI, what additional problems must be solved?
  3. An RO system runs at 85% recovery instead of 75%. What effect will this have on membrane life and cleaning frequency?
Section Summary

Key Takeaways

Pretreatment Process Summary

  • Barrier filtration: Used as "final clean-up" before RO; target SDI < 5
  • Water softening: Ion exchange (Na-form) removes Ca/Mg hardness to prevent scaling
  • Problem ions: Iron (valence change), Silica (multiple forms), Aluminum (pH-dependent solubility)
  • Acidification/degasification: Alternative to softener for high-flow/high-hardness applications
  • Nanofiltration: "Softening membrane" at lower pressure than RO
  • Organics control: Dual concern — incoming contamination AND internal growth
  • Microbial control: Multi-layer approach (chlorine, UV, heat, design)
  • pH/CO2 management: Critical for downstream RO/IX performance

Final Treatment Summary

  • Ion exchange: Two-bed (simple) vs. Mixed-bed (highest quality); co-current vs. counter-current regeneration
  • RO: TFC membranes preferred; two-pass for USP PW; recovery ~75%
  • RO limitations: CO2 pass-through; cannot run continuously at 80°C; O-ring risks
  • Cleaning indicator: >4 cleanings/year = pretreatment problem

Key Learning Points Review

Key Takeaways

  1. Pretreatment determines the life of final treatment: with good pretreatment, RO membranes last 3-5 years; with poor pretreatment, they need cleaning or even replacement every quarter.
  2. Source water analysis is the starting point: well water vs. surface water, hardness, iron/silica/aluminum content, chlorine vs. chloramine: these determine the whole pretreatment architecture.
  3. The chlorine contradiction: chlorine is relied on to control microorganisms, yet it damages downstream membranes and resins. The timing and method of chlorine removal are key design decisions.
  4. CO2 cannot be ignored: it passes through RO membranes, affects conductivity and adds load to ion exchange. pH management directly affects CO2 behavior.
  5. Choosing between IX and RO: IX involves complex chemical handling but offers flexible capacity; RO reduces chemicals but brings waste water and microbial issues. Most modern systems use an RO + IX polisher combination.

Comprehensive Practice

You are asked to design a pharmaceutical purified water system with the following source water conditions:

  • Municipal tap water, disinfected with chloramine (0.8 ppm)
  • Hardness: 180 ppm as CaCO3
  • Iron: 0.3 ppm (soluble)
  • pH：7.8
  • TOC：2.5 ppm
  • Required flow rate: 40 gpm

Plan the complete pretreatment + final treatment train, and explain the selection rationale and key design parameters for each unit.

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p206-p210

Learning Objectives

  1. Understand the thermal efficiency metrics (Performance Ratio and Thermal Economy) for distillation systems
  2. Compare energy consumption across Single Effect (SE), Multi-Effect (ME), and Vapor Compression (VC) distillers
  3. Explain water recovery ratios and the factors that limit recovery
  4. Describe the economics and thermodynamics of adding effects in ME design
  5. Identify the problems caused by non-condensable gases and the role of deaeration
  6. Interpret typical water analysis data and compare operating costs between ME and VC systems

11.6 Distillation for High Purity Water Systems

Original Text

11.6.1 Thermal Efficiency of Distillation Systems

Heat losses from a distillation system are due to:

  • Radiation
  • Venting
  • Heat exiting with the blowdown and distillate streams which are at higher temperature than the feed
  • Heat exiting with cooling water

Because of these heat losses, it takes more than one pound of plant steam to produce one pound of WFI in a single effect distiller. For this reason, Multi-Effect distillers are used in order to improve the performance of the system.

The Performance Ratio (R) of a distiller is defined as the amount of distillate produced in relation to the amount of steam consumed, and is given by: R = Md/Ms, where

  • R = Performance Ratio (dimensionless)
  • Md = distillate produced (lbs)
  • Ms = steam consumed (lbs)

Another way to measure the performance of a distiller is often stated in terms of Thermal Economy (E). This is defined as the amount of distillate produced in relation to the amount of energy input, and can be given by: E = Md/1000 BTU heat input.

A typical single effect distiller, without feedwater pre-heating, has an R of about 0.82 and an E of about 0.89. Pre-heating of the feedwater in the condenser raises the R to 0.93 and the E to 0.96 respectively.

Feedwater pre-heaters are sometimes used to elevate the temperature of the feedwater prior to entering each effect in a ME system, and thereby reduce the steam requirements of the distiller.

Typical performance values for a 600-gph VC and 5-effect ME:

* Economy takes into consideration the electrical energy consumption for the VC.

Commentary

Thermal Efficiency of Distillation Systems

Core Concept: Why Is Single Effect Distillation Not Enough?

The basic principle of distillation is simple: heat water until it evaporates, then condense the vapor to obtain pure water. The problem is energy loss. A single effect (SE) distiller has a Performance Ratio of only 0.82, meaning that each pound of steam consumed yields only 0.82 pounds of WFI, so the energy conversion is below 100%.

The key design feature of a multi-effect (ME) still is that the vapor produced in one effect becomes the heating source for the next effect: "one portion of steam does the work several times."

Interpreting the Formulas

Performance Ratio:  R = Md / Ms
  R < 1 (single effect)  →  low efficiency, less than one pound of output per pound of steam
  R > 1 (multi-effect)  →  steam is reused

Thermal Economy:    E = Md / 1000 BTU
  Considers total energy input rather than the quantity of steam
  VC has the highest E value (19.52), but it uses electricity rather than steam

Everyday Analogy: Heat Reuse in a Hot Pot Restaurant Chain

Imagine cooking hot pot. Single effect distillation is like heating one pot of water on one burner and letting the steam escape. Multi-effect distillation is like channeling the steam from the first pot to heat the second pot, then the steam from the second pot to heat the third... for the same fuel cost, you heat more pots of water.

VC distillation is like adding a "heat pump": the exhaust vapor is compressed to raise its temperature and then sent back for reuse, so almost nothing is wasted. That is why the performance ratio of VC (R = 27.67) far exceeds that of a 5-effect ME (R = 7.11).

Key Points: Interpreting VC vs ME Data

  • The VC Economy figure (19.52) includes electricity - it cannot be compared directly with the steam-based figures for ME
  • 5-effect ME Cold (6.8) vs Hot (3.84) - the Economy of the hot system is actually lower because the higher outlet temperature carries away more heat
  • Preheating the feedwater can raise the SE R value from 0.82 to 0.93 - this is the simplest energy-saving improvement

11.6.2 Water Recovery

Original Text

11.6.2 Water Recovery

A portion of the feedwater is continuously discharged to waste in order to limit the total dissolved solids content of the feedwater within the evaporator. That portion of feedwater not evaporated is termed blowdown, and is discharged continuously to waste.

The total quantity of feedwater (Mf) required is given by the sum of the distillate produced (Md) and the blowdown (Mb) discharged. The Recovery Ratio (Rc) of a distilling unit is determined by dividing the product rate (Md) by the feedwater rate (Mf).

Hence the Equations: Mf = Md + Mb and Rc = Md/Mf.

In order to minimize energy consumption and pre-treatment cost, it is desirable to have a high Rc. The Rc is limited by the scaling potential of the water. At low flow rates, it may be limited by practical considerations.

The higher the Total Dissolved Solids (TDS) of the feed, the lower is the recovery. With deionized feedwater, the recovery is 90%-95%. With softened feed, the recovery is typically 80%-85%.

Commentary

Water Recovery Ratio

Mass Balance Formula

Mf = Md + Mb
  Mf = feedwater
  Md = distillate
  Mb = blowdown

Rc = Md / Mf = recovery ratio
  Deionized water feed: Rc = 90-95%
  Softened water feed:  Rc = 80-85%

Core Concept: Why Can Recovery Not Reach 100%?

As feedwater evaporates in the still, salts and minerals do not travel with the steam and become progressively more concentrated. If part of this concentrated water (blowdown) is not discharged, minerals deposit as scale, which damages the heat transfer surfaces, reduces efficiency, and can even corrode the equipment.

This is why pretreatment quality directly determines the recovery ratio:

  • Deionized water (almost no minerals) → tolerates a higher concentration factor → Rc up to 95%
  • Softened water (still contains TDS) → higher scaling risk → Rc only 80-85%

Analogy: A Pot of Boiling Salt Water

Imagine you keep adding salt water to a pot and evaporating it to collect pure water. The salt at the bottom becomes more and more concentrated and eventually crystallizes. You must periodically pour off some of the concentrated brine (blowdown) to keep operating normally. The cleaner the feed, the longer you can evaporate before you need to "blow down."

Practical Impact

The recovery ratio directly affects operating costs:

  • Low recovery = more feedwater consumption + more pretreatment consumables + more blowdown (wastewater treatment cost)
  • Investing in better pretreatment (such as RO + EDI) increases capital expenditure but can raise the distiller recovery ratio and lower long-term operating costs
  • The economic benefit of high recovery is especially clear where water is expensive or wastewater discharge is restricted

11.6.3 Number of Effects or Columns in an ME Design

Original Text

11.6.3 Number of Effects or Columns in an ME Design

Increasing the number of effects in an ME system does not result in increased output, but it reduces the amount of steam and cooling water required to produce the same amount of distillate, compared to a system with fewer effects.

In order to maintain a temperature difference for heat transfer between the vapor from one effect and the boiling liquid of the next effect, the pressure within each succeeding effect must be lower than its predecessor. The temperature and pressure are highest in the first effect, and are lowest in the condenser and the last effect, which are nearly at the same pressure and temperature.

In order to minimize the heat rejection requirements of the condenser, it is desirable to operate the system over the lowest temperature range possible. Additionally, a lower temperature operation reduces the potential for scaling and corrosion.

If we call the temperature at which the first effect operates the top temperature and the temperature of the condenser downstream of the last effect the bottom temperature, that constitutes the temperature range for the multi-effect distiller.

In a non-pharmaceutical application, the bottom temperature can be below the boiling point at the prevailing atmosphere, i.e., operation is under vacuum. This is not an acceptable practice for the production of WFI quality water because of the potential for air leak into the system. In addition, a low temperature, below that considered sanitary, is unacceptable.

The top temperature is dependent on the pressure of the heating steam, normally 100-125 psig, while the bottom temperature is dependent on the condenser pressure, which is normally 7-10 psig.

The temperature range and equipment cost limit the number of effects that are justifiable. The fixed charges for additional effects ultimately dissipate the savings in energy. It is important to note that there is a certain amount of variety in the designs of multiple effect distillers, and that although their geometry may differ, they are thermodynamically all the same.

The larger the output of the system and the higher the cost of steam, the more economically justifiable it is to increase the number of effects.

Typical ME systems have 3-5 effects, and some have as many as 8 effects.

Commentary

Choosing the Number of Effects in an ME Design

Core Concept: Are More Effects Always Better?

Many people intuitively assume that "more effects = higher efficiency = better," but ISPE states clearly that adding effects does not increase output; it only reduces steam and cooling water consumption. There is also an economic balance point:

  • Each additional effect = another evaporator, piping, and control system = higher equipment cost and maintenance expense
  • Once the fixed equipment cost exceeds the energy savings, adding more effects is no longer worthwhile
  • Typical industry practice: 3-5 effects are the mainstream, and 8 effects is the upper limit

Analogy: A Bus Shuttle

Think of each effect as a bus stop. Steam is a passenger who boards at the first stop, and at each stop some passengers (energy) get off to do work. The more stops there are, the better each passenger is utilized. But every extra stop requires building another sign and shelter (equipment cost), and beyond a certain point it is no longer worth it.

Pharmaceutical Constraint: No Vacuum Operation

Non-pharmaceutical distillers can operate under vacuum (bottom temperature below the atmospheric boiling point), but pharmaceutical WFI stills do not allow this, for the following reasons:

  • Negative pressure = risk of air leakage - outside air can introduce microbial contamination
  • Low temperature = unhygienic - temperatures that are too low cannot suppress microbial growth
  • The bottom temperature is therefore limited by the condenser pressure (7-10 psig), and the top temperature by the inlet steam pressure (100-125 psig).

This temperature range is the total "budget" that is divided among the effects in an ME design. The more effects there are, the smaller the temperature difference each effect receives, and the lower the heat transfer driving force.

Design Decision Guidance

Key considerations when choosing the number of effects:

  • High output + expensive steam → adding effects is economically beneficial (for example 6-8 effects)
  • Low output + cheap steam → 3-4 effects are sufficient
  • Designs from different manufacturers may look different, but the thermodynamic principles are exactly the same

11.6.4 Non-Condensable Gases, Venting, and Deaeration

Original Text

11.6.4 Non-Condensable, Venting, and Deaeration

Non-condensable gases such as carbon dioxide and oxygen are dissolved within the feedwater and become liberated as the temperature of the water increases. These gases, if they are not removed, have two detrimental effects on distillation units:

  1. Since the gases are non-condensable, they can blanket the heat transfer surface and inhibit heat transfer in the condenser and evaporator. This translates into reduced output.
  2. These gases are corrosive and will contribute to the pitting, embrittlement, and cracking of stainless steel.

For these reasons, it is imperative for designers and operators to ensure proper and adequate venting of the distilling unit.

The steam carries non-condensables that are liberated from the raw water during evaporation. The gases do not travel alone and will not be vented without the associated steam.

There are two characteristics of non-condensable liberated by ordinary waters which allow designers to locate and vent these gases from an evaporator or condenser:

  • Oxygen and carbon dioxide are significantly heavier than the associated steam, and therefore collect mostly at the bottom.
  • Non-condensable gases tend to migrate to the coldest surface they can find.

Considering the points above, the gases liberated in each effect are processed through the bottom of each effect with the distillate and make their way to the final condenser which is vented along its length. Operators should ensure that a steady stream of steam is exiting the distiller vent at all times.

Improper venting and/or a malfunctioning distillate level control can result in CO2 being pulled into the distillate pump suction and processed across the conductivity cell, resulting in high conductivity reading.

Commentary

Non-Condensable Gases and Venting

Core Concept: The Double Hazard of Non-Condensable Gases

CO2 and O2 dissolved in the feedwater are released on heating. These gases are "non-condensable," meaning they do not turn into liquid in the condenser as steam does, but remain gaseous and occupy space:

  • Reduced efficiency: the gas covers the heat transfer surface like a blanket (gas blanketing), preventing steam from contacting the condensing surface and reducing heat transfer efficiency
  • Corrosion: CO2 dissolves in water to form carbonic acid, and O2 directly promotes oxidation → pitting, embrittlement, and stress corrosion cracking (SCC) of stainless steel

Analogy: Fog on a Bathroom Mirror

Imagine the mist (condensed water vapor) that forms on a mirror when you take a hot shower in winter. Now suppose someone sprayed a layer of cling film onto the mirror (non-condensable gas): the water vapor could no longer touch the mirror surface and could not condense. For a still, this "cling film" disables the condenser and output drops.

Operating Essentials: Correct Venting Practice

  • Continuous venting: the still vent must always show a steady discharge of steam, which indicates that non-condensable gases are being carried away
  • Vent location design: CO2 and O2 are heavier than steam and tend to settle to the bottom and migrate toward cold surfaces → place vent points at the bottom and along the condenser
  • Troubleshooting: if the distillate conductivity suddenly rises, first check whether venting is working properly - CO2 drawn into the distillate dissolves to form carbonic acid, which directly raises the conductivity reading

11.6.4.1 Deaerator (Decarbonator)

Original Text

11.6.4.1 Deaerator (also called Decarbonator)

All feed waters have dissolved gases the amount of which depends on water temperature, composition, and pH. The latter is dependent on the type of pretreatment used. Other gases occur due to the breakdown of some of the constituents during heating, as is the case of alkalinity.

It is very desirable, whenever practical, to remove the gases from the feed waters prior to entry into the evaporator in order to minimize corrosion and to improve heat transfer. This is often done in VC distillers, where a deaerator is installed between the feed heater and the evaporator.

Because of the configuration and relatively high operating pressure of the ME, it is difficult to incorporate a deaerator within the system. A stand-alone deaerator will be costly, and therefore is not included.

A typical VC deaerator consists of a cylindrical tower in which stripping steam and feedwater flow counter current to one another. Prior to entering the deaerator, the feedwater has been pre-heated in the blowdown cooler, distillate cooler, and feedwater pre-heater.

As the temperature of the feedwater increases, the non-condensable contained within the feed have a greater tendency to become liberated. Entering the deaerator at a temperature greater than 93°C, the feedwater is sprayed through a nozzle, which disperses the water in the shape of a cone to maximize the contact area with the counter current stripping steam.

The majority of gases within the feedwater are liberated at this point, and are vented to atmosphere. The evaporator vent is the source of the stripping steam which provides additional heating to the feedwater, prior to entering the evaporator. Deaerators are usually constructed of stainless steel.

Commentary

Deaerator Function and Design

Core Concept: How a Deaerator Works

A deaerator is essentially a countercurrent gas-liquid contact tower:

  1. Feedwater is sprayed in at the top (cone-shaped nozzles increase the contact area)
  2. Stripping steam flows upward from the bottom
  3. The two streams contact each other in countercurrent flow, and the steam "carries away" the dissolved gases
  4. The deaerated feedwater leaves the bottom and enters the evaporator
  5. The stripped gases are vented to atmosphere from the top

Key condition: the feedwater must first be preheated to >93°C, because the higher the temperature, the lower the gas solubility and the more easily gases are released.

ME vs VC Differences

This is an important system design difference:

  • VC stills: a deaerator can easily be built in (lower operating pressure, more flexible configuration)
  • ME stills: because of high-pressure operation and the series arrangement of effects, it is difficult to build in a deaerator; a separate external deaerator is too costly and is usually omitted

This means ME systems rely more heavily on correct venting design to handle the non-condensable gases released during evaporation.

Analogy: Opening a Soda Bottle

A cold soda produces only a few bubbles when opened. But if you warm the soda first and then open it, the bubbles burst out violently - this is the principle a deaerator uses. When feedwater is preheated above 93°C before being sprayed into the deaeration tower, the dissolved CO2 and O2 are released as quickly as from a shaken soda.

11.6.5 Typical Water Analysis Data

Original Text

11.6.5 Typical Water Analysis

The table below provides typical water analysis of a feed source for a high-purity water distillation system, and is used to compare pretreatment and operating cost for a 5-effect ME with a VC.

Feedwater Analysis — City of Ocala, FL. Plant Effluent

High Purity Water Production Costs — 600 GPH

Associated Costs (US$/year, based on 7000 hr/yr operation)

Utility costs basis: Steam $7.75/1000 lbs, Electricity $0.07/kWh, NaOH $0.13/lb, Brine $0.05/lb, HCl $0.06/lb, Cooling water $0.20/1000 gal.

Commentary

Water Analysis and Cost Comparison

Core Concept: Feedwater Quality Determines the System Choice

This example uses municipal tap water from Ocala, Florida, USA, with a TDS of 157 mg/l, which is moderately hard water. Note several key constituents:

  • Calcium 38 mg/l + Magnesium 14 mg/l = high hardness → high scaling risk
  • Sulfate 111 mg/l = the main anion, which influences the choice of pretreatment
  • Chlorine 13 mg/l = fairly high chlorine content, so ME systems need particular attention to corrosion
  • pH 8.2 = slightly alkaline, with a tendency toward calcium carbonate scaling

Key Point: The Cost Difference Is Striking

For the same 600 GPH output, operating costs differ greatly:

  • Hot system (82°C): VC $12.34 vs ME $20.95 per 1000 gal - VC is 41% cheaper
  • Cold system (30°C): VC $5.63 vs ME $21.23 per 1000 gal - VC is 73% cheaper
  • The VC advantage is most pronounced in the cold system because ME steam consumption stays the same regardless of outlet temperature (1,471 lbs/hr)

Cost Structure Analysis

The cost structures of ME and VC are completely different:

  • ME: steam is the largest cost (~91%), and electricity is very low ($736/year). Acid/caustic pretreatment is required.
  • VC: electricity is the largest cost ($13,000/year), and steam usage is much lower. Brine regeneration is required. No cooling water is needed.

The key question when choosing: is steam or electricity cheaper at your site? Some plants have surplus waste steam available, in which case ME has the advantage. Conversely, where electricity is plentiful, VC is more economical.

Pretreatment Differences

  • ME requires: acid (HCl 32,143 lb/yr) + caustic (NaOH 30,917 lb/yr) → large chemical handling volumes and greater environmental pressure
  • VC requires: brine regenerant (17,520 lb/yr) → smaller chemical quantities that are easier to manage
  • VC needs no cooling water (heat is rejected through its own vapor compression cycle), whereas ME requires 18-32 gpm of cooling water

11.6.5.1 Comparisons

Original Text

11.6.5.1 Comparisons

ME can be used as a standby clean steam generator; VC cannot be used because of the lower steam pressure.

Pretreatment: VC does not require as much pretreatment as ME. See Baseline pretreatment in the main chapter.

Size Selection: The cost of the VC does not scale down as well as the ME. VC is not offered in less than 100 gph, and equipment cost is most competitive with ME in the 300 gph and above.

For a hot (80°C) system, an 8-effect ME will approximately match the thermal economy of a VC. For an ambient system, it takes 24 effects to match the economy of VC. Obviously, such number of effects is not practical.

Chlorine Attack: Although the VC still is much more forgiving because it operates at lower temperature, it also is subject to stress corrosion cracking caused by the chlorine. However, at very low chlorine levels, the problems may not appear until after years of operation.

At the relatively high temperature of the ME, the attack is very rapid, and failure may occur in weeks or months.

Commentary

Overall ME vs VC Comparison

Chlorine Attack: The Fatal Weakness of ME

This is one of the risks most often overlooked when selecting a distillation system:

  • ME operates at high temperature, and chloride attack in the form of stress corrosion cracking (SCC) of stainless steel is extremely rapid
  • If residual chlorine in the feedwater is not completely removed, an ME may develop cracks within weeks to months
  • Because VC operates at lower temperature, even if a small amount of chlorine remains, the problem may not appear for years
  • Key takeaway: ME pretreatment must ensure thorough dechlorination (activated carbon or a reducing agent)

Selection Recommendation Summary

  • Choose ME when: a backup clean steam source is needed, output is small (<300 gph), site steam cost is low, or a hot system is required
  • Choose VC when: output is ≥300 gph, a cold system is needed, electricity is cheap, simpler pretreatment is desired, and no backup steam is needed
  • Both require attention: dechlorination must be thorough, and ME is especially demanding

Practice Questions

  1. Your facility needs 500 gph WFI at 80°C, with abundant low-cost steam but expensive electricity. Which distillation system would you recommend and why?
  2. A 5-effect ME distiller suddenly shows elevated conductivity in the distillate. What is the most likely cause related to non-condensable gases?
  3. Your VC distiller feedwater has a TDS of 200 mg/l (softened water). Estimate the approximate recovery ratio and explain why it cannot be higher.
  4. An engineer proposes adding a 9th effect to your 8-effect ME system. What economic argument would you use to evaluate this proposal?
  5. Your ME system uses city water with 15 mg/l residual chlorine. The pretreatment carbon bed is aging. What is the risk and what monitoring would you implement?

Section Summary

  • Thermal Efficiency: Performance Ratio (R = Md/Ms) and Thermal Economy (E = Md/1000 BTU) quantify distiller efficiency; SE R~0.82, 5ME R~3.39-7.11, VC R~27.67
  • Water Recovery: Rc = Md/Mf; deionized feed achieves 90-95%, softened feed 80-85%; limited by scaling potential
  • ME Effects: More effects reduce energy but not output; typical 3-5 effects; limited by temperature range (100-125 psig top, 7-10 psig bottom) and equipment cost
  • Non-Condensable Gases: CO2 and O2 cause gas blanketing (reduced output) and corrosion; proper venting is imperative; steady steam at vent confirms proper operation
  • Deaeration: VC can incorporate internal deaerators; ME typically cannot due to high pressure; feedwater preheated >93°C for effective gas stripping
  • Cost Comparison: VC is 41-73% cheaper in operating cost than 5ME; ME advantage: can serve as backup clean steam generator; VC advantage: lower pretreatment, no cooling water
  • Chlorine Attack: Critical risk for ME (failure in weeks/months at high temp); VC more tolerant but still at risk; thorough dechlorination essential
A7-9

Chapter A7-9: Appendix: Steam & Fabrication

p211-p236

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p211-p221

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the selection principles for Clean Steam Generators, including the calculation of the relationship between pressure differential and output
  • Understand how feed water pressure, pump selection and contact surface materials affect clean steam quality
  • Learn utility requirements planning: plant steam flow, feed water rate, electrical power and instrument air
  • Become familiar with the operating practices of periodic cleaning, derouging and conductivity monitoring
  • Understand the core concepts of microbial control in pharmaceutical water systems: Gram-negative bacteria, biofilm and endotoxin
  • Master the microbial limits for PW and WFI, alert/action levels and sampling strategies
  • Compare the sanitization design options for hot systems, cold systems, ozone systems and ultraviolet (UV) systems
  • Understand ozone generation methods, installation points and the advantages of ozone over chlorine disinfection
  • Master UV lamp design and selection: dose calculation, differences between low-pressure and medium-pressure lamps, and temperature and pressure limits

Chapter Contents

11.7 CS Generator Sizing 11.7.1 Feed Water & Surfaces 11.7.2 Utilities 11.7.3 Cleaning & Derouging 11.7.4 Conductivity 11.8.1 Bacteria Basics 11.8.2 Testing & Documentation 11.8.3 Sanitization Designs 11.8.4 Hot System 11.8.5 Cold System 11.8.6 Ozone 11.8.7 UV Light
11.7 Clean Steam Generator - System Selection & Sizing

Original Text

A generator designed to deliver the maximum output at a relatively high clean steam pressure will deliver significantly more steam at the lowest pressure and vice versa.

This appears to be an advantage and often is; however, there can be problems if the performance is not well understood and is taken into consideration at the design stage and later during operation.

Example Calculation

A generator is sized to deliver 2000 lbs/hr (909 kgs/hr) clean steam at 60 psig (4.2 kgs/cm² gauge), utilizing plant steam at 120 psig (8.43 kgs/cm² gauge). Operating at a reduced level of 30 psig (2.1 kgs/cm² gauge) clean steam pressure and assuming 5 psi (0.35 kgs/cm²) pressure drop across the steam control valve:

  • Net steam pressure to evaporator = 120 - 5 = 115 psig (8.08 kgs/cm²), saturated temperature 347.3°F (175.17°C)
  • CS temperature @ 60 psig = 307.63°F (153.13°C)
  • CS temperature @ 30 psig = 274.46°F (134.7°C)
  • ΔT @ 60 psig = 347.3 - 307.63 = 39.67°F (22°C)
  • ΔT @ 30 psig = 347.3 - 274.6 = 72.7°F (40.5°C)

The amount of steam produced: W = U × A × ΔT

Where: U = heat transfer coefficient (Btu/hr/ft²/°F), A = CS evaporator surface area (ft²).

Since the heat transfer surface is fixed, and the change in the heat transfer coefficient is negligible, the amount of clean steam produced is directly proportional to the temperature difference.

CS @ 30 psig = (72.7 / 39.67) × 2000 = 3,665 lbs/hr (1666 kgs/hr), or 183% of that at 60 psig.

Total volume of steam @ 30 psig = 9.403 × 3665 = 34,462 ft³/hr, or 296% of that @ 60 psig. Since the steam passage areas including the separator have not changed, the velocity of steam increases approximately 300%.

If this increase is not anticipated and planned for at the design stage, there can be a potential for carryover of contaminants through the separator due to the higher steam velocity.

In addition to the high velocity and carryover considerations, operating at a temperature difference above 70°F (21°C) is not recommended without consulting the CS manufacturer. At high temperature difference, a heat transfer phenomenon called "dry wall condition" can occur — the steam is so much hotter than the water on the other side that the surface cannot be maintained wet, leaving behind a residue (scale). The problem is more of a concern when the CS feed water has relatively high TDS, such as with softened water.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Relationship Between Pressure Differential and Output

A clean steam generator is essentially a heat exchanger: it heats feed water with plant steam and evaporates it to produce clean steam. Output depends on the temperature difference (ΔT) between the two.

When you lower the clean steam pressure, the CS saturation temperature drops while the plant steam temperature stays the same, so ΔT increases and output rises. This sounds attractive, but the hidden risks are a sharp increase in steam volume, a surge in velocity, and a separator that may no longer capture impurities effectively.

Key Formula

W = U × A × ΔT

W = steam output (lbs/hr)
U = heat transfer coefficient (Btu/hr/ft²/°F) -- approximately constant
A = evaporator area (ft²) -- fixed
ΔT = plant steam temperature - CS saturation temperature

Conclusion: output is proportional to ΔT

Analogy

Imagine boiling a pot of water over a high flame, with a fixed pot size (A unchanged) and constant heat output (U unchanged). If you loosen the pressure valve on the lid (lowering the CS pressure), the boiling point of the water drops, the temperature difference between the flame and the boiling water grows, and steam is generated faster. But the vent hole on the pot has not gotten any bigger, so the steam rushes out so fast that it may carry some of the liquid (impurities) out with it. That is the principle behind carryover.

Key Notes

  • ΔT > 70°F (21°C): Consult the manufacturer, otherwise there is a risk of a dry wall condition, in which overheating of the evaporating surface causes scale formation
  • Steam velocity increase of 300%: If the separator design did not take this operating condition into account, endotoxins and salts can pass through (carryover)
  • High-TDS feed water is more dangerous: Softened water has a higher TDS, and the dry wall effect accelerates scaling

Practice Questions

  1. If your plant steam pressure is 100 psig and the required clean steam pressure is 45 psig, approximately what is ΔT? Does it exceed the recommended upper limit of 70°F?
  2. Why does ISPE not recommend operating at a high ΔT even though output can be increased substantially? What is the impact on quality risk?
11.7.1 Feed Water Pressure, Pump & Contact Surfaces

Original Text

11.7.1.2 Feed Water Pressure and Pump

The feed pressure is typically 8-10 psi (0.5-0.7 kgs/cm²) above the clean steam pressure, which is typically 30-60 psig (2.1-4.2 kgs/cm² gauge), a feed pressure range of approximately 40-70 psig (2.8-4.9 kgs/cm² gauge). Most CS generators have outputs of less than 4,000 lbs/hr (1,816 kgs/hr). Many have outputs of less than 1,000 lbs/hr (450 kgs/hr). That is a feed rate of slightly more than 2 gpm (7.5 l/min).

The sanitary centrifugal feed pumps favored by the pharmaceutical industry will be operating near shutoff head at flow rates of less than 10 gpm (38 l/min), particularly with very small generators. Installing pump circulation loops complicates piping, and under certain scenarios may call for installing a heat exchanger to address temperature buildup. This adds undue complexity.

Consider the possibility of feeding the generator from an existing source with sufficient pressure. The dedicated pump, even at very low flow rate, is most likely the next best option.

11.7.1.3 Feed Water Contact Surfaces

It is common that users specify mechanical and electropolish and sanitary clamp connections for piping, heat exchangers, and vessels in contact with the feed water although such features are not necessary for achieving the desired clean steam quality.

Often, the reason given for such requirements is attributed to having the feed to the CS piped from the WFI or USP Purified Water loop. Such practices rule out the use of the less costly Simple CS Generator which otherwise may be acceptable.

Consider the economics of the proposed feed source at the selection stage. If WFI or USP Purified is the most logical source, consider the possibility of using an atmospheric break or sanitary backflow preventers so that the CS does not become an extension of the source loop.

Tutorial Commentary

Feed Water Pressure Design Logic

Feed water pressure = CS pressure + 8-10 psi. This "extra pressure" overcomes piping resistance and ensures water enters the evaporator steadily. If the pressure is insufficient, the feed becomes unstable, steam output fluctuates, and in severe cases the unit can even run dry.

The feed water flow of most CS generators is very small (around 2 gpm), which is too close to the shutoff head for a sanitary centrifugal pump. Operating in this region is extremely inefficient for the pump and it tends to overheat.

Key Note: Do Not Over-Design the Feed Water Piping

ISPE explicitly points out a common over-design trap here: because the feed water comes from a WFI or PW loop, people require all feed water piping to be electropolished with sanitary clamp connections.

In practice, the surface finish specification of the feed water piping does not need to be equivalent to that of the distribution system, because the water is evaporated once it enters the CS. Steam quality depends on the performance of the evaporator and separator, not on the roughness of the feed water piping.

Recommendation: Install an atmospheric break or backflow preventer between the feed water piping and the WFI/PW loop so that the CS does not become an "extension" of the distribution loop. This reduces the validation burden for the whole loop.

Analogy

It is like the water pipe in your home connected to a water heater: the inlet pipe of the heater does not need the same material grade as the outlet pipe of a water purifier. Water is heated and disinfected after entering the heater, so the finish of the inlet pipe has little effect on the final hot water quality. But without a check valve, the water in the heater could flow back and contaminate the cold water system.

11.7.2 Utilities Requirements

Original Text

Plant Steam Flow Rate, lbs/hr: Make provisions for 110-120% of the maximum expected clean steam production. Generators with feed heaters and low TDS feed water will use closer to the 110% rate, and those without feed heaters and with high TDS (high blowdown rate) will use closer to the 120% rate.

Plant Steam Pressure should be nominally 50-60 psig (3.5-4.2 kgs/cm² gauge) above the clean steam pressure.

Feed Water Rate: With very low TDS, DI, or equal water, the typical feed rate is 105%-110% of the CS production rate depending on the size of the generator. With higher TDS feed water, the blowdown rate is increased to lower the concentration of salts. The feed varies and often is 115%-120% of CS rate.

Electric Power: Other than controls, the only power requirement is for the feed/booster pump when used. Since almost all CS generators require relatively small flow rates, the power requirement is mainly dependent on the boost pressure. Motors will vary from 2-5 horsepower (1.5-3.75 kW).

Instrument Air: 80-100 psig (5.6-7.0 kgs/cm² gauge) instrument air is required for systems with air operated controls.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Note: The Relationship Between TDS and Blowdown Rate

The higher the TDS (total dissolved solids), the faster salts concentrate in the evaporator, and the more frequently blowdown is needed to prevent scaling. The more blowdown, the more feed water makeup is needed. This is why high-TDS feed water requires a feed rate of 115-120%, while low TDS needs only 105-110%.

Practical advice: Confirm the feed water quality at the URS stage, because it directly affects the sizing of the utilities. If the feed is later changed from DI water to softened water, all the utility calculations have to be redone.

11.7.3 Periodic Cleaning and Derouging

Original Text

It is common for CS generators and other stainless steel equipment used in high purity steam and water applications, to develop rouge or scale. Some of the cleaning chemicals can be very harsh and may etch the stainless surface. The extra cost on electropolishing becomes wasted after one harsh cleaning.

When contracting an outside service to perform the cleaning and any subsequent passivation, ensure chemicals used do not cause etching. It is common to avoid passivation with nitric acid if the surface is electropolished, using citric acid and ammonium citrate instead.

Tutorial Commentary

The Nature of Rouge

Rouge is an iron oxide deposit formed on stainless steel in high-temperature purified water/steam environments and is reddish-brown in appearance. It is very common in WFI and CS systems, especially in equipment operating continuously above 80°C. Rouge does not necessarily indicate a system failure, but excessive accumulation can affect water quality and clog filters.

Key Note: Choice of Cleaning Chemicals

  • Avoid nitric acid passivation: If the surface has already been electropolished (EP), nitric acid may attack the fine surface structure and waste the earlier investment
  • Recommended alternative: Citric acid + ammonium citrate, which is mild but effective
  • When cleaning is outsourced: Require the contractor to provide a list of chemicals and confirm that they will not cause etching

Analogy

Rouge is like the rainbow discoloration that appears on stainless steel cookware after long use at high temperature. You can restore it with a mild cleaner, but a strongly acidic cleaner (such as industrial hydrochloric acid) will corrode the surface. ISPE's reminder here is that an expensive electropolished surface can be ruined by a single harsh acid wash.

11.7.4 Conductivity Monitoring

Original Text

The conductivity of the CS condensate sample on a system with relatively high feed conductivity is an indication of carryover through the separator. Therefore, it serves as a good on-line warning that pyrogens may be present.

When the CS generator is operating on high purity DI feed water, the monitoring of CS sample conductivity or resistivity is meaningless; especially if the conductivity is lower than the limits set in the USP.

In order to verify that the separator system works as intended, it would have to be challenged. Sodium sulfite or other non-chloride-based salt can be injected in the feed to determine the corresponding product conductivity. Recommended salt concentration in the feed is 150-200 mg/l. Based on approximately 10% blowdown, the concentration inside the CS will be 1,500-2,000 mg/l.

The test is recommended after fieldwork or cleaning which may involve the disturbance of the separator system.

Tutorial Commentary

The Logic of Conductivity Monitoring

Conductivity reflects the ion concentration in water. If the conductivity of the CS condensate rises, dissolved salts have "passed through" the separator from the evaporator into the steam, which also means pyrogens (endotoxins) may have been carried over with them.

But there is a catch: If your feed water is itself high-purity DI water (extremely low conductivity), the conductivity of the CS condensate will naturally be very low as well, and you will not detect any difference even if the separator has failed. This is why ISPE says that conductivity monitoring is meaningless for systems with DI feed water.

Key Note: Separator Challenge Test

When conductivity monitoring is unreliable, a challenge test must be performed actively:

  • Inject sodium sulfite; do not use chloride-containing salts (to avoid corroding stainless steel)
  • Salt concentration in the feed water: 150-200 mg/l
  • At a 10% blowdown rate, the concentration inside the evaporator can reach 1,500-2,000 mg/l
  • Measure the conductivity of the CS condensate to determine the capture efficiency of the separator
  • When to perform it? After every repair, cleaning or any operation that could disturb the separator structure

Practice Questions

  1. Your CS system uses RO feed water (conductivity ~5 μS/cm), and the condensate conductivity suddenly rises from 1.2 to 3.5 μS/cm. What does this mean, and what action would you take?
  2. Why use sodium sulfite rather than sodium chloride (NaCl) for the challenge test?
11.8.1 Bacteria in Pharmaceutical Water Systems

Original Text

11.8.1.1 Background

Various types of bacteria can be found in the feedwater to purified water systems. Among the ways that these bacteria can be classified is Gram positive or Gram negative, which is based on their retention of a dye after a staining and washing procedure. The results of the Gram test are dependent on cell wall structure, and therefore indicative of many other characteristics.

Most aquatic bacteria are Gram negative, and bacteria commonly found in purified water systems are generally Gram negative because Gram positives cannot thrive on extremely low nutrient levels. Gram negative bacteria are more heat sensitive than Gram positives, and cannot proliferate at temperatures above 60°C. However, the possibility of Gram positive organisms in the system cannot be completely ruled out.

Bacteria generally range from approximately 0.5 μm to 5 μm. The rod-shaped Pseudomonas diminuta, which is used for challenging sterilizing filters, has a minimum dimension of approximately 0.3 μm.

When Gram negative organisms are killed they release endotoxins; lipopolysaccharide molecules which cause a fever when injected into the bloodstream. Because they cause fever, these substances are termed "pyrogens", meaning generating heat. These substances also have been linked to much more serious reactions when introduced into the blood stream, including lethal septic shock.

Bacteria have a tendency to attach to surfaces and form biofilms. Biofilms form an external polysaccharide layer. This external film protects the bacteria from antimicrobial agents and makes them much more resistant to sanitization procedures. In addition, disinfection of the biofilm does not necessarily remove it from the surfaces. Since the dead biofilm provides nutrients and attachment points for new biofilm, its removal must be considered when developing a sanitization strategy.

Biofilms are difficult to detect since water samples are used to measure free floating (planktonic) bacteria, and these bacterial counts do not directly correlate to biofilm concentration. Prevention or removal of biofilms can be affected by the various continuous and periodic microbial control methods described in this chapter.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Fundamentals of Water System Microbiology

To understand microbial control in pharmaceutical water, you need to grasp three key players:

  • Gram-negative bacteria: The main "residents" of purified water systems, able to survive in extremely low-nutrient environments. Killing them releases endotoxin, the number one enemy of injectable products
  • Endotoxin (pyrogen): A lipopolysaccharide molecule that is heat-stable and harder to remove than the bacteria themselves. Even after the bacteria are killed, the endotoxin remains
  • Biofilm: A "protective fortress" formed by bacteria attached to the pipe wall, which disinfectants find difficult to penetrate

Analogy: Biofilm Is Like the "Algae Layer" Inside a Pipe

Think of biofilm as the slimy green film on the wall of a fish tank. Soaking it in bleach kills the surface algae, but the slimy layer is still there. The dead layer then becomes "nutrients" and a "scaffold" for the next generation of algae. This is why ISPE stresses that disinfection is not the same as removal. An effective strategy must include physical removal, not just chemical killing.

Key Notes

  • 60°C is the dividing line: Gram-negative bacteria cannot multiply above 60°C, which is why hot loops are designed to operate at 75-85°C
  • Planktonic bacteria ≠ biofilm bacteria: A water sample detects planktonic bacteria, while the bacterial count in the biofilm may be far higher than the sampling result suggests
  • Pseudomonas diminuta (0.3 μm): This is why the standard challenge for sterilizing-grade filters uses a 0.22 μm membrane, which has to retain the smallest known pathogenic bacteria
  • The paradox of endotoxin release on killing: Killing bacteria in a distribution system can actually increase the endotoxin load, so WFI systems emphasize removing bacteria at the purification stage rather than relying on thermal kill in the distribution system

CDMO Practical Application

In an aseptic filling CDMO environment, biofilm management of the WFI system is one of the core day-to-day operating challenges. If biofilm is not completely removed after maintenance, the bacterial count will rebound within a few days even if sampling results are acceptable in the short term. This is why many FDA inspectors pay particular attention to whether your sanitization SOP includes a "removal" step, and not just a "killing" step.

11.8.1.2-1.3 Microbial Levels in PW and WFI

Original Text

11.8.1.2 Microbial Levels in USP Purified Water and Non-Compendial Water

Most techniques for measuring microbial populations in a water sample require a time delay of two to three days, and they do not yield an exact value. For this reason, the USP monograph for purified water does not contain a microbial limit. Instead, alert and action level guidelines must be established by the end user.

An action level of 100 CFU/ml is suggested in the USP, and the FDA Guide to Inspection of High Purity Water Systems states that any action level over 100 CFU/ml is unacceptable.

What may be more important than the total count is a continuous monitoring program that indicates trends before they become a problem. It also is important to identify the specific contaminants in addition to the total count. The water system should not add objectionable organisms to the final drug product. Objectionable organisms are defined in the FDA Inspection Guide as "any organisms that can cause infections when the drug product is used as directed, or any organisms capable of growth in the drug product."

Alert levels are set according to a statistical review of data to reveal those results that are above the normal operating range of the system. Action levels indicate that a problem exists and the problem must be investigated and an appropriate corrective action taken.

11.8.1.3 Microbial Limits in WFI

According to the USP, Water For Injection is "intended for use in the preparation of parenteral solutions." WFI is generally expected to be free of microorganisms. Since some microbial contamination may be encountered during sampling, an action limit of 10 CFU/100 ml is commonly specified.

The bacterial requirement which is explicitly stated on the monograph is for bacterial endotoxin. The USP endotoxin limit for WFI is 0.25 EU per ml.

Even bacteria which are destroyed quickly within the storage and distribution system will release endotoxin. Therefore, it is imperative that the treatment system removes bacteria before they get to the distribution system, rather than relying on protection measures within the distribution system (such as heat) to remove bacteria.

Although bacterial contamination in a WFI system will lead to the proliferation of endotoxin, it may be possible to pass an endotoxin limit, but not a bacterial limit so both tests should be performed.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Notes

  • Alert Level ≠ Action Level: An alert level means "attention, the trend is going wrong"; an action level means "you must investigate and take corrective action." Neither is a pass/fail criterion; both are management tools
  • Trends matter more than single results: A system that stays persistently close to its alert level is more dangerous than one that occasionally exceeds a limit once
  • Identify specific microorganisms: Do not look only at the total count; identify the species as well. Different species point to different contamination sources (Staph = sampling issue, Bacillus = environmental contamination, Gram-negative rods = the water system itself)
  • Killing bacteria does not remove endotoxin: Killing bacteria in a WFI system releases endotoxin, so bacteria must be removed at the purification stage rather than relying on thermal kill in the distribution loop

FDA Inspection Focus

The FDA has clearly stated that a PW action level above 100 CFU/ml is "not acceptable." This means that if your SOP sets the action level at 200 CFU/ml, an FDA inspector will issue an observation straight away.

In addition, objectionable organisms are defined as microorganisms that can grow in the drug product or cause infection under normal conditions of use. Even if the total count is acceptable, identification of an objectionable organism is still a serious quality event.

11.8.2 Testing and Documentation

Original Text

11.8.2.1 General Testing Considerations

All use points in the distribution loop should be represented in a monitoring regimen. Testing of the water being supplied to the loop, prior to dilution in the distribution tank, provides important information on the efficacy of the purification system.

For WFI or other water with endotoxin limitations, LAL testing is typically performed concurrently with the heterotrophic plate count. The water system is typically tested daily with individual sample ports being rotated through to include each point over a specified period.

Sampling each point every week has generally been found acceptable by the FDA. With smaller systems, this may result in only one sample from the system on any day.

The sampling performed should simulate the production use of that water. For example, water used through a hose should be sampled through the same hose. The flush performed prior to sampling (if any) should reflect the flush used prior to production use.

The quantity of water used for analysis is a minimum of 1 ml for purified and non-compendial, and 100 ml for WFI. Larger quantities such as 100 ml for purified and 250 ml for WFI are recommended.

11.8.2.2 Testing Documentation

Sampling procedures should be delineated in an approved protocol (SOP). Testing methods also should be specified in an approved SOP. This would include the equipment and materials to be used, the procedures, growth promotion testing of the media, and negative controls.

Organisms in pharmaceutical water will have adapted to a low nutrient environment, and a standard growth media may shock the organisms and result in erroneously low counts. Therefore, a low nutrient media may be more appropriate for the testing.

For new or modified systems, sampling and testing should be completed as part of a Performance Qualification (PQ). The sampling regimen should include samples taken immediately prior to sanitization in order to exhibit worst case conditions.

11.8.2.3 Analysis of Results

The primary purpose of reviewing test results is to reveal OOS findings. The identification of the organisms found is an important part of the investigation since this will provide strong evidence of the source of contamination:

  • Staphylococcus: typical of sample handling problems
  • Bacillus: typical of environmental contamination of the sample port
  • Gram negative rods: typical of water borne contamination

Tutorial Commentary

Sampling Strategy Design

ISPE's sampling recommendations can be summarized in the following principles:

  • Rotational sampling: Sample different points of use each day, ensuring each point is sampled at least once a week
  • Simulate production conditions: The sampling method must reflect actual use (if a hose is used, sample from the hose)
  • Value of multi-point sampling: If only one point is sampled on a given day and it exceeds the limit, you cannot tell whether the problem is local or systemic
  • Worst-case sampling: Sample before sanitization, which represents the worst state of the system

Key Note: The Need for Low-Nutrient Media

Bacteria in water systems have become "accustomed" to extremely low-nutrient environments. If you culture them on standard TSA (a high-nutrient medium), it is like suddenly dropping a desert cactus into a rainforest: they die from "nutrient shock," and the culture result comes out low.

Commonly used low-nutrient medium: R2A agar (incubation 5-7 days at 25-30°C). It reflects the true microbial load of a water system better than TSA.

Guide to Microbial Identification in OOS Investigations

Microbial identification is the "fingerprint evidence" of an OOS investigation: it quickly narrows the investigation from "searching everywhere" to "precision targeting."

11.8.3-11.8.4 Sanitization Designs & Hot System Distribution Loop

Original Text

11.8.3 Sterilization/Sanitization Designs

Various designs may be incorporated to facilitate heat sanitization of process water distribution systems. In this section, two designs for sanitization are considered:

  • Hot Pharmaceutical Water (80°C) System
  • Ambient Pharmaceutical Water System

11.8.4 Hot System Distribution Loop

In this system, the boundaries begin with the storage vessel and include pumps, distribution piping (all drops) and the return loop that terminates at the storage vessel.

The storage vessel should be a fully jacketed vessel with the capability of heating and cooling. Heating of the storage vessel is usually achieved with steam. Cooling, when required, is achieved with chilled or cooling tower water.

It has been found that a hot distillation system has a tendency to build heat when the effluent from the still is discharging into the tank. The effluent may be as high as 96°C. If the water is too hot, the distribution pump may cavitate or boiling will occur in the pump. In addition, the system may be more susceptible to "Rouge" when water is kept at temperatures exceeding 87°C.

A hot system is generally considered to be self sanitizing as long as the temperature of the circulating water is maintained between 75°C and 85°C.

When the system is down for maintenance or repairs, it will be necessary to sanitize the system. The method used is to flush the system, all sampling points, and all points of use with hot (80°C) water. Flushing with two to three volumes of the water in the distribution system should achieve sanitization.

The heating medium may be hot liquid or steam. It should be designed to heat the ambient process water from 15 to 80°C within a specified period (generally two to four hours).

As a safety note, all exposed piping and storage tank surfaces should be insulated to prevent accidental burning of personnel.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Points of Hot System Design

The greatest advantage of a hot water circulation system is that it is "self-sanitizing": as long as 75-85°C is maintained, Gram-negative bacteria cannot survive. But this temperature window has two boundaries:

  • Lower limit 75°C: Below this temperature, some heat-resistant bacteria may survive
  • Upper limit 85-87°C: Above this temperature, rouge formation accelerates, and pumps may cavitate because they are close to the boiling point

Key Note: The Temperature Management Dilemma

  • Distillation outlet water can reach 96°C: Cooling capacity must be considered in the storage tank design, otherwise the pump will cavitate
  • Rouge risk rises sharply above 87°C: A balance must be struck between "sanitization effectiveness" and "material life"
  • Re-sanitization after maintenance: Flush with 2-3 system volumes of 80°C hot water of a quality no lower than that of the system water
