  • Heat-up time of 2-4 hours: This must be specified clearly in the URS, as it affects the design of the jacketed heat exchanger and the steam supply

Analogy

A hot water circulation system is like the central heating in your home: the heating water circulates continuously through the pipes to stay hot, and bacteria are like water droplets trying to freeze inside a heating pipe in winter, which cannot exist as long as the temperature is high enough. But if the heating is off for a while (a maintenance shutdown), when restarting you need to flush the whole system with the hottest water first to make sure every corner is heated.

Safety Reminder

ISPE specifically emphasizes that all exposed piping and tank surfaces must be insulated. Water at 80°C causes severe scalds on contact with skin. During a sanitization cycle, all points of use must be attended. This is not only a GMP requirement but also a basic EHS (environment, health and safety) responsibility.

11.8.5 Cold System Design

Original Text

The boundaries are the same as a hot system. A by-pass line may be provided to recirculate process water through the pretreatment train to avoid stagnant flow conditions.

Sanitization of a cold system may be accomplished using hot water using two stainless steel double tube sheet heat exchangers, one for cooling and one for heating.

The heat exchanger used for cooling is typically installed after the distribution pump(s). The heat exchanger used for heat sanitization is usually installed on the return loop just prior to the return to the storage tank.

One heat exchanger to heat and cool the process water is a viable alternative, but should be designed for the more extreme temperature range.

The heat exchanger for cooling may be cooled with cooling tower water, chilled, or domestic cold water. The cool down cycle should be designed to cool 80°C process water to approximately 15°C over a specified period (based on the operational needs). The initial cooling should be very gradual to prevent thermal shock.

Tutorial Commentary

Sanitization Design Logic for Cold Systems

A cold system operates at ambient temperature and lacks the "self-sanitizing" advantage of a hot system, so it requires periodic heat-up sanitization. The core of the design is two heat exchangers:

  • Cooling heat exchanger: Installed after the distribution pump, it cools the water to the use temperature during routine operation
  • Heating heat exchanger: Installed in the return line before the storage tank, it heats the water to 80°C during sanitization

A double tube sheet design is used to avoid cross-contamination between the utility medium (cooling water/steam) and the pharmaceutical water: even if one tube sheet leaks, the drain space in between prevents mixing.

Key Note: Thermal Shock

ISPE stresses that cooling must be "very slow" to prevent thermal shock. Rapid cooling may cause:

  • Thermal stress cracks in pipe welds
  • Shrinkage and leakage of valve seals
  • Damage to instrument diaphragms

The cooling rate (for example, no more than 2-3°C per minute) should be specified in the SOP and verified in the PQ.

Practice Questions

  1. If your cold system uses only one heat exchanger for both heating and cooling, what additional design considerations are needed?
  2. Why is the heating heat exchanger installed in the return line (rather than the supply line)? What is the effect on sanitization effectiveness?
11.8.6 Ozone System

Original Text

Ozone (O&sub3) is a naturally occurring triatomic form of oxygen. O&sub3 is unstable at atmospheric temperatures and pressures, and decomposes readily into molecular oxygen (O&sub2). There are no objectionable by-products or residues when water is disinfected with ozone.

Very low concentrations of ozone of the order of 0.1 to 0.2 mg/l have been shown to control microbiological growth to below 1 CFU/100ml. Due to the limited half life, ozone must be produced on-site.

11.8.6.1 Materials of Construction

Few organic materials are unaffected by ozone contact. Gaskets, piping, vessels, filters, and ion exchange resins may be subject to attack. PVC will be attacked by ozone, but PVDF and PTFE are not so vulnerable. Stainless steels that may be pitted by Chlorine resist ozone.

11.8.6.2 Comparisons with Chlorine

Chlorine is relatively stable in water and is not described by any half-life characteristic. Ozone has a relatively short half-life depending on temperature and pH. At neutral pH and 20°C, this can be two hours in high purity water.

Removal of chlorine and many of its bi-products are comparatively difficult to remove. Chlorine may convert many organic substances into derivatives that have been identified with carcinogenicity.

Ozone reverts to oxygen and/or carbon dioxide.

Chlorine requires diffusion through the cell walls of the organism. Tests indicate that chlorine required more than 400 hours at 0.1 mg/l to destroy 99% of 60,000 CFU of E. Coli. Ozone, when added to water using an efficient mixer, kills bacteria in seconds. At an equivalent CFU level, ozone will destroy more than 99% in less than one minute.

11.8.6.3 Ozone Generation

There are two commercial methods to manufacture ozone:

  • Corona discharge: uses an air/oxygen feed. Feed stream must be sufficiently low in nitrogen and moisture to avoid production of harmful levels of nitric acid. Pharmaceutical design typically employs a dryer and a molecular sieve.
  • Electrolytic generation: low conductivity water is used as the feed stream to a catalytic generation cell. Can be operated directly immersed in a side stream or produce gas for a conventional contactor. Typically produces lower contamination levels but more capital cost intensive.

11.8.6.4 Ozone System Installation

The optimum location for ozone injection is in the loop return, just before it re-enters the storage tank. To ensure ozone does not contaminate the pharmaceutical product, it may be removed shortly before the first "point of use."

Ozone destruction can be accomplished by: Catalytic, Thermal, UV, Chemical, and Time (half life in solution is 20 to 100 min).

Ozone monitoring is required: in solution prior to and following ozone destruction, return line to the storage tank, return ozone levels from loop piping during sanitation sequences, and ambient air surrounding the system.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Advantages of Ozone Systems

Ozone has three unmatched advantages for pharmaceutical water disinfection:

  • Extremely fast kill rate: A concentration of 0.1-0.2 mg/l controls microorganisms to <1 CFU/100ml, with kill in seconds (vs 400+ hours for chlorine)
  • No residual by-products: After decomposition only O&sub2 and CO&sub2 remain, unlike chlorine, which produces carcinogenic chlorinated by-products
  • Natural decomposition: Half-life of 20-100 minutes; it can be removed naturally or by forced removal before the point of use

Key Note: Ozone System Design Considerations

  • Material selection: PVC is attacked by ozone; PVDF or PTFE gaskets and fittings must be used
  • The corona discharge trap: Excess nitrogen and moisture in the feed gas produce nitric acid, which lowers the pH and conductivity of the water and corrodes stainless steel. A dryer and molecular sieve must be provided
  • Injection location: In the return line (before the loop returns to the tank), not at the outlet side, so that the entire loop is disinfected
  • Removal location: Before the first point of use, to ensure ozone does not enter the product
  • Environmental monitoring: Ozone is harmful to humans, so the ozone concentration in the surrounding air must be monitored (OSHA PEL: 0.1 ppm)

Analogy

Ozone disinfection is like using ultraviolet light to disinfect a water bottle: the effect is immediate, and no chemical residue is left afterward. Chlorine disinfection is like adding bleach to the water: it does kill bacteria, but afterward you must put considerable effort into removing the bleach smell and residues, and the bleach may produce harmful by-products in the water.

11.8.7 UV Light

Original Text

11.8.7.1 Ultraviolet Lights

Flowing water may be exposed, at a controlled rate, to ultraviolet light waves. With proper maintenance, UV systems are simple and reliable, generating a high reduction in bacteria (99% plus) when operating at 254 nm wavelengths. However, the feedwater to a UV needs to be free of suspended solids which can "shadow" bacteria, preventing adequate UV contact.

Two different types of mercury vapor arc tubes generate UV light: low pressure and medium pressure. Low pressure is near a vacuum and generates a narrow wavelength band in the 254 nm range. Medium pressure arc tubes, near 1 atmosphere, generate wavelengths ranging from 180 to 365 nm. The peak germicidal efficiency for UV light is between 254 and 265 nm.

11.8.7.2 Design and Installation of UV Lights

Water from a storage tank can be recirculated through UV light systems so that the entire system volume is treated. The minimum recycle rate to maintain disinfection is between 4-6 times per hour.

Conventional UV units have a maximum temperature limitation of 120°F (48.9°C) and require special cooling arrangements to withstand temperatures up to 150°F (65.6°C). Medium pressure units can withstand much higher temperatures up to 212°F (100°C).

Pressure limitations also need to be recognized. Most UV units have an operating pressure limit of 100-120 psi.

Most UV units are installed in a horizontal position to eliminate air pockets.

The use of UV units does not negate the need for periodic sanitization of the system.

11.8.7.3 UV Light Dosage and Sizing Requirements

Proper UV dosage measured in milli-watt seconds per square centimeter (mW·s/cm²) at the chamber wall determines the performance. Each microorganism has been assigned a kill dose, known as D10 that produces a 1 log, or 90% kill rate.

E. Coli has a D10 of 3 mW·s/cm². To achieve a 3 log reduction, a dose of 6 mW·s/cm² is required. Most pharmaceutical water systems require at least a minimum of 30 mW·s/cm² dosage.

Factors involved in UV unit selection:

  • Flow-rate: units must be sized to handle the maximum flow possible
  • Water quality: UV absorbing compounds (iron, manganese, dissolved organics, turbidity) affect germicidal efficiency
  • Identification of microorganisms: high concentration or multiple organisms may dictate a larger UV unit
  • Temperature: low pressure units have specific temperature limitations; in very cold (5-10°C) or hot water (>45°C) the arc tube efficiency is greatly reduced
  • Recirculation rate: minimum 4 times per hour for the entire volume

11.8.7.4 Characteristics

Low-pressure units usually have multiple lamps in a chamber. Medium pressure units usually employ a single lamp approach due to the much higher output. The lamps are enclosed within quartz sleeves which contact the water. All other wetted parts should be manufactured from 316L SS. UV intensity and frequency should be monitored continuously and recorded.

The specified dosage should be obtained even at the end of lamp life (EOL), as all UV lamps degrade and lose germicidal efficiency over time. The units should be sized so that a safety margin is built in so that even at EOL, the minimum dose requirement is always exceeded.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Knowledge of UV Disinfection Systems

UV disinfection works by using 254 nm ultraviolet light to damage the DNA of microorganisms so that they cannot reproduce. The key is "direct irradiation": any obstruction (suspended solids, air bubbles) creates a "shadow zone" and reduces the disinfection effect.

UV Dose Calculation

D10 = dose required for 1 log kill (mW·s/cm²)

E. coli: D10 = 3 mW·s/cm²
  1 log (90% kill) = 3 mW·s/cm²
  2 log (99% kill) = 2 × D10 = 6 mW·s/cm²
  3 log (99.9% kill) = 3 × D10 = 9 mW·s/cm²

Minimum dose for pharmaceutical water systems: 30 mW·s/cm²
(equivalent to a 10 log kill capability for E. coli)

Design rule: the minimum dose requirement must still be
met at the end of lamp life (EOL) → a built-in safety margin is required

Key Notes

  • UV does not replace periodic sanitization: UV is a "continuous control" measure, not a "once and for all" solution. The system still requires periodic thermal or chemical sanitization
  • Horizontal installation: Avoids the formation of air pockets, which block UV light and create sanitization dead zones
  • Continuous monitoring: UV intensity and frequency must be monitored and recorded in real time as evidence of normal system operation
  • Quartz sleeve: Must be cleaned periodically, as scale and biofilm accumulate on the sleeve surface and reduce light transmittance
  • Circulation rate: The entire tank/loop volume should be circulated at least 4-6 times per hour to ensure sufficient accumulated dose
  • EOL design: UV lamps decay over time, so selection must ensure that the output dose is still ≥ 30 mW·s/cm² at the end of lamp life

Practice Questions

  1. Your WFI system is a hot circulation design at 80°C. Do you still need to install UV? If so, should you choose low-pressure or medium-pressure lamps?
  2. If your PW system has a maximum flow of 50 GPM and very clean water (UV transmittance >98%), how would you select the UV equipment? What parameters must be considered?
  3. Why does ISPE say that "UV disinfection cannot replace periodic sanitization"? What are the limitations of UV systems in biofilm management?

Section Summary

  • Core of CS generator selection: Output is proportional to ΔT (W = U × A × ΔT), but ΔT > 70°F carries a dry wall risk, and low-pressure operation causes a 300% surge in steam velocity, leading to carryover
  • Pragmatic principles for feed water design: Feed water pressure = CS pressure + 8-10 psi; feed water piping does not need the same surface finish as the distribution system; use an atmospheric break or backflow preventer to isolate it from the source water loop
  • Utility calculations: Plant steam flow 110-120% of CS output, with pressure 50-60 psig higher; feed rate 105-120% depending on TDS
  • Rouge and cleaning: Avoid nitric acid passivation of electropolished surfaces; citric acid + ammonium citrate is recommended. Outsourced cleaning requires review of the chemicals
  • Limitations of conductivity monitoring: Ineffective for DI feed water systems; a separator challenge test (sodium sulfite 150-200 mg/l) is required
  • Three elements of microbial control: Gram-negative bacteria (cannot multiply above 60°C), endotoxin (remains after killing), biofilm (disinfection is not removal)
  • PW action level 100 CFU/ml, WFI 10 CFU/100ml: Microbial identification is a key lead in OOS investigations
  • Choice of sanitization system: Hot systems self-sanitize at 75-85°C; cold systems need double tube sheet heat exchangers and periodic heat-up; ozone kills in seconds with no residue; UV at 254 nm provides continuous control but does not replace periodic sanitization
  • Ozone advantages: 0.1-0.2 mg/l controls bacteria to <1 CFU/100ml, kills thousands of times faster than chlorine, with no carcinogenic by-products
  • UV design essentials: Minimum dose of 30 mW·s/cm², which must still be met at EOL; circulation rate ≥ 4 times/hour; horizontal installation to avoid air pockets; continuous intensity monitoring
ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p222-p236

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the strict demands that high-purity water distribution systems place on fabrication precision and surface finish, and why piping fabrication must be carried out in a clean environment
  • Know the chemical composition of 316L stainless steel, why it resists corrosion, and the critical influence of sulfur content on welding and polishing
  • Recognize the five main types of stainless steel corrosion: intergranular corrosion, galvanic corrosion, contact corrosion, pitting, and stress corrosion cracking
  • Learn the causes, identification and treatment of passivation and rouging
  • Compare solid drawn and welded seam tubing, and know when non-metallic PVDF piping is appropriate
  • Know the quality criteria for orbital welding, welder qualification requirements, and the borescoping procedure
  • Identify 14 common weld defects together with their root causes and corrective actions
  • Understand the design purpose and typical applications of sanitary clamps

Chapter Contents

11.9.1 Introduction 11.9.2 Materials 11.9.3 SS Types 11.9.4 Weldability 11.9.5 Corrosion 11.9.6 Passivation 11.9.7 Surface Finish 11.9.8 SS Piping 11.9.9 PVDF 11.10 Design 11.11 Fabrication 11.11.4 Orbital Welding 11.11.6 Weld Criteria 11.11.7 Weld Defects 11.11.8 PVDF Welding 11.11.9 Remedial Action 11.11.10 Sanitary Clamps
11.9.1 Introduction

Original Text

Fabrication of the distribution system must be made with extreme care and precision to ensure a smooth internal finish that will not allow any crevices that will support or promote bacterial growth, metal corrosion, or particulate generation.

The chapter provides an understanding of the requirements of fabrication, materials of construction, and specialized equipment required to fabricate a piping distribution system.

Decisions concerning the material of construction, the orbital welding, the special handling, and the special environment required must be made and understood by the pharmaceutical manager responsible for the high purity water distribution piping fabrication and installation.

Unlike the equipment selection, the piping fabrication will require selections of material with specific compositions, welding using inert gas envelopes, and a cleanroom environment.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Distribution system fabrication: This is not ordinary pipefitting. From the outset ISPE stresses "extreme care and precision": in pharmaceutical high-purity water piping, every internal surface and every weld must be close to perfectly smooth. Any tiny crevice can become a breeding ground for microorganisms, a starting point for corrosion, or a source of particles.

Three threats: bacterial growth, metal corrosion, and particulate generation. Every choice of material, welding technique and environmental control ultimately aims to eliminate these three enemies.

Analogy

Imagine performing a heart bypass operation: the graft you implant must have a perfectly smooth inner wall with no burrs or poorly fitted joints, or a blood clot will form there. High-purity water piping is the "vascular system" of a pharmaceutical plant, and the standard is just as demanding. Moreover, this operation (installation) must be performed in a clean room, so that dust and grease do not contaminate the inner wall of the "blood vessel".

Key Notes

Note that ISPE specifically says "unlike the equipment selection": piping fabrication demands more care than equipment selection. Equipment is usually purchased as a complete unit, but piping is built on site, so installation quality depends entirely on: (1) consistent chemical composition of the material, (2) welding under inert gas shielding, and (3) a clean, controlled working environment throughout. None of these three conditions can be omitted.

11.9.2 Materials of Construction

Original Text

Material selection should be consistent throughout the distribution, storage, and processing systems. The material should be rigid, capable of withstanding steam sterilizing temperatures (as required), cleaning solutions, passivation solutions (as required), and capable of maintaining a durable and corrosion resistant surface finish.

The material in common use in pharmaceutical water systems is a stainless metal. Based on cost and ease of fabrication, this material is nickel chromium steel.

Inert non-metallic materials also are available that can withstand steam sterilization (as required). These materials are used extensively in the electronics industry for ultra-pure water systems where they have proved themselves capable of containing and preserving the high level of purity used in the semi-conductor industry.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Principle of material consistency: From the distribution piping to the storage tank and the point of use, the materials must be "consistent". This is not just a matter of appearance: mixing different materials causes galvanic corrosion, just as copper and iron placed in salt water corrode faster.

Conditions the material must withstand: steam sterilization temperatures (121°C+), cleaning solutions (CIP chemicals), and passivation solutions. The material must keep its corrosion-resistant surface quality under these harsh conditions.

Key Notes

ISPE plants an important hint here: non-metallic materials (the PVDF discussed later) are already widely used in the semiconductor industry and have been shown to maintain ultrapure water quality. This suggests that the pharmaceutical industry can learn from semiconductors. In fact, semiconductor water quality requirements are stricter than pharmaceutical ones, so materials proven in semiconductor service should also be suitable for pharmaceutical use.

11.9.3 Types of Stainless Steel & Corrosive Resistance

Original Text

Type 316L is the preferred steel for a high purity water generation and distribution system. The "L" designation indicates a low level of carbon compared to the non "L" grade.

Alternates to 316L grades are 317L with its higher chromium and molybdenum contents and 304L with its higher chromium content but lower nickel and molybdenum levels.

11.9.3.1 Corrosive Resistance

Chromium content is the most important alloying element in stainless steel followed by nickel and molybdenum.

Steels with chromium in excess of 11.5% will form a protective film of chrome oxide on the metal surface. The presence of nickel in amounts more than 7% enhances the corrosion resistance over the straight chromium grade and improves its ductility.

The performance of stainless steels is governed by the oxidizing characteristics of the environment, similarly to the oxidization of ordinary steels. Strong oxidizing conditions generate a superior protective coating for the stainless steel and a powdery rust on ordinary steels which eventually, if left unchecked, will consume all of the metal.

Tutorial Commentary

The Three Key Elements of Corrosion Resistance

Chromium (Cr) > 11.5%: forms a protective chromium oxide film, which is the fundamental reason "stainless" steel does not rust. Without enough chromium there is no protective film.

Nickel (Ni) > 7%: improves corrosion resistance and ductility, making the steel easier to form and weld.

Molybdenum (Mo): increases resistance to pitting, especially in chloride-containing environments. 316L contains Mo and 304L does not, which is why 316L is the first choice for high-purity water systems.

Analogy

The chromium oxide film on stainless steel is like human skin: healthy skin resists outside infection. If the chromium content is too low (below 11.5%), it is like a break in the skin, and bacteria and corrosive agents can get in. The "L" stands for Low Carbon; the low carbon content reduces the risk of chromium carbide precipitation, like avoiding scar tissue in the skin, so the protective layer stays more intact.

11.9.4 Weldability and Polishability

Original Text

Austenitic stainless steels such as AISI type 316L contain impurities in addition to the major elements described. These include: Sulfur (S), Aluminum (Al), Oxygen (O), Silicon (Si), Manganese (Mn), Phosphorus (P), Titanium (Ti), Calcium (Ca).

The effects of some of these elements are cumulative inasmuch as the oxygen can offset some of the welding characteristics of low sulfur steels and the weld penetrations experienced with sulfur may be offset by high levels of aluminum. In addition, the ratio of the trace elements of aluminum to silicon has been shown to effect slag formation during welding which in turn produces inclusions.

Manganese has been shown to combine with the sulfur and form manganese sulfide (MnS) inclusions on or in the surface of the metal.

11.9.4.1 The Impact of Sulfur on Welding and Internal Finishing

Small amounts of sulfur improves machinability as well as weldability; however, with the advent of modern techniques in the steel refining process, the mills are able to produce steels at a cost with very specific chemical compositions.

Low sulfur AISI 316L steels when based on the specification limit of 0.03% have some advantages to obtaining an unpitted polish, but other impurities such as manganese, silicon, oxygen, aluminum, calcium, titanium, and chromium can contribute to the oxide inclusions.

A mid-range sulfur content would be ideal for welding but any mismatch in the sulfur content of the mating parts will easily outweigh the advantages of low or lower sulfur levels.

An ideal compromise would be a level between 0.005% and 0.02% or a modified maximum limit of 0.02% since the lower limit will probably not be attained.

11.9.4.2 Heat Number and its Impact on Weldability

Weld parameters will remain consistent and orbital welding processors will reproduce similar welds if the material remains the same. Since the melting point of steel varies over a range depending upon the concentration of each element present in the steel, the amount of heat required to melt and thus attain the liquefaction of the steel will vary from concentration to concentration.

Each "melt" of steel is assigned a "Heat Number" to identify the mix or exact composition of the steel.

316L stainless steel has a wide tolerance of "active" elements such as chromium, molybdenum, nickel, and carbon which vary as much as 100% and as little as 12%. These variations will change the melting point and the electrical current input of the welding machine considerably.

Where possible, all fittings, valves, tubing, and weldable pieces of the same nominal size (diameter) should be purchased and manufactured from AISI 316L steel with the same Heat Number in order to standardize the weld quality for each tubing size.

Tutorial Commentary

The Sulfur Dilemma

Sulfur plays a contradictory role in stainless steel welding:

  • The benefit: a moderate amount of sulfur improves machinability and weld penetration
  • The drawback: sulfur combines with manganese to form MnS inclusions, which remain at the metal surface and become corrosion initiation sites
  • The biggest risk: when the sulfur contents of two mating parts are mismatched, weld quality deteriorates severely

Ideal range: 0.005% to 0.02%. This is the "sweet spot" given by ISPE, balancing weld quality and surface finish.

Why Heat Number Control Matters in Practice

This is a key point that many new engineers overlook. The 316L specification range is actually quite wide: Cr can range from 16% to 18% and Mo from 2% to 3%, and these differences significantly change the melting point. If, on the same line, the tube is Heat #A, the elbow is Heat #B and the valve is Heat #C, the welding parameters must be readjusted at every joint, and consistent quality is hard to guarantee.

Practical advice: At purchase, require that all components of the same size (tube, fittings, valves) come from the same heat number. Heat numbers for valves and fittings are harder to control, but at least the tubing should be uniform.

Analogy

A heat number is like the batch number of flour in baking. Different batches of flour from the same brand may differ slightly in protein content. If you switch flour batches halfway through making a cake, the texture will be different. Welding works the same way: even if all are nominally 316L, steel from different heats can melt quite differently under the same welding parameters. So use "the same batch of flour" for the whole cake.

Practice Questions

  1. If the supplier cannot provide fittings and tubing from the same heat number, what remedial measures should you take?
  2. Why does ISPE recommend a sulfur upper limit of 0.02% instead of the original specification of 0.03%? What is the effect on the quality of the inner pipe wall?
11.9.5 Corrosion Hazards of Stainless Steels

Original Text

While being protected by the chrome oxide film, which will guard against most corrosive attacks, there remains five corrosive hazards associated with the successful use of stainless steels.

a) Intergranular Corrosion — Austenitic 300 series stainless steels which do not contain any of the stabilizing elements, titanium, or columbium are susceptible to intergranular corrosion which can cause early failure or reduced life.

b) Galvanic Corrosion — Occurs when an assembly of dissimilar metals is immersed in any solution which acts as an electrolyte. Therefore, any transition of drain or condensate piping to a dissimilar metal should use a dielectric union to prevent electrolysis.

c) Contact Corrosion — Occurs when small pieces of carbon steel, scale, copper or other foreign material is lodged on the surface of stainless steel.

d) Pitting or Pinhole Corrosion — Solutions containing chlorides may attack stainless steel in a pitting action. This is usually due to high concentrations of the chloride ion due to evaporation.

e) Stress Corrosion Cracking — Chloride solutions are the worst offenders in promoting stress cracking. Cracking is most likely to occur in hot rather than cold solutions. High and low stresses in the same member produce a condition likely to result in stress corrosion if chlorides are present.

Corrective action is to keep the internal strain in stainless steels as low as possible by fully annealing or utilizing stress relieved versions of stainless steels at 1200°F.

Select tubing with good concentricity and close wall thickness tolerance to avoid high and uneven stresses when tubes are rolled into tube bundle headers etc. Avoid joining dissimilar metals and avoid cyclic bending.

Tutorial Commentary

CDMO Practical Warning

Contact corrosion is the easiest mistake to make on a construction site. If the installation crew has previously worked on carbon steel piping and then uses the same tools on stainless steel, carbon steel particles left on the grinding wheel become embedded in the stainless steel surface and form corrosion nuclei. This is why ISPE later requires a "clean area devoid of any equipment previously used on carbon steels".

Chlorides are the natural enemy of stainless steel. In a CDMO environment, if a cleaning formulation contains sodium hypochlorite (NaClO), it must be rinsed out thoroughly after use. Otherwise residual chloride ions concentrate as they evaporate on the pipe wall and cause pitting. The risk is higher in hot systems (WFI loops).

Analogy

Imagine the chromium oxide film on stainless steel as armor. The five types of corrosion are like five different kinds of attack: intergranular corrosion seeps in through the seams of the armor (along grain boundaries); galvanic corrosion is a "chemical battery" created when two metals are paired, attacking itself; contact corrosion is an enemy spy (carbon steel fragments) infiltrating our ranks; pitting is a precise sniper shot (chloride ions finding a weak spot); and stress corrosion cracking is the armor itself splitting under high stress. Each attack needs a different defensive strategy.

11.9.6 Corrosion Protection, Passivation & Rouging

Original Text

11.9.6 Corrosion Protection of Stainless Steel

Surface protection of chromium nickel stainless steel is inherent when chromium levels are in excess of 11.5%.

A passive or inert surface is established naturally with these steels due to exposure to air, aerated water, and other oxidizing atmospheres.

Airborne impurities, heating, and other direct contact materials can damage this protective film causing the metal to be compromised in its ability to ward off the corrosive process.

11.9.6.1 Passivation of Stainless Steels to Restore Protective Film

Passivation is the process that establishes a surface or film on nickel-chrome steels and maximizes the corrosion resistance.

The passivation process cleans the exposed surfaces of the contaminates, soils, and surface impurities that cause film damage. The process forms a new strong protective film in areas that have become "active" or sensitized due to welding.

11.9.6.2 Rouging or Ferric Oxide Discoloration of SS Systems

Rouging is seen in many water systems, but is more prominent in hot, distilled, and clean steam systems.

Rouge can be in the form of dust or a light film that can be wiped off. Rouge also can be in the form of a bonded multi-layer that requires scraping with a sharp tool to remove.

Rouge is found in many forms:

  • Orange — found in high purity/high temperature systems
  • Light Red — found in high purity/high temperature systems
  • Red — found in high purity/high temperature systems
  • Reddish Brown — found in high purity/high temperature systems
  • Purple — found in clean steam and high temperature water systems
  • Blue — found in clean steam systems
  • Gray — found in clean steam systems
  • Black — found in clean steam systems

Tutorial Commentary

The Principle of Passivation

Passivation does not "plate" anything onto the surface. It uses a chemical treatment (usually nitric acid or citric acid) to remove iron, contaminants and inclusions from the surface, exposing the chromium atoms so that a dense protective film of chromium oxide (Cr2O3) forms naturally in air.

When passivation is needed:

  • After a new system has been installed (pipe welding destroys the original protective film)
  • After system repair or modification
  • After rouge is found
  • As periodic preventive maintenance

Rouge Classification and Countermeasures

The color of rouge is the "thermometer" of system health:

  • Orange / light red: early-stage rouge, which can usually be removed by wiping with acid, followed by passivation
  • Reddish brown: mid-stage rouge, which requires chemical cleaning (CIP derouging) plus re-passivation
  • Purple / blue / gray / black: high-temperature rouge found in steam systems. This is in the Fe3O4 (magnetite) form, is very difficult to remove, and may require scraping off and re-electropolishing

Practical experience: In a CDMO plant, hot WFI circulation systems (80°C+) almost always develop some degree of rouge. The key is to establish a regular monitoring and derouging plan rather than waiting until the problem is severe.

Analogy

Passivation is like tuning a piano regularly: the piano strings (the stainless steel surface) drift away from their best condition with use, and the tuner (the passivation procedure) restores them to the best pitch (maximum corrosion resistance). Rouge is like rust spots on the strings, with the color from light to dark showing the problem from mild to severe. Light-colored spots can simply be wiped off; dark ones may need a new string (re-treatment of the surface).

11.9.7 Castings/Forgings & 11.9.8 Surface Finish of Stainless Steels

Original Text

11.9.7 Castings and Forgings

Most castings and forgings are used for components attached to vessels, such as agitator impeller hubs and instrument housings. Castings typically contain higher levels of ferrite that can cause potential problems with components in contact with high purity water (rouging). In addition, castings are usually more porous or grainier, and will not typically take a polish higher than a 4 at best. Forgings are less susceptible to rouging and can be polished up to a 8 mirror finish.

11.9.8.1 Cold Rolled Stainless Steel Finishes

300 series stainless steel "sheet" may be produced with a very fine grain, slightly milky appearance through to a bright highly reflective finish produced on mirror polished rolls.

Mills also relate to the ASTM or AISI finishes which range from 1 to 8, where 8 is a "mirror finish." This method also is subjective and relates to the method used to obtain the finish.

11.9.8.2 Stainless Steel Polishes and Improved Finishes

A third identification method commonly used to define surface finishes is the "Grit Finish" which is the number of scratch lines per inch of surface produced by an abrasive wheel or belt.

Grit finishes come in grades related to the standard abrasive tools or surfaces:

  • 20 grit = 100+ RMS and 90+ RA
  • 180 = same as a 2B finish = 80 RMS and 70 RA approximately
  • 240 = between a 2B and a 3A finish = 15 to 63 RMS and 14 to 57 RA
  • 320 = same as an 8 finish = 10 to 32 RMS and 9 to 29 RA
  • 500 = same as a 9 finish = 4 to 16 RMS and 4 to 14 RA

11.9.8.3 Electro-Polishing of Stainless Steel

Mechanical finishing has certain inherent deficiencies, one of which is the tendency to enlarge the exposed surface area.

Electro-polishing is able to improve the mechanical finish by rounding off the sharp peaks of the "scratch lines."

The advantages of electro-polishing are:

  • reduces the surface areas
  • provides a sanitary acceptable surface finish
  • cleans the surface
  • passivates the surface of stainless steel with a chromium layer
  • removes impurities trapped below folded layers of mechanically formed ridges
  • reveals defects that have been hidden by mechanical polishing through smearing effect

Electro-polishing requires a mechanical polish preparation developed with a uniform progressive grit polishing application.

Tutorial Commentary

The Six Advantages of Electropolishing

Electropolishing is not just about "making the surface shinier". It has six key functions:

  1. Reduces surface area: smoothed peaks mean less area for microorganisms to attach to
  2. Achieves a sanitary surface: meets cGMP cleaning requirements
  3. Cleans the surface: removes residues left by mechanical polishing
  4. Passivates at the same time: forms a chromium-rich protective layer, two benefits in one step
  5. Reveals hidden defects: mechanical polishing has a "smearing effect" that covers up defects, whereas EP exposes them
  6. Removes deep impurities: impurities may be hidden under the folded layer created by mechanical polishing

Key Differences between Castings and Forgings

When selecting vessel components, forgings are preferable to castings:

  • Castings contain more ferrite, so they are more prone to rouging
  • Castings are porous with a coarse structure, and can be polished to #4 at most
  • Forgings have a dense structure and can be polished to a #8 mirror finish

Conclusion: in high-purity water systems, parts in contact with the water should preferably be forgings.

Surface Roughness Conversion Table

Ra (Roughness Average): the average value of surface roughness. A common pharmaceutical specification is Ra ≤ 25 micro-inch (0.6 μm) mechanical polish plus electropolishing (EP). Many WFI systems require Ra ≤ 15 micro-inch (0.4 μm).

11.9.9 Stainless Steel Distribution Piping & Bends

Original Text

11.9.9.1 Solid Drawn Thin Wall Tubing

The selected materials are available in solid drawn tube suitable for the type of service expected from a high purity water distribution system. This is an acceptable material for a high purity water loop and should conform to ASTM A632.

Tubing should be 16 gauge (.065 in./0.165 cm) for tubing diameters of between 1 and 3 inches inclusive and 14 gauge for 4 in. diameter.

11.9.9.2 Welded Seam Thin Wall Tubing

Acceptable thin wall tubing is available at a reduced cost with a welded seam produced in an oxygen free environment (TIG).

The tubing is available with a uniform smooth surface in accordance with ASTM A269 and may be polished mechanically and electrically to a suitable surface finish.

The metal is cut and cold rolled to form a round tube. The tube is clamped so that the two edges make contact. The inside and outside of the tube is blanketed with Argon gas to expel all oxygen and the seam is welded without filler, producing a consistent smooth and oxidation free surface inside and out.

11.9.10 Elbows/Bends In Tubing

Current practice is to use tight welded elbows for each directional change in the distribution piping. Considering the extent of high purity water distribution piping, the total number of elbows can range from 130 to 500 and from 60 to 1000 welds to install them.

The purchase of longer lengths than the standard 20 ft (6 m) lengths (say 40 ft (12 m) lengths) and the utilization of long sweeping bends, instead of the traditional tight welded elbows can reduce the total number of welds by up to 60% and the total pipe used by 10%.

Large sweeping bends are preferred in order to reduce the potential of wrinkling and/or damage to the internal pipe finish.

Tutorial Commentary

Bend Strategy: 60% Fewer Welds

This is a very valuable cost and quality optimization suggestion:

  • Traditional approach: use a welded elbow at every turn, so one elbow = 2 welds
  • Optimized approach: use 40 ft tube lengths with sweeping bends, reducing the number of welds by up to 60%
  • Every weld eliminated = one fewer potential defect site + one fewer place for microorganisms to settle

A large WFI system may have 500+ welds. A 60% reduction means 300 fewer welds to inspect, document and validate. The improvement in construction quality and validation efficiency is enormous.

Wall Thickness Specifications

16 gauge (0.065 in / 1.65 mm): for 1"-3" tube sizes

14 gauge (0.083 in / 2.11 mm): for 4" tube size

For larger sizes, choose an appropriate wall thickness for the application and location. The principle is that the wall must be thick enough to withstand pressure and welding heat, but not so thick that welding becomes difficult.

11.9.11 Types of Non-Metallic Materials (PVDF)

Original Text

Few compatible non-metallic piping materials are available that will withstand the rigors of a pharmaceutical high purity water system, such as: 80 to 90°C operation or periodic sanitization, 121°C steam sterilizing temperatures, ozone and/or chlorine contact.

One such material that will support the above requirements and limit extractable to a minimum level compatible with stainless steel is polyvinylidene fluoride (PVDF).

This material is available in a compatible range of pipe diameters, surface finishes, and automated orbital welding capability.

11.9.11.1 Corrosive Resistance of PVDF

PVDF is inert and will not exhibit any surface corrosion when in contact with 90°C high purity water or commonly used oxidizers.

11.9.11.2 Weldability and Polishability of PVDF

The surface finish of PVDF is equivalent to electro-polished stainless steel and the fusion welding equipment and capabilities are similar to stainless steel orbital welders.

11.9.11.3 PVDF Distribution Piping and Fittings

Weldable fittings, elbows, tees, reducers, adapters, diaphragm valves, zero static valves, flow meters, regulators, etc. are available for PVDF pipe.

11.9.11.4 Pressure Rating of PVDF Piping Systems

Rated pressures for PVDF piping ranges from 230 psi at 68°C to 50 psi at 149°C.

Due to softening at elevated temperatures, continuous support is recommended for systems that are operated at 65°C or above.

Tutorial Commentary

Pressure-Temperature Limits of PVDF

This is the most critical engineering consideration when selecting PVDF. The strength of plastic materials drops sharply as temperature rises:

  • 68°C → 230 psi (normal operation, adequate strength)
  • 149°C → 50 psi (during steam sterilization, strength drops substantially)

Design point: For operation above 65°C, continuous support (angle iron or an equivalent structure) must be used to prevent the pipe from sagging as it softens and changing the slope. Thermal expansion is also much greater than for stainless steel, so expansion loops or other compensation measures must be included in the design.

Analogy

PVDF is like high-grade silicone: chemically it fears almost nothing, but physically it is "soft". Stainless steel is like armor: hard enough but with chemical weaknesses (chloride ions, rouge). Selecting materials is like choosing equipment: if your water system does not run too hot and does not need steam sterilization, PVDF may be the more worry-free choice (no passivation, no rouge). But if the system must withstand high temperature and pressure, the "hardness" advantage of stainless steel cannot be replaced.

11.10 Design of a WFI/Purified Water Distribution System

Original Text

The layout and general design of a high purity water system (WFI or purified water) should be consistent and follow good manufacturing guidelines in respect to installation, support, natural drainage, flow rates, dead or stagnation areas (dead legs), and minimization of areas that may promote micro-organism growth.

11.10.1 Fittings and Equipment

All equipment that, when installed, comes in direct contact with the high purity water should use a suitable stainless steel or non-metallic material for all contact areas, except for valve diaphragms and tri-clamp gaskets.

Valve design should be sanitary diaphragm type, installed at approximately 60° to the vertical to optimize drainability. Hash marks or drain dots are provided on many designs to allow precise orientation of the valves in horizontal installations.

PTFE Teflon faced diaphragms are preferred for hot water applications, covering a temperature range from -30 to 350°F (0 to 177°C).

Connecting flanges for all valves and fittings should be tri-clamp design.

11.10.2 Natural Drainage

Well designed distribution loops should be installed overhead and point of use valves should be located at a convenient location within the process or user area, below the distribution loop.

Slopes should be verified externally after hanging and before insulation is installed.

Piping distribution from an unavoidable low point should be designed with a clear low point and a sanitary drain valve installed with a maximum of 6 diameter dead leg (no less than 6 diameters from the centerline of the main line to the valve center line based on the main line diameter).

Slope of distribution system process pipe or tubing should be a minimum of 1/16 in./ft (0.52 cm/m).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Design Parameters of the Distribution System

  • Pipe slope: minimum 1/16 in./ft (0.52 cm/m), to ensure natural drainage
  • Valve installation angle: 60° from the vertical, to optimize drainability
  • Dead leg limit: maximum 6 times the main pipe diameter; beyond this the water stagnates
  • Flange type: tri-clamp sanitary connections, easy to disassemble and clean
  • Diaphragm material: PTFE (Teflon), suitable for high temperatures up to 177°C

The 6D Rule for Dead Legs

The "6 diameter dead leg" is one of the design criteria most often checked by auditors for high-purity water systems. For example, if the main pipe diameter is 2 inches, the distance from the center of the main pipe to the center of the branch valve must not exceed 12 inches (6 x 2").

Why it matters: Water in a dead leg is stagnant, so turbulence in the main line cannot carry away microorganisms and biofilm. FDA inspectors will measure with a tape measure: this is not a theoretical issue but a practical one.

When to verify the slope: ISPE specifically reminds us to verify the slope "after hanging and before insulation". Once the insulation is on, it can no longer be seen, and the weight of the insulation may change the slope of the pipe.

Practice Questions

  1. Why should valves be installed at 60° rather than vertically or horizontally? What problems arise if the installation angle is wrong?
  2. If a dead leg in the system design exceeds the 6D limit and cannot be avoided, what engineering compensating measures would you recommend?
11.11 Fabrication of a WFI/PW Distribution System

Original Text

11.11.1 Assurance of Quality Distribution Piping

The distribution piping and storage systems should be installed in accordance with cGMPs and should be fabricated, manufactured, procured, and installed in strict accordance with explicit operating procedures.

11.11.2 Operating Procedures Should Include:

Purchase of components from a list of selected/preferred vendors. Procurement based on preferred part numbers. Procurement of other components using approved specifications.

Inspection of all components on receipt for compliance and/or damage. Verification of the bill of lading.

Fabrication requirements:

  • Receiving, fabrication, cutting, end facing, and welding to be performed in a clean area devoid of any equipment previously used on "carbon" steels
  • Tubing specification, heat lot, diameter to be used for the specific application
  • Check tubing for crushing and confirm the nominal diameter
  • Examine interior and exterior for physical damage, scratches (interior) dirt or grease
  • Clean tubing using approved cleaning solvent isopropyl alcohol (IPA) and lint free cloth
  • Cut tubing to length and finish with a square deburred butt end

11.11.2.1 Cleaning

  • Place a lint free cloth in tubing prior to facing and/or deburring
  • Use only Isopropyl alcohol for SS and other appropriate solvent for PVDF
  • Use only lint free cloths such as "Texwipe"
  • Clean both inside and outside of tube ends and do not touch the end area with bare hands or soiled material

11.11.2.2 Protection

  • Bag the prepared tubing with clear plastic bags if not to be welded within two hours
  • Weld fittings should be received sealed in a plastic or other lint free, non-metallic sealed bag
  • The fitting should remain in the bag until just prior to welding or for inspection

Welding should be performed inside the fabrication cleanroom, using TIG orbital welding for SS and PVDF fusion machine for PVDF.

Purging with Argon gas 99.999% pure containing less than 1 ppm of moisture or 2 ppm of oxygen. The complete removal of all air should be accomplished prior to commencing welding.

Tutorial Commentary

Overview of Fabrication Quality Assurance Procedures

ISPE establishes here a complete quality chain from procurement to installation:

  1. Procurement control: AVL (Approved Vendor List) + specified part numbers
  2. Incoming inspection: check the bill of lading and inspect for damage
  3. Preparation procedure: cutting, end facing, and cleaning
  4. Protection procedure: the 2-hour rule, under which unwelded tube ends must be bagged
  5. Welding procedure: performed in a clean room under argon shielding

The "2-Hour Rule" and Carbon Steel Segregation

Two requirements that are easily overlooked but extremely important:

1. The two-hour bagging rule: If a prepared tube end is not welded within 2 hours, it must be sealed in a clear plastic bag. This is because a clean tube end exposed to air accumulates dust, oil vapor and moisture, which contaminate the weld area.

2. Zero tolerance for carbon steel: No equipment that has ever been used on carbon steel may be present in the work area. Even trace carbon steel particles, once embedded in the stainless steel surface, become corrosion initiation sites. Many contractors do not understand this and use the same grinder to "grind carbon steel first and then stainless steel". This is absolutely not allowed.

Argon Purity Requirements

99.999% purity (5N), moisture <1 ppm, oxygen <2 ppm.

This requirement is one grade higher than the argon purity used in general industrial welding (99.95% to 99.99%). The reason is that even trace amounts of oxygen or moisture react with the stainless steel surface at welding temperatures and cause oxidation discoloration, which is one of the root causes of many of the weld defects listed later.

Liquid argon (Dewar) is preferred: Liquid argon is inherently high in purity (impurities are separated out during liquefaction). Adding a purifier such as Nanochem can reduce contaminants to the ppb level.

Original Text

11.11.3 Clean Preparation Area

A cleanroom/trailer should be used to ensure that welding, bending, and fabrication of high purity water piping are not contaminated with metal or non-metallic particulates.

The area should be filtered to control particulates with limited controlled access. The following procedures should be followed:

  • All cutting and welding equipment should be cleaned of any impurities or particulates
  • The room should be maintained clean at all times and access should be across sticky floor mats
  • All piping and fittings should be cleaned prior to and following welding
  • Assemblies should be bagged following welding and the bags removed just prior to making field welds
  • Welding and fitting technicians should use overshoes and cleanroom gloves. The gloves are worn to prevent finger print oils from getting onto the pipe

The above procedures are currently typical for a high purity piping system for the semi-conductor industry and is considered advisable for pharmaceutical installations with similar requirements.

Inspection of completed welds is sometimes not possible or practical. Common practice is to provide a validated process for all welds, a precise instrument to make the welds, an inert environment around the weld and a clean contact surface.

Spot checks of the outside and inside surfaces on a percentage or time basis will provide assurance of the system efficiency.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Controls for the Clean Fabrication Area

The clean fabrication specification recommended by ISPE is actually borrowed from semiconductor industry practice:

  • Filtered ventilation: controls airborne particles
  • Sticky mats: remove dust at the entrance
  • Restricted access: only trained personnel may enter
  • Personal protection: shoe covers + clean room gloves (to prevent fingerprint oils)
  • Bagging after welding: bag assemblies immediately after welding and unbag them only on site

The Concept of a "Validated Process"

Because inspecting the internal welds of a pipe is often impossible or impractical (the pipe is too long or the location is hard to reach), ISPE's strategy is: rather than inspecting each result, validate the whole process.

This is typical "process validation" thinking:

  1. Precise welding equipment (validated instrument)
  2. Inert gas environment (inert environment)
  3. Clean contact surfaces (clean contact surface)
  4. Qualified welders (qualified welder)

When all four conditions are validated and controlled, one can reasonably trust that every weld meets the quality requirements, without 100% inspection.

11.11.4-11.11.5 Orbital Welding & Borescoping

Original Text

11.11.4 Joints Using Fusion Butt Welding

Orbital welding is the recommended industry standard for high purity water systems in biotech, pharmaceutical and semi-conductor industries. This is due to the smooth inner weld bead that is characteristic of this joining process.

11.11.4.1 Orbital Welding

Orbital welding is a welding method used for joining stainless steel piping and tubing with electric arc in a welding machine not using any filler (welding rod) under inert atmosphere argon (Ar). Electric power characteristics are displayed on the control panel instruments and, if required, a printout of the welding parameters can be obtained.

11.11.4.2 Borescoping

A borescope is a fiber optic instrument with a monitor (TV screen and optional video tape recorder) used for inspecting the weld inside the pipe or tubing. The fiber-glass cable is a maximum 20 ft. long with a light and lens at the end.

With the control of the welding process and the processing area, borescope inspection of the inside of the weld is only considered necessary as a means of control and is recommended as part of the qualifying process for the weld size and daily restart checks.

Video recording each weld is considered un-necessary and difficult to justify.

11.11.5 High Tech. Orbital Tube and Pipe Welding for SS

Semi-automatic, programmable, orbital, inert gas purged welding equipment for both tube and pipe is available from many manufacturers. The system is an automatic "Heliarc" or "TIG" (Tungsten Inert Gas) process where Inert Argon gas from a cryogenic or low temperature source is used to protect the molten steel from oxidation during the metal fusion.

Liquid argon in Dewars is preferred due to its high purity at 99.999%. A gas purifier such as the Nanochem should be used during purging to bring the contaminants down to the ppb levels which is preferred.

Tutorial Commentary

Principle of Orbital Welding

Why "orbital": The weld head rotates automatically around the outer circumference of the tube (orbits around the tube), so no manual control of the torch direction is needed.

Key features:

  • Autogenous: no filler wire is added; the joint is formed by fusion of the base metal itself
  • TIG/GTAW process: tungsten inert gas / gas tungsten arc welding
  • Programmable: current, speed, pulsing and other parameters can be set precisely and recorded
  • Reproducible: same parameters + same material = same weld quality

Borescope Inspection Strategy

ISPE takes a pragmatic attitude toward borescope inspection:

  • Not every weld needs to be examined, because the process has been validated
  • Recommended for: welder qualification and the daily restart check
  • Recording every weld is not necessary: ISPE explicitly says it is "difficult to justify"

Practical advice: Establish a sampling plan, for example borescope the first 3 welds of each welder each day, plus random sampling of 5-10%. This is more cost-effective than 100% inspection while still providing sufficient quality assurance.

Analogy

Orbital welding is like a precision 3D printer "printing" a perfect ring of fused metal at the tube joint. Manual welding is like handwritten calligraphy: a master can write beautifully, but the quality is not stable. Orbital welding is like a printer: every output is almost identical. This is also why "process validation" can replace "inspecting every weld": since the machine does the same thing every time, you only need to verify that the settings are right in order to trust the output.

11.11.6 Weld Criteria & Welder Qualification

Original Text

11.11.6 Weld Criteria

  • Weld quality must meet the strictest standards
  • All welds must be fully penetrated around the entire weld perimeter with no crevices or entrapment sites. These areas are particularly vulnerable to crevice corrosion
  • All welds should be smooth, uniform, complete and flat, not concave, on the outside
  • The weld should have a uniform and complete weld bead width on the inside with little or no convexity
  • The inner weld bead should contain no concavity
  • There should be no visible signs of oxidation/discoloration of the inner weld
  • The joints should be square facing and properly aligned. Tubing surfaces should not be offset in any plane or direction by more than +/-0.003 in.
  • The weld width should be nominal 1/8 in. wide

11.11.6.1 Welder Qualification

Each welder performing welding on certain metals must be qualified for this type of metal and welding. The American Society of Mechanical Engineers (ASME) and the American Welding Society (AWS) have welding procedures for welder qualifications.

As a general rule, welders and welding procedures are qualified to ASME B 31.3 (chemical plant and petroleum refinery piping), that incorporates ASME Section IX by reference.

11.11.6.2 Common Test Methods

Test methods on installed piping are: pressure testing, verification of slope, and the last step of passivation requiring the testing of the final rinse water.

Tutorial Commentary

Eight Weld Quality Criteria

  1. Full penetration: the weld must penetrate the entire wall thickness, 360° with no gaps
  2. No crevices: any crevice becomes a site for crevice corrosion and microbial habitation
  3. Flat on the outside: must not be concave
  4. Uniform inner bead: as free of protrusion as possible
  5. No concavity on the inside: a concave area traps water and becomes a dead spot
  6. No oxidation discoloration: the inner weld must be silvery white
  7. Alignment accuracy: wall offset ≤ 0.003 in. (0.076 mm), finer than a single hair
  8. Bead width: nominal 1/8 in. (3.2 mm)

Welder Qualification

ASME B31.3 + Section IX are the standard basis for qualifying pharmaceutical piping welders. Every welder must:

  • Be qualified for the specific metal type and welding method
  • Hold a formal welder qualification certificate
  • Be requalified before the start of each working day
  • Be requalified when the tube size changes

Practical reminder: The "daily restart check" is not a formality. It ensures that the welding equipment is still in optimal condition after being shut down overnight, and also verifies that the welder's skill has not drifted after a break. Only after the first weld has been checked with a borescope and its quality confirmed can production welding continue.

The Significance of the 0.003 in. Offset Tolerance

0.003 inch = 0.076 mm, roughly the thickness of a sheet of copier paper. The tolerance is so strict because a wall misalignment creates a "step" inside the weld, and this step is a potential site for microbial attachment and a starting point for crevice corrosion. To achieve this precision, besides high-quality orbital welding equipment, the concentricity and wall thickness uniformity of the tube itself must also be very good.

11.11.7 Weld Defects Examples

Original Text

The following are common weld defects with their causes and corrective actions:

11.11.7.1 Joint Misalignment — Poor alignment of joint caused by equipment or procedure failure. Follow GMP welding procedures/use tube alignment gauge.

11.11.7.2 Lack of Penetration — Weld bead does not completely penetrate the ID of tube. Caused by poor setting of weld program parameters, material thickness and/or composition issues, or improper welding amperage from power fluctuations.

11.11.7.3 Excessive Penetration — Weld bead over penetrates causing excessive concavity or spikes. Caused by incorrect weld program (too hot), excessive amperage, or material variations.

11.11.7.4 Lack of I.D. Purge — Discoloration/oxidization in the heat affected zone caused by lack of ID purge or impure purge gas. Sugaring of the weld bead caused by a lack of ID purge.

11.11.7.5 Interrupted Purge — Short segment of discoloration/oxidation and excessive penetration caused by momentary purge gas pressure drop, commonly from a pinched gas line.

11.11.7.6 Purge Gas Purity Problems — Coloration/oxidation along weld area caused by impurities in weld purge gas or in/on the metal surface.

11.11.7.7 Heat Tint/Haze — Light coloration/haze along weld area caused by minor impurities in weld purge gas or metal surface.

11.11.7.8 Oxidation/Discoloration — Discoloration ranging from blue/brown haze to dark blue with black edges. Caused by oxygen contaminated purge gas, incomplete sealing, or insufficient pre-purge.

11.11.7.9 Insufficient Tie-In — Weld on OD is good, but ID is incomplete and does not overlap weld start. Caused by incorrect speed or time in weld program (uncalibrated weld head).

11.11.7.10 Electrical Current Fluctuations — Significant narrowing of ID weld bead and/or lack of penetration caused by power fluctuations (not using a dedicated circuit).

11.11.7.11 Inclusions/Dross — Non-metallic formation on ID weld caused by tungsten inclusion.

11.11.7.12 Pinholing — Small holes in weld bead caused by weld puddle being cooled too rapidly. Ramp (feather) the welding amperage properly and provide post purge.

11.11.7.13 Porosity — Metallic or non-metallic impurities trapped in the weld bead (tungsten or slag). Caused by introduction of impurities from outside or from the weld material.

11.11.7.14 Cracking — Cracks in the surface of the weld caused by brittle metal or very hot weld program with rapid cooling. Use only good quality materials and acceptable welding programs. Do not quench or cool the weld.

Tutorial Commentary

Gas Shielding Problems Account for 5/14 = 36%

Of the 14 defect types, 5 (more than one third) are related to argon shielding. This tells us: controlling the shielding gas well can prevent more than one third of welding problems.

Three elements of gas management:

  1. Purity: 99.999%, using liquid argon + a purifier
  2. Flow: monitor the actual flow at the weld site, not just the regulator setting
  3. Sealing: make sure the gas hose has no leaks and is not crushed, and that both ends of the pipe are completely sealed

"Sugaring": the Most Serious Gas Shielding Failure

"Sugaring" is a rough, granular oxide formed inside the weld because of a complete lack of shielding gas, and it looks like crystallized sugar. It is one of the most serious weld defects because:

  • A rough surface is an ideal habitat for microbial biofilm
  • It cannot be repaired by cleaning or passivation
  • The only solution is to cut out the weld and re-weld it

Practice Questions

  1. If on one day of welding the 50th weld suddenly shows blue discoloration, what is the most likely root cause? What would you check?
  2. Why does ISPE recommend a "dedicated circuit"? How can this be ensured on a construction site?
  3. If the outside of a weld looks perfect but the borescope shows pinholes inside, what are the possible causes?
11.11.8 PVDF Welding

Original Text

Semi automatic fusion welding machines are available for orbital welding of PVDF piping.

An elastic pressure element backs up the inside of the fusion zone, totally avoiding an inner bead.

A controlled pressure thermoplastic melt optimizes the homogeneous joint between the plastic parts, producing a consistent joint quality.

Tutorial Commentary

PVDF Welding vs SS Welding

PVDF welding is fundamentally different from stainless steel welding:

  • Different principle: SS uses arc fusion, whereas PVDF uses hot plate / infrared heat fusion
  • No shielding gas needed: plastic does not oxidize, so no argon system is required
  • No inner bead: an elastic pressure element supports the inner wall and prevents a protruding bead from forming
  • Homogeneous joint: the thermoplastic fuses at the molecular level, so joint quality is very consistent

The biggest advantage of PVDF welding is that there is "no inner bead": even the best stainless steel weld still leaves a slightly raised bead inside, whereas the inner wall of PVDF can be made almost perfectly smooth.

11.11.9 Remedial Action for Defective Welds

Original Text

Rewelding or weld repairs are not acceptable.

Weld Replacements — The following is a procedure for replacing defective welds:

  1. Cut out rejected weld using a band or cut-off saw
  2. Where possible, retain section for further inspection
  3. Prepare faces of tubing and check for signs of discoloration
  4. Ensure that all traces of any heat affected areas are removed
  5. Cut and shape pipe ends using proprietary facing equipment
  6. Debur and reweld
  7. Assign a "R" suffix to weld number

Tutorial Commentary

The "Zero Repair" Principle: the Strictest Weld Quality Control

"Rewelding or weld repairs are not acceptable": This means that if a weld is rejected, you cannot re-weld or repair it in place. The entire weld must be cut out, the tube ends prepared again, and the joint welded again.

Reasons:

  • Rewelding or weld repair subjects the same location to repeated heat effects, which alters the metal microstructure
  • Repeated heating may cause chromium carbide precipitation (sensitization), increasing the risk of intergranular corrosion
  • A repair weld cannot guarantee perfect fusion with the original bead and may create hidden crevices

The 7 Steps of Weld Replacement

Note step (g): a replacement weld must have the suffix "R" added after the weld number. For example, when original weld W-047 is replaced, the new weld is numbered W-047R. This ensures full traceability: anyone seeing the "R" knows this is a replacement weld and can trace back to the original defect report.

Step (b), "retain section for further inspection", is also important: keeping the cut-out weld section allows root cause analysis (RCA) and helps prevent the same defect from recurring.

11.11.10 Joints Using Sanitary Clamps

Original Text

The sanitary clamp system of quick disconnect fittings are designed to provide a smooth non-contacting or non-corrosive environment.

The sanitary joints may be cleaned in place, provide leak tight connections and may be adapted to other forms of piping.

These joints should be used for all distribution pipe connections to valves, sensor housings, and fittings not adaptable to orbital fusion welding.

Tutorial Commentary

The Role of Sanitary Clamps (Tri-Clamp)

Sanitary clamps are the only acceptable "non-welded" connection method in high-purity water systems. Their design features:

  • Quick assembly and disassembly: no special tools needed, convenient for maintenance and inspection
  • Smooth inner wall: two mating faces + an EPDM/PTFE gasket form a continuous smooth inner surface
  • CIP-cleanable: can be treated by the clean-in-place system without disassembly
  • Leak-tight: appropriate clamp torque ensures sealing

Typical Applications

Tri-clamp connections are used wherever a position "needs to be removable":

  • Valve connections: valve diaphragms may need periodic replacement
  • Sensor installation: temperature, pressure and conductivity sensors need calibration and replacement
  • Locations where orbital welding is not possible: confined spaces or final connection points

Note: The gasket is the weakest link and must be inspected and replaced regularly, especially in hot WFI systems, where gaskets age faster.

Chapter Summary

  • Material selection: 316L is the first choice because its low carbon content reduces the risk of intergranular corrosion and Mo increases pitting resistance. Use the same heat number for all parts wherever possible to ensure welding consistency
  • Sulfur control: the ideal range is 0.005%-0.02%. Sulfur mismatch between mating parts is one of the biggest threats to weld quality
  • Five corrosion types: intergranular, galvanic, contact, pitting, and stress corrosion cracking, each with its own specific prevention strategy
  • Passivation and rouge: passivation is the key procedure for restoring the protective film; rouge color from light to dark indicates increasing severity
  • Surface finish: electropolishing (EP) is better than mechanical polishing alone; it cleans, smooths and passivates at the same time, and reveals hidden defects
  • PVDF alternative: chemically inert, no rouge risk, surface equivalent to EP, but strength falls at high temperature so continuous support is needed
  • Fabrication environment: must be a clean room, zero tolerance for carbon steel tools, the 2-hour bagging rule, and personnel wearing shoe covers and gloves
  • Orbital welding: the industry standard, an autogenous TIG process. Borescope is used for qualification and daily sampling, not 100% inspection
  • Weld quality: eight criteria, with an offset tolerance of 0.003 in. 36% of the 14 common defects are related to gas shielding
  • Defect handling: the zero repair principle: a rejected weld must be cut out and redone, not repaired in place. A replacement weld number gets the suffix "R"
  • Sanitary clamps: used wherever a connection must be removable (valves, sensors, final connections)
A10-12

Chapter A10-12: Appendix: Design & Glossary

p229-p250

ISPE Baseline® Guide Volume 4: Water and Steam Systems | p229–p250

Learning Objectives for This Section

  • Understand the design principles of WFI/PW distribution systems: fitting selection, self-draining slope and dead leg control
  • Master the complete installation procedure for high-purity water piping, from incoming inspection and cleaning/protection to weld environment control
  • Understand the principles of orbital welding, weld acceptance criteria and welder qualification requirements
  • Identify the causes and corrective actions for 14 common weld defects, and build the ability to judge weld quality
  • Understand the special requirements for welding PVDF piping and the procedure for handling defective welds
  • Become familiar with the full set of terms and abbreviations used in pharmaceutical water systems, as a day-to-day reference

Section Navigation

11.10 Distribution Design 11.11 Fabrication 11.11.3 Clean Area 11.11.4 Orbital Welding 11.11.6 Weld Criteria 11.11.7 Weld Defects 11.11.8 PVDF Welding 11.11.9 Remedial Action 11.11.10 Sanitary Clamps 11.12.1 Definitions 11.12.2 Abbreviations
11.10 Design of a WFI/Purified Water Distribution System

Original Text

11.10 Design of a WFI/Purified Water Distribution System

The layout and general design of a high purity water system (WFI or purified water) should be consistent and follow good manufacturing guidelines in respect to installation, support, natural drainage, flow rates, dead or stagnation areas (dead legs), and minimization of areas that may promote micro-organism growth.

11.10.1 Fittings and Equipment

All equipment that, when installed, comes in direct contact with the high purity water should use a suitable stainless steel or non-metallic material for all contact areas, except for valve diaphragms and tri-clamp gaskets.

This includes all valves and monitoring sensors.

Valve design should be sanitary diaphragm type, installed at approximately 60° to the vertical to optimize drainability. Hash marks or drain dots are provided on many designs to allow precise orientation of the valves in horizontal installations.

PTFE Teflon faced diaphragms are preferred for hot water applications, covering a temperature range from -30 to 350°F (0 to 177°C).

Connecting flanges for all valves and fittings should be tri-clamp design.

11.10.2 Natural Drainage

Well designed distribution loops should be installed overhead and point of use valves should be located at a convenient location within the process or user area, below the distribution loop.

Slopes should be verified externally after hanging and before insulation is installed.

Hangers should use steel clamps/fittings with Viton or similar inserts designed for use with the particular tubing, installed for SS at least on 10 ft (3 m) centers. Support for PVDF piping should be appropriate for the operating temperatures. Continuous support of plastic pipe should be considered using angle iron or equivalent to prevent slope changes due to expansion and contraction and point stresses from hangers.

Piping distribution from an unavoidable low point in the system, such as the storage tank discharge elbow, should be designed with a clear low point and a sanitary drain valve installed with a maximum of 6 diameter dead leg (no less than 6 diameters from the centerline of the main line to the valve center line based on the main line diameter).

Slope of distribution system process pipe or tubing should be a minimum of 1/16 in./ft (0.52 cm/m).

Location of all drain valves should be totally and easily accessible.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Principles of Distribution System Design

The design of a high-purity water distribution system must meet three requirements at the same time:

  • Drainability: all piping must be fully drainable, to avoid stagnant areas
  • Prevention of microbial growth: eliminate dead legs and ensure continuous flow
  • Material compatibility: all contact surfaces must be stainless steel or qualified non-metallic materials

Key Numbers to Remember

  • Valve installation angle: 60° to vertical (not installed horizontally), to optimize drainage
  • Maximum dead leg: 6 pipe diameters (from the main pipe centerline to the valve centerline)
  • Minimum slope: 1/16 in./ft = 0.52 cm/m
  • SS support spacing: one support every 10 ft (3 m)
  • PVDF piping: requires continuous support (because it softens at high temperature)

Analogy

Imagine a mountain stream: the designer wants the water in every section of pipe to "flow naturally and drain naturally" like stream water. If the piping has a sag or a horizontal run, it is like a pool in the stream -- the water stagnates and microorganisms begin to grow. So every section of pipe must have a clear slope, so that the water drains out by gravity when the system is shut down.

Practical Focus: Tri-clamp Design

The tri-clamp (three-piece clamp) is the standard for connecting pharmaceutical-grade fittings. All valves and sensors should be connected with tri-clamps because they:

  • Provide a smooth internal contact surface (no thread crevices)
  • Can be quickly disassembled for cleaning and maintenance
  • Use EPDM or PTFE gaskets to ensure sealing
11.11 Fabrication of a WFI/PW Distribution System

Original Text

11.11 Fabrication of a WFI/Purified Water Distribution System

High purity water distribution systems using the material and finishes specified above must be joined using acceptable welding or other sanitary techniques.

11.11.1 Assurance of Quality Distribution Piping

The distribution piping and storage systems should be installed in accordance with cGMPs and should be fabricated, manufactured, procured, and installed in strict accordance with explicit operating procedures.

11.11.2 Operating Procedures Should Include the Following:

Purchase of components for the distribution and storage system from a list of selected/preferred vendors. Procurement of specific components based on preferred part numbers, listed for each vendor where applicable. Procurement of other components, such as valves, pumps, filters, instruments, and tanks etc. using approved specifications.

Inspection of all components of the system on receipt for compliance and/or damage. Verification of the bill of lading using a detailed list of purchased parts.

The qualification of welding procedures in accordance with a detailed list of set-up parameters for:

  1. Receiving, fabrication, cutting, end facing, and welding to be performed in a clean area devoid of any equipment previously used on “carbon” steels.
  2. Tubing specification, heat lot, diameter to be used for the specific application:
    • Check tubing for crushing and confirm the nominal diameter.
    • Examine interior and exterior of tubing for physical damage, scratches (interior) dirt or grease.
    • Clean tubing of grease and dirt using an approved cleaning solvent isopropyl alcohol (IPA) and lint free cloth. Repeat cleaning until lint free cloth is visually free of grease or dirt.
  3. Length of tubing and facing specifications. Cut tubing to length and finish with a square deburred butt end or as recommended by the fusion welding equipment supplier.

11.11.2.1 Cleaning

  • Place a lint free cloth in tubing prior to facing and/or deburring.
  • Use only Isopropyl alcohol as a cleaner solvent for SS and other appropriate solvent for PVDF. Use sparingly and ensure that all traces have evaporated prior to bagging.
  • Use only lint free cloths such as “Texwipe.”
  • Clean both inside and outside of tube ends and do not touch the end area with bare hands or soiled material.

11.11.2.2 Protection

  1. Bag the prepared tubing or the ends of the tubing with clear plastic bags and tape the bag to the tubing if the tubing is not to be welded within two hours or if the tube is to be welded outside the clean fabrication area.
  2. Weld fittings, elbows, tees, etc. should be examined similar to the tubing and cleaned of all dirt or grease prior to welding. All weld fittings should be received, sealed in a plastic or other lint free, non-metallic sealed bag.
  3. The fitting should remain in the bag until just prior to welding or for inspection following receipt.

Valves and instruments, etc. should be procured with tri-clamp or suitable sanitary type fittings and mating fittings should be welded onto the tubing in accordance with the system design.

Welding of tubing, weld fittings, tri-clamp, or suitable sanitary type fittings should be performed inside the fabrication cleanroom, using TIG orbital welding for SS throughout and PVDF fusion machine for PVDF.
