Purging of each assembly with Argon Purge gas should be completed for all SS prior to welding. The gas should be 99.999% pure and contain less than 1 ppm of moisture or 2 ppm of oxygen.

The complete removal of all air should be accomplished prior to commencing welding.

Welder qualifications should be performed for all welders and re-qualification at the commencement of each work-day and/or the change to a different diameter.

Tutorial Commentary

The Complete Chain of Installation Quality Control

ISPE treats piping installation as a complete quality chain, in which no link may break:

  1. Procurement control: use an approved vendor list (AVL) and specified part numbers
  2. Receiving inspection: check each item against the Bill of Lading
  3. Cleaning preparation: IPA cleaning + wiping with lint-free cloths
  4. Protective measures: components not welded within 2 hours must be bagged
  5. Welding environment: work in a clean room; carbon steel equipment is prohibited
  6. Shielding gas: 99.999% purity argon
  7. Personnel qualification: re-qualification is required before work starts each day

Why Must Carbon Steel Equipment Not Be Mixed In?

If carbon steel particles remain on the inner wall of stainless steel piping, they become the starting point of galvanic corrosion. Even trace carbon steel debris that is invisible to the naked eye can trigger rouge formation in a high-purity water environment and then contaminate the entire water system.

This is why ISPE requires the installation area to be completely segregated from carbon steel work.

Analogy: Aseptic Practice in an Operating Room

Installing high-purity water piping is like performing surgery: the fittings are the "organs", the weld area is the "operating room", the argon is the "sterile environment", and IPA cleaning is the "disinfecting wipe". A surgeon would not use a scalpel that has touched the floor; likewise, installers must not use tools that have touched carbon steel.

The 2-hour bagging time limit is like the sterile shelf life of surgical instruments -- once the time is exceeded, they must be prepared again.

Argon Purity Requirement -- 99.999%

This "five nines" purity requirement means:

  • Moisture < 1 ppm
  • Oxygen < 2 ppm

Liquid argon (Dewar flasks) is better than high-pressure cylinders, because liquid argon naturally reaches very high purity as it evaporates. Combined with a gas purifier such as Nanochem, impurities can be reduced to the ppb level.

11.11.3 Clean Preparation Area

Original Text

11.11.3 Clean Preparation Area

A cleanroom/trailer should be used to ensure that welding, bending, and fabrication of high purity water piping are not contaminated with metal or non-metallic particulates.

The area should be filtered to control particulates with limited controlled access. The following procedures should be followed:

  • All cutting and welding equipment should be cleaned of any impurities or particulates.
  • The room should be maintained clean at all times and access should be across sticky floor mats.
  • All piping and fittings should be cleaned prior to and following welding.
  • Assemblies should be bagged following welding and the bags removed just prior to making field welds.
  • Welding and fitting technicians should use overshoes and cleanroom gloves. The gloves are worn to prevent finger print oils from getting onto the pipe.

The above procedures are currently typical for a high purity piping system for the semi-conductor industry and is considered advisable for pharmaceutical installations with similar requirements.

"Inspection of completed welds is sometimes not possible or practical. Remote borescope inspection is limiting and often not precise. Common practice and the practice recommended is to provide a validated process for all welds, a precise instrument to make the welds, an inert environment around the weld and a clean contact surface."

Skin oils and metal particulates will contaminate the surfaces of the mating tubing. The mating surfaces must be cleaned and maintained clean through the welding process to ensure a contamination free weld and therefore a validated weld.

The use of a clean area and clean area procedures will protect the welds and reduce the defect incidents associated with contaminates in the welding areas. (This approach has been shown to be cost effective by the cost sensitive semi-conductor industry.)

Spot checks of the outside and inside surfaces on a percentage or time basis will provide assurance of the system efficiency.

Tutorial Commentary

The Concept of the Validated Weld

ISPE's core idea is that, rather than inspecting every weld one by one after the fact, it is better to validate the whole welding process. If the following conditions are all controlled:

  • Welding machine parameters are validated
  • The welding environment is clean and controlled
  • Material surfaces are clean
  • Shielding gas purity meets the requirement
  • Welders pass daily qualification

then it is reasonable to presume that the welds produced are consistent and acceptable. This is entirely consistent with Process Validation thinking in the pharmaceutical industry.

Lessons from the Semiconductor Industry

It is no coincidence that ISPE specifically cites semiconductor industry practice. Semiconductor processes demand even stricter cleanliness than pharmaceuticals, and the industry has already shown that a clean installation area is an effective way to reduce cost -- because it reduces rework, scrap and quality problems.

The pharmaceutical industry can borrow directly from this mature practice, without having to work it out for itself.

Why Is Finger Grease the Enemy?

Human skin oils (mainly fatty acids and squalene) decompose at welding temperatures to produce carbides. These carbides will:

  • Become embedded in the weld metal as inclusions
  • Interfere with formation of the passive film
  • Become starting points for microbial attachment

Wearing gloves is not just a "habit"; it is a basic safeguard of weld quality.

11.11.4–11.11.5 Orbital Welding & Borescoping

Original Text

11.11.4 Joints Using Fusion Butt Welding

Orbital welding is the recommended industry standard for high purity water systems in biotech, pharmaceutical and semi-conductor industries. This is due to the smooth inner weld bead that is characteristic of this joining process.

11.11.4.1 Orbital Welding

Orbital welding is a welding method used for joining stainless steel piping and tubing with electric arc in a welding machine not using any filler (welding rod) under inert atmosphere argon (Ar). Electric power characteristics are displayed on the control panel instruments and, if required, a printout of the welding parameters can be obtained.

11.11.4.2 Borescoping

A borescope is a fiber optic instrument with a monitor (TV screen and optional video tape recorder) used for inspecting the weld inside the pipe or tubing. The fiber-glass cable is a maximum 20 ft. long with a light and lens at the end. Borescoping is done for verification of the weld quality.

With the control of the welding process and the processing area, borescope inspection of the inside of the weld is only considered necessary as a means of control and is recommended as part of the qualifying process for the weld size and daily restart checks.

"Video recording each weld is considered un-necessary and difficult to justify."

11.11.5 High Tech. Orbital Tube and Pipe Welding for Stainless Steel

Semi-automatic, programmable, orbital, inert gas purged welding equipment for both tube and pipe is available from many manufacturers. The basic principals and techniques are the same for all commercial machines.

The system is an automatic “Heliarc” or “TIG” (Tungsten Inert Gas) process where Inert Argon gas from a cryogenic or low temperature source is used to protect the molten steel from oxidation during the metal fusion.

Liquid argon in Dewers is preferred due to its high purity at 99.999%. A gas purifier such as the Nanochem should be used during purging to bring the contaminants down to the ppb levels which is preferred.

The above equipment will weld stainless steels, nickel based alloys, titanium, and aluminum.

11.11.5.1 Accessories

Welding accessories are available to assist in set-up, alignment of pipe butts, purging, fitting alignment, pre-tacking fixtures, and finishing of the pipe ends in preparation for welding.

Tutorial Commentary

Why Is Orbital Welding the Industry Standard?

Orbital welding has become the first choice for high-purity water systems for three key reasons:

  • Autogenous welding: no filler wire is used, so no foreign material is introduced
  • Machine control: welding parameters (current, speed, pulse) are precisely controlled by program, eliminating human variation
  • Inner wall quality: the characteristic smooth inner weld bead has no irregular surface where microorganisms could accumulate

A Brief Look at TIG Welding Principles

TIG = Tungsten Inert Gas, also called GTAW (Gas Tungsten Arc Welding):

  • The tungsten electrode generates the arc, which melts the pipe wall base metal
  • Argon covers the weld area, excluding oxygen and moisture from the air
  • Back-purge gas (purge gas) is fed simultaneously inside the pipe
  • The weld head rotates 360° around the pipe (this is the meaning of "Orbital")

The Role of Borescope Inspection

Note ISPE's position: provided the process validation is complete, borescope inspection is not required for every weld; it is used for:

  • First-piece confirmation before work starts each day
  • Qualification when the pipe size is changed
  • Spot-check follow-up of process problems

Video recording of every weld is regarded as "unnecessary and difficult to justify" -- a pragmatic, risk-based position.

Question to Consider

Your site requires every weld to be video-recorded and archived. In ISPE's view, is this practice reasonable? If you were the quality head, how would you balance "over-inspection" against "quality assurance"?

11.11.6 Weld Criteria & Welder Qualification

Original Text

11.11.6 Weld Criteria

  • Weld quality must meet the strictest standards.
  • All welds must be fully penetrated around the entire weld perimeter with no crevices or entrapment sites. These areas are particularly vulnerable to crevice corrosion.
  • All welds should be smooth, uniform, complete and flat, not concave, on the outside.
  • The weld should have a uniform and complete weld bead width on the inside with little or no convexity.
  • The inner weld bead should contain no concavity.
  • There should be no visible signs of oxidation/discoloration of the inner weld.
  • The joints should be square facing and properly aligned. Tubing surfaces should not be offset in any plane or direction by more than +/-0.003 in.
  • The weld width should be nominal 1/8 in. wide.

11.11.6.1 Welder Qualification

Each welder performing welding on certain metals must be qualified for this type of metal and welding. The American Society of Mechanical Engineers (ASME) and the American Welding Society (AWS) have welding procedures for welder qualifications. Each qualified welder should be formally qualified with a certificate.

As a general rule, welders and welding procedures are qualified to ASME B 31.3 (chemical plant and petroleum refinery piping), that incorporates ASME Section IX by reference.

11.11.6.2 Common Test Methods for Installed Piping

Test methods on installed piping are: pressure testing, verification of slope and the last step of passivation requiring the testing of the final rinse water.

Tutorial Commentary

Why Is an Internal Concavity Unacceptable?

Concavity of the inner weld bead (suck-back / concavity) causes:

  • Reduced wall thickness at the weld, lowering structural strength
  • A stagnant area at the concavity, where microorganisms attach easily
  • CIP solution cannot effectively contact the bottom of the concavity

An ideal weld inner surface should be slightly convex or completely flush.

The Welder Qualification System

ASME B31.3 is the piping standard for chemical plants and petroleum refineries, and it references ASME Section IX (welding and brazing qualifications). Key points of welder qualification:

  • Each welder must hold a certificate for the specific metal type and welding method
  • A test weld and borescope confirmation are required before work starts each day
  • A change of pipe size requires re-qualification
  • This system ensures quality assurance across people + procedure + equipment as a single whole
11.11.7 Weld Defect Catalog

Original Text

11.11.7 Weld Defects Examples

11.11.7.1 Joint Misalignment

Poor alignment of joint caused by equipment or procedure failure.

Remedy: Follow GMP welding procedures / use tube alignment gauge.

11.11.7.2 Lack of Penetration

Weld bead does not completely penetrate the ID of tube caused by poor setting of weld program parameters, material thickness and/or composition did not allow for complete penetration or improper welding amperage caused by power fluctuations.

Remedy: Develop proper welding parameters / adjust program to compensate for thickness or material / adjust for amperage fluctuation.

11.11.7.3 Excessive Penetration

Weld bead over penetrates causing excessive concavity or spikes caused by incorrect weld program (too hot), excessive welding amperage caused by power fluctuations or material thickness and/or composition which caused excessive penetration.

Remedy: Develop proper welding parameters / adjust program / use dedicated circuit.

11.11.7.4 Lack of I.D. Purge

Discoloration/oxidization in the heat effected zone caused by a lack of ID purge or the use of an impure purge gas. Sugaring of the weld bead caused by a lack of ID purge.

Remedy: Monitor the purge-flow at the weld site and ensure the hose is not pinched.

11.11.7.5 Interrupted Purge

A short segment of discoloration/oxidation and excessive penetration caused by a momentary purge gas pressure drop commonly caused by a pinched gas line.

Remedy: Monitor purge-flow and ensure the gas hose is not pinched.

11.11.7.6 Purge Gas Purity Problems

Coloration/oxidation along the weld area caused by impurities in the weld purge gas or in or on the metal surface.

Remedy: Eliminate purge and surface impurities and check for excessive concentrations of oxygen and moisture.

11.11.7.7 Heat Tint/Haze

Light coloration/haze along weld area caused by minor impurities in weld purge gas or metal surface.

Remedy: Eliminate impurities in weld zone.

11.11.7.8 Oxidation/Discoloration

Discoloration ranging from blue/brown haze to dark blue with black edges caused by oxygen contaminated purge gas, incomplete sealing, or insufficient pre-purge.

Remedy: Ensure the complete sealing of the purged line and monitor the purge flow and purity.

11.11.7.9 Insufficient Tie-In

Weld on OD is good, but the ID is incomplete and does not overlap weld start caused by incorrect speed or time in the weld program. (Uncalibrated weld head.)

Remedy: Use high quality welding equipment and check for appropriate procedures.

11.11.7.10 Electrical Current Fluctuations

Significant narrowing of ID weld bead width and/or lack of penetration of tube ID in affected areas caused by electrical power fluctuations (not using a dedicated circuit).

Remedy: Ensure that a dedicated circuit is used.

11.11.7.11 Inclusions/Dross (non-metallic substance)

Non-metallic formation on ID weld caused by tungsten inclusion.

Remedy: Use high quality base material for welding rod.

11.11.7.12 Pinholing

Small holes in the weld bead caused by the weld puddle being cooled too rapidly.

Remedy: Ramp (feather) the welding amperage properly and provide post purge.

11.11.7.13 Porosity

Metallic or non-metallic impurities trapped in the weld bead (tungsten or slag) caused by the introduction of impurities from outside or from the weld material.

Remedy: Eliminate impurities from outside or from the metal.

11.11.7.14 Cracking

Cracks in either the surface of the weld caused by brittle metal or use of a very hot weld program with rapid cooling.

Remedy: Use only good quality materials and acceptable welding programs. Do not quench or cool the weld.

Tutorial Commentary

The Most Common Problem: Shielding Gas

Of the 14 defects, 5 (36%) are directly related to shielding gas. This tells us that:

  • Argon supply, purity and flow monitoring are the first line of defense for weld quality
  • A kinked gas line is the most common elementary mistake on site -- simple but fatal
  • Investing in a gas purifier (such as Nanochem) has a high return on investment

"Sugaring" -- One of the Most Serious Defects

Sugaring refers to a rough, porous accumulation of oxide on the inner weld surface that looks like granulated sugar. It means there was no effective internal shielding gas at all during welding. A sugared weld is completely unacceptable, because:

  • The rough surface provides many microbial attachment sites
  • The oxide continues to flake off and contaminate the water
  • CIP cannot clean it thoroughly

Rules for Judging Discoloration

Weld discoloration is the "thermometer" of shielding gas problems:

  • Colorless/gold: good shielding (acceptable)
  • Light blue/hazy: trace impurities, borderline case
  • Dark blue + black edges: severe oxidation, unacceptable
  • White/gray, sugar-like: no shielding at all, scrap

Field Troubleshooting Exercise

Scenario: you find irregular discolored bands on the inner wall of three consecutive welds. Based on the defect catalog, list your troubleshooting sequence and what to check at each step. (Hint: analyze in order from 11.11.7.4 to 11.11.7.8.)

Classification of the 14 Weld Defects

These defects can be divided by root cause into four categories:

11.11.8 PVDF Piping Welding

Original Text

11.11.8 High Tech. Orbital Tube and Pipe Welding for PVDF Piping

Semi automatic fusion welding machines are available for orbital welding of PVDF piping.

An elastic pressure element backs up the inside of the fusion zone, totally avoiding an inner bead.

A controlled pressure thermoplastic melt optimizes the homogeneous joint between the plastic parts, producing a consistent joint quality.

Tutorial Commentary

Where PVDF Has the Advantage

PVDF piping does not have rouge problems and is chemically resistant. It is particularly suitable for:

  • Ambient or medium-temperature (≤90°C) PW systems
  • Systems sanitized with ozone
  • Applications where leaching of metal ions must be avoided

However, high-temperature systems (>90°C) and WFI distillation systems still rely mainly on stainless steel.

11.11.9 Remedial Action for Defective Welds

Original Text

11.11.9 Remedial Action for Defective Welds – SS or PVDF Piping

Rewelding or weld repairs are not acceptable.

Weld Replacements

The following is a procedure for replacing defective welds:

  1. Cut out rejected weld using a band or cut-off saw.
  2. Where possible, retain section for further inspection.
  3. Prepare faces of tubing and check for signs of discoloration.
  4. Ensure that all traces of any heat effected areas are removed.
  5. Cut and shape pipe ends using proprietary facing equipment.
  6. Debur and reweld.
  7. Assign a “R” suffix to weld number.

Tutorial Commentary

Absolute Principle: Replace, Do Not Repair

This is an iron rule of high-purity water welding: a nonconforming weld must be cut out and re-welded as a whole section; repairing on the original weld is not allowed. The reasons:

  • Repair welding creates multiple heat-affected zones (HAZ) in the same area
  • Repeated heating changes the metal microstructure and lowers corrosion resistance
  • The inner wall quality of a repaired weld can hardly ever meet the standard

The 7-Step Weld Replacement Procedure

The standard procedure specified by ISPE is a complete quality cycle:

  • Steps a-b: cut out + retain the sample (for root cause analysis)
  • Steps c-d: confirm the HAZ is completely removed (all discolored areas must be cut away)
  • Steps e-f: re-prepare the pipe ends + re-weld according to the standard procedure
  • Step g: traceability marking -- add an "R" suffix to the replacement weld, to ensure a complete weld history

Why Retain the Weld That Was Cut Out?

Just as a hospital keeps excised tissue for pathological analysis, retaining the nonconforming weld makes it possible to:

  • Perform root cause analysis (a parameter problem? a gas problem? a material problem?)
  • Use it as a teaching sample for welder retraining
  • Accumulate defect data and optimize the welding program
11.11.10 Joints Using Sanitary Clamps

Original Text

11.11.10 Joints Using Sanitary Clamps

The sanitary clamp system of quick disconnect fittings are designed to provide a smooth non-contacting or non-corrosive environment.

The sanitary joints may be cleaned in place, provide leak tight connections and may be adapted to other forms of piping.

These joints should be used for all distribution pipe connections to valves, sensor housings, and fittings not adaptable to orbital fusion welding.

Tutorial Commentary

Where Sanitary Clamps Apply

Sanitary clamps are used at connection points that require periodic disassembly for maintenance:

  • Valve connections (diaphragms must be replaced periodically)
  • Sensor housings (periodic calibration/replacement needed)
  • Sampling points (periodic sanitization needed)
  • Fitting connections where orbital welding is not possible

Design principle: weld wherever welding is possible (permanent, no dead legs); use a clamp only where welding is not possible.

Maintenance Points for Clamp Joints

  • The gasket is the only consumable item and must be inspected and replaced periodically
  • EPDM gaskets suit hot water; PTFE suits ozone environments
  • During installation, make sure the gasket is centered and does not protrude into the pipe, which would create a dead leg
  • Clamp torque must be appropriate -- too loose leaks, too tight crushes the gasket
11.12 Abbreviations and Definitions

11.12.1 Definitions (Glossary)

Original Definitions (A–E)

Chinese Equivalents and Key Explanations

Reminder on Easily Confused Terms

  • Conductivity vs. Resistivity: reciprocals of each other. High-purity water uses resistivity (MΩ-cm); ordinary water uses conductivity (μS/cm)
  • Calibration vs. Qualification: calibration is the accuracy adjustment of an instrument; qualification is confirmation of equipment function
  • Commissioning vs. Qualification: commissioning is engineering start-up; qualification is GMP validation

Original Definitions (F–O)

Chinese Equivalents and Key Explanations

The Terms Most Often Encountered in Practice

In day-to-day water system operation, the following terms appear most frequently:

  • Conductivity / Resistivity: core indicators for water quality monitoring
  • Endotoxin: a critical quality attribute of WFI
  • TOC: indicator for monitoring organic matter
  • Passivation: stainless steel surface treatment
  • Reverse Osmosis: the main purification unit

Original Definitions (P–Z)

Chinese Equivalents and Key Explanations

Rouge -- The Hidden Killer of Stainless Steel

Rouge is defined very briefly by ISPE, but it is one of the problems most often encountered in the long-term operation of a water system. Rouge formation is directly related to system temperature, water quality and stainless steel grade. High-temperature WFI systems (>80°C) are particularly prone to it.

Treatment: periodic passivation, and derouging when necessary.

PW vs. WFI -- Specification Comparison

The WFI microbial limit is 1000 times stricter than that of PW, and there is an additional endotoxin requirement.

11.12.2 Acronyms and Abbreviations

Abbreviations Reference Table

Chinese Equivalents

Abbreviations Reference Table (cont.)

Chinese Equivalents

Quick Reference: Still Types

  • ME (Multi-Effect): multi-effect still -- the type most commonly used for WFI production
  • VC (Vapor Compression): vapor compression still -- energy-saving type

Both can produce water that meets the USP WFI specification; ME is the more traditional and reliable option, while VC has lower energy consumption.

Section Summary

Key Takeaways

  • Distribution Design: Natural drainage (min 1/16 in./ft slope), dead leg control (≤6 diameters), sanitary diaphragm valves at 60° to vertical, tri-clamp connections throughout.
  • Fabrication Quality Chain: Approved vendors → incoming inspection → clean area fabrication → IPA cleaning → 2-hour bagging rule → orbital welding in cleanroom → 99.999% Ar purge.
  • Orbital Welding: TIG/autogenous process, no filler metal, programmable parameters, the industry standard for high purity water systems.
  • Weld Criteria: Full penetration, no concavity, no oxidation, ≤±0.003 in. misalignment, 1/8 in. bead width. Qualified per ASME B31.3 / Section IX.
  • 14 Weld Defects: 36% are purge-gas related. No repair allowed — cut out and replace with “R” suffix tracking.
  • PVDF Welding: Thermal fusion with elastic backup element, no inner bead, controlled pressure melt.
  • Glossary: 60+ definitions and 70+ abbreviations covering water treatment, microbiology, instrumentation, and regulatory terminology.

Chapter Summary

Three Key Takeaways

  1. Installation as validation: ISPE's philosophy is to ensure quality by controlling the entire installation process (environment, materials, personnel, equipment, parameters), rather than relying on inspecting every weld one by one afterwards.
  2. Zero tolerance for repair: a nonconforming weld can only be cut out and re-welded; repair is not allowed. This seemingly strict requirement is in fact the most economical approach -- because a repaired weld can never reach the original quality.
  3. Terminology is the foundation of communication: mastering these standard terms and abbreviations is what allows smooth communication with equipment suppliers, engineering consultants and regulatory reviewers. Mastering the Chinese-English equivalents is especially important.

Self-Check Questions

  • Can you list the 7 links in the piping installation quality chain?
  • How many of the 8 major standards for weld quality do you remember?
  • Of the 14 weld defects, which are related to shielding gas?
  • By what factor do PW and WFI differ in microbial limits?
  • Why can a nonconforming weld not be repaired, only replaced?
